# Preface to the Fifth Edition.

---

The arrangement of this volume of Kant also corresponds entirely to those previously edited by me (Volumes 39–41, 45, and 46 of the *Philosophical Library* <note>The "Philosophische Bibliothek" is a famous series of foundational philosophical texts published by Meiner</note>). For the text and the history of the work's origin, I was pleased to be able to use the proof sheets of the Academy Edition of the *Critique of Practical Reason* prepared by Paul Natorp, which my esteemed friend, Professor Natorp, gratefully made available to me; meanwhile, I had participated in the revision of the text for that very same Academy Edition. The page numbers of the latter have been indicated by us in square brackets in the margin; regarding the history of the work's origin, the relevant page numbers from Natorp’s introduction and factual explanations were not yet available at the conclusion of this printing.

Solingen, July 31, 1906.

**Prof. Dr. Karl Vorländer.**

# Regarding the Sixth Edition.

---

In the introduction, and especially the variant readings <note>original: "Lesarten"; refers to different versions of the text found in various manuscripts or editions</note>, all contributions published since the appearance of the previous edition (see below, p. XLVI) have received conscientious consideration.

A remarkable coincidence of circumstances dictates that this new edition of Kant’s ethical

<summary>Karl Vorländer introduces the fifth and sixth editions of Kant's work, highlighting his collaboration with Paul Natorp and the integration of the definitive Academy Edition's pagination and corrections.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the preface written by the editor Karl Vorländer at the onset of World War I, transitioning from the historical context of the publication to the philosophical core of the work.</meta>

# Preface

main work has become necessary at the same time that a terrible war rages through Europe, indeed, across the entire globe. May Kant’s <term>categorical imperative</term> <note>Kant's fundamental moral law: "Act only according to that maxim whereby you can, at the same time, will that it should become a universal law."</note> of duty—which has taught our people to endure and heroically wage this war, which was not desired by the great majority—accompany them until a victorious perseverance is achieved! But may also, as soon as an honorable peace is reached, Kant’s <term>idea of humanity</term> <note>The principle of treating humanity always as an end and never merely as a means.</note> drive us, despite everything, to pursue anew and undeterred the inalienable ideal goods of mankind.

Solingen, November 1914.

**Karl Vorländer.**

<summary>In this concluding section of the preface, editor Karl Vorländer invokes Kantian ethics to frame the German experience of early World War I, balancing the "duty" of war with the ultimate "ideal goods" of humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Immanuel Kant, categorical imperative, duty, idea of humanity, Karl Vorländer, Solingen, World War I, ethics, nationalism</keywords>

# Introduction.

## I. History of Origin and the Initial Impact of the Work.

*Regarding the first section, compare Paul Natorp’s precise investigation in the introduction to his edition in the <term>Academy Edition</term> of Kant's Collected Works, Volume V. The passages from the correspondence of Kant and others are cited only by date, for the reasons we already stated in Volume 40, page V of the <term>Philosophical Library</term>.*
<note>The "Academy Edition" (Akademie-Ausgabe) is the definitive scholarly collection of Immanuel Kant's writings. The "Philosophical Library" (Philosophische Bibliothek) refers to a long-running series of affordable, scholarly editions of philosophical classics in German.</note>

1. I have already described the history of Kant’s ethical authorship—or more precisely, his literary plans regarding ethics—up until the publication of the *Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals* (1785) in a brief outline on pages VII–XIV of my edition of that work (*Philosophical Library, Vol. 41*). In that developmental history, we found no mention of a plan for a specific "Critique of Practical Reason" alongside the *Critique of Pure Reason*, which was gradually taking shape over ten years of labor. Rather, it appears almost certain that the *Critique of Pure Reason* was intended to include the critical foundation for the second part of his system: ethics, or in the language of the time, the "Metaphysics of Morals." This is evident, for one, from the passage we reprinted in the place cited above (p. XI) from his letter to Moses Mendelssohn dated August 16, 1783<gloss>1</gloss>, written after the publication of the *Critique*; but it is also evident from the fact—

---

<gloss>1</gloss>) Secondarily, one should also compare the letters of Hamann and Hartknoch, which I used in the place cited above (p. X), as well as Schütz’s letter to Kant from July 10, 1784.
<note>Johann Georg Hamann and Christian Gottfried Schütz were influential contemporary philosophers and critics who corresponded with Kant.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the historical background of the "Critique of Practical Reason," explaining that Kant originally intended his ethical theories to be part of his first Critique rather than a standalone volume.</summary>

<keywords>Immanuel Kant, Paul Natorp, Academy Edition, Philosophical Library, ethics, Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals, Critique of Pure Reason, Moses Mendelssohn, history of philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page VII, which ended with "fact—". The text provides evidence that Kant's first "Critique" was originally intended to be his only "Critique," covering both theoretical and practical philosophy before he realized a second work was necessary.</meta>

VIII
<header>Introduction.</header>

fact that the latter work nowhere envisions a second Critique as being additionally necessary; rather, it assumes that by securing the idea of freedom—even if only theoretically—it has simultaneously established the foundation for practical reason (morality).¹) Core ideas of the two main ethical writings (the *Groundwork* of 1785 and the *Critique of Practical Reason* of 1788) already appear in the first edition of the *Critique of Pure Reason* (1781), such as: the characterization of the <term>ought</term> <note>German: *Sollen*; refers to moral obligation or what should happen, as opposed to what actually "is."</note> in contrast to <term>being</term> <note>German: *Sein*; refers to the natural world of facts and laws.</note>, the aforementioned grounding of moral principles on the idea of freedom, the <term>postulates</term> <note>Necessary assumptions—such as the existence of God or the immortality of the soul—that Kant argues we must accept for morality to make sense, even if they cannot be proven by science.</note>, and the distinction between the speculative and practical use of reason.²) However, the *Architectonic of Pure Reason* (1st edition pp. 840 f. and 850 f., 2nd edition pp. 868 f. and 878) speaks most clearly on the question that interests us here. Although it distinguishes in the most definite manner between the legislation of nature and that of freedom, and between the metaphysics of nature and the metaphysics of morals, there is no mention anywhere of a "second Critique" yet to come.

However, when Kant began work on the "Metaphysics of Morals"—which both friends and foes expected—after completing his first *Critique*, the preparation provided by the main theoretical work no longer seemed sufficient, as it was only occasional and lacked a tight internal connection. Following several shifts in direction (which we have described <note>original: "a. a. O." (am angeführten Ort); in the work cited above.</note>), the *Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals* (1785) appeared. — But did this satisfy the requirement? Did it not offer the "metaphysical foundations of practical philosophy" that Kant had wanted to write two decades earlier?³) Indeed, this seems to have been his opinion at the—

³) For, the idea that he had already completed it and then merely refrained from publication, as I [suggested] in my *Intro-* <note>The text cuts off here at "Ein-" likely referring to "Einleitung" (Introduction).</note>

<summary>The editor argues that Kant originally believed his first Critique adequately covered the foundations of morality, as evidenced by his earlier drafts and the "Architectonic" section. It was only while attempting to write his "Metaphysics of Morals" that he realized a separate "Groundwork" (and eventually a second Critique) was necessary.</summary>
<keywords>Critique of Pure Reason, Architectonic, Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals, Paul Natorp, Freedom, Ought vs Being, Practical Reason, Postulates</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page VIII, concluding the argument that Kant’s initial plan did not include a second Critique at the time he published his first major work on ethics.</meta>

IX
->**Introduction**<-

time when he published the *Groundwork*. For he expressly declares in its preface (page 8 in our edition) that a critique of practical reason is not so necessary because "human reason in the realm of morality can easily be brought to a high degree of correctness and detail, even in the case of the <term>common understanding</term> <note>German: *gemeinsten Verstand*; this refers to the everyday moral sense of an ordinary person, which Kant believed was naturally quite accurate.</note>." Furthermore, he argues that such a critique would require a demonstration of the unity of practical and speculative reason "in a common principle," "because in the end, it can only be one and the same reason, which must be distinguished merely in its application." However, he feels such a demonstration could not be provided "without bringing in considerations of a completely different kind and confusing the reader." Moreover, he believes he has provided the "main features sufficient for his purpose" (p. 73) of such a critique in the third section of that work, which indeed bears the heading "*Transition from the Metaphysics of Morals to the Critique of Pure Practical Reason*." In any case, a separate work with this title does not appear to have been planned; instead, a *Metaphysics of Morals* is announced with full certainty (p. 8, cf. p. 45 note), for which the *Groundwork* is intended to be the precursor.

This state of affairs is also confirmed by the philosopher's correspondence in the period immediately following. For example, on September 13, 1785, he wrote to Schütz: "Now I am proceeding without delay to the full elaboration of the Metaphysics of Morals." And on April 7, 1786, to Bering: he says he will postpone the revision of the theoretical metaphysics for at least two more years "in order to gain time for the system of <term>practical philosophy</term> <note>German: *praktischen Weltweisheit*; literally "practical world-wisdom," an older term for philosophy.</note>, which is closely related to the former and requires a similar treatment, although the difficulty involved is not as great." And as late as May 14, 1787, Jenisch could write to Kant: "Everyone looks forward with longing to your Metaphysics of Morals—"

---

The assumption made in the introduction to the *Groundwork* on page VII—strictly speaking—does not follow from the passage of the letter cited there, but rather only implies that its "material lay ready before him."

<summary>This page uses Kant's prefaces and personal letters to show that he originally intended to write a "Metaphysics of Morals" rather than a second Critique, believing the common person's moral sense was sufficient. It traces his shifting plans between 1785 and 1787 as he worked to unify his theoretical and practical philosophical systems.</summary>

<keywords>Immanuel Kant, Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals, Critique of Practical Reason, correspondence, Schütz, Bering, Jenisch, practical philosophy, common understanding</keywords>

X
# Introduction
->**Introduction**<-

cs against"; just as Bering in Marburg noted to his regret that he could not find the long-awaited "System of Pure Speculative and Practical Philosophy" from the pen of Kant in the Leipzig Trade Fair catalog <note>The *Leipziger Meßkatalog* was the definitive bibliography of new books published in the German-speaking world, issued for the major book fairs in Leipzig.</note> (Bering to Kant, May 28, 1787).¹)

Of course, the "similar treatment" of which Kant speaks in the letter to Bering—previously, he had been discussing the second edition of the *Critique of Pure Reason* in comparison with the first—could also refer to a work of the same type as our current *Critique of Practical Reason*, especially since here again, as in the similar case of the *Groundwork* (see above), the "lesser difficulty" <note>Kant often suggested that the practical side of philosophy was easier for people to grasp than the highly abstract theoretical side.</note> is emphasized. Kant must have had yet another plan in the autumn of 1786: namely, to incorporate the *Critique of Practical Reason* into the second edition of the *Critique of Pure Reason*, which was then in the process of being prepared. On November 21, 1786, an announcement of this second edition appeared in the Jena *General Literature Gazette* (No. 276), in the course of which it was stated: ". . . also, a Critique of Pure Practical Reason will be added in the second edition to the Critique of Pure Speculative Reason contained in the first; this will then serve to secure the principle of morality against objections already made or yet to be made, and to complete the whole of the critical investigations that must precede the system of the philosophy of pure reason." This announcement, however, had been initiated by Kant himself; Schütz, the editor of the *Literature Gazette*, apologizes to him in a letter dated November 3, 1786, stating that in the press of business he had "neglected to announce this news, which is highly interesting to myself as well as to many others; but now I have immediately sent the notice to the printer"—"the notice" being, presumably, one drafted or at least closely [supervised by Kant himself].

¹) See also the letter from Ewerbeck dated April 3, 1787: "Oh, how eagerly I await the completed practical part of your philosophy."

XI
# Introduction
->**Introduction**<-

laid out. Now, one might certainly assume—so striking does this news sound—that it was possible Schütz <note>Christian Gottfried Schütz (1747–1832) was a professor and the influential editor of the *General Literature Gazette*.</note> had misunderstood Kant's meaning; and this assumption was indeed made by B. Erdmann, who reprinted that announcement for the first time in the Academy Edition <gloss>1)</gloss> <note>The *Akademie-Ausgabe* is the definitive, multi-volume scholarly edition of all of Kant’s writings.</note>. However, not only would Kant likely have protested then, but our information is fully confirmed by a fact overlooked by both Erdmann and Natorp <note>Paul Natorp was a prominent Neo-Kantian scholar.</note>: that Professor Born in Leipzig received the same news from Kant at that same time. For Born replied on November 8 to Kant's letter of September 24 of that year: "Moreover, I am already immensely looking forward to the important addition of a *Critique of Pure Practical Reason*, with which you will further embellish your excellent work." It would not even be impossible that Kant had sent that announcement to Schütz as early as May 26; for on that day he had written to both Schütz and Born (see *Academy Edition* Vol. X, pp. 445–448). Hamann’s remark in a letter to Jacobi on January 30, 1787, clearly refers to this as well: "I have seen from the newspaper that it"—namely <note>The original uses the Latin abbreviation *sc.* for *scilicet*, meaning 'that is to say.'</note>, the second edition of the *Critique of Pure Reason*—"will be augmented with a *Critique of Practical Reason*."

However that may be, Kant must have abandoned his intention of uniting both "Critiques" into one and the same work just two months after that announcement. Perhaps this happened at the beginning of January 1787, when he complained to Hamann <note>Johann Georg Hamann (1730–1788) was a philosopher and close friend of Kant in Königsberg.</note> during the latter's visit that the new edition of the *Critique* was "weighing heavily" on him; whereas shortly thereafter he reached a decision: "The following week the manuscript was sent off" (Hamann to Jacobi, January 30, 1787). In any case, the second edition appeared in the spring of 1787—the new preface is dated "in the month of April 1787"—without the announced addition. On the other hand, the *Critique of Practical Reason* was already nearly ready for the press by the end of June of that same year; Kant

<summary>This page explores the historical evidence regarding Kant's brief plan to publish his second major "Critique" as an addition to the second edition of his first. Through letters from contemporaries like Born and Hamann, the author confirms that Kant initially intended to merge the works before deciding to publish the Critique of Practical Reason separately in 1787.</summary>

XII
# Introduction
->**Introduction**<-

[Kant] writes to Schütz on June 25: “I have my *Critique of Practical Reason* ready to the point that I intend to send it to Halle for printing next week.”

The words from this letter that now follow also make it clear which secondary considerations moved the author to publish his work. He continues: “This work will do a better job than all the controversies with Feder and Abel <note>Johann Georg Heinrich Feder (1740–1821) and Jacob Friedrich von Abel (1751–1829) were contemporary philosophers who criticized Kant's work, often defending traditional or empirical psychology against his new 'critical' approach.</note> (the first of whom asserts no *a priori* knowledge <term>a priori: knowledge that is independent of experience, such as mathematics or the basic laws of logic</term> at all, while the other asserts a middle ground between the empirical and the *a priori*). It will prove and make comprehensible the supplementation of what I denied to speculative reason <term>speculative reason: the use of reason to try and understand things beyond physical experience, such as the nature of God or the soul</term> through pure practical reason, as well as the possibility thereof. This, after all, is the actual 'stumbling block' that compels those men to take the most impractical, even absurd paths in order to extend the speculative faculty to the supersensible, rather than submit to that verdict of the *Critique* which seems so utterly bleak to them.” Aside from the systematic motive—which naturally took first place and to which we shall return in the second part of our introduction—there were also polemical considerations that moved Kant to the rapid drafting and publication of the new work.

This is also confirmed by further personal testimonies from the philosopher. For instance, immediately after the book appeared, on December 28, 1787, he wrote to Reinhold <note>Karl Leonhard Reinhold (1757–1823) was an influential philosopher who helped popularize Kant's difficult ideas to a wider audience.</note>: “In this little book, many contradictions which the adherents of the old school believe they find in my *Critique* are sufficiently resolved; on the other hand, those contradictions in which they inevitably involve themselves, should they refuse to give up their old patchwork, are placed clearly before their eyes.” Even before this—likely on September 11 of that year, according to Reicke <note>Rudolf Reicke (1825–1905) was a prominent Kant scholar and editor of his correspondence.</note>—he had expressed himself similarly to L. H. Jakob in Halle regarding the work still being printed: it contained “much that can remove the misunderstandings of the <note>original: "sc. Kritik"</note> (that is to say, the Critique) of theoretical <note>original: "sc. Vernunft"</note> (that is to say, reason).” Likewise, the...

# Introduction. XIII

the finished book itself points to such polemical motives in various places: specifically on page 7 f. (“By this I mean to shed clear light”), page 9 note, page 10 f. (regarding the “truth-loving reviewer”), page 11 (“many other objections”), page 12 (middle) of the preface, and page 183 (against Wizenmann).¹) It therefore seems appropriate to give the reader at least a brief overview of those counter-writings or opposing reviews that were certainly taken into account by Kant in his work:

1) The first “authorial matter” that, according to Hamann’s <note>Johann Georg Hamann (1730–1788), a philosopher and friend of Kant, known for his more mystical and irrationalist views.</note> letter to Jacobi <note>Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi (1743–1819), a philosopher famous for his critique of the Enlightenment and his focus on faith over reason.</note> on May 13, 1786, was “swirling in our philosopher’s head,” was the review of the *Groundwork* <term>Groundwork for the Metaphysics of Morals: Kant’s earlier 1785 work on ethics</term> in the *Tübingen Scholarly Reports* (*Tübinger gelehrten Anzeigen*), 14th issue from February 16, 1786, page 105 ff. It was written by the regular philosophical reviewer of this journal, the Tübingen Professor J. Fr. Flatt, who—in this as well as his numerous other reviews of pro- and anti-Kant writings—tended to accuse the critical philosopher particularly of “inconsistency.” To him, therefore, refer passages such as the note to page 4 as well as the longer paragraph on page 6; see also our subject index under *consistent*.

2) Even more heavily considered appears the “refutation” already mentioned in our introduction to the *Groundwork* (page XVI f.) by Church Councilor Gottlob August Tittel of Karlsruhe in his work: *Concerning Mr. Kant's Moral Reform* (*Über Herrn Kants Moralreform*). Frankfurt and Leipzig 1786. This commentator on the well-known Göttingen opponent of Kant, Feder, criticized Kant’s “all too frequent use of abstract terminologies” (page 4) and repeatedly accused him of “proclaiming long-known things as new in an unintelligible language” (page 25); against which Kant defends himself on page 122²² ff. He also raised the accusation of “mysticism” <term>mysticism: in this context, the belief that one can have direct, supernatural knowledge of the divine or the soul, which Kant’s critics often confused with his abstract philosophy</term> against him and praised the “innocent and charming system that [unites] happiness and morality,” which Kant so energetically fought against in the new work...

The “reviewer” who “hit the mark better than he himself may have intended” was indeed the Baden church councilor <note>Gottlob August Tittel (1739–1816), a professor of philosophy and church official who criticized Kant’s ethics as being merely a new way of saying old things.</note>, who on page 35 of his work had remarked: “Should the entire Kantian reform of morals perhaps be restricted to just a new formula?”, cf. page 55: “Mr. Kant, after he believes he has executed and secured his supposed new principle of ethics in this way . . .”

XVI
# Introduction.

(see *Natorp’s Substantive Explanations* in his edition, p. 507 f.) <note>Paul Natorp (1854–1924) was a leading figure of the Marburg School of Neo-Kantianism, known for his rigorous editorial work on Kant's texts.</note>

In response to these numerous critics, *Kant* initially seems to have intended a purely polemical counter-writing. However, several of his followers and friends, such as *Biester* and *Schütz*, <note>Johann Erich Biester and Christian Gottfried Schütz were influential Enlightenment figures and supporters of Kant; Schütz was a co-founder of the *Allgemeine Literatur-Zeitung*.</note> advised him—rightly so, we would say today—against wasting his precious time on such polemical trifles. Added to this was the fact that, at least in the summer semester of 1786, a "burdensome... academic business that fell to him," namely the Rectorate of the University of Königsberg, "robbed him of nearly all time for it," so that he "had to let pass all the distorted, and occasionally even malicious, judgments until then" (to *Jakob*, May 26, 1786). <note>Ludwig Heinrich von Jakob (1759–1827) was a philosopher who helped popularize Kant's work in Germany and Russia.</note> Thus, he let the matter rest with his essay on *Orientation* published in October 1786 (see above) <note>Referring to the essay *What does it mean to orient oneself in thinking?*</note> and occupied himself instead primarily with the completion of the second edition of the *Critique of Pure Reason*, and after its completion, with the *Critique of Practical Reason*. As he writes toward the end of his preface to the former (p. XLIII f. of the original), he wanted henceforth to "be economical" with his time and leave the defense of his system, as well as the clarification of individual obscurities, to other "deserving men." For his part, he "could not involve himself in disputes from now on, although I shall pay careful attention to all hints, whether from friends or opponents." Instead, he preferred to carry out his plan to "deliver the Metaphysics of Nature as well as of Morals as a confirmation of the correctness of the critique of both speculative and practical reason." By March 1787 at the latest,¹) he had finally decided to compose a systematic work in which, however, he intended to give polemical consideration to the most important of the objections raised. In any case, the latter—

XVII
# Introduction.

B

...—this, as *Natorp* has shown through the simple collation of facts, brought to maturity *Kant’s* decision to publish his work soon, and to a higher degree than previously assumed; for in his opinion, “only a detailed *Critique of Practical Reason* could remove all this misinterpretation and place the consistent way of thinking, which constitutes its greatest advantage, into a clear light” (*Critique of Practical Reason*, Preface p. 7 f.).

He therefore also drafted the text with unusual speed. After the new edition of the *Critique of Pure Reason* had only just left the press (June 1787), the new work was already almost ready for printing by June 25 of that year, as we have already seen (p. XII above). However, the printing was prolonged against *Kant's* wish and expectation because the printer (*Grunert* in Halle) wanted to “print this work with new and sharp letters,” which the “typefounder had not yet finished, but only delivered eight days after the Michaelmas Fair” (*Grunert* to *Kant*, in December 1787). <note>The Michaelmas Fair (Michaelis-Messe) was a major book trade fair held in Leipzig every autumn; delays in typefounding often disrupted the publishing schedule for the following winter season.</note> Therefore, the complimentary copies of the work, over which he had already made arrangements on September 11, were only sent out around Christmas of that year. ^1)

2. We are also quite well-informed today, through *Kant's* correspondence, regarding the effects that the second Critique of Reason provoked among his contemporaries. The first and most enthusiastic to express himself was the well-known herald of *Kantian* philosophy at the University of Jena, *Wieland's* son-in-law *Reinhold*. <note>Karl Leonhard Reinhold (1757–1823) was a central figure in popularizing Kant’s work through his "Letters on the Kantian Philosophy."</note> “What shall I say to you . . . regarding the priceless gift of the *Critique of Practical Reason*, of which I received my assigned copy today, but which I already devoured eight days ago? My current silence and my entire future life may thank you. If heaven grants me a son . . . then your letter and that copy shall be the un-”

> “God bless you! — You are a great, a very great instrument in the hand of God; I do not flatter — your philosophy will be a far greater, more blessed, and more universal revolution than *Luther’s* Reformation. For as soon as one has well grasped the Critique of Reason, one sees that no refutation is possible; consequently, your philosophy must be eternal and unchangeable, and your beneficial effects will lead the religion of Jesus back to its original purity, where it has holiness alone as its purpose; all sciences will become more systematic, pure, and certain

> “You also do very well to seek the final satisfaction of your spirit—which strives for a secure ground of doctrine and hope—in the Gospel, that imperishable guide of true wisdom. With this guide, a reason that has completed its speculation <note>In this context, "speculation" refers to purely theoretical or abstract reasoning</note> not only coincides, but from it reason also receives a new light regarding that which remains dark to it, even after it has traversed its entire field, and about which it nonetheless requires instruction.”

The rest, which has come down to us only as a draft, provides—following Jung-Stilling’s suggestion—an application of the categories to the highest principles of the philosophy of law (*Kant's Correspondence XI, p. 10*).

The prorector <note>A deputy headmaster or high-ranking academic official</note> of the Elisabeth-Gymnasium in Breslau, J. G. Schummel, praises the man “who has already so long been the object of thinking Germany, who divides the entire philosophical world into two parties,” as having come to him “as if from heaven,” in that he had destroyed the “rotten pillars” of the Wolffian system <note>The rationalist school of Christian Wolff, which dominated German philosophy before Kant's "Critiques"</note> for him as well.

> “The time will come, it must come, when your philosophy becomes popular: certainly not yet, when the Tittels <note>Gottlob August Tittel, a philosopher known for his early criticisms of Kant</note> and even the Göttingen reviewers can misunderstand you so incredibly.” (Schummel to Kant, March 28, 1789.)

Kosegarten calls Kant, among other things, the man who “taught me to properly value my moral self and to pay homage solely to the idol of the truly enlightened and upright human being, called Duty . . . Who appears just as amiable, simple, and humanly beautiful in his investigations of our practical faculty of reason, as he may initially appear fearsome, daunting, and cruel in the analysis of all speculation.” In a note to this, he suggests that now even Herder <note>Johann Gottfried von Herder, a famous philosopher and former student of Kant who became a bitter critic</note> will regret his entirely unnecessary outbursts against Kantian philosophy in the *Dialogues on God*.

XXI
### Introduction.

“It seems impossible to me to read that last Kantian work <note>original: "sc. die Kr. d. pr. V.", referring to the *Critique of Practical Reason*</note>, or at least a great many passages within it, without falling around the neck of its author—who is as soulful as he is profound—and forever renouncing all anger, heatedness, blustering, and mockery, which are the weapons of passion and not of conviction, against him” (dated June 4, 1789, in the supplement to Kant's Correspondence XII, p. 370). — For J. S. Beck, “the *Critique of Practical Reason* has been his Bible since its appearance” (Beck to Kant, October 6, 1791).

Master <term>J. H. Abicht|Johann Heinrich Abicht (1762–1816), a philosopher who initially struggled to reconcile Kant's work with his own theories</term> in Erlangen, on the other hand, in his long-winded outpouring of a letter from April 22, 1789, still struggles with certain doubts: “I doubted, along with others, whether you—in the *Critique of Practical Reason*, since you renounced the honor of priority for the incentives of pleasure—had done justice to that principle” — <note>namely</note> the “voice of nature, which echoes in every person” — and speaks of the “impression that this *Critique* did not make,” although he “listened with the sharpest ear for the sensations and judgments it would produce in the public.” “Much I could not reconcile enough, and I sought closer explanations in vain.” Nevertheless, he wishes to dedicate all his strength to the victory of the philosophy of the man to whom he “owes so many blessed moments” (Abicht to Kant, April 22, 1789). — By contrast, it was precisely Kant's “moral principles” that made the Berlin High Court Counselor <term>Klein|Ernst Ferdinand Klein (1744–1810), a prominent Prussian jurist and reformer</term> into a follower. He had accepted them even before he became acquainted with the *Critique of Pure Reason*, having already “arrived at the thought on his own early on that morality could not be dependent on theory,” and had acted accordingly in the education of his children (to Kant, April 23, 1789). Since the correctness of Kant's moral system “is so obvious at the very first glance, because it is so suited to moral feelings and biblical ethics,” he “cannot wonder enough” in a later letter that the general approval one would expect has still

Nevertheless, the *Critique of Practical Reason* seems by now to have "found increasing distribution," for the younger *Hartknoch* <note>Johann Friedrich Hartknoch the Younger (1769–1819), the son of Kant's primary publisher in Riga.</note> wishes in August of this year to arrange for a second *edition*, specifically with a print run of 2,000 copies (*J. Fr. Hartknoch* to *Kant*, August 26, 1789). A few weeks later, he reports that the printer "has already received the copies of the two *Critiques*" — at the same time, the 3rd edition of the *Critique of Pure Reason* was to be produced — "so the printing can soon be undertaken and will certainly be ready by the coming <term>Easter Fair|The Leipzig Book Fair, the most important event for the German publishing industry, where new books were traditionally released.</term>" (to *Kant*, September 29, "old style" <note>original: "alten Stils"; referring to the Julian calendar, which was still used in the Baltic region where Hartknoch was based.</note> 1789). In reality, however, only the new edition of the critique of theoretical reason <note>the *Critique of Pure Reason*</note> appeared at the announced date; the second edition of our work <note>the *Critique of Practical Reason*</note>, on the other hand, bears the year 1792¹).

The distribution of *Kant's* moral teaching gradually had to struggle more and more with difficulties under the <term>Wöllner regime|The administration of Johann Christoph von Wöllner, a high-ranking Prussian official who issued edicts in 1788 to enforce religious orthodoxy and censorship.</term>. Already on December 15, 1789, *Kiesewetter* <note>Johann Gottfried Kiesewetter (1766–1819), a student and loyal propagator of Kant's philosophy in Berlin.</note> reports from *Berlin* that in the first of his lectures on the *Critique of Practical Reason*—attended by about 25 listeners, especially from the merchant class—a strange young person had sneaked in, who "wrote down my entire lecture word-for-word and drew the attention of everyone by his diligent anxiety." The lecturer, having been warned beforehand, therefore had the caution to emphasize from the very start of this first lecture the agreement of the *Kantian* moral law with the teachings of Christianity; so that this spy

(Kant to Hellwag, January 3, 1791.)¹). — From Kiel, Reinhold <note>Karl Leonhard Reinhold (1757–1823), whose "Letters on the Kantian Philosophy" were instrumental in making Kant a celebrity.</note>, who had since moved there, wrote on March 29, 1795, that "here, too, the **Gospel of practical reason** has found no less entrance than in Jena."

On January 21, 1797, the publisher (J. Fr. Hartknoch in Riga) informed Kant that the stock of the *<term>Groundwork|The Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals (1785), Kant's first mature work on moral philosophy.</term>* and the *Critique of Practical Reason* was exhausted, and inquired whether both writings should be reprinted unchanged or not. XXIV
<header>Introduction.</header>

The philosopher responded to Hartknoch’s <note>Johann Friedrich Hartknoch the Younger (1769–1819), Kant’s publisher in Riga.</note> request immediately on January 28, initially asking for a fourteen-day period of reflection to consider—while the printing of the *Groundwork* <note>original: *Grundlegung*; referring to the *Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals* (1785).</note> could already begin—"whether he might find it good to make some changes to the preface." But then he quickly changed his mind and continued: "Since, however, these could not be of particular importance, and my current indisposition also makes all mental labor very difficult for me, it can stay as it was." (*Correspondence, Vol. XII, p. 145 f.*) The edition that appeared at Easter 1797 is designated as the fourth. No trace of a third edition has been found so far. There is much to be said for the conjecture of *Natorp* <note>Paul Natorp (1854–1924), a prominent Neo-Kantian philosopher and editor of the Academy edition of Kant’s works.</note> (*op. cit.* p. 498), that *Hartknoch* had this actually third edition labeled as the fourth because the second edition had been printed in a run of 2000 copies instead of the usual 1000. — A fifth edition appeared in 1818, a sixth in 1827: both almost exactly at the same time as the last two (6th and 7th) editions of the *Critique of Pure Reason*; additionally, a reprint appeared (*Graz* 1796), and two in *Frankfurt* and *Leipzig* (1791 and 1795).

## II. Train of Thought of the Work.

### a) The Preface (pp. 3—17)

begins immediately with the discussion of very difficult problems, for the understanding of which a precise knowledge of the *Critique of Pure Reason* is required, such as the distinction between practical reason in general and pure practical reason, the compatibility of the transcendental concept of freedom with that of natural necessity, and its connection with the ideas of *God* and *Immortality*: matters which had already been repeatedly discussed by *Kant* in his first three major critical writings (*Critique of Pure Reason, Prolegomena, Groundwork*), but which had nevertheless been subject to various misunderstandings. Much becomes understandable only if one considers the [circumstances] in our [period of] development—

The beginner is therefore best advised, after first becoming properly familiar with the *Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals (Vol. 41 of the Philosophical Library)*, to begin either immediately with the introduction of our text (p. 18) or at least with the paragraph on page 9 of our edition: "Whether such a system, etc." The philosopher himself assumes an acquaintance with the *Groundwork*, at least "insofar as it makes a preliminary acquaintance with the principle of duty and provides and justifies a specific formula for it" (p. 9). The next few pages then deal with the objections raised against this first ethical treatise. Of methodological importance is especially the note on p. 9 regarding the value of the formula, which highlights and justifies with conscious clarity the "formalism" <term>Formalism: The philosophical view that the moral value of an action is determined by its form—its universal lawfulness—rather than its content, goals, or consequences.</term> of critical ethics that is still so often attacked today.

The task of a critique of practical reason is described—quite analogous to the relationship found in the critique of theoretical reason <note>Original: "theoretischen Vernunftkritik," referring to the *Critique of Pure Reason* (1781).</note>—as the complete determination of the principles of its possibility, its scope, and its limits; while its relation to the actual constitution of human beings (e.g., the listing and classification of duties) is to be reserved for a later work (10) <note>This later work is the *Metaphysics of Morals* (1797).</note>. Kant intends to respond to an objection concerning the relationship between the moral principle and the concept of the good in the second chapter of his *Analytic* <term>Analytic: The section of the book that breaks down the basic concepts and principles of moral reasoning.</term> (this occurs on pages 76 ff. of our edition), while an extensive note discusses the relationship of the faculty of desire <term>Faculty of desire: The capacity of a being to be the cause of the objects of its representations through those representations; the "will" to make something real.</term> to the feeling of pleasure, providing interesting definitions of both concepts as well as that of life. 

Toward the followers of the "old systems," our philosopher already displays a strong sense of self-confidence here (11 f.); he also proudly rejects the accusation of an incomprehensible "new language," which, incidentally, he believes one would hardly make against his current, more popular work. If only people would popularize him! He "only wishes to be understood." Only on a few expressions that, in his opinion, are more likely to be misunderstood—

### **b) Introduction (pp. 18—20)**

is very brief. It limits itself to a justification of the title and the division of the work. In contrast to theoretical reason, pure reason here—as the first paragraph of the preface already pointed out—does not need to be "criticized" <note>In this context, to "criticize" means to investigate the limits and boundaries of what a faculty can achieve.</note>. Instead, its existence merely needs to be demonstrated; it must be shown that empirical conditions alone do not determine the will. Accordingly, the division must also take a slightly different path. Indeed, the present work can also be divided into a *Doctrine of Elements* and a *Doctrine of Method*, and the former can again be split into an *Analytic* and a *Dialectic*. But, in reverse order to that earlier work <note>The *Critique of Pure Reason* (1781).</note>, the *Critique of Practical Reason* begins with *Principles* (pp. 23—74), proceeds from there to *Concepts* (namely [scilicet], of an object of pure practical reason, pp. 75—92), and from these "finally, if possible, to the *senses*" (in this case, the "*incentives*" <note>Original: "Triebfedern." This refers to the inner motives or "springs of action" that drive a person to act according to the moral law.</note>, pp. 93—115).

On this occasion, it should be noted that the layout of the writing is by no means "extraordinarily simple and clear," as Houston Stewart Chamberlain <note>H. St. Chamberlain (1855–1927) was a prolific author and Kantian scholar who often emphasized the aesthetic and structural clarity of Kant’s thought.</note> claims it to be for every single book by Kant. Rather, it is not very clear and even less symmetrical. Of the two main parts, the first (the *Doctrine of Elements*) contains 168 pages, while the second (the *Doctrine of Method*) contains only 13 pages—that is, not even 1/15th of the entire work, whereas in the *Critique of Pure Reason* it amounted to at least 1/6th. XXVII
->Introduction.<-

<note>The text begins mid-word, following from the previous page's discussion of proportions.</note> Analytic constitutes 2/7 and the Dialectic 3/7 of the whole, here the former takes up half, while the latter occupies only a quarter. Of the three chapters of the Analytic, the first—the doctrine of principles—actually fills only 42 pages, followed by a section of different content (pp. 66–74); the second chapter (concerning the object of pure practical reason) strictly speaking covers only 13 pages, to which a section on the Typic of pure practical judgment <term>Typic: A method by which the abstract moral law is applied to concrete actions by using the "law of nature" as a model.</term> (pp. 87–92) is attached; and the third (concerning the incentives of pure practical reason) spans 22 pages, followed—without a specific heading—by a critical illumination of the entire Analytic for a further 22 pages. — The Dialectic, however, begins with two short chapters of 4 and 5 pages respectively, only to conclude with no fewer than 9 further individual sections without a collective heading. A table of contents is entirely absent in the original.

In contrast to the analytic method of the *Groundwork* <note>Referring to Kant’s earlier work, the *Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals* (1785).</note>, the *Critique of Practical Reason* proceeds synthetically. It leads us immediately into the core of the system, into the doctrine

### c) On the Principles of Pure Practical Reason (pp. 23—65, or 74).

Outwardly almost reminiscent of the style of Spinoza <note>Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677), a Dutch philosopher who wrote his *Ethics* in a "geometrical" style with definitions and theorems.</note>—whom our philosopher held in such low regard—Kant begins with an "Explanation," or more precisely three definitions, followed by a longer note (pp. 23–26). He then develops, in three "theorems" with an attached "corollary" (p. 26 ff.) and two "problems" (p. 36 ff.), the "Fundamental Law of Pure Practical Reason" or the Moral Law (pp. 39–43), which is then followed by a fourth theorem (p. 43 f.) that, like the earlier ones, is accompanied by longer "notes." These propositions, compressed into eight paragraphs, contain the foundational structure of critical ethics in an unbroken chain.

The "practical principles," which are considered the prerequisite and starting point of every system of ethics, are either 1. Maxims, i.e., subjective principles, held by the individual and for the individual

XXVIII
->Introduction.<-

accepted, without universal validity, or 2. practical laws, that is, principles valid for the will of every rational being, thus objective principles. Therefore, only the latter are also imperatives in the strict, categorical sense; only they can be considered for a scientific ethics (§ 1). Now, however, all practical principles that presuppose an object of our desire as the **determining ground** <term>determining ground: the underlying reason or motive that triggers an act of will.</term> of our will are empirical and dependent on the pleasure or displeasure of the individual; consequently, they are not usable for an *a priori* law <term>a priori: independent of experience; known by reason alone.</term>, as ethics requires (§ 2). All of these “material” moral principles fall under the principle of self-love or one's own **happiness** (§ 3). Where the feeling of pleasure originates—whether from sensory or intellectual representations—is fundamentally indifferent; even the feeling of one's own power (we moderns think here of Nietzsche!) <note>Friedrich Nietzsche (1844–1900), who famously emphasized the "Will to Power," writing long after Kant but before this introduction was penned.</note> does not make the matter any better. Even “super-finely” conceived matter always remains matter. Consistency, however, is the greatest obligation of a philosopher (*Note I*). Certainly, happiness is the desire of every finite, and thus needy, rational being, but it always remains a subjective principle valid only for the individual, and even universal agreement on it would still be merely coincidental (*Note II*).

Thus, for the sought-after practical laws, the only remaining standard is: their capacity to be universally legislative (§ 4. The following *Note* then elaborates on this with an example from life—which, incidentally, is not particularly well-chosen). But a will that is determined solely by this mere legislative form is, according to its determining ground, independent of the law of **causality** in nature; that is, it is free “in the strictest, i.e., transcendental sense (§ 5).” And conversely: a free will can be determined only and solely by that legislative form (§ 6). **Freedom** and the unconditional practical law point back to one another. The moral law, of which we become immediately conscious as soon as we form **maxims** <term>maxims: personal rules or subjective principles that an individual chooses to follow.</term>, leads to the concept of freedom, which speculative reason <term>speculative reason: the capacity of the mind to think about things beyond physical experience, though it cannot "prove" them in the same way science does.</term> could not

<meta>This page continues the editorial summary of Kant’s "Analytic of Pure Practical Reason." It picks up from the discussion of practical laws, explaining how the formal structure of a law allows for the Categorical Imperative and the autonomy of the will.</meta>

XXIX
->Introduction.<-

was able to resolve (*Note*). From the mere, formal thought of a possible universal legislation, there arises—as the fundamental law of pure practical reason or the moral law—the command: Act in such a way that the maxim of your will could always at the same time hold as a principle of universal legislation (§ 7). The consciousness of this fundamental law, which forces itself upon us, is the sole **fact of pure reason** <term>fact of pure reason: (Latin: *factum rationis*) something given immediately in our consciousness of moral obligation that cannot be derived from prior experience, but proves that reason is practical.</term> (*Note 1*). From this arise a number of concepts—already discussed in the *Groundwork*—such as: universal validity for all rational beings, the imperative, duty, the infinite **progress** <term>progressus: a continuous advancement or development toward a goal.</term> toward the ideal, and so forth (*Note 2*). That universal legislation, however, emerges from the free will of man; it is thus **autonomy** (self-legislation) of the will in contrast to the “heteronomy” (legislation by another) of choice (§ 8). To be sure, every act of willing must have an object; but this object—for example, the happiness of others—does not need to be its ultimate **determining ground** <term>determining ground: the underlying reason or motive that triggers an act of will.</term> (*Note I*). Morality and egoism, duty and personal advantage are easily distinguishable even for the common man. Even if universal happiness is thought of as the goal, the principle of happiness can at most be general, not universal; it can at most advise, but never command. The feeling of being deserving of punishment also proves our moral disposition and argues against **eudaimonism** <term>eudaimonism: the ethical theory that happiness or personal well-being is the highest good and the basis of morality.</term>. And the assumption of a special "moral sense" (for example, by the Englishman Hutcheson) <note>Francis Hutcheson (1694–1746), a philosopher of the Scottish Enlightenment who argued that humans possess a "moral sense" akin to sight or hearing.</note> already presupposes the idea of the moral law (*Note II*).

Thus, Kant’s formal ethics consciously places itself in the sharpest contrast to all previous "material" principles of morality, which are then compiled in a table (p. 53), such as: education (Montaigne), civil constitution (Mandeville), physical or moral feeling (Epicurus, Hutcheson), perfection (the Stoics, Wolff), and the will of God (Crusius and the theologians). That the principle of perfection remains relatively the best, yet is also insufficient (p. 54), had already been explained in more detail in the ***Groundwork*** (pp. 69–71 of our edition).

XXX
# Introduction.
->Introduction.<-

Two additional sections are appended to the first chapter. The first (pp. 55–65) is titled: *On the Deduction of the Principles of Pure Practical Reason*. However, initially, only a repeated "exposition" (p. 60, bottom) of the moral law is provided. The fact of our moral **autonomy** <term>autonomy: the capacity of a rational being to give themselves the moral law, rather than being governed by external desires or authorities.</term> is inseparably linked with the consciousness of the **freedom of the will** and positively defines an **intelligible world** <term>intelligible world: a realm of reality that can be understood by the mind/reason, as opposed to the "sensible world" perceived through the senses.</term> (an order of things), which was inexplicable for theoretical reason. The moral law is the fundamental law of a **supersensible nature**—that is, a nature governed by the autonomy of pure practical reason. In the moral assessment of our actions (for example, regarding false testimony or suicide), we represent those actions to ourselves as a universal law of nature. The actual success or outcome of our acting does not matter in this regard. The possibility of freedom or of moral consciousness itself cannot be further explained (cf. *Groundwork for the Metaphysics of Morals, p. 89 ff.*). Only then do we come to the question of the **deduction** <term>deduction: in a legal or philosophical sense, a "deduction" is a justification or proof of the right to use a concept, explaining how it is possible and valid.</term> of the moral law—that is, the justification of its universal validity and insight into its **a priori** <term>a priori: (Latin: "from the earlier") knowledge or principles that are independent of experience, derived purely from reason.</term> possibility (p. 61, top). Experience leaves us in the lurch here, and fundamental forces or faculties cannot be further derived from elsewhere. Instead, something quite "contrary to sense" takes the place of this vainly sought deduction: the moral law itself serves as the principle for the deduction of freedom and proves its reality within us. It does this by defining the concept of freedom—which was previously thought of only negatively—in a positive way, namely as a **causality** <term>causality: the relationship between cause and effect; here, Kant argues that reason itself can be a "cause" that produces actions.</term> of pure reason. Through this, the law of natural causality is neither abolished nor expanded beyond its limits—"speculative reason gains nothing in insight" (p. 64)—but freedom is secured and realized.

Following this, the second appendix (pp. 66–74) elaborates once more on the *authority of pure reason in its practical use for an extension that is not possible for it in its speculative use*, without actually introducing any new thoughts. Once again, Hume’s empirical **skepticism** <term>skepticism: the philosophical position that certain knowledge is impossible; David Hume famously doubted that we could ever "prove" the necessity of cause and effect.</term> regarding the law of causality is criticized, which could only be [overcome] by the...

<summary>This page summarizes the "Deduction" of Kant's moral law, explaining how the moral law itself serves as proof that human beings possess free will and a "positive causality" independent of natural laws. It also introduces a critique of David Hume's skepticism regarding causality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the editorial introduction to Kant's "Analytic of Pure Practical Reason." The previous page discussed the "Deduction" (justification) of the moral law and how autonomy relates to an "intelligible world." This page specifically addresses how Kant's distinction between appearances and things-in-themselves allows for freedom, and then moves into Chapter II: "The Object of Pure Practical Reason."</meta>

XXXI
# Introduction.
->Introduction.<-

the distinction established in the *Critique of Pure Reason* between the "thing in itself" and "appearance" can be resolved. Objects are to be recognized and determined only through the application of the categories, but noumena <term>noumena: things as they are in themselves, which can be thought by the mind but not perceived by the senses.</term> (objects of thought beyond experience) are at least thinkable for the pure understanding. The concept of a pure will already contains within itself the concept of a causality "with freedom" (<note>original: *causa noumenon*</note> a noumenal cause).

The second chapter of the Analytic which now follows moves from the fundamental principles to the

**d) Object of Pure Practical Reason (pp. 75–92)**

The judgment of whether something is an object of pure practical reason or not has nothing at all to do with our physical capacity, but refers only to the question of whether we are *permitted* to will the action in question, and thus refers to its moral possibility. "The sole objects of a practical reason are therefore the concepts of good and evil" (76). These, however, are not to be derived further; otherwise, we inevitably arrive at the purely empirical <term>empirical: based on observation or experience rather than theory or pure logic.</term> concepts of the pleasant and unpleasant, the useful and harmful, well-being or woe, which rest on the feeling of pleasure (gratification) or displeasure (pain). Now, our well-being and woe certainly matter "very much," but they do not constitute "everything overall." After all, man is not "entirely animal," even if his reason has "an unavoidable mandate from the side of sensibility <term>sensibility: our capacity for feeling and desire, as opposed to our capacity for rational thought.</term> to care for its interests" (80). Rather, what is good or evil in itself (immediately) can only be determined through the moral law, which must precede the definition of the concept of the "good," and not follow it. Kant believes that the disregard for this method explains "all the errors of philosophers" regarding the moral principle (80). Instead of starting from the purely formal law, they all started from the object (the "matter") of willing; hence the development of the doctrine of the highest good—to be discussed later in the Dialectic (see below)—among the ancient philosophers. Since the concepts of good and evil presuppose a causality of pure reason—

<summary>This page explains how Kant defines "good" and "evil" as the sole objects of practical reason, arguing that they must be derived from the moral law itself rather than from feelings of pleasure or pain.</summary>
<keywords>Kant, Critique of Practical Reason, Good and Evil, Noumena, Causality, Sensibility, Moral Law, Highest Good</keywords>

XXXII
# Introduction.
->Introduction.<-

set, so an ethical **table of categories**, a "Table of the Categories of Freedom" according to Quality, Quantity, Relation, and Modality, can also be established (it can be found on page 86).

The short section following these explanations, *On the Typic of Pure Practical Judgment* (pp. 87–92), is intended to bring concrete individual cases of our actions under the practical rule of pure reason. How is this possible, however, given the seemingly unbridgeable contrast between the world of the senses and the supersensible idea of the Morally Good? Well, physical action certainly falls under natural concepts, the schema <term>schema: a mental bridge or rule that connects abstract concepts to our sensory experiences.</term> of which is designed by the transcendental **imagination** (according to the *Critique of Pure Reason*). For the idea of the good, however, the law of nature itself is the schema that can be placed beneath the moral law as its "**Type**" <term>Type: (German: Typus) A symbolic model or framework used to help us visualize how a moral law would function if it were a law of nature.</term>. We conceive of the case in question as a universal law of nature (cf. p. XXX), by asking: "Ask yourself whether, if the action you intend were to happen according to a law of nature of which you yourself were a part, you could indeed regard it as possible through your will?" (p. 90). In this way, the Typic of Pure Practical Judgment prevents both the **mysticism** of practical reason, which is based on supersensible intuitions <note>Kant warns against mysticism because it relies on private, "supernatural" feelings rather than shared human reason.</note>, and the even more dangerous **empiricism**, which degrades humanity, and instead remains within a healthy **rationalism**.

From the object, Chapter III leads us to the

### c) **Incentives of Pure Practical Reason** (pp. 93–115).

While the previous discussions were sometimes quite abstract, this chapter is particularly rich in popular and morally uplifting observations, so that it can be highly recommended as a primary introduction to the spirit of Kantian ethics. We, of course, must be content, as before, with sketching the train of thought.

The moral law must determine the will directly. Otherwise, **legality** <term>legality: acting in accordance with the law, but perhaps for the wrong reasons (e.g., fear of punishment).</term> arises instead of **morality** <term>morality: acting out of respect for the moral law itself.</term>, and the **letter** of the law prevails instead of the **spirit**. XXXIII
# Introduction.
->Introduction.<-

In what way does the moral law now act as an **incentive** <term>incentive: (German: *Triebfeder*) The internal "spring" or motivating force that drives the human will to act.</term> within us? It rejects all inclinations and sensory impulses, strikes down **self-conceit**, allows only for a "rational self-love" to exist, and above all awakens the feeling of **respect**: the only feeling that can be known <term>a priori: knowledge that is independent of any experience; something known through reason alone.</term> <note>original: "a priori"</note>, because it is produced solely by reason. At the same time, it is the only truly moral feeling, and its unique mixture of pleasure and pain is illustrated with several beautifully chosen examples. Thus, the moral law is simultaneously the formal (as law), material (as good and evil), and subjective (as incentive) determining ground (pp. 97 f.). This incentive drives us toward a moral interest, and this interest leads us to a moral **maxim** <term>maxim: a personal rule or principle that an individual chooses to follow.</term>. From the feeling of respect, understood as the consciousness of a free submission to the law, there further arises the concept of **duty**. The philosopher now describes duty (pp. 104 ff.) from all sides, contrasting it with mere outward compliance, personal inclination, religious fanaticism, and sentimentality, finally concluding with the famous **tribute to duty** (pp. 111 f.). The ultimate root of duty is the human being as a **personality**; from this, the further ethical concepts of humanity, of the human being as an end in themselves, and as the author of the moral law, follow naturally. These are all self-evident even to the common man, who, when it counts, is perfectly capable of distinguishing the dignity of duty from mere worldly pleasure.

The conclusion of the first book is formed by a

f) **Critical Elucidation of the Analytic** (pp. 115–137), which consists of subsequent explanatory and justifying remarks, particularly comparing it with the *Analytic* of theoretical pure reason <note>theoretical pure reason: This refers to the subject of Kant's first Critique, which examined how we perceive and understand the physical world through the senses and intellect.</note>. As the introduction already noted, the course of the investigation here had to be reversed: first came "Logic," followed by the "Aesthetics" of pure practical reason. Kant subsequently brings the three-part division of the Analytic into analogy with the three parts of the **syllogism** <term>syllogism: a logical argument where a conclusion is reached from two linked premises (e.g., All men are mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore Socrates is mortal).</term> (p. 117).

The only possible "deduction" <term>deduction: in Kantian philosophy, this is not just a logical move from general to specific, but a "legal" justification or proof of why a concept is valid and applicable.</term> of the supreme moral principle consisted in its inseparable connection with freedom.

XXXV
# Introduction.
->Introduction.<-

...his doctrine of the ideality of time <term>ideality of time: Kant's theory that time is not an external thing, but rather the "form" of our internal experience; this allows him to argue that while our actions appear determined by cause-and-effect in time, our true "inner self" remains free.</term> much more easily than with the opposing views of Mendelssohn or Spinoza. <note>Moses Mendelssohn and Baruch Spinoza were influential philosophers whose views on God and nature Kant often contrasted with his own "Critical" system.</note> — In conclusion, he then raises the question of why the idea of freedom in particular possesses such a remarkably great productivity. He finds the reason first in its nature as a dynamic (rather than mathematical) idea, but then and above all in its direct connection with morality, whereas the connection between the idea of God and morality is only an indirect one. Finally, Kant draws particular attention to the unsolicited, exact agreement between the most important principles of practical reason and those of speculative reason. With this, the first book—the "Analytic"—ends. It is followed by the second:

**g) Dialectic of Pure Practical Reason (pp. 133—188).**

The short first chapter: *Of a Dialectic of Pure Practical Reason in General* (pp. 133—141) is merely introductory in character. Just as with theoretical reason, the apparent contradiction between the conditioned and the unconditioned <note>Kant uses "conditioned" to refer to things dependent on causes, and "unconditioned" to refer to an ultimate starting point or absolute.</note> drives us in practical reason to seek a solution in a "Dialectic." Specifically, this contradiction emerges here in the concept of the "highest good," a topic the ethics of antiquity treated with great preference. The moral law is the sole determining ground of the pure will, while the highest good is its object. On the other hand, the moral law is contained within the highest good as its supreme condition. How then do the two relate to one another? To this, the second chapter answers:

*Of the Dialectic of Pure Practical Reason in Determining the Concept of the Highest Good* (pp. 142—145). The concept of the "highest" contains both that of the "supreme" good and that of the "complete" good. The former consists of virtue, while the latter consists of virtue together with the happiness that corresponds to it. For the Stoics and Epicureans <note>Ancient Greek schools of philosophy. The Stoics prioritized virtue, while the Epicureans prioritized pleasure/happiness.</note>, both concepts were identical—though viewed from different standpoints—whereas according to the "Analytic," they often enough contradict one another. How, then, is the *a priori* <term>a priori: knowledge that is independent of experience, such as "all triangles have three sides."</term> necessary highest good practically possible? This is now addressed in nine successive individual sections...

XXXVI
# Introduction.
->Introduction.<-

...sections (145—188) in greater detail. First,

I. The **antinomy of practical reason** <note>An "antinomy" is a contradiction between two laws or principles that both seem equally logical.</note> is once again clearly formulated: happiness must not be the motive for virtue, and virtue cannot be the cause of happiness *from the start* <note>original: "a priori"; a Latin term referring to knowledge that comes from logic rather than experience.</note> (pp. 145 f.).

II. The critical resolution of this antinomy is provided—as in the *Critique of Pure Reason*—by the dual nature of man as an appearance (a being of nature, or <term>phenomenon: a thing as it appears to our senses</term>) and as pure intelligence (a being of reason, or <term>noumenon: a thing as it is in itself, beyond sensory perception</term>). Certainly, the mere pursuit of happiness can never be the ground of true virtue; however, virtue can indeed result in happiness, mediated—by an "intelligible author of nature" <note>Kant refers here to a divine creator or a rational order-giver of the universe.</note>. The satisfaction we feel when our will is determined directly by the moral law is a purely intellectual contentment. This has nothing to do with sensory impulses or feelings of pleasure; rather, it consists precisely in independence from inclinations—which are always accompanied by a certain dissatisfaction (pp. 146—153).

III. With this, the main question posed above (the critical resolution of the antinomy) would actually be solved. However, in the following sections, Kant adds a series of related discussions.

First, the idea of the **primacy of pure practical reason** over speculative reason. The latter <note>speculative or theoretical reason, which we use to understand the physical world</note> remains incomplete without the former, and all interest—even that of knowledge—is ultimately practical (moral). Yet both must be connected, because at their core they are "one and the same" reason acting in both (pp. 153—156).

IV. Practical reason carries with it three propositions as necessary consequences, though they certainly cannot be proven theoretically; these are known as **postulates**:
1. **The immortality of the soul**: because the complete harmony of one's disposition with the moral law, as required by that law, can only be achieved in a progress extending into infinity. Anyone who is conscious of striving for the better with all their energy may hope for an uninterrupted continuation...

# Introduction. XXXVII
->Introduction. XXXVII<-

...of this striving, even beyond earthly life, which he hopes the Infinite Being will make possible for him (pages 156 to 158). With this we have arrived

V. at the 2nd postulate, the <term>Existence of God</term> <term>Dasein Gottes: the necessary assumption that a divine power exists to harmonize morality and happiness</term>. Since the <term>moral law</term> <term>Sittengesetz: the internal rule of duty</term> itself has nothing at all to do with natural conditions—and thus nothing to do with <term>happiness</term> <term>Glückseligkeit</term>, which is only possible through such conditions—but on the other hand, the attainment of the <term>highest good</term> <term>höchstes Gut: the union of virtue and happiness</term> is practically necessary: then, according to Kant, we must assume a being distinct from nature, endowed with understanding and will (God), who is at the same time the author of all nature and is thus able to bring about the "exact correspondence of <term>happiness</term> with morality" (bottom of page 159) required by our moral need. Such an assumption is certainly not objectively necessary, but it is subjectively necessary—not as the ground of our moral obligation, but as a "<term>pure rational faith</term>" <term>reiner Vernunftglaube: a belief based on the needs of reason rather than on revelation or proof</term>. In this regard, Christianity stands closer to the strictest moral demands than <term>Stoicism</term> <term>Stoa</term> and <term>Epicureanism</term> <term>Epikureismus</term>. It demands an relentlessly strict moral disposition with no motive other than the <term>moral law</term> itself <note>original: "[? d. H.]", likely "das heißt" (that is)</note> and makes <term>happiness</term> merely an object of hope. The <term>moral law</term>, however, leads in this way—through the concept of the <term>highest good</term> as the final end—to religion, that is, the "recognition of all duties as divine commands" (page 165). However, to avoid being misunderstood, Kant takes pains to emphasize repeatedly that the fulfillment of duty must always come first, that <term>worthiness to be happy</term> <term>Glückwürdigkeit</term> must always precede <term>happiness</term>, and that God is not merely kind, but also holy (pages 158–168).

Section VI gives a brief but important summary of the three postulates—among which <term>freedom</term> <term>Freiheit</term> now also appears—and their connection with the three ideas of theoretical reason (pages 168–170). — Section VII then elaborates on a thought mentioned here: namely, that although <term>pure reason</term> <term>reine Vernunft</term> is indeed expanded by these postulates, it is not expanded "speculatively" (meaning in regard to any potential new knowledge), but only "for practical purposes." Nothing new is carried out, but—

XXXVIII
# Introduction. XXXVIII
->Introduction. XXXVIII<-

...but only further establishes the thought already touched upon in the preface: that the theoretically problematic ideas of God, freedom, and immortality receive objective reality—that is, a real object—through their practical use. If they were previously <term>transcendent</term> <term>transzendent: lying beyond the limits of all possible experience</term> and merely <term>regulative</term> <term>regulativ: providing a rule for organization without defining a thing's nature</term>, they now become <term>immanent</term> <term>immanent: remaining within the boundaries of experience</term> and <term>constitutive</term> <term>konstitutiv: actively defining the structure of reality</term>, without one needing to fall into <term>anthropomorphism</term> <term>the attribution of human characteristics to God</term> or mysticism as a result. Kant’s concept of God aims to be purely moral, not physical or metaphysical. To take refuge in the deity for scientific explanations is to admit that one has come to the end of their philosophy (page 176, bottom). Even the so-called attributes of God—His omnipotence, omniscience, etc.—can only be established through moral means. The restriction of speculative use and the expansion of the practical use of reason: that is the path of science (pages 170–180).

Section VIII also further elaborates on an idea already expressed, namely: that a need of pure reason leads to <term>postulates</term> <term>Postulate: assumptions that are necessary for practical action even if they cannot be proven</term>, just as it leads to hypotheses—albeit unavoidable ones—in the theoretical field. The postulates are grounded in our duty to promote the <term>highest good</term> <term>höchstes Gut: the union of virtue and happiness</term>. The moral disposition must presuppose the possibility of the highest good, and therefore also its physical or metaphysical conditions. The righteous person can say: "I will that there be a God, that there be freedom, that my existence be endless," and he does not allow this <term>pure practical rational faith</term> <term>reiner praktischer Vernunftglaube: a belief based on moral necessity rather than logical proof</term> to be taken from him; for it rests on a necessary need of reason, not a coincidental need of inclination (pages 180–186).

IX. Finally, the relationship of the human faculties of knowledge to one another—especially the inadequacy of <term>speculative reason</term> <term>spekulative Vernunft: reason used to gain knowledge of things beyond experience</term>—is wisely suited to the practical destiny of man. Without this, on one hand, the inclinations would dominate, and on the other, God and eternity in their "awesome majesty" <note>original: "furchtbaren Majestät"</note> would stand incessantly before our eyes; as a result, most of our actions would be performed out of fear, few out of hop—

And so, the <term>Doctrine of Elements</term> <note>German: *Elementarlehre*; the first major part of Kant's work, laying out the basic components of practical reason</note> concludes: "the inscrutable wisdom through which we exist is no less worthy of veneration in what it has denied us than in what it has granted us" (pages 186–188).

### h) **The Doctrine of Method of Pure Practical Reason (pp. 189–204),**

which constitutes the second part of the entire work, is thoroughly popular in character. One could describe it as a kind of <term>moral didactics</term> <note>the theory and practice of teaching morality</note> or pedagogy, for it aims—even if only in general outlines (see the conclusion on page 204)—to show how one might grant the moral law access to, and influence over, the human mind. The core idea (which was already expressed in the <term>Groundwork</term> <note>original: *Grundlegung*; referring to Kant’s 1785 book *Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals*</note>) is that the moral law, precisely in its purity and severity, has a stronger effect on the uncorrupted person than all enticements and threats. Only in the case of still uneducated or wild minds is there a need for such <term>leading-strings</term> <note>original: *Gängelband*; a metaphor for the artificial supports used to help a child learn to walk</note> as a preparation; as soon as it has had some effect, one must allow the purely moral motive to work. This alone grants a person character, dignity, and strength of soul, proving itself to be the most powerful and only true <term>incentive</term> <note>original: *Triebfeder*; literally a "drive-spring," the internal mechanism that moves one to act</note> for the good. The receptivity to this is already evident in the tendency to express moral value judgments (the act of <term>reasoning</term> <note>original: *Räsonieren*; here meaning the natural human inclination to debate and judge the rightness of actions</note>) about persons—a tendency inherent in every human being, including businesspeople, women, and even children. Educators of youth should take advantage of this to sharpen the moral <term>judgment</term> <note>original: *Urteilskraft*</note> of their <term>pupils</term> <note>original: *Zöglinge*</note> using examples from biographies of ancient and modern times, thereby laying a solid moral foundation. In doing so, however, one should not emphasize the noble, magnanimous, or "extra-meritorious" nature of such actions—as is so often done today, producing either moral pride, sentimentality, or fancifulness—but rather the feeling of simple duty and obligation; for it is not on fleeting emotional outbursts,

Kant argues that the pure concept of duty is a more effective teaching tool than rewards, threats, or appeals to "noble" feelings.</summary>
