# Introduction.
XL

...but rather depends on firm principles. As for what pure morality is, common reason knows this just as well as the "difference between the right and left hand" (196). Kant himself, to support his view, elaborates on a longer example (pages 196 and following) and a shorter one (pages 199 and following), and cites a third from <term>Juvenal</term> <note>Decimus Junius Juvenalis, a Roman poet known for his satires on Roman society</note> (200). First, one must habituate a person to the question of whether the action in question is objectively in accordance with the moral law; then, however, whether it also happens for the sake of the law; eventually, they will come to love the law itself. Once the moral "apprentice" has become conscious of their capacity for inner freedom, they will find it increasingly easy to triumph over the <term>inclinations</term> <note>German: *Neigungen*; our natural desires and impulses which Kant argues must be subordinate to duty</note> that ultimately only leave them dissatisfied. The

->**I) Conclusion (pp. 205–207)**<-

summarizes once more, in beautiful and uplifting language, the two great fields that form the subject of Kant's first two systematic major works—nature and morality—in the famous words inscribed on the marble tablet opposite his tomb: **The starry heavens above me and the moral law within me.** There, we find the infinity of the physical world; here, the infinity of my invisible self, the personality. But, just as in the physical realm, we also need a Newton here, who replaces mere admiration with methodical research and dissects the morally judging reason. For "science (critically sought and methodically introduced) is alone the narrow gate that leads to the doctrine of wisdom" (207).

***

Kant has indeed become the Newton of ethics. Much like the author of the <term>Mathematical Principles of Natural Philosophy</term> <note>original: *Philosophiae naturalis principia mathematica*; Isaac Newton's 1687 work which laid the foundations of classical mechanics</note> did for mathematical natural science, Kant has provided morality with that methodical foundation which alone promises permanence—a foundation that may be improved in its details, but is essentially neither to be destroyed nor replaced. <page-num>XLI</page-num>
<header>Introduction.</header>

has our own time recognized to a greater degree than even Kant’s contemporaries. On the latter, as we have already seen and could confirm through a series of further examples¹), it was primarily the practical side of Kant’s ethics that had an effect—the purity, rigor, and sublimity of the new moral principle he proclaimed; and this practical effect of the <term>categorical imperative</term> <note>Kant's fundamental moral rule: "Act only according to that maxim whereby you can at the same time will that it should become a universal law."</note>, which the philosopher predicted in his *Doctrine of Method*, has never been entirely extinguished since then, from Friedrich Schiller and the men of the Prussian Reform era (1807 to 1813) down to the present day. Far less enduring, however, was the philosophical—that is, the methodical—influence. To be sure, one can count a number of "total" or "partial" Kantianists in the field of ethics as well²), yet most of them allowed themselves to be enticed by the brilliant speculative systems of his successors to become unfaithful to the simple critical method. These successors, however (<term>Fichte, Schelling, Hegel</term> <note>The primary figures of German Idealism who, while building on Kant, moved toward more abstract metaphysical systems that Kant himself had sought to limit</note>), are not developers of Kant, but rather his opponents. And even when <term>F. A. Lange</term> <note>Friedrich Albert Lange (1828–1875), a philosopher known for his "History of Materialism," who helped spark the "Back to Kant" movement</note> and others returned to Kant around the mid-1860s, it was initially declared—at least by Lange—that "the entire practical philosophy" was the "changeable and transitory part of Kantian philosophy." It was not until <term>Hermann Cohen</term> <note>A Jewish-German philosopher and founder of the Marburg School of Neo-Kantianism, who argued for the continued relevance of Kant's ethics</note> and his book *Kant’s Foundation of Ethics*, published in 1877, that critical ethics was established anew; he sought to deepen this foundation by highlighting even more sharply the "transcendental" method peculiar to Kant, which—

In his own system, which has been in the process of appearing since 1902 and of which the second part forms the *Ethics of the Pure Will* (2nd ed. 1907), Cohen has undertaken to further develop ethics in a <term>transcendental</term> <note>In Kantian philosophy, "transcendental" refers to the study of the conditions that make our experience and knowledge possible, rather than the study of the objects themselves.</note> sense—namely, establishing it as the "Logic of the <term>Human Sciences</term>" <note>original: "Geisteswissenschaften." This term refers to disciplines like history, law, and philosophy that study the products of the human spirit, as opposed to the natural sciences.</note> and as the "Theory of Principles of the Philosophy of Law and the State." For about a decade, Kant’s ethics has also played a significant role in social-philosophical discussions. Anyone interested in this is referred to my book treating these matters in detail: *Kant and Marx* (Tübingen 1911).

A complete picture of Kantian ethics, or even just an exhaustive characterization of its method, cannot be given at this point, insofar as it is not already contained in our line of thought reproduced here as well as in the introduction to Volume 41; rather, that is the task of a specialized monograph or a history of philosophy. I myself have sought to present Kant's ethical method in a Solingen high school program: *The Kantian Foundation of the Moral Principle* (1889), and more thoroughly in my dissertation: *The Formalism of Kantian Ethics in its Necessity and Fruitfulness* (Marburg 1894). I defended it there and in *Kant-Schiller-Goethe* (see previous page) against the charge of an over-strict <term>rigorism</term> <note>A common criticism that Kant's ethics are too rigid or "cold" because they demand duty for duty's sake, regardless of personal feelings or consequences.</note>, and I provided a concise overall description of his ethics in Chapter XIV of the 2nd volume of my *History of Philosophy* (4th ed., pp. 224–246). The primary work has remained, even now, the one by H. Cohen mentioned above (from 1877, second significantly expanded edition 1910). In addition, to delve deeper into the systematic nature of Kant’s ethics, I recommend the first, general part of Paul Natorp’s *Social Pedagogy* (3rd edition 1909); for the relationship between the transcendental and the psychological, see *A. Hegler, Psychology in Kant’s Ethics*, 1891. A generally accessible introduction to the content of Kant's ethical writings and their main problems is provided by *A. Messer, Kant’s Ethics* (Leipzig 1904), a shorter

### XLIII

# Introduction.

*A. Buchenau, Kant’s Doctrine of the Categorical Imperative* (Leipzig 1913). For those who find Messer’s bibliographic details insufficient, approximately 150 writings, treatises, and essays on Kant's ethics are named in Volume III of Überweg's *Compendium* <note>original: "Grundriss"</note> regarding § 38 (11th ed. 1914).

Finally, a word about the position of the *Critique of Practical Reason* within Kant's system—that is, its relationship to his other ethical writings. We can set aside the smaller treatises that discuss specialized problems here. Within the three main works: the *Groundwork* (1785), the *Critique of Practical Reason* (1788), and the *Metaphysics of Morals* (1797), our current volume occupies a middle position, not just chronologically but also substantively, though in such a way that it moves closer to the first-mentioned work. The *Groundwork* provides a comprehensive introduction, the *Critique of Practical Reason* the systematic core, and the *Metaphysics of Morals* the application (in the doctrines of virtue and law). In terms of content, our text offers hardly anything new compared to the *Groundwork*. The difference actually lies only in the different arrangement of the material necessitated by the different purpose, which naturally also accounts for the greater length of this main work. The primary concepts are the same in both; however, the *Groundwork* proceeds essentially <term>analytically</term> <note>An analytic method starts with common knowledge or experience and works backward to find the underlying principles that make it possible.</note>, while the *Critique of Practical Reason* proceeds <term>synthetically</term> <note>A synthetic method starts with established principles and works forward to show how they produce a complete system of knowledge.</note>. <gloss>¹)</gloss> For this reason, as one can see from a glance at our two subject indexes, the systematic concepts come more to the fore in the second work: law, principles, <term>antinomy</term> <note>A contradiction between two laws or principles that both seem equally logical or necessary.</note>, <term>deduction</term> <note>In Kantian terms, a "deduction" is a legal-style justification of how a concept has the right to be applied to experience.</note>, theoretical and practical knowledge, the mere legislating form, the concept of freedom, the moral law, and (entirely new): the

---

<gloss>¹)</gloss> Natorp judges similarly in the introduction to his edition: 
> "Every reader of both works knows that the *Groundwork* and the *Critique of Practical Reason* actually cover the same main content; they differ almost only formally, specifically in that the development of thought in the *Groundwork* follows a more analytic path, and in the *Critique of Practical Reason*, a more synthetic one." 

Cf. also Messer, as cited above, p. 95 ff.

XLIV

Introduction.

...proceeds <term>synthetically</term> <note>A synthetic method builds up a system from its foundational principles to its applications, whereas an analytic method breaks down existing knowledge to find its foundations.</note>, focusing on the categories of freedom, the primacy [of practical reason over theoretical reason], the <term>Typic of Judgment</term> <note>The "Typic" is the rule Kant uses to apply the abstract moral law to concrete, physical reality.</note>, the postulates, and the entire related doctrine of the highest good. In the *Groundwork* <note>original: "Grundlegung"</note>, by contrast, besides common fundamental concepts such as: autonomy, idea, interest, maxim, incentive, etc., only a few other, more introductory and popular concepts are brought to the fore, such as: the good will, the kingdom of ends, the self-end or final end, and above all—in the well-known threefold formulation—the categorical imperative. For the rest, I refer the reader to the study of both writings themselves with the help of my introductions and subject indexes.

## **B. Textual History.**
### *1. The Original Editions.*

Of the five or six original editions, the fifth and sixth are—aside from very minor oversights (such as punctuation errors)—exact reprints of the fourth, just as the fourth was a reprint of the first. The only difference is that in the fifth edition, through tighter printing, the 292 pages of the first were condensed into 285, and in the sixth edition, they were squeezed into only 236 pages. Therefore, only the first and second editions come into consideration for a <term>textual comparison</term> <note>The process of looking at two versions of a book side-by-side to find changes made by the author or printer.</note>. Even these two are not very different from one another. To be sure, the second edition contains a number of obvious improvements, but also several "deteriorations" of the first edition (compare our list of all significant <term>variants</term> <note>original: "Varianten"; these are specific instances where the wording differs between editions.</note> provided below the main text). From this alone, one can conclude that Kant did not personally supervise the printing of the second edition. This is also made probable by Hartknoch’s <note>Johann Friedrich Hartknoch was Kant's primary publisher.</note> letter to Kant from September 29, 1789, which suggests that not Kant, but a certain Hertel (Hartknoch’s representative in Leipzig) sent a copy of the work to the printer (Mauke in Jena). In the case of the fourth edition, this is even explicitly attested by Kant’s letter to Hartknoch on January 28, 1797 (see page XXIV above). Since Kant thus only took a verifiable part in the printing of the first edition—notably, even out of the four corrections found in his personal copy, only one was included in the second edition—

XLVI

Introduction.

— found — so Kehrbach and Natorp were right to use these as the basis for their text; naturally while also taking into account the <term>variants</term> <note>Differences in wording between different printings.</note> of the later editions.

## 2. *On the History of Textual Criticism.*

The first "list of printer's errors" was provided by Grillo (Berlin) in the *Philosophical Gazette, Year I 1795, issues 41–48*, but this was not even used in the original 1797 edition, and even less so by later editors; this was no great loss, as (according to Natorp) it contains only about a dozen real corrections alongside much that is worthless—corrections which, in any case, almost all later editors found on their own. Rosenkranz, who based his text on the second edition, as in other matters, hardly concerned himself with the shaping of the text. In contrast, numerous improvements here are also thanks to Hartenstein, who used the 6th edition—while considering the second—as his foundation. Kirchmann’s edition is, as usual, a mere reprint of Hartenstein’s from 1867. Kehrbach also followed Hartenstein almost entirely; only three readings (pages 59, 67, and 107 of our edition) originate from him. Of all previous editors, Natorp has performed the most <term>textual critical</term> <note>The scholarly process of comparing versions of a text to identify the author's original intent and remove errors.</note> work; in this, as he notes in his Academy Edition, he was supported by Görland (Hamburg), Nolte (Kassel), and K. Vorländer (Solingen). Natorp was also able to use the <term>conjectures</term> <note>original: "Konjekturen"; educated guesses by scholars to fix parts of the text that seem broken or nonsensical.</note> of E. Adickes (*Kant Studies V 211 ff.*) and Wille (ibid. *VIII 467 ff.*). There followed the remarks of O. Schöndörffer (*Old Prussian Monthly 48, 1 ff.*), E. von Aster (*Kant Studies XIV, 468 ff.*), K. Vorländer (*Journal for Philosophy 138, 137 ff.*) and the 2nd edition of the <term>Academy Edition</term> <note>original: "Akad. Ausg."; the standard, authoritative collection of Kant's complete works.</note> (1913); finally, in 1914, the edition by B. Kellermann (see above, 14.), which carefully utilized all previous ones and brought forward several noteworthy suggestions of its own.

Our own edition is based on a renewed, precise comparison with the Academy Edition, taking into account all those that have appeared since—

## Immanuel Kant.

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page begins the Preface to Immanuel Kant’s "Critique of Practical Reason" (1788). It follows the title pages and serves as the introductory justification for the work's scope.</meta>

# Preface.

***

Why this critique is not titled a Critique of *Pure* Practical Reason, but simply a **Critique of Practical Reason** in general—even though the parallelism between this work and the Critique of Speculative Reason seems to require the former—is sufficiently explained in this treatise. Its purpose is merely to demonstrate that there is such a thing as <term>pure practical reason</term> <note>Reason that can determine the will and guide action on its own, without being driven by desires or sensory impulses.</note>, and with this intent, it critiques the entirety of our practical faculty. <margin>10</margin> If it succeeds in this task, there is no need to critique the pure faculty itself to see whether reason is overstepping its bounds by making a mere empty claim (as often happens with <term>speculative reason</term> <note>Theoretical reason used for gaining knowledge of the world; the subject of Kant's first "Critique."</note>). For if reason, as pure reason, is actually practical, it proves its own reality and the reality of its concepts through the *deed*, and all abstract arguing against the possibility of it being so is in vain.

With the establishment of this faculty, <term>transcendental freedom</term> <note>The capacity to start a chain of events entirely on one's own, independent of prior causes in the natural world.</note> is also now firmly established. It is taken here in that <margin>20</margin> absolute sense which speculative reason required when using the concept of **causality**. This was necessary for reason to rescue itself from the <term>antinomy</term> <note>A logical conflict where two contradictory conclusions both seem equally valid; here, the conflict between "everything has a cause" and "we are free."</note> it inevitably falls into when trying to think of the **unconditioned** <note>Something that exists or acts on its own, not dependent on anything else.</note> within a series of cause-and-effect relationships. However, speculative reason could only propose this concept "problematically"—as something not impossible to think—without being able to guarantee its objective reality. It did so only to avoid the claim that such freedom was impossible, which would have <margin>30</margin>

<summary>Kant explains why this book is titled the "Critique of Practical Reason," arguing that if we can prove humans are capable of acting on pure reason alone, we simultaneously prove the existence of true freedom.</summary>
<keywords>pure practical reason, speculative reason, transcendental freedom, causality, antinomy, the unconditioned, Kantian ethics</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface of the "Critique of Practical Reason." It concludes a thought on skepticism from the previous page and introduces the central role of Freedom as the foundation for the concepts of God and Immortality.</meta>

# Preface.

***

...must be allowed as thinkable, being challenged in its essence and plunged into an abyss of skepticism.

The concept of **freedom**, insofar as its **reality** is proven by an **apodictic law** <note>A law that is absolutely certain or necessary, not merely probable.</note> of **practical reason**, now forms the **keystone** of the entire structure of a system of **pure**, even <margin>[4]</margin> of **speculative reason**. All other concepts (those of **God** and **immortality**), which remain unsupported as mere **ideas** within speculative reason, <margin>10</margin> now attach themselves to it and gain, with and through it, **stability** and **objective reality**; that is, their **possibility** is proven by the fact that **freedom** is actual; for this idea reveals itself through the **moral law**.

**Freedom**, however, is also the only one among all the ideas of speculative reason whose possibility we know <term>prior to experience</term> <note>original: *a priori*</note>, without yet having insight into it, because it is the **condition**\*) of the **moral law**, which <margin>20</margin> we know. The ideas of **God** and **immortality**, however, are not conditions of the moral law, but only conditions of the **necessary object** of a **will** determined by this law—that is, of the merely **practical use** of our pure reason. Thus, regarding those ideas, we cannot claim to recognize or have insight into—I will not say merely their **actuality**—but not even their **possibility**. Nevertheless, they are the **conditions** for the **application** of the morally determined...

***

\*) So that one does not imagine finding inconsistencies here, when I now call freedom the condition of the moral law and later in the treatise assert that the moral law is the condition under which we can first become aware of freedom, I will only remind you that freedom is indeed the <term>ground of being</term> <note>original: *ratio essendi*; the reason why the moral law exists in the first place.</note> of the moral law, but the moral law is the <term>ground of knowing</term> <note>original: *ratio cognoscendi*; the way through which we become aware of our freedom.</note> freedom. For had the moral law not been clearly thought in our reason first, we would never consider ourselves justified in assuming something such as freedom (even if it is not self-contradictory). If, however, there were no freedom, the moral law would not be found in us at all.

<summary>Kant establishes Freedom as the "keystone" of his philosophical system, arguing that while we cannot "prove" God or Immortality through logic, their possibility is secured once the reality of Freedom is proven through the Moral Law. He clarifies the relationship between Freedom and Moral Law: Freedom makes the law possible, but the law is how we come to know we are free.</summary>
<keywords>Freedom, Moral Law, God, Immortality, Practical Reason, Speculative Reason, Ground of Being, Ground of Knowing, Apodictic Law</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface of the "Critique of Practical Reason," picking up from the discussion on how the moral law determines the will toward its necessary object.</meta>

# Preface.

...will towards its object given to it <term>prior to experience</term> <note>original: *a priori*; knowledge that is independent of sensory experience.</note> (the **highest good**). Consequently, their possibility can and must be assumed in this practical relationship, without however recognizing or understanding them theoretically. For this latter demand, it is sufficient for a practical purpose that they contain no internal impossibility (contradiction). Here we now have a ground for <margin>[10]</margin> **holding-to-be-true** <note>original: *Fürwahrhalten*; the mental act of accepting something as true, which Kant distinguishes based on whether the grounds are subjective or objective.</note> that is merely subjective when compared to speculative reason, yet is objectively valid for an equally pure but **practical reason**.

Through this, the ideas of **God** and **immortality** are granted objective reality and authority—even subjective necessity (a need of pure reason) to assume them—by means of the concept of **freedom**. This is achieved <margin>[5]</margin> without expanding reason in its theoretical knowledge; instead, the possibility, which was previously only a problem <note>In Kantian logic, a "problem" is a concept that contains no contradiction but whose objective reality cannot be proven.</note>, here becomes an **assertion** <note>original: *Assertion*; a positive statement of truth or fact.</note>. Thus, the practical use of reason is linked with the elements of the theoretical. 

This need is not <margin>[20]</margin> some hypothetical requirement for an arbitrary purpose of speculation—where one must assume something if they wish to reach the completion of reason’s use in speculation—but rather a lawful requirement to assume something without which that which one must unceasingly set as the aim of one’s conduct cannot happen. <margin>[10]</margin>

It would certainly be more satisfying for our **speculative reason** to resolve those tasks for itself without this detour and to preserve them as insight for practical use; however, our capacity for speculation is simply not that well-off. Those who boast of such high <margin>[30]</margin> insights should not hold back with them, but should present them publicly for examination and esteem. They want to prove them; well then! Let them prove them, and the Critique will lay all its armor at their feet as victors. *Why do you stand still? They are unwilling. And yet they are permitted to be happy.* <note>original: "Quid statis? Nolunt. Atqui licet esse beatis." From Horace, Satires I, 1, 19. Kant uses this to mock those who claim to have metaphysical proofs of God but refuse to produce them when challenged.</note> — Since they in fact do not want to, presumably because <margin>[40]</margin> they cannot...

<summary>Kant explains that while God and immortality cannot be proven theoretically, they must be assumed as "assertions" to make sense of the moral life. He challenges dogmatic philosophers to produce their supposed proofs or admit the limits of human reason.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Preface of the "Critique of Practical Reason." It resolves a perceived contradiction between Kant's first Critique (which restricted knowledge to the physical world) and this second Critique (which finds reality in moral concepts).</meta>

# Preface.

...if they cannot do so, we must nonetheless take them up once again. We must seek the concepts of **God**, **freedom**, and **immortality**—for which **speculation** <note>Speculation, in this context, refers to the attempt to gain knowledge through pure logic and reasoning alone, without using sensory evidence.</note> cannot find a sufficient guarantee of their possibility—in the moral use of reason, and ground them upon that use.

<margin>10</margin> Here, for the first time, the "riddle" of the <term>Critique</term> <note>Kant refers to his previous work, the *Critique of Pure Reason*, which set strict limits on what the human mind can know.</note> is explained. It explains how one can deny **objective reality** <note>Validity or "truth" when applied to real objects.</note> to the **categories** <note>The basic concepts of the human mind, such as "cause and effect," which we use to organize our thoughts.</note> when they are used speculatively to reach beyond the senses, yet grant them this same reality regarding the objects of pure **practical reason**. Until one knows this practical use by more than just name, such a move must necessarily appear inconsistent. However, once we undergo a complete analysis of the latter, we realize that the reality mentioned here does not involve any theoretical definition of the categories <margin>20</margin> or an expansion of knowledge into the **supersensible** <note>The realm beyond the physical world and our five senses.</note>. Instead, it simply means that, in this relationship, an object belongs to them. This is because these categories are either contained in the necessary determination of the will <term>prior to experience</term> <note>original: *a priori*; knowledge that is independent of sensory experience.</note> or are inseparably connected with the object of that will. Thus, the inconsistency vanishes because we are using these <margin>[6]</margin> concepts in a different way than speculative reason requires.

In contrast, we now find a hardly expected and very satisfying confirmation of the consistent way of thinking in the speculative Critique. That earlier work insisted <margin>30</margin> that objects of experience as such—including our own selves as subjects—should be regarded only as **appearances**, while nonetheless placing **things-in-themselves** <note>The true nature of things as they exist independently of our human perception.</note> at their foundation. It urged us not to consider everything beyond the senses as a mere fiction or its concept as empty. Now, practical reason, on its own and without having made any prior agreement with speculative reason, grants reality to a supersensible object of the...

a) Erdmann: "it." <note>A scholarly correction regarding the pronoun reference in the original German text.</note>
b) Natorp, Adickes: "of the." <note>A scholarly correction regarding the grammar of the definite article in the German source.</note>

<summary>Kant explains the "riddle" of his philosophy: while we cannot "know" things like God or freedom through scientific logic, they gain "objective reality" through our moral actions and the requirements of our will. This provides a bridge between his theories on human knowledge and his theories on human ethics.</summary>
<keywords>God, freedom, immortality, speculation, practical reason, categories, objective reality, appearances, things-in-themselves, Critique of Pure Reason, Immanuel Kant</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Preface of the "Critique of Practical Reason." It resolves the tension between Kant's first Critique (Pure Reason) and his second (Practical Reason) by explaining how "freedom" moves from a mere abstract thought to a proven fact through our moral actions.</meta>

# Preface.

...category of **causality**, namely **freedom**, and grants it **reality** (though as a practical concept, this reality is only for practical use). In doing so, it confirms through a fact what could previously only be thought. At the same time, the strange but undeniable claim of **speculative critique** <note>Kant refers to his previous work, the *Critique of Pure Reason*, which examined how we think and know things.</note>—that even the thinking **subject** <note>The "subject" is the person doing the thinking or experiencing.</note> appears to itself in **inner intuition** merely as an **appearance**—receives its full confirmation <margin>10</margin> in the *Critique of Practical Reason*. This confirmation is so strong that one would be forced to arrive at this conclusion even if the former work had not proven the point at all.*)

Through this, I also understand why the most significant **objections** against the *Critique* that have come to my attention so far revolve around these two hinges: first, the **objective reality** of the **categories** applied to **noumena** <note>The "noumenon" (plural "noumena") is the reality of a thing as it exists in itself, which Kant argues we cannot know through our physical senses.</note>, which is denied in theoretical knowledge but asserted in practical knowledge; and second, the paradoxical demand to view oneself <margin>20</margin> as a subject of freedom (a **noumenon**) while simultaneously viewing oneself as a **phenomenon** <note>The "phenomenon" is the world as we experience it through our senses and the laws of physics—how things "appear" to us.</note> in relation to nature within one's own empirical consciousness.

For as long as one did not have specific concepts of **morality** and freedom, one could not guess what should be placed at the foundation of a supposed appearance as a **noumenon**. Nor could one tell whether it was even possible to form a concept of it at all, especially if one had already dedicated all concepts of pure understanding <margin>30</margin> in theoretical use exclusively to mere appearances. Only a detailed *Critique of Practical Reason* [7] can remove all these misunderstandings and [demonstrate] the consistency...

---

*) The union of **causality** as freedom with causality as the **mechanism of nature** is impossible without representing the human being in two ways. The first is established through the **moral law**, the second through the **law of nature**, and both exist within one and the same subject: the human being. This requires us to view the human in relation to the moral law as a **being in itself** <note>Original: *Wesen an sich selbst*. This refers to the core, spiritual, or essential reality of a person that exists beyond their physical body.</note> and in relation to nature as an appearance. The former exists in pure consciousness, the latter in empirical <note>Based on observation and experience.</note> consciousness. Without this distinction, the contradiction of reason with itself is unavoidable.

<summary>Kant explains that the human being must be viewed in two ways: as a free moral agent (a noumenon) and as a physical being subject to the laws of nature (a phenomenon). This distinction allows freedom and scientific laws to coexist without contradicting each other.</summary>
<keywords>causality, freedom, reality, speculative critique, subject, intuition, appearance, practical reason, objections, noumena, categories, phenomenon, morality, mechanism of nature, moral law, law of nature, being in itself</keywords>

# Preface.

...consistent **way of thinking**, which constitutes its greatest advantage, into a clear light.

So much for the justification of why, in this work, the concepts and principles of **pure speculative reason** <note>This refers to the theoretical side of reason—how we understand the physical world—which Kant explored in his previous book.</note>—which have already undergone their own specific critique—are here and there subjected to examination once again. In the systematic development of a science, such a move is otherwise not very appropriate (since matters already judged should ideally only be <margin>10</margin> cited and not brought up for discussion again). However, it was permitted here, and even necessary, because reason is now being considered with those concepts in transition to a completely different **use** than the one it made of them before. Such a transition makes a comparison of the older use with the newer one necessary, in order to distinguish the "new track" from the previous one and, at the same time, to make the connection between them noticeable. Therefore, one should not view reflections of this kind—among others, <margin>20</margin> those directed once more toward the concept of **freedom**, but now in the **practical use** <note>The "practical use" of reason refers to how we decide what we *ought* to do (morality and ethics), rather than just knowing what *is* (science).</note> of pure reason—as mere "inserts" that only serve to fill gaps in the system of speculative reason. (For that system is complete in its own purpose.) One should not see them as one sees an over-hasty construction project <insert>and</insert> <note>original: "und"; corrected by the editor Hartenstein to "um" (in order to)</note>, which adds supports and buttresses after the fact. Instead, these are true members of the structure that make the connection of the system visible, allowing concepts that could only be presented **problematically** <note>In Kant’s terms, a "problematic" concept is one that is logically possible but cannot be proven to exist in reality through theory alone.</note> there <margin>30</margin> to be seen now in their **real representation**. 

This reminder applies primarily to the concept of **freedom**. One must note with some surprise that so many still boast of being able to understand it perfectly and explain its <insert>possibility</insert> <note>This correction appears in the second edition.</note> by viewing it merely in a **psychological** context. However, if they had previously considered it precisely in a **transcendental** <note>The "transcendental" perspective looks at the underlying rules that make experience possible in the first place, rather than just observing how people behave.</note> context, they would have recognized both its indispensability...

As if, before him, the world had been ignorant of what duty is, or in thoroughgoing error! But anyone who knows what a **formula** means to a mathematician—which defines quite precisely what must be done to solve a problem and does not allow for failure—will not consider a formula that does this in regard to all duty in general to be something insignificant and dispensable.

# Preface.

...completeness was not added, as was done in the **Critique of Speculative Reason** <note>The *Critique of Pure Reason*, which dealt with how we know things through science and logic.</note>; there is a valid reason for this in the very nature of this **practical faculty of reason**. For the specific classification of duties as **human duties** <note>Specific moral obligations that apply specifically to human beings, rather than all rational beings in general.</note>—to divide them into categories—is only possible if the subject of this classification (man) is first understood in his actual condition, at least as much as is necessary in relation to duty in general. However, this study does not belong in a *Critique of Practical Reason* in general, which is intended only to provide a complete account of the principles of its possibility, its extent, and its limits without specific reference to **human nature**. Therefore, the classification belongs here to the **system of science** <note>Kant refers here to the "Metaphysics of Morals," the actual application of these principles, which he published later.</note>, not to the system of critique.

I have responded to a certain truth-loving and sharp—and thus always respectable—reviewer of that *Groundwork for the Metaphysics of Morals* <note>A reference to Gottlob August Tittel or perhaps Hermann Andreas Pistorius, who were early critics of Kant's moral theory.</note> regarding his objection: that the concept

\*) One might also raise the objection: why have I not also previously explained the concept of the **faculty of desire** or the **feeling of pleasure**? Yet this reproach would be unfair, because one should reasonably be able to assume this explanation as having been provided in **psychology**. However, a definition there could certainly be arranged in such a way that the feeling of pleasure is made the basis of the determination of the faculty of desire (as is indeed commonly done); but through this, the supreme principle of practical philosophy would necessarily turn out to be **empirical** <note>Based on sensory experience or physical feelings like pleasure/pain, rather than on pure reason.</note>, which is the very thing that remains to be determined and is entirely refuted in this Critique. Therefore, I will give this explanation here as it must be, in order to leave this disputed point undecided at the beginning, as is only fair. — **Life** is the capacity of a being to act according to the laws of the **faculty of desire**. # Preface.

...the Analytic <note>The "Analytic" is the first major section of the book, where Kant breaks down the principles of reason into their basic building blocks.</note>, as I hope, I have given sufficient satisfaction; I have likewise taken into account several other objections sent to me by men who show that the discovery of truth is close to their hearts (for those who have only their old system before their eyes, and who have already decided beforehand what is to be approved or disapproved, do not require any explanation that might stand in the way of their private purposes); <margin>10</margin>
and I shall continue to do so in the future.

When we are concerned with determining a particular faculty <note>A "faculty" (*Vermögen*) is a specific power or capacity of the mind, such as the power to reason, to desire, or to perceive.</note> of the human soul according to its sources, contents, and limits, the nature of human knowledge makes it impossible to do otherwise <margin>[10]</margin> than to begin with a precise and complete description of its parts <note>original: "nicht anders als von den Teilen derselben, ihrer genauen..." — a footnote in the 2nd edition changes "not" to "nothing".</note>...

...of the object or the action with the **subjective** conditions of life, that is, with the **faculty** of **causality** <note>In Kant’s technical vocabulary, causality here refers to the mind’s power to bring an object into reality simply by wanting or "representing" it.</note> of a representation in regard to the **actuality** of its object (or the **determination** of the subject’s powers to the action of producing it). I need nothing more for the purpose of the **critique** of concepts borrowed from **psychology**; the **critique** itself provides the rest. It is easy to see that the question of whether **pleasure** must always be the ground of the **faculty of desire**, or whether under certain conditions it only follows upon the **determination** of that faculty, remains undecided by this explanation; for it is composed of nothing but markers of the **pure understanding**, that is, **categories**, which contain nothing empirical. Such **caution** is highly recommended in all of philosophy and is yet often neglected—namely, not to anticipate one’s judgments by a daring **definition** before the complete **analysis** <note>Kant uses the word *Zergliederung*, literally "dismembering," to describe the logical process of breaking a complex idea down into its simplest parts.</note> of the concept, which is often only achieved very late. One will also notice throughout the entire course of the critique (of both theoretical and practical reason) that various opportunities arise to supplement many deficiencies in the old dogmatic path of philosophy and to correct errors that are not noticed until one makes a **use of reason** <note>The scholar Paul Natorp questioned the original phrasing "reason a use," suggesting the standard "use of reason" was intended.</note> that concerns its entirety.

<summary>Kant defends his use of precise, abstract definitions over traditional psychological ones, arguing that true philosophical progress requires analyzing concepts in their entirety rather than jumping to conclusions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the preface to the "Critique of Practical Reason." Kant is transitioning from the "analytic" stage (breaking down principles) to the "architectonic" stage (viewing the system as a whole). # Preface.

...and (as much as is possible according to the current state of our already acquired elements <note>The scholar Vorländer suggests "elements" here refers to "cognitions" or "items of knowledge".</note> of the same) to begin with a complete presentation. However, there is a second level of attention required, which is more philosophical and **architectonic** <note>The "architectonic" is what Kant calls the "art of systems." It is the method of turning a collection of facts into a structured, organized body of science.</note>: namely, to correctly grasp the **idea of the whole** and, from that idea, to view all those parts in their reciprocal relationship to one another, by means of their derivation from the concept of that whole within a pure faculty of reason. <margin>10</margin> This examination and verification is only possible through the most intimate acquaintance with the system. Those who were too weary to pursue the first stage of research, and thus did not think it worth the effort to acquire this acquaintance, never reach the second stage: the overview. This overview is a **synthetic** return to what was previously given **analytically** <note>In philosophy, "analysis" means breaking a concept down into its parts, while "synthesis" means putting those parts together to see how they form a unified whole.</note>. It is no wonder if such readers find inconsistencies everywhere, <margin>20</margin> even though the "gaps" they suspect are not found in the system itself, but merely in their own disjointed train of thought.

Regarding this treatise, I fear nothing from the reproach of wanting to introduce a "new language," because here the mode of cognition naturally approaches **popularity** <note>In Kant’s time, "popularity" meant making a subject accessible to the general public rather than just academic specialists.</note>. This reproach could not have occurred to anyone regarding the first *Critique* <note>Referring to his previous work, the *Critique of Pure Reason*.</note> who had not merely leafed through it, but had actually thought through it. To invent new words <margin>30</margin> where the language already has no lack of expressions for given concepts is a childish effort to distinguish oneself among the crowd—if not through new and true thoughts, then by putting a new patch on an old garment. Therefore, if readers of that work know of more popular expressions that are just as appropriate to the thought as mine seem to be—or if they feel confident they can demonstrate the futility of these thoughts themselves, and thus of every expression designating them—they would <margin>40</margin> greatly [oblige me] by the former...

<summary>Kant argues that a true understanding of his philosophy requires seeing the "entire system" rather than just its parts, and he defends his technical terminology as necessary for precision, dismissing the idea that he is merely inventing new words for the sake of it.</summary>

# Preface.

...new language, for I only wish to be understood; regarding the second point <note>This refers to the "architectonic" or systematic structure mentioned on the previous page.</note>, however, one would earn merit for philosophy. But as long as those thoughts still stand, I doubt very much that more appropriate and yet more common expressions could be found for them. [11] *)

---

*) More than that lack of clarity, I fear occasional misunderstandings here and there regarding certain expressions that I selected with the greatest care so as not to miss the concept to which they point. Thus, in the Table of the Categories of Practical Reason, under the heading of **Modality**, the **Permitted** and **Forbidden** (the practically-objectively possible and impossible) have almost the same meaning in common usage as the subsequent categories of **Duty** and that which is **Contrary to Duty**. 

However, in this system, the former is intended to mean that which is in agreement or conflict with a merely *possible* practical rule (such as the solution to all problems in geometry and mechanics), while the latter refers to what stands in such a relationship to a law *actually* residing in reason in general. This distinction in meaning is not entirely foreign to common usage, although it is somewhat unusual. For example, it is **forbidden** for an orator, as such, to forge new words or phrases; for a poet, it is permitted to a certain extent; in neither case is "duty" considered here. For if someone wants to ruin their reputation as an orator, no one can stop them. Here, we are only concerned with the distinction of **imperatives** <note>An "imperative" is a command of reason. Kant distinguishes between those that are optional and those that are morally required.</note> based on **problematic**, **assertoric**, and **apodictic** <note term="problematic, assertoric, apodictic">These are logical terms for levels of certainty: **problematic** refers to what is possible (might be), **assertoric** to what is actual (is), and **apodictic** to what is necessary (must be).</note> grounds of determination. 

Likewise, in that note where I compared the moral ideas of practical perfection in various philosophical schools, I distinguished the idea of **Wisdom** from that of **Holiness**, even though I have explained them as being essentially and objectively the same thing. However, in this context, I mean only that wisdom which a human (the Stoic <note>The Stoics were ancient Greek philosophers who taught that virtue is the highest good and that humans should remain indifferent to external circumstances.</note>) claims for himself—that is, a quality subjectively attributed to man. (Perhaps the term **Virtue**, with which the Stoic also made a great display, might better characterize the essence of his school.) But the expression of a **postulate** <note term="postulate">In Kant's philosophy, a "postulate" is a requirement of pure practical reason—something we must assume to be true (like free will or the existence of God) in order for morality to make sense, even if we cannot prove it through scientific logic.</note> of pure practical reason could <note>The scholar Hartenstein suggests the reading "could" (*könnte*) instead of the original "was able to" (*konnte*).</note> still cause the most misunderstanding, if one were to confuse it with the meaning...

<summary>Kant defends his technical terminology, specifically explaining why he distinguishes between "permitted actions" and "duties," and clarifies his use of terms like Wisdom, Virtue, and Postulate.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the preface to the "Critique of Practical Reason." In the preceding text, Kant defended his use of technical terminology, arguing that precise language is necessary for a scientific system of philosophy. Here, he begins to conclude his overview of the mind’s faculties—knowledge and desire—and addresses potential skeptics who might deny the existence of knowledge independent of experience.</meta>

# Preface.

<margin>[12]</margin> In this way, the <term>a priori principles</term><note>Principles that are known by reason alone, independent of sensory experience.</note> of two faculties of the mind—the **faculty of knowledge** and the **faculty of desire**—have now been identified. They have been defined according to the conditions, the scope, and the limits of their use. By doing this, a secure foundation has been laid for a systematic philosophy, both theoretical and practical, as a proper science.

But no worse fate could befall these efforts than if someone were to make the unexpected <margin>10</margin> discovery that there is no such thing as *a priori* knowledge at all, nor could there ever be. But there is no cause for concern here. To claim such a thing would be the same as if someone tried to use reason to prove that reason does not exist. For we only say that we know something through reason when we are aware that we could have known it even if it had not appeared to us in experience; therefore, rational knowledge and *a priori* knowledge are one and the same. To try to squeeze necessity out of a statement based on experience—to try, as the saying goes, <margin>20</margin> to <gloss>squeeze water from a pumice stone</gloss><note>original Latin: "ex pumice aquam"</note>—and to try to give a judgment true universality by this means is a direct contradiction. (Without universality, there can be no logical conclusion, not even a conclusion by <term>analogy</term><note>A comparison between two things; Kant argues even this requires an underlying assumption of universal rules.</note>, which at least presumes a likely universality and objective necessity). Subjective necessity, that is,

---

...which the <term>postulates</term><note>Assumptions that must be made in order to act or to make sense of a system.</note> of pure mathematics have, and which carry <term>apodictic certainty</term><note>Absolute, necessary certainty that cannot be doubted.</note> with them. But these postulate the possibility of an action whose object has already been recognized *a priori* and theoretically with complete certainty as being possible. The former [the practical postulate], however, postulates the possibility of an object itself (**God** and the **immortality of the soul**) based on certain practical laws—thus solely for the sake of <term>practical reason</term><note>Reason as it relates to moral action and the will.</note>. In this case, the certainty of this postulated possibility is not theoretical, and thus not "apodictic" (that is, it is not a necessity recognized in the object itself). Instead, it is a necessary assumption for the subject (the human being) in order to follow its objective but practical laws. Therefore, it is merely a necessary **hypothesis**. I could find no better way to describe this subjective, yet true and unconditional, necessity of reason.

---

<gloss>a) = water from a pumice stone (i.e., something impossible). Plautus, Persa Act I, Scene 1, line 41.</gloss>

<summary>Kant asserts that he has successfully established the foundational principles for both theoretical and practical philosophy. He defends the existence of "a priori" knowledge against skeptics, arguing that reason itself depends on it, and introduces the idea of "postulates" like God and immortality as necessary assumptions for moral life.</summary>
<keywords>a priori, faculty of knowledge, faculty of desire, reason, necessity, postulates, practical reason, hypothesis, God, immortality of the soul, Plautus</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues Kant’s argument against skepticism. Having established that rational knowledge must be "a priori" (independent of experience), he now examines the consequences of rejecting this necessity. He specifically addresses David Hume’s view that our understanding of cause and effect is merely a "habit" rather than a law of reason.</meta>

# Preface.

To substitute habit for objective necessity (which only exists in <term>a priori</term><note>Latin: "a priori." Refers to knowledge that comes from pure reason rather than from sensory experience.</note> judgments) is to deny reason the power to judge an object. It would mean we could not truly know an object or what belongs to it. For example, regarding something that frequently and always follows a certain preceding state, we could not say that one can **conclude from the former to the latter** (for that would imply <term>objective necessity</term><note>The idea that something must happen by a universal law, not just by chance.</note> and the concept of a connection known independent of experience). Instead, we would only be allowed <margin>10</margin> to expect similar cases (in a manner similar to animals). This would essentially reject the concept of "cause" as false and a mere mental deception.

If one tried to remedy this lack of objective (and thus universal) validity by saying we see no reason to assign a different way of thinking to other rational beings—and if that were treated as a valid conclusion—then our ignorance would do more to expand our knowledge than all our reflection. For simply because we do not know of any rational beings other than <margin>20</margin> humans, we would have a right to assume they are constituted just as we know ourselves to be; that is, we would claim to truly know them.

I will not even mention here that it is not the universal consensus of "holding-to-be-true" that proves the objective validity of a judgment (that is, its validity as <margin>[13]</margin> knowledge). Even if such a consensus happened to occur by chance, it still would not provide proof of agreement with the object. Rather, it is objective validity alone that forms the basis <margin>30</margin> for a necessary, universal agreement.

Hume would feel very much at home with this system of universal <term>empiricism</term><note>The philosophical belief that all knowledge comes only or primarily from sensory experience.</note> regarding fundamental principles. For, as is well known, he asked for nothing more than that a merely subjective necessity—namely **habit**—be assumed in the concept of cause instead of any objective meaning of necessity. He did this in order to deny reason any judgment concerning God, freedom, and immortality. And he certainly knew how <margin>40</margin> to draw conclusions with total logical consistency from those principles, once they were granted to him. But even Hume did not make empiricism so universal as to include...

<summary>Kant argues that if we treat causality as a mere habit (as Hume did), we lose the ability to truly know the world and reduce human reasoning to animal-like expectation. He insists that universal agreement among people does not prove a truth; rather, a truth is only universal because it is objectively valid.</summary>

<keywords>David Hume, causality, habit, a priori, objective validity, empiricism, reason, judgment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kant’s defense of the necessity of reason. Here, he addresses how David Hume’s skeptical approach to knowledge would, if applied universally, undermine even the certainty of mathematics.</meta>

# Preface.

Even Hume himself did not design his <term>empiricism</term><note>The philosophical theory that all knowledge is derived from sensory experience.</note> to include mathematics. He considered its propositions to be <term>analytic</term><note>Statements that are true by definition; for example, "all circles are round" is analytic because the concept of "roundness" is already contained within the concept of "circle."</note>, and if that were correct, they would indeed also be <term>apodictic</term><note>Latin: "apodicticus." Describing a truth that is necessary and absolutely certain, leaving no room for doubt or contradiction.</note>. Nevertheless, one could not then conclude that reason has the power to make apodictic judgments in philosophy as well—specifically those that are <term>synthetic</term><note>Statements that add new information to a subject rather than just defining it; for example, "the house is blue." Kant argues that the law of cause and effect is synthetic but still necessary.</note> (such as the law of causality). However, if one were to assume a universal empiricism of principles, <margin>10</margin> then mathematics would also be entangled in it a).

Now, when mathematics comes into conflict with a reason that only permits empirical principles—as is inevitable in the <term>Antinomy</term><note>A logical contradiction where two opposing conclusions can both be proven. Kant uses the example of space being both infinitely divisible (mathematics) and made of simple parts (empiricism).</note>, where mathematics irrefutably proves the infinite divisibility of space, while empiricism cannot allow it—the greatest possible evidence of mathematical demonstration stands in open contradiction with the alleged conclusions drawn from principles of experience. <margin>20</margin> At this point, like the blind man described by Cheselden b), one must ask: what is deceiving me, my sight or my touch? (For empiricism is based on a **felt** necessity, whereas rationalism is based on a **perceived** necessity.) Thus, universal empiricism reveals itself as genuine <term>skepticism</term><note>The philosophical position that certain knowledge is impossible.</note>. This was falsely attributed to Hume in such an unlimited sense *), since he at least [held on to] one secure...

---

For although he does not merely concede, but insists that real [objects correspond to our representations of external things]...

<meta>This page transitions from the Preface to the formal Introduction of the Critique of Practical Reason. # Introduction.

---

### **On the Idea of a Critique of Practical Reason.**

The theoretical use of reason was concerned with objects of the mere <term>faculty of cognition</term><note>The mental power used for knowing and understanding things; our capacity to acquire knowledge.</note>, and a critique of reason in regard to that use concerned only the pure faculty of cognition, because this faculty raised a suspicion—which was later confirmed—that it easily loses itself beyond its boundaries, among unattainable <margin>10</margin> objects or even contradictory concepts. It is quite different with the <term>practical use of reason</term><note>Reason applied to our choices and actions—how we "ought" to act—rather than just understanding how the world "is."</note>. In this use, reason is concerned with the <term>determining grounds of the will</term><note>The reasons or motivations that lead a person to make a choice or take an action.</note>. The will is a faculty that either produces objects corresponding to <term>representations</term><note>The mental thoughts or goals we have in mind before we act.</note> or at least determines itself to bring them about (whether the physical power is sufficient or not)—that is, it determines its <term>causality</term><note>The power to act as a cause that produces an effect in the world.</note>. For in this case, reason can at least reach as far as the <margin>20</margin> determination of the will and, so far as it is a matter of volition alone, reason always has objective reality. Here, then, is the first question: whether pure reason is sufficient of itself to determine the will, or whether it can be a determining ground of the will only as something <term>empirically conditioned</term><note>Dependent on or influenced by sensory experiences, desires, or physical circumstances.</note>?^a) Now, at this point, a concept of causality enters which was justified by the *Critique of Pure Reason*, though it is not capable of being represented through experience: namely, the concept of **freedom**.

---

a) Kant wrote "of the same" [derselben]; corrected by Hartenstein. <note>The scholar Hartenstein corrected a grammatical ambiguity in Kant's original German to clarify that "determining ground" refers back to the "will."</note>

<summary>Kant introduces the distinction between theoretical reason (understanding objects) and practical reason (determining the will to act). He proposes the central question of the book: can pure reason guide our actions on its own, or is it always dependent on our sensory desires?</summary>
<keywords>theoretical reason, practical reason, faculty of cognition, will, causality, freedom, determining grounds, pure reason</keywords>

# Introduction.

<margin>10 [16]</margin>
<margin>20</margin>
...and if we can now find grounds to prove that this property <note>Referring to the capacity of "pure reason" to be practical—that is, to determine the will on its own.</note> actually belongs to the human will (and thus also to the will of all rational beings), then it is not only demonstrated that pure reason can be practical, but that it alone, and not reason restricted by <term>empirical</term><note>Based on experience, physical desires, or sensory input rather than pure logic.</note> factors, is unconditionally practical. Consequently, we will not have to produce a "Critique of *Pure* Practical Reason," but only a "Critique of Practical Reason" in general. For pure reason, once it has been demonstrated that such a thing exists, requires no critique. It is reason itself which contains the <term>standard</term><note>German: *Richtschnur*. A guiding rule or yardstick used to judge whether an action is correct.</note> for the critique of all its use. The critique of practical reason in general therefore has the obligation to prevent <term>empirically conditioned reason</term><note>Reasoning that is "conditioned" or driven by our physical needs, emotions, or circumstances.</note> from the arrogant claim that it alone and exclusively provides the determining ground of the will. The use of pure reason—if it is established that such a thing exists—is exclusively <term>immanent</term><note>Staying within its proper, legitimate boundaries.</note>; whereas the empirically conditioned use, which claims sole mastery, is on the contrary <term>transcedent</term><note>Overstepping its boundaries; making demands or rules that go beyond its rightful territory.</note> and expresses itself in requirements and commands that go entirely beyond its domain. This is exactly the opposite relationship to what could be said of pure reason in its <term>speculative</term><note>Theoretical or "thinking" use of reason, aimed at knowing objects, as explored in Kant’s first major book.</note> use.

<margin>30</margin>
<margin>40</margin>
However, since it is still pure reason that provides the foundation for practical use here, the division of a Critique of Practical Reason must be arranged, according to its general outline, in accordance with the speculative one. We will therefore need to have a **Doctrine of Elements** and a **Doctrine of Method**; and in the former, as the first part, an **Analytic** as a rule of truth, and a **Dialectic** as the presentation and resolution of <term>illusion</term><note>German: *Schein*. A logical "trick" or false appearance that reason creates when it tries to apply rules where they don't belong.</note> in the judgments of practical reason. Yet the order in the subdivision of the Analytic will again be the inverse of that in the Critique of Pure Speculative Reason. For in the present work, we shall begin with **principles** and move to **concepts**, and from these, if possible, proceed finally to the **senses**; whereas in the case of speculative reason, we had to begin—

<meta>This page continues the Introduction to the Critique of Practical Reason. It contrasts the methodology of this book (Practical Reason) with the earlier Critique of Pure (Speculative) Reason. # Introduction.

...whereas in the case of **speculative reason** <note>Reason used for theoretical knowledge—understanding how the world works through observation.</note> we had to begin with the senses and end with **principles**. The reason for this, in turn, lies in the fact that we are now dealing with a will, and we must consider reason not in relation to objects, but in relation to this will and its **causality** <note>The relationship between a cause and its effect; specifically, how the will "causes" an action to happen.</note>. Therefore, the principles of **empirically unconditioned causality** <note>A cause (the will) that is not triggered by physical laws, instincts, or external circumstances, but acts on its own.</note> must make the beginning. Only after this can we attempt to establish our concepts of the **determining ground** <note>The specific motivation or reason that prompts the will to choose a certain path.</note> of such a will, their application to objects, and finally to the subject and its **sensibility** <note>Our capacity to receive impressions through our senses.</note>. The **law of causality from freedom**—that is, a pure **practical principle**—inevitably forms the starting point here and determines the objects to which it can be applied.

# Introduction.

...whereas in the case of **speculative reason** <note>Reason used for theoretical knowledge—understanding how the physical world works through observation and logic.</note> we had to begin with the senses and end with **principles**. The reason for this, in turn, lies in the fact that we are now dealing with a will, and we must consider reason not in relation to objects, but in relation to this will and its **causality** <note>The relationship between a cause and its effect; here, it refers to the will’s power to bring about an action.</note>. Therefore, the principles of **empirically unconditioned causality** <note>A cause—the human will—that is not forced by physical laws, biological instincts, or external circumstances, but acts independently.</note> must make the beginning. Only after this can we attempt to establish our concepts of the **determining ground** <note>The specific motivation or moral reason that moves the will to choose one action over another.</note> of such a will, their application to objects, and finally to the subject and its **sensibility** <note>Our capacity to receive impressions through our senses, which usually drives our desires.</note>. The **law of causality from freedom**—that is, a pure **practical principle**—inevitably forms the starting point here and determines the objects to which it can be applied.

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the "Analytic," the first major section of the book. ### Book One.

# The Analytic of Pure Practical Reason.

---

### First Chapter.

### Concerning the
## Principles of Pure Practical Reason.

### § 1.
### Definition.

Practical principles are propositions which contain a general determination of the will <note>original: "Bestimmung des Willens." This refers to the process by which the mind decides on a course of action.</note>, which <margin>10</margin> has several practical rules under it. They are subjective, or **maxims**, when the condition is regarded by the subject as valid only for their own will; but they are objective, or **practical laws**, when the condition is recognized as objective, that is, valid for the will of every rational being.

### Remark.

If one assumes that pure reason can contain within itself a practical ground—that is, one sufficient for the determination of the will—then there are practical <margin>20</margin> laws; but if not, then all practical principles will be mere maxims. In the will of a rational being that is pathologically affected <note>In Kant's terminology, "pathological" does not mean "diseased." It refers to being influenced by "pathos"—emotions, physical desires, or sensory impulses—rather than by pure logic alone.</note>, a conflict of maxims can be found that goes against the practical laws recognized by the subject themselves. For example, someone may make it their maxim to suffer no insult to go unavenged, and yet at the same time

<summary>Kant defines the building blocks of his ethics, distinguishing between "maxims" (personal rules for acting) and "laws" (universal rules that apply to everyone). He introduces the idea that while we can recognize universal laws, our personal desires often create internal conflict.</summary>

<keywords>Analytic, determination of the will, maxims, practical laws, pure reason, pathological affection, ethics, Immanuel Kant</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues Kant’s "Remark" on the first definition of practical principles. Having just provided the example of a person seeking revenge, Kant now explains why such personal rules (maxims) cannot be considered universal laws, and introduces the concept of the "imperative."</meta>

**First Part, Book I, Chapter 1. § 1.**

realize that this is not a practical law, but only his maxim; on the other hand, as a rule for the will of every rational being, it could not harmonize with itself in one and the same maxim. In the knowledge of nature, the principles of what happens (e.g., the principle of equality of action and reaction in the communication of motion) are at the same time laws of nature; <margin>[20]</margin> for there the use of reason is theoretical <margin>10</margin> and determined by the nature of the object. In practical knowledge—that is, the kind which deals merely with the determining grounds of the will—principles that one makes for oneself are not yet laws under which one is inevitably placed; because in practical matters, reason deals with the subject, namely the **faculty of desire** <note>original: "Begehrungsvermögen." This refers to the human capacity to be moved toward an object or action by one's own desires or needs.</note>, according to whose specific nature the rule can be adjusted in many ways. — A practical rule is always a product of reason, because it prescribes action <margin>20</margin> as a means to an effect as an intended aim. This rule, however, for a being in whom reason is not the sole determining ground of the will, is an **imperative**; that is, a rule characterized by an **"ought"** <note>original: "Sollen." This denotes a sense of moral obligation or necessity.</note>, which expresses the objective necessity of the action,ᵃ) and signifies that if reason fully determined the will, the action would inevitably occur according to this rule. Imperatives, therefore, hold objectively and are entirely <margin>30</margin> distinct from maxims, which are subjective principles. These [imperatives], however, determine either the conditions of the **causality** <note>In this context, causality refers to the power of a rational being to bring about an effect in the world through their will.</note> of the rational being as an efficient cause merely in regard to the effect and its adequacy, or they determine only the will, whether it be sufficient for the effect or not. The former would be **hypothetical imperatives** and contain mere prescriptions of skill; the latter, on the other hand, would be **categorical** and alone would be practical laws. Maxims are thus indeed principles, but not <margin>40</margin> imperatives. The imperatives themselves, however, when they are...

<summary>Kant distinguishes between theoretical laws of nature and the "imperatives" of human behavior. He introduces the crucial distinction between "hypothetical imperatives" (rules for achieving a specific goal) and "categorical" laws (moral rules that apply regardless of one's goals).</summary>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of practical principles. Kant is distinguishing between rules that are merely "practical precepts" (instructions for a specific goal) and "laws" (absolute moral requirements). He introduces the crucial distinction between hypothetical and categorical imperatives.</meta>

# Of the Principles of Pure Practical Reason.

...are conditioned—that is, they do not determine the will simply as the will, but only in regard to a desired effect—they are **hypothetical imperatives**. These are indeed practical precepts, but they are not **laws**. The latter must sufficiently determine the will as the will, even before I ask whether I even have the power required for a desired effect, or what I must do to produce it. Thus, they must be **categorical**; otherwise <margin>10</margin> they are not laws. This is because they lack that necessity which, if it is to be practical, must be independent of <term>pathological</term> <note>In Kant's philosophy, "pathological" does not mean "diseased" or "sickly." It refers to anything determined by our feelings, sensory desires, or physical impulses rather than by pure reason.</note> conditions—things that only incidentally attach themselves to the will.

Tell someone, for example, that he must work and save in his youth so as not to suffer in old age; this is a correct and at the same time important practical precept of the will. However, one sees immediately that the will is here directed toward something else, which it is assumed he desires. This desire must be left to the <margin>20</margin> actor himself: whether he foresees other resources besides his own earned wealth, or whether he has no hope of growing old, or thinks that in case of need he might manage to get by poorly. Reason—from which alone any rule containing necessity can arise—does indeed put necessity into this precept (for without it, it would not be an **imperative**), but this necessity is only subjectively conditioned and cannot be assumed to exist in all subjects in the same degree.

For reason’s <margin>30</margin> **legislation**, however, it is required that it need only presuppose itself. This is because the rule is only <insert>[21]</insert> then objectively and universally valid when it holds without the incidental, subjective conditions that distinguish one rational being from another. Now, tell someone that he should never make a lying promise; this is a rule that concerns only his will, regardless of whether the intentions the person may have can be achieved by it or not. The mere willing is what is to be determined entirely <term>a priori</term> <note>original: "a priori." Meaning "from the beginning" or "before experience." This refers to knowledge or rules that come from pure reason rather than from sensory observation.</note> <margin>40</margin> by that rule. If it is now found that this rule is practically correct, then it is a **law**, because it is a **categorical imperative**.

<summary>Kant distinguishes between hypothetical imperatives, which are conditional rules for achieving specific goals, and categorical imperatives, which are absolute moral laws that apply to all rational beings regardless of their personal desires.</summary>

<keywords>hypothetical imperative, categorical imperative, legislation, practical reason, a priori, pathologically conditioned, the will</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of practical principles in Kant’s moral philosophy. On the previous page, Kant argued that moral laws must be categorical (absolute) rather than hypothetical (dependent on a specific goal). Here, he begins to prove that if our motivation for acting is based on a desire for a specific result or "object," that rule cannot count as a universal moral law.</meta>

26.

Erster Teil, I. Buch, 1. Hauptstück. § 2. 3.
First Part, Book I, Chapter 1. §§ 2, 3.

Therefore, practical laws refer only to the will, regardless of what is achieved through the causality of that will. One can set aside the latter (as something belonging to the world of the senses) in order to consider these laws in their pure form.

### § 2.

### Theorem I.

**All practical principles that assume an object (the matter) of the faculty of desire as the determining ground <margin>10</margin> of the will are, taken together, empirical and can provide no practical laws.**

By the "matter" of the faculty of desire, I mean an object whose reality is wanted. If the desire for this object precedes the practical rule and is the condition for making it a principle <insert>to make <note>Kant originally wrote "make"; corrected by the editor Grillo.</note></insert>, then I say: first, this principle is always empirical. <margin>20</margin> This is because the determining ground <term>determining ground</term> <note>The fundamental reason or motivation that moves the will to act.</note> of the power of choice is then the representation of an object and its relationship to the subject, through which the faculty of desire is moved to make that object real. Such a relationship to the subject is called "pleasure" in the reality of an object. Thus, this pleasure would have to be assumed as a condition for the possibility of determining the power of choice. However, it is impossible to know through reason alone <note>original: "a priori"</note> from any representation of an object—no matter what it may be—whether it will be associated with pleasure or displeasure, or if it will be indifferent. <margin>30</margin> Therefore, in such a case, the determining ground of the power of choice must always be empirical, and consequently, so must the practical material principle which assumed that desire as a condition.

Now (secondly), a principle that is based only on the subjective condition of susceptibility to a pleasure or displeasure (which can only ever be known through experience and cannot be valid in the same way for all rational beings) may indeed serve for the...

<summary>Kant argues that if a moral rule depends on a person's desire for a specific result or "object" (which brings pleasure), it is merely an empirical observation and cannot be a universal law. He asserts that pleasure and pain are subjective experiences that cannot be predicted by reason alone.</summary>

<keywords>faculty of desire, determining ground, empirical, pleasure, displeasure, power of choice, material principle, practical laws, Immanuel Kant</keywords>

# Of the Principles of Pure Practical Reason.
