### § 3.

#### Theorem II.

All material practical principles are, as <margin>10</margin> such, altogether of one and the same kind and fall under the general principle of self-love or one's own happiness.

Pleasure derived from the representation of a thing's existence, insofar as it is meant to be a determining ground <term>determining ground</term> <note>The underlying reason or motivation that moves the will to act.</note> for the desire of this thing, is based on the subject's receptivity <term>receptivity</term> <note>The capacity to be affected by the world through our senses.</note>, because it depends on the existence of an object. Consequently, it belongs to the senses (feeling) and not to the understanding, which expresses a relationship of a representation to an object according to <margin>20</margin> concepts, but not to the subject according to feelings. It is therefore practical only insofar as the sensation of pleasantness, which the subject expects from the reality of the object, determines the faculty of desire. Now, a rational being's consciousness of the pleasantness of life, which accompanies his whole existence without interruption, is happiness, and the principle of making this the highest determining ground of choice <term>choice</term> <note>original: "Willkür." The power of a rational being to choose between different possible actions.</note> is the principle of self-love. <margin>30</margin> Thus, all material principles—which place the determining ground of choice in the pleasure or displeasure to be felt from the reality of any object—are entirely of the same kind, in that they all belong to the principle of self-love or one's own happiness.

<margin>30</margin> **satisfaction** one expects from them, which drives the activity toward producing the object), is of one and the same kind. <margin>30</margin> duty of a philosopher and yet is most rarely encountered. The ancient Greek schools provide us with more examples of this than we find in our **syncretistic** <note>The practice of attempting to merge different, often contradictory, beliefs or schools of thought into one system.</note> age, where a certain "coalition system" of contradictory principles, full of dishonesty and shallowness, is contrived because it better appeals to a public that is content to know a little bit of everything and nothing as a whole, and thus tries to be "all things to all people" <note>original: "in allen Sätteln gerecht zu sein." The capacity to want or seek objects based on the pleasure they provide.</note>...

The part of our mind that chooses actions based on reason rather than just reacting to physical urges.</note> <note>The word "higher" was added in Kant's personal copy of the book.</note> at all, or **pure reason** must be practical of itself alone. This means reason must be able to determine the **will** <term>will</term> <note>original: "Wille." The capacity to act according to principles or rules.</note> through the mere form of a practical rule, without presupposing any feeling—and therefore without any ideas of what is "pleasant" or "unpleasant." These feelings are the "matter" of the faculty of desire and always act as a sensory, or **empirical** <term>empirical</term> <note>Based on observation or experience rather than theory or pure logic.</note>, condition for our principles. Only when reason determines the will by itself (and is not merely a servant to our inclinations) is it a true higher faculty of desire. The **pathologically** <term>pathologically</term> <note>In Kant's philosophy, "pathological" refers to being moved by feelings, emotions, or physical desires rather than by the moral law. It does not mean "diseased" in this context.</note> determinable faculty is subordinate to this higher one and is truly, even fundamentally, different from it. In fact, even the slightest mixture of impulses from the lower faculty detracts from the strength and superiority of reason; just as the smallest empirical element, if used as a condition in a mathematical demonstration, degrades and destroys its dignity and authority. In a practical law, reason determines the will directly, not through an intervening feeling of pleasure or pain—not even a pleasure felt *for* the law itself. It is only because reason can be practical as "pure reason" that it is possible for it to be law-giving.

<margin>30</margin>
To be happy is necessarily the desire of every rational but **finite** <term>finite</term> <note>A being with limits, such as a human, who has needs and can be influenced by external factors, as opposed to an infinite or perfect being.</note> being, and is therefore an unavoidable determining ground of its faculty of desire. For satisfaction with one’s entire existence is not an original possession or a state of **bliss** <note>original: "Seligkeit." A state of perfect, divine happiness that requires nothing else.</note>—which would presuppose a consciousness of independent **self-sufficiency** <note>The 6th edition by Hartenstein uses the term "Selbstgenügsamkeit" (self-sufficiency).</note>—but is instead a problem forced upon the being by its own finite nature. # On the Principles of Pure Practical Reason

...might wish to eat bread, he would have to devise a mill. 
But practical prescriptions <note>original: "Vorschriften." These are rules or instructions for how to act to achieve a specific result.</note> that are based on these needs can never be universal; for the *determining ground* <term>determining ground</term> <note>original: "Bestimmungsgrund." The underlying reason or motive that triggers our desire to act.</note> of the *faculty of desire* <term>faculty of desire</term> <note>original: "Begehrungsvermögen." Our capacity to be the cause of objects through our ideas—essentially, our will and what drives it.</note> is founded on the feeling of **pleasure and displeasure**, which can never be assumed to be directed universally toward the same objects.

But suppose that finite rational beings were to think entirely alike, even in regard to what they should take as objects of their <margin>10</margin> feelings of pleasure or pain, and likewise regarding the means they must use to achieve the former and prevent the latter: even then, the principle of **self-love** could still not be passed off by them as a practical law. For this unanimity would itself be merely accidental. The determining ground would still be only subjectively valid and merely *empirical* <term>empirical</term> <note>original: "empirisch." Derived from experience or the senses, rather than from pure logic.</note>; it would lack that necessity which is conceived in every law—namely, an *objective* necessity based on grounds **a priori** <term>a priori</term> <note>original: "a priori." A term meaning "from before"—knowledge that is independent of any sensory experience and based on pure reason.</note>. <margin>20</margin> Otherwise, one would have to pass off this necessity not as practical, but as merely *physical*—namely, that our actions were as inevitably forced upon us by our *inclination* <note>original: "Neigung." A habitual desire or preference based on feelings.</note> as **yawning** is when we see others yawn. One might sooner assert that there are no practical laws at all, but only *counsels* <note>original: "Anratungen." Suggestions or advice intended to satisfy our desires, rather than moral commands.</note> for the sake of our desires, than that merely subjective principles be raised to the rank of practical laws. For laws must have an entirely objective and not merely <margin>30</margin> subjective necessity, and they must be recognized by reason *a priori*, not through experience (however empirically universal that experience might be). Even the rules of uniform appearances are only called *laws of nature* (e.g., mechanical laws) when one either recognizes them as truly *a priori* or assumes (as with chemical laws) that they would be recognized *a priori* from objective grounds if our insight...

<summary>Kant argues that if morality were based on pleasure, it would be a mere physical compulsion—like yawning when others yawn—rather than a law of reason. He insists that true laws must be objective and "a priori," meaning they do not depend on our changing feelings or experiences.</summary>

Part One, Book I, Chapter 1. § 4.

...if one’s view went deeper. However, with purely subjective practical principles, it is expressly made a condition that they must be based on subjective, not objective, conditions of the **power of choice** <term>power of choice</term> <note>original: "Willkür." This refers to the human capacity to choose between different impulses or to act according to rules, rather than being driven by mere instinct.</note>; consequently, they must always be presented only as mere **maxims**, and never as **practical laws**. This latter remark seems at first glance to be mere word-splitting <note>original: "Wortklauberei." Hair-splitting or quibbling over definitions.</note>; however, it is <insert>it is</insert> <note>The words "it is" were missing in Kant’s personal copy and were supplemented by later editors; others, like Hartenstein and Kirchmann, suggested "it contains."</note> the definition of the most important distinction that can ever come into consideration in practical investigations.

<margin>[27]</margin> ### § 4.
### Theorem III.
If a rational being is to think of its maxims as universal practical laws, it can only think of them as principles that contain the **determining ground** <term>determining ground</term> <note>original: "Bestimmungsgrund." The fundamental reason or motive that moves the will to act.</note> of the will, not according to their **matter**, but solely according to their **form**.

<margin>20.</margin> The **matter** <term>matter</term> <note>original: "Materie." In this context, the "matter" of a law is the actual goal or object you want to achieve (like "getting a snack" or "becoming famous").</note> of a practical principle is the object of the will. This object is either the determining ground of the will, or it is not. If it were the determining ground, then the rule of the will would be subject to an **empirical** <term>empirical</term> <note>original: "empirisch." Based on experience or sensory feelings rather than pure logic.</note> condition (namely, the relationship between the idea of the object and the feeling of **pleasure or displeasure**); consequently, it would not be a practical law. Now, if one separates all matter—that is, every object of the will (as a determining ground)—from a law, <margin>30</margin> nothing remains but the mere **form of a universal legislation**. Therefore, a rational being either cannot think of its subjective-practical principles (that is, its **maxims**) as being universal laws at all, or it must assume that it is the mere form of these principles—the logical way they fit into a system of universal legislation—that makes them into a practical law by itself.

---
a) The missing "it is" was added in Kant’s personal copy; Hartenstein (Kirchmann): "it contains."

<summary>Kant explains that a moral law cannot be based on the "matter" (the specific goal or desire) of an action, because that is always subjective. Instead, a rule only becomes a universal law if its "form"—its logical consistency as a rule for everyone—is the reason for acting.</summary>
<keywords>Maxims, Practical Law, Form versus Matter, Determining Ground, Universal Legislation, Power of Choice, Rational Being</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kant’s exploration of the "Form" of moral laws. Having established that a law must be universal, he now provides a practical example—the famous "deposit" case—to show how any person can test whether their personal rule (maxim) qualifies as a universal law of reason.</meta>

<header>Of the Principles of Pure Practical Reason.</header>
<sig>3*</sig>

### Remark.

Which form in a maxim <term>maxim</term> <note>A maxim is a personal rule or "policy" that you set for yourself, such as "I will always be punctual."</note> is fit for universal legislation, and which is not, is something the commonest understanding can distinguish without instruction. For example, I have made it my maxim to increase my wealth by all safe means. Now I have in my possession a deposit <term>deposit</term> <note>original: "Depositum." This refers to money or property entrusted to someone for safekeeping, such as a modern-day trust or a bank deposit.</note> whose owner has died without leaving any written record of it. Naturally, this is a case <margin>10</margin> that falls under my maxim. Now I simply want to know whether that maxim could also hold as a universal practical law. I therefore apply it to the present case and ask whether it could take the form of a law, and whether I could therefore through my maxim simultaneously give such a law: that everyone may deny a deposit if no one can prove that it was made. I immediately become aware that such a principle, if made into a law, would destroy itself, because it would result in there being no deposits at all. A practical law, which <margin>20</margin> I recognize as such, must qualify for universal legislation; this is an identical proposition <note>An "identical proposition" is one that is true by definition, like saying "a triangle has three sides." Kant means it is self-evident that a universal law must be capable of being universal.</note> and is thus clear in itself. Now, if I say that my will is subject to a practical law, I cannot cite my inclination <term>inclination</term> <note>original: "Neigung." A desire, feeling, or impulse based on personal preference or physical need, rather than reason.</note> (for instance, in this case, my greed) as the determining ground <margin>[28]</margin> suitable for a universal practical law; for greed, far from being fit for universal legislation, would instead destroy <margin>30</margin> itself if put into the form of a universal law.

It is therefore strange how, since the desire for happiness <term>happiness</term> <note>original: "Glückseligkeit." For Kant, this is the state where everything goes according to one's own wish and will.</note>—and thus also the maxim by which everyone makes this happiness the determining ground of their will—is universal, it could have occurred to intelligent men to pass it off <note>original: "es," likely referring to the desire for happiness. The editor Adickes suggests it should read "sie" (it/them) to refer back to the maxims.</note> as a universal practical law. For whereas a universal law of nature makes everything harmonious, here, if one were to give the maxim the universal...
<br>

<summary>Kant illustrates his "Categorical Imperative" by showing that a rule allowing one to keep a secret deposit cannot be a universal law, as it would make the very concept of a deposit impossible. He argues that personal desires like greed or the search for happiness cannot serve as the foundation for moral laws because they lead to self-contradiction.</summary>

Kant is distinguishing between being moved by a physical desire versus a logical principle.</note> of the will is distinguished from all determining grounds of events in nature according to the law of causality <note>The law of causality states that every physical effect has a physical cause. Such independence is called **freedom** in the strictest, i.e., transcendental <term>transcendental</term> <note>In Kant’s philosophy, "transcendental" refers to the necessary underlying conditions that make our experience and reasoning possible in the first place.</note> sense. Therefore, a will to which the mere legislative form of a maxim alone can serve as a law is a free will.

## § 6.
### **Task II.**

<margin>20</margin>**Supposing** that a will is free: find the law that alone is capable of necessarily determining it.

Since the matter of the practical law—that is, an object of the maxim <term>maxim</term> <note>A "maxim" is a personal rule of conduct, such as "I will never borrow money I cannot repay."</note>—can never be given except empirically <note>Meaning it is based on physical experience, feelings, or specific circumstances.</note>, yet the free will must be determinable despite being independent of empirical conditions (i.e., those belonging to the world of sense), a free will, independent of the matter of the law, must nevertheless find a determining ground <margin>30</margin> in the law. However, besides the matter of the law, there is nothing further contained in it except the legislative form. Thus, the legislative form, insofar as it is contained in the maxim, is the only thing that can constitute a determining ground of the will<sup>a)</sup>.

### First Part, I. Book, 1. Main Section. § 6.

...now [we do] not [know] whether they are also different in fact, and whether an unconditional law is not rather merely the self-consciousness of a **pure practical reason**, and this [reason] is entirely the same as the positive concept of freedom; instead, [the question is] from where our knowledge of the **unconditionally practical** <term>unconditionally practical</term> <note>This refers to moral principles that apply to everyone at all times, regardless of their personal desires or the specific situation.</note> begins: whether from **freedom** or from the **practical law**. 

It <margin>a) Kant originally used a neuter pronoun; scholar Vorländer corrected it to a feminine pronoun to agree with the noun "knowledge."</margin> cannot begin from freedom; for we cannot become immediately conscious of freedom, because its <margin>b) Kant's original grammar was corrected here by the editor Hartenstein.</margin> first concept is **negative** <note>A "negative" concept of freedom is defined by what it is *not*—namely, not being forced by outside causes. A "positive" concept would be the actual power to start a chain of events oneself.</note>, nor can we infer it from experience, for experience gives us only the law of appearances—consequently the **mechanism of nature**, the exact opposite of freedom—to be known. 

Therefore it is the **moral law**, of which we become immediately conscious (as soon as we draft **maxims** <term>maxims</term> <note>A maxim is a personal rule of action, such as "I will always keep my promises."</note> of the will for ourselves), which first presents itself to us. Because reason represents that law as a **determining ground** <term>determining ground</term> <note>The underlying reason or motivation that causes the will to choose a specific path.</note> that is not to be outweighed by any sensory condition—indeed, as entirely independent of them—it leads directly to the concept of **freedom**. 

But how is consciousness of that moral law also possible? We can become conscious of **pure practical laws** just as we are conscious of pure theoretical principles, by paying attention to the **necessity** with which reason prescribes them to us and to the setting aside of all **empirical conditions** <note>Empirical conditions are factors based on physical experience, like our feelings, environment, or biological urges.</note> to which reason directs us. The concept of a **pure will** arises from the former <margin>c) Scholar Natorp suggests this refers to the "laws" or "principles" mentioned above.</margin>, just as the consciousness of a **pure understanding** arises from the latter. 

That this is the true subordination of our concepts—and that **morality** first discovers the concept of freedom for us, and consequently that **practical reason** first presents the most insoluble problem to **speculative reason** <term>speculative reason</term> <note>Speculative (or theoretical) reason is our ability to think about how the world *is*; practical reason is our ability to decide how we *ought* to act.</note> with this concept, in order to put it into the greatest embarrassment through it—is already clear from the fact that, since nothing in appearances can be explained <margin>d) Scholar Adickes suggests this should be read as "may be explained."</margin> by the concept of freedom, but here always the **natural mechanism** [must be the guide...]

<summary>Kant argues that we cannot know we are free through direct experience or intuition; instead, we only discover our freedom through our awareness of the moral law, which commands us regardless of our physical desires.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 38 regarding the relationship between the moral law and freedom. It concludes that while nature operates through cause-and-effect (mechanism), the existence of the moral law within us is the only thing that "forces" us to accept that we are truly free beings.</meta>

# On the Principles of Pure Practical Reason.

[mecha]nism <note>The word begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page's discussion of "the mechanism of nature."</note> must constitute the guiding thread, and moreover, the **antinomy of pure reason** <term>antinomy</term> <note>An antinomy is a logical conflict where two contradictory conclusions both seem true. Here, Kant refers to the conflict between the idea that everything has a cause (nature) and the idea that we are free (will).</note>, when it seeks to ascend to the **Unconditioned** <term>the Unconditioned</term> <note>That which exists on its own and is not caused by something else; the ultimate "first cause."</note> in the series of causes, becomes entangled in such incomprehensible difficulties in one case as much as the other. However, since the latter (mechanism) at least has some utility in explaining the appearances of nature, one would never have undertaken the bold venture of introducing **freedom** into science, had the **moral law** <margin>10</margin> not existed and forced this concept upon us.

But experience also confirms this order of concepts within us. Suppose that someone claims his lustful inclination is entirely irresistible to him when the beloved object and the opportunity are present. [Ask him] whether, if a **gallows** were erected in front of the house where he finds this opportunity, in order to hang him immediately after he has enjoyed his lust, he would not then <margin>20</margin> conquer his inclination? One does not have to guess long for his answer. 

But ask him whether, if his prince demanded—under threat of the same immediate penalty of death—that he give false witness against an honest man whom the prince would like to ruin under plausible pretexts, whether he would then consider it possible to overcome his love of life, however great it may be? Whether he would do it or not, he might perhaps not venture to say; but that it is **possible** for him, he <margin>30</margin> must admit without hesitation. He judges, therefore, that he **can** do something because he is conscious that he **ought** to do it, and he recognizes within himself the **freedom** which would otherwise have remained unknown to him without the moral law.

->§ 7<-

## Fundamental Law of Pure Practical Reason.

**Act so that the maxim of your will could always hold at the same time as a principle of a universal legislation.** <note>This is the famous "Categorical Imperative." It suggests that before you act, you should ask: "Would I want everyone else in the world to follow this same rule?"</note>

<summary>Kant uses the famous "gallows example" to show that while we can resist our physical desires out of fear, our awareness of moral duty ("ought") proves we have the freedom to choose what is right, even over life itself. This leads to his central moral law: the Categorical Imperative.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how the moral law functions within the human mind, following the previous page's illustration of a man facing a moral choice between his life and his integrity.</meta>

<margin>[31]</margin>
## Remark.

Pure geometry has postulates <term>postulates</term> <note>In geometry, a postulate is an assumption or a starting point for a proof, like "it is possible to draw a straight line between any two points."</note> as practical propositions; however, these contain nothing more than the assumption that one *could* do something if it were required that one *should* do it, and these are the only propositions in geometry that concern existence. They are therefore practical rules under a **problematic condition** of the will. Here, however, the rule says: one **ought** to proceed in a certain way absolutely <term>absolutely</term> <note>Meaning "categorically," or without any "if" or "but."</note>. The practical rule is therefore unconditional, and is thus presented as a **categorically practical proposition *a priori*** <term>*a priori*</term> <note>original: "a priori". Knowledge that is independent of any experience; something known by reason alone.</note>. Through this, the will is objectively determined immediately and absolutely by the practical rule itself—which here functions as a **law**. 

For pure reason, which is practical in itself, is here directly <margin>10</margin> legislative. The will is thought of as independent of empirical <term>empirical</term> <note>Based on or verified by experience or the senses, rather than theory or pure logic.</note> conditions; therefore, as a **pure will**, it is determined by the mere **form of the law**. This basis for determination is regarded as the supreme condition of all **maxims** <term>maxims</term> <note>A maxim is a personal rule or principle that a person follows when they act.</note>. 

The matter is strange enough and has no equivalent in the rest of practical knowledge. For the *a priori* thought of a possible universal legislation—which is therefore merely "problematic" or theoretical—is here commanded unconditionally as a law, without borrowing <margin>20</margin> anything from experience or any external will. However, it is not a prescription according to which an action should happen so that a desired effect becomes possible (for then the rule would always be physically conditioned); instead, it is a rule that determines the will *a priori* merely in respect to the form of its maxims. 

It is, at the very least, not impossible to conceive of a law that serves only the purpose of the **subjective form** of principles, yet acts as a basis for determination through the **objective form** of a law in general. One can call the consciousness of this fundamental law a **Fact of Reason** <margin>30</margin>, because one cannot reason it out from preceding data of reason—such as the consciousness of **freedom** (for this is not given to us beforehand)—but rather because it forces itself upon us on its own as a **synthetic** <term>synthetic</term> <note>A "synthetic" proposition is one where the predicate adds something new to the subject, rather than just analyzing what is already there. Kant is saying the moral law isn't just a definition; it's a substantial "fact" our reason gives us.</note> ... <margin>40</margin>

<summary>Kant explains that the moral law is a "Fact of Reason" that dictates our actions unconditionally, unlike the rules of geometry which only apply if we want to achieve a specific result. He argues that this moral law is not something we learn from experience, but something our reason imposes on us directly.</summary>
<keywords>pure reason, postulates, categorical imperative, a priori, maxims, Fact of Reason, freedom, moral law</keywords>

# On the Principles of Pure Practical Reason.

<margin>10</margin>
...practical proposition *a priori* <term>*a priori*</term> <note>original: "a priori". This refers to knowledge that is independent of any experience; it is known by reason alone.</note>, which is not grounded on any intuition, whether pure or empirical <term>empirical</term> <note>Based on observation or experience through the senses.</note>, although it would be analytical if one presupposed the **freedom of the will**. However, to presuppose freedom as a positive concept would require an **intellectual intuition** <term>intellectual intuition</term> <note>A hypothetical power to perceive things directly through the mind without using the senses. Kant famously argues that human beings do not possess this ability.</note>, which cannot be assumed here at all. Yet, to avoid misinterpreting this law as something given, one must carefully note that it is not an empirical fact, but rather the sole **fact of pure reason**, which through this announces itself as originally legislative (I will it so, I command it so <note>original: "sic volo, sic iubeo".</note>).

### Corollary.

Pure reason is practical of itself alone and gives (to the human being) a universal law which we call the **moral law**.

<margin>[32]</margin>
### Remark.

<margin>20, 30</margin>
The aforementioned fact is undeniable. One need only analyze the judgment that people pass on the lawfulness of their actions: one will always find that, regardless of what inclination <note>Inclination refers to our desires, feelings, or biological urges.</note> might say in between, their reason nevertheless—incorruptible and self-constrained—always holds the **maxim** <term>maxim</term> <note>A personal rule or subjective principle that a person follows when they act.</note> of the will in an action up to the pure will, that is, to itself, insofar as it regards itself as practical *a priori*. Now, this principle of morality, precisely for the sake of the universality of legislation (which makes it the formal supreme determining ground of the will regardless of all subjective differences <insert>b)</insert> of that will), is simultaneously declared by reason to be a law for all **rational beings**. This applies insofar as they have a will at all—that is, a capacity to determine their **causality** <term>causality</term> <note>The relationship between cause and effect; here, the ability of a being to be the cause of its own actions.</note> through the representation of rules—and thus insofar as they are capable of actions according to principles, and consequently according to practical principles *a priori* (for these alone have that necessity which reason demands for a principle). It is therefore not limited merely to humans, but extends to all

In the former case, however, the law takes the form of an **imperative** <term>imperative</term> <note>A command or rule that tells us what we ought to do.</note>, because in such a being—while regarded as rational—one can assume a pure will, but as a being affected by needs and sensory motives, one cannot assume a **holy will**, that is, a will incapable of any maxims <term>maxims</term> <note>Personal rules or principles that a person chooses to follow in their actions.</note> that conflict with the moral law.

It thus requires resistance from practical reason—which can be called an internal but intellectual compulsion—as a moral constraint.

<margin>20</margin> In the most self-sufficient intelligence, choice is rightly represented as being incapable of any maxim that could not simultaneously be an objective law. The concept of **holiness**, which is attributed to it for this reason, places it not above all practical laws, but certainly above all practically restrictive laws, and thus beyond obligation and duty. 

<margin>30</margin> This holiness of the will is nevertheless a **practical idea**, which must necessarily serve as a model; to approach this model infinitely is the only thing that is appropriate for all finite rational beings. The pure moral law—which is itself called holy for this reason—constantly and correctly holds this idea before their eyes. ### § 8.
### Proposition IV.
<margin>10</margin> The **autonomy** <term>autonomy</term> <note>Literally "self-law"; the capacity of the will to be a law to itself, independent of any outside influence.</note> of the will is the sole principle of all moral laws and the duties that conform to them. In contrast, all **heteronomy** <term>heteronomy</term> <note>Literally "other-law"; when the will is determined by something outside of reason, such as desires, emotions, or external authorities.</note> of the choice not only fails to establish any obligation but is actually opposed to the principle of obligation and the morality of the will. The sole principle of morality consists in independence from all **matter** of the law (namely, a desired object) and, at the same time, the determination of choice through the mere universal legislative form that a maxim must be capable of. <margin>20</margin> This independence is freedom in the **negative** sense, whereas this self-legislation of pure—and as such, practical—reason is freedom in the **positive** sense. Therefore, the moral law expresses nothing other than the autonomy of pure practical reason, that is, <note>a</note> freedom; and this freedom is itself the formal condition of all maxims, under which alone they can coincide with the supreme practical law. If the "matter" of willing—which can be nothing other than the object of a desire <margin>30</margin> that is linked to the law—is included in the practical law as a condition of its possibility, the result is heteronomy of choice. This is effectively a dependence on natural law, following some impulse or inclination. In this case, the will does not give itself the law, but only the instruction for the rational following of **pathological laws** <term>pathological laws</term> <note>original: "pathologische Gesetze". In this context, laws governing our sensory desires and feelings rather than our rational mind.</note>; the maxim, however, which in such...

### Remark I.

<margin>[34]</margin> Therefore, a <margin>10</margin> practical precept that carries a **material** (and thus **empirical**) condition with it must never be counted as a practical law. For the law of the **pure** will, which is **free**, places this will in a completely different sphere than the **empirical** <term>empirical</term> <note>Based on experience or observation through the senses, rather than pure logic.</note> one; and the **necessity** it expresses, since it is not intended to be a **natural necessity** <note>Natural necessity refers to the laws of physics or biology—things that *must* happen, like a stone falling. Moral necessity is a "duty" that a rational being *ought* to follow.</note>, can therefore consist only in the **formal** conditions of the **possibility** of a law in general. All **matter** <term>matter</term> <note>The "matter" of a rule is the specific goal or object one wants to achieve, like wealth or pleasure.</note> of practical rules always rests on **subjective** conditions, which provide them<gloss>a)</gloss> with no **universality** for rational beings <margin>20</margin> other than a merely **conditional** one (in the case that I desire this or that, what I must then do to make it real). These rules revolve, as a whole, around the principle of one's own **happiness**. Now, it is certainly undeniable that every act of willing must also have an **object**, and thus a **matter**; but this object is not for that reason the **determining ground** and **condition** of the **maxim** <term>maxim</term> <note>A personal rule of action, such as "I will help others to feel good about myself."</note>. For if it were, the maxim could not be represented in a **universal legislative form**, because the expectation of the <margin>30</margin> **existence** of the object would then be the determining cause of the choice. In that case, the **dependence** of the **faculty of desire** on the **existence** of some thing would have to be the basis of the willing—a dependence which can only ever be sought in **empirical conditions** and therefore can never provide the basis for a **necessary** and **universal rule**. Thus, the happiness of other beings [becomes] the object...

an object for everyone’s choice, but merely 
from the fact that the form of universality—which 
reason requires as a condition for giving 
a maxim of self-love the objective validity of 
a law—becomes the determining ground of 
the will. And so, the object (the happiness 
of others) was not the determining ground 
of the pure will; rather, it was the mere legal 
form alone by which I restricted my <margin>30</margin> 
maxim based on inclination, in order to grant 
it the universality of a law and so [35] 
make it appropriate for **pure practical reason**. 
It is from this restriction—and not the 
addition of an external incentive—that the 
concept of **obligation** to extend the maxim 
of my self-love to the happiness of others 
could <gloss>d)</gloss> <note>Kant: "könnte" (could); corrected by Natorp.</note> alone arise.

<summary>Kant explains that helping others is a moral duty not because we happen to feel sympathy, but because the logical form of a universal law requires us to include others' well-being alongside our own. True morality comes from the "form" of the rule, not just the "matter" or the goal being pursued.</summary>

<keywords>pure practical reason, maxims, happiness, universality, self-love, obligation, determining ground, moral law</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues Kant’s exploration of the "determining ground" of the will. Having previously argued that a moral law cannot be based on the specific "matter" or object of a desire (like personal happiness), he now contrasts the principle of morality directly with the principle of self-interest. He aims to show that while we all seek happiness, it cannot serve as a universal law because it lacks the necessary legislative form.</meta>

Part One, Book I, Chapter 1. § 8.

### Remark II.

The direct opposite of the principle of morality is when the principle of one's own happiness <note>original: "Glückseligkeit"; this refers to a state of lasting well-being or "blessedness" rather than just a passing emotion.</note> is made the determining ground <note>original: "Bestimmungsgrund"; the fundamental motive or reason that triggers the will to act.</note> of the will. As I have shown above, this includes everything that places the determining ground—which is supposed to serve as a law—in anything other than the legislative form of the maxim <term>maxim</term> <note>A personal rule of conduct or a subjective principle of acting.</note>. This conflict, however, is not merely logical, like the conflict between <margin>10</margin> empirically-conditioned rules <note>Rules based on experience or observation rather than pure logic.</note> that someone nonetheless wanted to elevate to necessary principles of knowledge; rather, it is a practical conflict. If the voice of reason <note>original: "Vernunft"; the human capacity for logic and moral judgment.</note> in relation to the will were not so clear, so impossible to overstep, and so audible even to the commonest person, it would utterly ruin morality. As it stands, however, morality can only maintain itself in the head-spinning speculations of the schools <note>A reference to academic philosophers who create overly complex theories that contradict common sense.</note>, who are bold enough to make themselves deaf to that heavenly voice <margin>20</margin> in order to uphold a theory that requires no brain-wracking.

Suppose a friend, whom you otherwise liked, thought he could justify himself to you for having given false testimony by first pleading what he claimed was the sacred duty of his own happiness. Suppose he then enumerated the advantages he had gained by it, pointed out the prudence <note>original: "Klugheit"; the practical skill of looking after one's own interests.</note> he observed to be safe from all discovery, even from you—to whom he reveals the secret only so that he can deny it at any time. <margin>30</margin> If he then claimed, in all seriousness, that he had performed a true human duty: you would either laugh right in his face or shrink back from him in horror. And yet, if someone has steered his principles solely toward his own advantages, you would not have the slightest objection against this measure as a matter of strategy. Or suppose someone recommends a man to you as a steward <note>original: "Haushalter"; someone hired to manage a household or estate.</note>, to whom you could blindly entrust all your affairs, and to inspire <margin>40</margin> confidence in you, he praised him as a prudent man who is a master of his own advantage...

<summary>Kant argues that self-interest is the direct opposite of morality and that common human reason easily recognizes this distinction, even if academic philosophers try to obscure it. He uses the examples of a dishonest friend and a self-serving steward to illustrate how absurd it is to treat personal happiness as a moral duty.</summary>

<keywords>morality, happiness, determining ground, maxim, reason, duty, prudence, false testimony</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues Kant’s hypothetical illustration of a man who is praised for his cleverness and refined tastes, yet admits he would steal if he could get away with it. Kant uses this example to show how our common sense immediately recognizes that such behavior, while perhaps "smart" for self-interest, is fundamentally immoral. He then begins to explain why happiness—being based on subjective experience—cannot provide the stable foundation required for a universal moral law.</meta>

# Of the Principles of Pure Practical Reason.

...expertly understood [it], and as someone restlessly active who would let no opportunity for it pass by unused; finally, to ensure that no concerns about a vulgar self-interest stood in the way, he boasted of how he knew how to live quite elegantly—seeking his pleasure not in amassing money or brutal luxury, but in the expansion of his knowledge, in well-chosen, instructive company, and even in doing good for the needy. He added, however, that regarding the <margin>10</margin> means (which, after all, borrow their value or lack thereof only from the purpose), he was not scrupulous, and other people's money and property were as good as his own for this purpose, as long as he knew he could <margin>[36]</margin> do so undetected and unhindered: you would either believe that the person recommending him was mocking you, or that he had lost his mind. — The boundaries between **morality** <term>morality</term> <note>original: "Sittlichkeit"; the quality of being in accord with standards of right or good conduct.</note> and **self-love** <note>original: "Selbstliebe"; acting out of personal interest or desire for one's own well-being.</note> are so clearly and sharply <margin>20</margin> drawn that even the commonest eye cannot fail to distinguish whether something belongs to one or the other. Although the following few remarks may seem superfluous given such an obvious truth, they serve at least to provide the judgment of common human reason with somewhat more clarity.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The principle of **happiness** <note>original: "Glückseligkeit"; often translated as "happiness" or "bliss," referring here to the satisfaction of all our desires and inclinations.</note> can indeed provide maxims <term>maxims</term> <note>Subjective principles or personal rules that an individual follows in their daily life.</note>, but never such as would be fit for laws of the will, even if one made universal happiness one's object. For because the knowledge of this [happiness] rests on mere data of <margin>30</margin> **experience** <note>original: "Erfahrungsdatis"; information gained through the senses and observation rather than through pure logic.</note>, and because every judgment regarding it depends very much on each person's opinion—which is itself highly changeable—it can indeed yield general rules (those that on average most often hold true), but never **universal** ones (those that must be valid at all times and necessarily). Consequently, no practical laws can be founded upon it. Precisely for this reason—because here an object of choice <note>original: "Willkür"; the capacity of the human will to choose between different possible actions or desires.</note> is made the basis <gloss>a)</gloss> of the rule for that choice, and must therefore precede it—this rule <margin>40</margin> cannot be based on anything other than what one...

a) <note>original: "sc. werden"; Latin/German shorthand meaning "namely, to be." It indicates that the verb "to be" is grammatically implied to complete the phrase "is made the basis."</note>

<summary>Kant argues that the distinction between morality and self-interest is obvious to everyone, and that personal happiness cannot serve as a universal law because it is based on subjective, changing experiences rather than necessary principles.</summary>

recommends<sup>a)</sup> <note>original: "empfiehlt"; the Hartenstein edition reads "feels" (empfindet).</note>, and is therefore referred to and based upon experience; thus the variation in judgment must be endless. This principle, then, does not prescribe the same practical rules to all rational beings, even though they fall under a common title, namely that of **happiness** <term>happiness</term> <note>original: "Glückseligkeit"; in this context, it refers to the state of a rational being in the world for whom everything goes according to their wish and will.</note>. The moral law, however, is thought of as objectively necessary only because it is intended to hold for everyone who has reason and will.

<margin>10</margin> The maxim of **self-love** (prudence) merely advises; the law of **morality** <term>morality</term> <note>original: "Sittlichkeit"; the system of rules or principles governing right conduct.</note> commands. There is, however, a great difference between that which we are advised to do and that which we are obligated to do.

What is to be done according to the principle of the **autonomy** <term>autonomy</term> <note>original: "Autonomie"; the capacity of the will to be a law to itself, independent of any property of the objects of volition.</note> of the will is quite easy for even the most common intellect to see without hesitation; what is to be done under the presupposition of its **heteronomy** <term>heteronomy</term> <note>original: "Heteronomie"; when the will is determined by something outside of itself, such as desires, emotions, or external circumstances.</note> is difficult and requires knowledge of the world; that is to say: what **duty** <note>original: "Pflicht"</note> <margin>20</margin> is, presents itself to everyone of its own accord; but what brings a true, lasting advantage—if this is to be extended to one's entire existence—is always shrouded in impenetrable darkness and requires much prudence to adapt the rule, practically tuned<sup>b)</sup> <note>original: "gestimmte"; the 6th edition and Hartenstein read "determined" (bestimmte).</note> toward it, to the purposes of life in even a tolerable way through skillful exceptions. Nevertheless, the moral law commands everyone, and indeed the most punctual observance. Therefore, the judgment of what is to be done according to it must not be so <margin>30</margin> difficult that even the most common and unpracticed intellect, even without worldly wisdom, would not know how to handle it.

To satisfy the **categorical command** <term>categorical command</term> <note>original: "kategorisches Gebot"; an absolute, unconditional requirement that must be obeyed in all circumstances.</note> of morality is within everyone's power at all times; to satisfy the <margin>[37]</margin> empirically conditioned precept of happiness is possible for everyone only rarely, and is far from being possible even in respect to a single intention. The reason is that with the former, it depends only on the<sup>c)</sup> <note>Natorp suggests the reading "must therefore the judgment..." (muß also die).</note>...

<summary>Kant argues that while seeking happiness requires complex "worldly wisdom" and often fails, the demands of morality (duty) are simple, clear, and achievable for every rational person at any time. <page-num>49</page-num>
# On the Principles of Pure Practical Reason.

<margin>10</margin>
depends on the **maxim** <term>maxim</term> <note>original: "Maxime"; a subjective principle of action or a personal rule that someone sets for themselves.</note>, which must be genuine and pure; in the case of the latter, however, it also depends on one's powers and physical ability to actually bring about a desired object. A command that everyone should seek to make themselves happy would be foolish; for one never commands someone to do what they already inevitably want to do of their own accord. One would merely have to command or rather offer them the practical measures to take, because they cannot do everything that they want to do. Commanding **morality** <term>morality</term> <note>original: "Sittlichkeit"; the quality of being in accord with standards of right or good conduct.</note>, however, under the name of **duty**, is entirely reasonable; for not everyone is initially willing to obey its prescription, especially when it is in conflict with their inclinations; and as for the measures concerning how one can follow this law, these do not need to be taught here; for what a person wills in this regard<sup>a)</sup>, they are also able to do.

<margin>20</margin>
One who has lost at gambling may well be annoyed with themselves and their own imprudence; but if they are aware of having cheated in the game (even if they won by doing so), they must despise themselves as soon as they compare themselves with the moral law. This must, therefore, surely be something other than the principle of one's own happiness. For to have to say to oneself: "I am a worthless person, even though I have filled my purse," one must have a different benchmark of judgment than to give oneself approval and say: "I am a clever person, for I have enriched my treasury."

<margin>30, 40</margin>
Finally, there is something else in the idea of our practical reason which accompanies the transgression of a moral law: namely, its **deservingness of punishment** <term>deservingness of punishment</term> <note>original: "Strafwürdigkeit"; the moral state of being worthy of receiving a penalty for a wrong action.</note>. Now, the concept of punishment as such cannot be connected at all with the idea of becoming a participant in happiness. For although the one who punishes may perhaps simultaneously have the benevolent intention of directing this punishment toward that end, it must nonetheless first be justified as punishment—that is, as a mere evil in itself—so that the punished person, if matters stopped there and they could see no

<summary>Kant argues that while happiness cannot be commanded because it is a natural desire, duty must be commanded because it often contradicts our inclinations. <page-num>50</page-num>
# First Part, Book I, Chapter 1. § 8.

...even if he looked beyond this severity to a hidden favor, he must admit to himself that justice was done and that his lot is perfectly suited to his behavior. In every punishment as such, there must first be **justice**, and this constitutes the very essence of this concept. While **benevolence** <term>benevolence</term> <note>original: "Gütigkeit"; the quality of being well-meaning or kind.</note> can certainly be combined with justice, the person deserving of punishment has not the slightest reason to count on this, given their conduct. Therefore, punishment is a **physical evil**, which, even if it were not connected as a natural 
<margin>10</margin> consequence to **moral evil** <term>moral evil</term> <note>original: "Moralisch-Bösen"; an intentional violation of the moral law, as opposed to a mere mistake or natural disaster.</note>, would nonetheless have to be connected as a consequence according to the principles of a **moral legislation**. Now, if every crime is punishable in itself, even without regard for the physical consequences for the perpetrator—that is, if it forfeits **happiness** <term>happiness</term> <note>original: "Glückseligkeit"; a state of lasting satisfaction with one's condition.</note> (at least in part)—it would obviously be absurd to say that the crime consisted precisely in the fact that he incurred a punishment by causing harm to his own 
<margin>[38] 20</margin> **happiness** (which, according to the principle of **self-love** <term>self-love</term> <note>original: "Selbstliebe"; the desire for one's own well-being and satisfaction.</note>, would have to be the actual concept of all crime). In this way, the punishment would be the reason for calling something a crime, and justice would instead have to consist in omitting all punishment and even preventing the natural consequences; for then there would be nothing evil left in the action, because the evils that otherwise followed it, and for the sake of which alone the action was called evil, would now be prevented. Furthermore, to view all 
<margin>30</margin> punishing and rewarding merely as the **machinery** in the hand of a higher power, which is supposed to serve only to set rational beings into activity toward their final purpose (happiness), is too obviously a mechanism of their will that abolishes all **freedom** <term>freedom</term> <note>original: "Freiheit"; the capacity to act independently of natural necessity or external coercion.</note> for it to be necessary to linger on this point.

More subtle, though equally untrue, is the claim of those who assume a certain special **moral sense**, which—and not **reason**—determines the 
<margin>40</margin> moral law; according to them, the consciousness of **virtue** <term>virtue</term> <note>original: "Tugend"; the moral strength of a person's will in fulfilling their duty.</note> is directly associated with satisfaction and pleasure, whereas that of **vice** <term>vice</term> <note>original: "Laster"; a practice or habit considered immoral or wicked.</note> is associated with restlessness of soul...

<summary>Kant argues that punishment must be based on justice rather than personal happiness or utility, asserting that treating punishment merely as a tool for behavior modification destroys human freedom. He also begins to critique the "moral sense" theory, which prioritizes feelings over reason.</summary>

<sig>4*</sig>

...and pain were connected, and thus everything would still depend on the desire for one's own **happiness** <term>happiness</term> <note>original: "Glückseligkeit"; a state of lasting satisfaction with one's condition.</note>. Without drawing in what was said above, I only want to point out the deception occurring here. To imagine the wicked person as being plagued by **unease of mind** <term>unease of mind</term> <note>original: "Gemütsunruhe"; a state of inner turmoil resulting from guilt.</note> through the consciousness of his transgressions, one must already imagine him in advance, according to the most noble foundation of his character, as being—at least in some degree—**morally good**. Similarly, one must imagine him <margin>10</margin> who is delighted by the consciousness of actions in accordance with duty as being already **virtuous**. Therefore, the concept of **morality** and **duty** had to precede any consideration of this satisfaction and cannot be derived from it at all.

Now, one must first value the importance of what we call **duty**, the authority of the **moral law**, and the immediate worth that the following of it gives a person in their own eyes, in order to feel that satisfaction in the consciousness <margin>20</margin> of its adequacy to the same [law]<sup>a)</sup> and the bitter reproach when one can blame oneself for its transgression. One cannot, therefore, feel this satisfaction or **unease of soul**<sup>b)</sup> <term>unease of soul</term> <note>original: "Seelenunruhe"; inner distress or a "guilty conscience."</note> before the awareness of **obligation** <term>obligation</term> <note>original: "Verbindlichkeit"; the state of being bound to follow a law.</note> and make it the basis of the latter. One must already be an honest man at least halfway to even be able to form a representation of those sensations. Moreover, I do not at all deny that, just as the human will is directly determinable by the <margin>30</margin> **moral law** by virtue of freedom, the frequent exercise in accordance with this **determining ground** <term>determining ground</term> <note>original: "Bestimmungsgrund"; the fundamental reason or motive that prompts the will to act.</note> can ultimately produce subjectively a feeling of **satisfaction with oneself**. On the contrary, it is even a duty to establish and cultivate this feeling, which actually alone deserves to be called the **moral feeling**; but the concept of duty cannot be derived from it; otherwise, we would have to imagine a feeling...

<summary>Kant argues that the feelings of satisfaction or guilt following an action cannot be the basis of morality, because one must already be "halfway honest" to experience them. Instead, the moral law itself must be the primary cause of our actions, and the "moral feeling" is a secondary effect cultivated through practice.</summary>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>Following the table on page 53, Kant now analyzes the two columns of "material" motives. He explains why the "objective" grounds (perfection and God's will) are no better than "subjective" ones (feelings and education) if they rely on a desired result rather than the form of the law itself.</meta>

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>First Part, Book I, Chapter 1. Section 8.</header>

<margin>[41]</margin> Those listed on the left side are altogether empirical <note>Based on experience or observation rather than pure logic.</note> and are clearly not suitable for the universal principle of morality. However, those on the right side are founded on **reason** (for **perfection**, as a quality of things, and the **highest perfection** represented in substance, i.e., **God**, can both only be thought through concepts of reason). But the former concept—namely, that of perfection—can be taken either in a theoretical
<margin>10</margin> meaning, where it signifies nothing but the completeness of each thing in its kind (<term>transcendental</term> <note>original: "transzendentale"; here referring to the essential conditions that make a thing what it is.</note>), or of a thing merely as a thing in general (<term>metaphysical</term> <note>original: "metaphysische"; referring to the abstract nature of existence.</note>), and that cannot be the subject of discussion here. The concept of perfection in its **practical** meaning, however, is the suitability or sufficiency of a thing for all sorts of ends <note>Goals or purposes.</note>. This perfection, as a quality of man and therefore internal, is nothing other than **talent** and—what strengthens or completes it—**skill**.
<margin>20</margin> The highest perfection in substance, i.e., **God**, and therefore external (viewed from a practical perspective), is the sufficiency of this being for all ends in general. Now, if ends must first be given to us, in relation to which the concept of perfection (either an internal one in ourselves or an external one in God) can alone become the <term>determining ground</term> <note>original: "Bestimmungsgrund"; the fundamental reason that moves the will to act.</note> of the will—but if an end as an object must precede the determination of the will by a practical rule and must contain the ground of the possi-
<margin>30</margin> bility of such a rule, then the **matter** of the will (taken as its determining ground) is always empirical. Consequently, it can serve the **Epicurean** <note>Referring to the philosophy of Epicurus, which Kant associates here with seeking pleasure or happiness as the primary goal.</note> principle of the <term>doctrine of happiness</term> <note>original: "Glückseligkeitslehre"; the study of how to achieve well-being or contentment.</note>, but never the pure rational principle of the <term>doctrine of morals</term> <note>original: "Sittenlehre"; the study of duty and ethical laws.</note> and of **duty**. (For example, talents and their promotion only become a motive because they contribute to the advantages of life; or the will of God, if harmony with it is taken as the object of the will without a prior practical principle independent of that idea, can only become a

...principles established are **material**; secondly: that they encompass all possible **material** principles, and from this finally follows the conclusion: that because **material principles** are entirely unsuitable for the supreme moral law (as has been proven), the formal practical principle of pure reason—according to which the mere form of a universal legislation made possible through our maxims <term>maxims</term> <note>Personal rules or principles of action that an individual chooses to follow.</note> must constitute the supreme and immediate determining ground of the will—is the <margin>10</margin> only possible one. It is the only one suitable for **categorical imperatives**, i.e., **practical laws** (which make actions a duty), and generally for the principle of **morality** <note>original: "Sittlichkeit"</note> both in judging actions and in applying it to the human will to determine it.

### I.
<margin>[42]</margin>
# On the Deduction of the Principles of Pure Practical Reason.

This Analytic demonstrates that **pure reason** can be **practical**; that is, it can determine the will for itself, independent of everything empirical <note>Based on sensory experience or observation.</note>. <margin>20</margin> It does this through a fact <note>Kant famously calls this the "Fact of Reason" (Faktum der Vernunft), where our awareness of the moral law is a self-evident reality.</note> in which pure reason actually proves itself practical in us: namely, the **autonomy in the principle of morality**, by which it determines the will to action. — It shows at the same time that this fact is inseparably connected with, indeed identical to, the **consciousness of the freedom of the will**. By this, the will of a rational being—who, as belonging to the sensible world <term>sensible world</term> <note>original: "Sinnenwelt"; the world of physical objects and cause-and-effect as we perceive them.</note>, recognizes itself as necessarily subject to the laws of <margin>30</margin> **causality** like other efficient causes—is nevertheless in practical matters simultaneously conscious of another side of its existence. Specifically, as a **being in itself** <note>original: "Wesen an sich selbst"; the true nature of a thing independent of how it appears to our senses.</note>, it is conscious of its existence as it can be determined in an **intelligible order** of things. This is not through a special intuition of itself, but according to certain dynamic laws that can determine its causality in the sensible world; for that **freedom**, if it is attributed to us...

<summary>Kant concludes that only a formal principle (the form of law itself) can serve as a basis for morality. He begins the "Deduction," arguing that our consciousness of the moral law is a "fact" that proves we are free and belong to an "intelligible" world beyond simple cause-and-effect.</summary>
<keywords>Deduction, Pure Practical Reason, Moral Law, Maxims, Categorical Imperative, Morality, Autonomy, Freedom of the Will, Sensible World, Causality, Being in itself, Intelligible Order</keywords>

...placed us into an **intelligible order of things**, has been sufficiently proven elsewhere.<gloss>a) <note>original: "sc. in der Grundlegung"; namely, in the *Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals*.</note></gloss>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Now, if we compare this with the analytical part of the **Critique of Pure Speculative Reason**, a remarkable contrast between the two reveals itself. There, it was not principles, but pure **sensory intuition** <term>intuition</term> <note>In philosophy, this refers to the immediate way our mind receives data from the senses, specifically through space and time.</note> (space and time) that was the first datum <note>A "datum" is a given piece of information or a starting point for reasoning.</note> which made knowledge *a priori* <note>Knowledge that is independent of all experience.</note> possible—and then only for objects of the senses. —
<margin>10</margin> Synthetic principles from mere concepts without intuition were impossible; rather, these could only occur in relation to intuition, which was sensory, and therefore only in relation to objects of possible experience. This is because the concepts of the understanding, when combined with this intuition, alone make possible that knowledge which we call experience. — Beyond objects of experience—that is, regarding things as **noumena** <term>noumena</term> <note>Things as they are in themselves, rather than how they appear to our senses (phenomena).</note>—every positive piece of knowledge was quite rightly <margin>20</margin> denied to speculative reason. — Yet, speculative reason did achieve this much: it secured the concept of noumena—that is, the possibility, indeed the necessity, of thinking such things. For example, it rescued the idea of **freedom** (viewed negatively) from all objections by showing it to be entirely compatible with those principles and limitations of pure theoretical <margin>[43]</margin> reason, even without providing any definite or expanding knowledge of such objects, as it instead completely cut off any such prospect.
<margin>30</margin> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In contrast, the **moral law**, while it provides no such prospect <note>i.e., no sensory proof</note>, nevertheless offers a fact that is absolutely inexplicable from all the data of the world of sense and the entire scope of our theoretical use of reason. This fact points to a **pure world of understanding**, and even defines it positively, letting us know something of it: namely, a law.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;This law is intended to provide the world of sense—as a sensory nature (insofar as rational beings are concerned)—with the form of a **world of understanding**, i.e., a **supersensible nature**, yet without <margin>40</margin> interfering with the former's laws...

<summary>Kant contrasts his moral philosophy with his earlier theory of knowledge, arguing that while speculative reason cannot "know" the realm beyond the senses, the moral law acts as a "fact" that gives us positive access to this intelligible world.</summary>

<page-num>57</page-num>

...to interfere with their mechanism. Now, nature in the most general sense is the existence of things under laws. The sensory nature of rational beings in general is their existence under empirically conditioned laws, which for reason constitutes *heteronomy* <term>heteronomy</term> <note>From the Greek 'heteros' (other) and 'nomos' (law); this refers to the will being determined by something outside itself, such as desires or external circumstances.</note>. The supersensible nature of these same beings, on the other hand, is their existence according to laws that are independent of every empirical condition, and thus belong to the *autonomy* of *pure reason*. And since the laws according to which the existence of things <margin>10</margin> depends on knowledge are practical, supersensible nature—so far as we can form a concept of it—is nothing other than a nature under the *autonomy* of pure *practical reason*. The law of this autonomy, however, is the *moral law*; which is therefore the fundamental law of a supersensible nature and a world of pure understanding, whose counterpart is to exist in the sensory world, yet without interfering with the laws of that world. One could call the former the archetypal nature <note>original: *natura archetypa*</note>, <margin>20</margin> which we cognize solely in reason; the latter, however, because it contains the possible effect of the idea of the former as the determining ground of the will, could be called the derivative nature <note>original: *natura ectypa*; an 'ectype' is a copy or image of an archetype</note>. For in fact, the *moral law* transfers us, in idea, into a nature in which pure reason—if it were accompanied by the appropriate physical power—would produce the *highest good*, and it determines our *will* to provide the form of the sensory worldᵃ) as a whole of rational beings. <margin>30</margin>

Common attention to oneself confirms that this idea actually serves, as it were, as a blueprint or pattern for our *determinations of the will*.

It is obvious that, in this way, it would compel everyone to *truthfulness*. For it cannot coexist with the universality of a law of nature that statements <margin>40</margin>

to be accepted as proving something and yet as intentionally untrue. Likewise, the maxim <term>maxim</term> <note>A personal rule of conduct or a subjective principle of action.</note> that I adopt regarding the free disposal of my life is immediately determined when I ask myself how it would have to be in order for a nature to maintain itself according to a law of that maxim. Clearly, no one in such a nature would be able to end their life arbitrarily, for such a constitution would not be a lasting order of nature; and so it is in all other <margin>10</margin> cases. However, in actual nature, as it is an object of experience, the free will is not automatically determined to such maxims that could themselves establish a nature according to universal laws, or that would automatically fit into a nature ordered according to them; rather, it is private inclinations that constitute a natural whole according to pathological <term>pathological</term> <note>In Kant’s philosophy, this does not mean "diseased," but rather "pertaining to the senses or emotions"—actions driven by feelings and desires rather than by reason.</note> (physical) laws, but not a nature that would be possible through our will alone according to pure practical laws. Nevertheless, <margin>20</margin> we are conscious through reason of a law to which all our maxims are subject, as if a natural order had to arise through our will at the same time. Therefore, this must be the idea of a nature not empirically given and yet possible through freedom—a supersensible <term>supersensible</term> <note>Refers to a realm or reality that exists beyond the reach of human sensory experience (sight, touch, etc.).</note> nature—to which we give objective reality, at least in a practical sense, because we view it as the object of our will as pure rational beings.

The difference, therefore, between the laws of a <margin>30</margin> nature to which the will is subject, and a nature which is subject to a will (in regard to that which has a relation<sup>a)</sup> to its free actions), rests on this: that in the former, the objects must be the causes of the representations <note>mental images or concepts</note> that determine the will, whereas in the latter, the will is to be the cause of the objects, so that their causality <term>causality</term> <note>The relationship between cause and effect; here, the power of the will to make things happen in the world.</note> has its determining ground solely in the pure faculty of reason, which can therefore <margin>40</margin> also be called a pure practical reason.

<page-num>59</page-num>

# On the Deduction of Principles

The two tasks, then, are very different: how pure reason, on the one hand, can know objects <term>a priori</term> <note>original: "a priori". Refers to knowledge that is independent of all experience.</note>, and how, on the other hand, it can be an immediate determining ground of the will. That is, how reason can be the cause for a rational being to act and bring objects into reality, solely through the thought that its own rules of action (<term>maxims</term> <note>Subjective principles or personal rules that a person follows when acting.</note>) could hold the weight of universal laws.ª)

The first task belongs to the *Critique of Pure Speculative Reason*. It requires an explanation of how <term>intuitions</term> <note>The immediate way our minds receive information from objects, which Kant argues only happens through our senses.</note> are possible independently of experience. Without these, no object can be given to us, and thus nothing can be known in a way that connects different ideas together. The solution to this task is that our intuitions are entirely sensory. Because of this, we cannot have speculative knowledge that goes any further than what possible experience can reach. Therefore, all the principles of that pure speculativeᵇ) reason do nothing more than make experience possible—either experience of objects already given to us, or those that might be given in an infinite series, but which are never fully completed.

The second task belongs to the *Critique of Practical Reason*. It does not require an explanation of how the objects of our <term>faculty of desire</term> <note>The capacity to be the cause of the objects of one's ideas through those very ideas; essentially, the power to want something and try to make it happen.</note> are possible, for that is a task for the theoretical study of nature left to the *Critique of [Speculative] Reason*. Instead, it only asks how reason can determine the will's path. It asks whether this happens only through <term>empirical</term> <note>Based on observation or experience, such as seeking pleasure or avoiding pain.</note> ideasᶜ) acting as the reasons for our choices, or whether pure reason can also be "practical"—meaning it could be the law for an order of nature that is possible but not perceivable through the senses at all. The possibility of such a <term>supersensible nature</term> <note>A reality that exists beyond the physical world of cause and effect, governed by laws of freedom rather than laws of physics.</note>, the concept of which could simultaneously be the reason why that nature becomes real through our free will, does not need an <term>a priori</term> intuition (of an intelligible world). In fact, such an intuition of a world beyond the senses would be impossible for us anyway. For it comes...

c) Kant's original text used the singular "representation"; corrected to the plural "representations" by editor Hartenstein.

<summary>Kant distinguishes between speculative reason, which is limited to making sense of physical experience, and practical reason, which can determine the will according to moral laws of a "supersensible" world.</summary>

<page-num>60</page-num>

<center>First Part, Book I, First Chapter. I.</center>

...depends only on the <term>determining ground</term> <note>The "determining ground" (Bestimmungsgrund) is the underlying reason or motive that triggers an act of will.</note> of the willing within its <term>maxims</term> <note>A "maxim" is a personal rule or principle that an individual follows when making a decision.</note>; specifically, whether that ground is empirical <note>Based on sensory experience, feelings, or desires.</note> or a concept of <term>pure reason</term> <note>Reason that functions independently of sensory experience.</note> (concerning the universal lawfulness of reason in general), and how it can be the latter. Whether the <term>causality</term> of the will is sufficient to make objects a reality or not is left to the theoretical principles of reason to judge, as an investigation into the possibility of the objects of willing. Therefore, the <term>intuition</term> <note>The direct mental representation of an object.</note> of these objects forms <margin>10</margin>no part of the practical task itself. The only thing that matters here is the determination of the will and the determining ground of its maxim as a free will, not the result. For if the will is lawful for pure reason alone, then it does not matter how its power fares in the actual execution; whether or not a "nature" actually arises from these maxims [46] according to the legislation of a possible nature, the Critique does not concern itself with this at all. The Critique investigates whether and how pure <margin>20</margin>reason can be "practical"—that is, whether it can immediately determine the will.

In this work, the Critique can therefore start without blame—and indeed must start—from pure practical laws and their reality. Instead of intuition, however, it provides these laws with the foundation of the concept of their existence in the <term>intelligible world</term> <note>The "intelligible world" (intelligibele Welt) refers to a reality beyond our physical senses, where things are understood by the intellect alone.</note>—namely, <term>freedom</term>. For freedom means nothing else, and those laws are only possible in relation to the freedom of the will; yet, assuming freedom, they are <margin>30</margin>necessary. Or, conversely: freedom is necessary because those laws are necessary as <term>practical postulates</term> <note>A "postulate" is something we must assume to be true in order for moral action to make sense, even if we cannot prove it through science.</note>. Exactly how this consciousness of moral laws (or, what is the same thing, the consciousness of freedom) is possible cannot be explained further; one can only defend its validity within the Critique of theoretical reason.

The exposition of the supreme principle of practical reason has now been accomplished. That is, it has been shown, first, what it contains—that it exists entirely <term>a priori</term> <margin>40</margin><note>Known independently of experience.</note> and independent of empirical principles—and then, how it differs from all other practical principles. With the...

<summary>Kant argues that practical reason cares only about the internal motive (the "determining ground") of the will rather than the physical success of an action. He identifies freedom as the essential foundation that allows us to act according to moral laws within an "intelligible world."</summary>
