<page-num>61</page-num>

# On the Deduction of the Principles #

In the <term>deduction</term> <note>In a philosophical context, a "deduction" is not just a logical argument, but a legalistic justification of why a concept or principle has the right to be used.</note>—that is, the justification of its objective and universal validity and the insight into the possibility of such a <term>synthetic proposition a priori</term> <note>A "synthetic proposition a priori" is a statement that adds new information (it is "synthetic") but is known to be true independently of any sensory experience (it is "a priori"). For example, "Every event has a cause."</note>—one cannot hope to proceed as smoothly as was possible with the principles of the pure <term>theoretical understanding</term>. For the latter referred to objects of possible experience, namely to <term>appearances</term> <note>Kant distinguishes between things as they appear to our senses ("appearances") and things as they are in themselves.</note>; and one could prove that only by bringing these appearances under the <term>categories</term> <note>The "categories" are the basic blueprints or rules the mind uses to organize sensory data into meaningful objects, such as "cause and effect."</note> in accordance with those laws could these appearances be recognized as objects of experience. Consequently, all possible experience must conform to these laws. However, I cannot take such a path with the deduction of the **moral law**. For it does not concern the knowledge of the properties of objects that may be given to reason from elsewhere, but rather a knowledge insofar as it can become the ground of the existence of the objects themselves, and through which reason has <term>causality</term> in a rational being. That is, it concerns **pure reason**, which can be viewed as a faculty immediately determining the will.

Now, however, all human insight comes to an end as soon as we reach <margin>47</margin> **fundamental forces** or **fundamental capacities**; for their possibility cannot be understood through anything else, yet neither may they be arbitrarily invented or assumed. Therefore, in the theoretical use of reason, only experience can justify us in assuming them. This <term>surrogate</term> <note>A "surrogate" is a substitute. Here, Kant means that since we can't prove why these powers exist using logic alone, we use the "substitute" of seeing them work in experience.</note> of providing empirical proofs instead of a deduction from <term>sources of knowledge a priori</term> is, however, also denied to us here regarding the pure practical faculty of reason. For whatever requires the ground of its reality to be drawn from experience must, according to the grounds of its possibility, be dependent on principles of experience; yet pure and yet practical reason, by its very concept, must be held as impossible if it were so dependent. Furthermore, the moral law is given, as it were, as a **fact of pure reason** <note>The "fact of pure reason" (Faktum der Vernunft) is one of Kant's most famous ideas: that our awareness of the moral law is an undeniable reality of our consciousness that does not need a complex proof—it is simply "there."</note> of which we are conscious *a priori*, and which is <term>apodictically</term> <note>"Apodictic" certainty means it is not just true, but necessarily true; it could not be otherwise.</note> certain.

<summary>Kant explains that the moral law cannot be proven the same way as laws of nature because it doesn't describe how the world *is*, but how the will *ought* to be. He concludes that the moral law is a "fact of reason" that we simply know to be true without needing a sensory proof.</summary>

<keywords>deduction, principles, justification, synthetic a priori, theoretical understanding, experience, categories, moral law, reason, causality, fundamental capacities, surrogate, a priori knowledge, empirical proof, practical reason, fact of reason, apodictic certainty</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Deduction of the Principles" from page 61. Kant is arguing that unlike the laws of physics, the moral law cannot be proven by looking at the world around us (experience). Instead, he is about to turn the argument around: the moral law itself will be the proof that we are actually free.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>

### First Part, Book I, Chapter 1. I. ###

...that one could not find even a single example in experience where it <note>Referring to the moral law discussed on the previous page.</note> had been followed exactly.ᵃ) Therefore, the objective reality of the moral law cannot be proven by any <term>deduction</term> <note>A legalistic justification of a concept’s validity.</note>—by any effort of theoretical, speculative, or empirically supported reason—and thus, even if one were willing to renounce <term>apodictic certainty</term> <note>Absolute, undeniable certainty.</note>, it cannot be confirmed by any experience and so proven <term>a posteriori</term> <note>original: "a posteriori"; knowledge gained from experience or after the fact.</note>, yet it still stands firm on its own.

<margin>10</margin>
However, something else and quite paradoxical takes the place of this vainly sought deduction of the moral principle; namely, that it serves instead as the principle for the deduction of an unfathomable faculty which no experience can proveᶜ), but which <term>speculative reason</term> <note>The use of reason to understand the world of nature and physics.</note> (in order to find the <term>unconditioned</term> <note>The "first cause" or ultimate starting point that isn't caused by anything else.</note> within its cosmological ideas regarding causality, so that it does not contradict itself) had to at least assume as possible: namely, that of **freedom**. The moral law—which itself requires no justifying grounds—proves not just the possibility but the **reality** of freedom in beings who recognize this law as binding for them.
<margin>20</margin>
The moral law is, in fact, a law of causality through freedom and thus of the possibility of a **supersensible nature**, just as the metaphysical law of events in the <term>sensible world</term> <note>The "world of the senses," or the physical world we perceive around us.</note> was a law of the causality of sensible nature. The moral law therefore determines what speculative philosophy had to leave undetermined—namely, the law for a causality whose concept in the latter was only negative <note>In theoretical science, we can only say freedom is "not" physical necessity; we can't say what it "is."</note>—and thus provides it for the first time with objective reality.
<margin>30</margin>

<margin>48</margin>
This kind of **credential** for the moral law—since it is itself established as a principle for the deduction of freedom as a causality of pure reason—is quite sufficient to satisfy a need of theoretical reason, which was forced to at least assume the possibility of freedom.

<summary>Kant explains that the moral law cannot be proven through sensory experience, but it serves a unique purpose: it provides the only proof we have that human freedom is real. He describes the moral law as a "law of causality" for a higher, non-physical reality.</summary>

<page-num>63</page-num>

# On the Deduction of Principles #

<term>A priori</term> <note>original: "a priori"; referring to knowledge that is independent of experience.</note> justification is completely sufficient. For the moral law proves its reality, in a way that is satisfying even for the <term>critique of speculative reason</term> <note>The investigation into the limits of what we can know about the physical world through logic alone.</note>, by adding a positive definition to a causality that was previously only thought of negatively. The possibility of such a causality was incomprehensible to speculative reason, and yet reason was forced to assume it. The moral law adds the concept of a reason that determines the will directly (through the condition of a universal law-like form of its <term>maxims</term> <note>Personal rules or principles that guide an individual's actions.</note>). Thus, for the first time, it is able to give objective, albeit only practical, reality to reason—which always became <term>extravagant</term> <note>In this context, "extravagant" means overstepping its bounds or going beyond what can be proven.</note> when it tried to proceed speculatively with its ideas. It transforms the <term>transcendent</term> <note>Usage of reason that goes beyond the limits of possible experience.</note> use of reason into an <term>immanent</term> <note>Usage of reason that stays within the bounds of experience, though here it refers to reason acting as a real cause through ideas.</note> use (acting as a cause within the field of experience through the ideas themselves <note>a) Kant: "causes"; corrected by Hartenstein.</note>).

The determination of the causality of beings in the <term>world of the senses</term> <note>The physical world we perceive through our eyes, ears, and touch.</note> could never be <term>unconditioned</term> <note>Something that is not caused by anything else; a "first cause."</note>. And yet, for every series of conditions, there must necessarily be something unconditioned, and therefore there must be a causality that determines itself entirely on its own. Therefore, the idea of **freedom**, as a capacity of absolute spontaneity, was not just a need, but—as far as its possibility is concerned—an **analytical principle** of pure speculative reason. 

However, since it is absolutely impossible to provide an example of freedom in any experience (because among the causes of things as <term>appearances</term> <note>The world as it appears to us, which is governed by the laws of physics and cause-and-effect.</note>, no absolutely unconditioned causality can be found), we could only defend the thought of a freely acting cause by applying it to a being in the world of the senses insofar as it is also considered a <term>noumenon</term> <note>original: "Noumenon"; a thing as it exists in itself, independent of our sensory perception.</note>. We did this by showing that it is not contradictory to view all of a being's actions as physically conditioned (insofar as they are appearances) and yet, at the same time, to view the causality of those same actions as physically unconditioned (insofar as the acting being is a <term>being of understanding</term> <note>A being capable of rational thought, not just governed by physical impulses.</note>). In this way, we make the concept of freedom a <term>regulative principle</term> <note>A rule that directs our thoughts and actions, even if we cannot "see" the object (freedom) itself.</note>...

<summary>Kant explains that the moral law provides the "positive" proof of freedom that speculative reason could only guess at. He argues that humans can be seen as both physically determined (as part of nature) and free (as rational beings).</summary>

This is something reason cannot achieve at all, and which it does not even consider as practical reason. Instead, it only places the determining ground of the causality of man as a <term>sensory being</term> <note>A human being as part of the physical world, experiencing things through the senses.</note> (which is already given) within pure reason (which is therefore called "practical"). # On the Deduction of Principles.

5

from objects for the sake of theoretical knowledge, it can here entirely abstract (because this concept is always found in the <term>understanding</term> <note>The faculty of the mind that organizes and categorizes our experiences.</note>, even independent of all <term>intuition</term> <note>Direct sensory perception; the way we "see" or "feel" things in the world.</note>, <term>a priori</term> <note>Knowledge that exists in the mind prior to, and independent of, experience.</note>), not in order to know objects, but to determine <term>causality</term> <note>The relationship between a cause and its effect.</note> in respect to them in general. For it receives meaning <margin>20</margin> regardless—though only for practical use—namely through the **moral law**. Even when considered theoretically, it remains a pure, *a priori* given concept of the understanding that can be applied to objects, whether they are given through the senses or not; although in the latter case it has no specific theoretical meaning or application, but is merely a formal yet essential thought of the understanding regarding an object in general. The <margin>30</margin> meaning which reason procures for it through the **moral law** is solely practical, since the idea of the law of a causality (of the will) itself has causality, or is its determining ground.

## II.

### On the Authority of Pure Reason in its Practical Use
*for an Extension Which is Not Possible for it in its Speculative Use.*

In the moral principle, we have established a law of <term>causality</term> <note>The relationship between a cause and its effect; here, the way the moral law causes the will to act.</note> which places the <term>determining ground</term> <note>The fundamental reason or motive that triggers an action.</note> of the latter beyond all conditions of the <term>world of sense</term> <note>The physical world we experience through our five senses.</note>. We have not merely <margin>10</margin> thought^a^) <note>original: "gedacht"; This and the following verbs "determined" and "expanded" are past participles tied to the earlier phrase "we have."</note> of the <term>will</term> <note>The faculty of choice or the capacity to act according to principles.</note> as being determinable insofar as it belongs to an <term>intelligible world</term> <note>The realm of reality that exists beyond sensory experience, accessible only to the mind.</note>—and thus thought of the subject of this will (the human being) not only as belonging to a pure world of understanding, even though in this respect he remains unknown to us (as was the case in the *Critique of Pure Speculative Reason*). Rather, we have also determined^a^) <note>original: "bestimmt"</note> him in respect of his causality by means of a law which cannot be counted among any of the laws of nature in the world of sense. Thus, we have expanded^a^) <note>original: "erweitert"</note> our knowledge beyond the limits of the <margin>20</margin>^b^) <note>The original text used the masculine "des," but has been corrected to the feminine "der" to match "Sinnenwelt."</note> latter [the world of sense]. This is a presumption that the *Critique of Pure Reason* declared null and void in all <term>speculation</term> <note>Theoretical thinking that tries to reach truths beyond possible experience.</note>. How then is the practical use of <term>pure reason</term> <note>Reasoning that does not rely on sensory data.</note> now to be reconciled with its theoretical use regarding the determination of the limits of its power?

David Hume, of whom it can be said that he actually began all the challenges^c^) <note>The original singular "challenge" (Anfechtung) is corrected here to the plural "challenges" to match the grammar of the sentence.</note> to the rights of a pure reason—which necessitated a total investigation of the same—concluded thus: The <margin>[51]</margin> concept of <term>cause</term> is a concept that contains the <margin>30</margin> necessity of the connection of the existence of different things, and indeed, insofar as they are different; so that if A is posited, I recognize that something entirely different from it, B, must also necessarily exist. # Extension of Pure Reason in its Practical Use

Now, he [Hume] says, it is impossible to recognize the connection between one thing and another (or one determination and another, entirely different from it <gloss>namely, consists (Kehrbach)</gloss>), if they are not given in perception, **a priori** and as necessary. <margin>10</margin>

Thus, the concept of a cause is surreptitiously obtained and not lawfully 
<margin>20</margin> 
acquired; indeed, it can never be acquired or authenticated, because it demands a connection that is null in itself, chimerical <note>Imaginary or impossible to achieve.</note>, and unsustainable by any reason, to which no object can ever correspond. — Thus, first in regard to all knowledge concerning the existence of things (mathematics was thus still excluded), <term>Empiricism</term> <note>The philosophical theory that all knowledge comes only or primarily from sensory experience.</note> was introduced as the only source of principles, but with it simultaneously the harshest <term>Skepticism</term> <note>The view that certain knowledge is impossible.</note> even in regard to the whole of natural science 
<margin>30</margin> 
(viewed as philosophy). Indeed, of no event could one say: something must have 
<margin>40</margin> 
preceded it, upon which it necessarily followed.

# Expansion of Pure Reason in Practical Use.

Whether common reason—faced with such a terrible upheaval as we see occurring at the very foundations of knowledge—would fare better, or would instead become even more irrecoverably entangled in this same destruction of all knowledge, and whether a universal skepticism <margin>10</margin> might not follow from these same principles (which would, of course, primarily affect scholars), I will leave for everyone to judge for themselves.

Regarding my work in the *Critique of Pure Reason*, which was indeed prompted by Hume’s skeptical doctrine, but went much further and covered the entire field of pure theoretical reason in its productive use <note>original: "synthetischen Gebrauche"; knowledge that adds new information to a concept rather than just analyzing what is already there.</note>, and thus also what is generally called **metaphysics** <note>The branch of philosophy that deals with the first principles of things, including abstract concepts such as being, knowing, and cause.</note> in general: <margin>[53]</margin> I proceeded in the following way regarding the doubts of the Scottish <margin>20</margin> philosopher concerning the concept of **causality**. Hume was quite right that if one takes the objects of experience for **things in themselves** (as is almost universally done), the concept of cause is deceptive and a false illusion. For regarding things in themselves and their properties as such, it cannot be understood why, if something A is posed, something else B <margin>[48]</margin> must also necessarily be posed; thus, he could not allow for any such knowledge **prior to experience** <note>original: "a priori"; knowledge that is independent of sensory observation.</note> regarding things in <margin>30</margin> themselves. This insightful man could even less allow for an empirical origin <note>An origin based on sensory experience or observation.</note> of this concept, because that directly contradicts the **necessity** of the connection, which constitutes the very essence of the concept of causality; consequently, the concept was banished, and its place was taken by **habit** in observing the course of perceptions.

However, from my investigations, it emerged that the objects with which we deal in experience <margin>40</margin> are by no means things in themselves, but merely **appearances**; and that, although...

# First Part, Book I, Chapter 1, Section II.

...even if, regarding things in themselves, it is impossible to see—indeed, impossible to comprehend—how, if one thing (A) is established, it would be contradictory not to establish another thing (B) which is entirely different from A (this refers to the necessity of the link between A as a **cause** <term>cause: a thing that gives rise to an action or phenomenon</term> and B as an **effect** <term>effect: a change that is a result or consequence of an action</term>). Nevertheless, it can certainly be imagined that, when they appear to us within our experience, they must be linked in a necessary way (for example, regarding how they occur in time). They 
<margin>10</margin> cannot be separated without contradicting the very connection that makes this experience possible—an experience in which they are objects and are alone knowable by us. And this indeed turned out to be the case. I was able to prove the concept of cause not only according to its objective reality regarding the things we experience, but I could also **deduce** <term>deduce: to arrive at a logical conclusion from a general principle</term> it as an **a priori** <term>a priori: knowledge that comes from theoretical deduction rather than from observation</term> concept because of the necessity of the connection it brings with it. This means I could demonstrate its possibility through the **pure understanding** <term>pure understanding: the faculty of the mind that produces concepts without relying on the senses</term> 
<margin>20</margin> without using any sources from experience. By removing the idea that these concepts come only from experience (**empiricism** <term>empiricism: the theory that all knowledge is derived from sense-experience</term>), I could also remove its unavoidable consequence: **skepticism** <term>skepticism: the theory that certain knowledge is impossible</term>. I did this first regarding **natural science**, and then—following perfectly from the same reasons—regarding **mathematics**. Both of these sciences deal with objects of possible experience. In doing so, I was able to eliminate the 
<margin>[54]</margin> total doubt about everything that **theoretical reason** <term>theoretical reason: our ability to understand how the physical world works through logic</term> claims to understand.
<margin>30</margin> But how should we apply this category of **causality** <term>causality: the principle that everything has a cause</term> (and all other categories, for without them we cannot bring about any knowledge of that which exists) to things that are *not* objects of possible experience, but lie beyond those boundaries? After all, I have only been able to **deduce** <note>original: "deduzieren"; here meaning to provide a legal-style justification for using a concept.</note> the objective reality of these concepts in relation to objects of possible experience. 
Yet the very fact that I rescued these concepts in that case—showing that we 
<margin>40</margin> can at least *think* of objects through them, even if we cannot define them ahead of time (**a priori**) <note>original: "a priori bestimmen"</note>—is what gives them a place in the pure understanding, from which they are...

<meta>This page continues the transition from the "Analytic of Pure Theoretical Reason" to the "Analytic of Pure Practical Reason." # Expansion of Pure Reason in its Practical Use.

...be related to objects in general (whether they are perceived by the senses or not). If something is still lacking, it is the condition for applying these categories—and specifically the concept of **causality** <term>causality: the principle that every change has a cause</term>—to objects. This condition is **intuition** <term>intuition: in philosophy, the immediate way our mind grasps an object, like seeing a shape</term>. Where this intuition is not provided, it is impossible to apply the categories for the purpose of gaining theoretical knowledge of an object as a **noumenon** <term>noumenon: a thing as it is in itself, beyond what our senses can perceive</term>. Therefore, if anyone dares to attempt such an application (as was done in the *Critique of Pure Reason*), it is <margin>10</margin> strictly forbidden. Nevertheless, the objective reality of the concept remains; it can still be used in relation to **noumena** <note>original: "Noumenen", the plural of noumenon</note>, but without being able to define this concept theoretically in the slightest or to produce knowledge by doing so. 

It was already proved that this concept contains nothing impossible, even in relation to an object of the mind. This was done by securing its place in the **pure understanding** <term>pure understanding: the mind’s ability to form concepts without relying on sensory data</term> for every application to objects of the senses. Even if it is later applied to **things-in-themselves** <margin>20</margin> (which cannot be objects of experience) and is thus unable to be defined into a representation of a specific object for the sake of theoretical knowledge, it could still be capable of being defined for some other purpose (perhaps a **practical** <term>practical: relating to action, ethics, and the will</term> one). This would not be possible if, according to Hume, this concept of causality contained something that is impossible to think of under any circumstances.

To now discover this condition for applying the concept in question to noumena, <margin>30</margin> we only need to look back at why we are not satisfied with applying it to objects of experience, but also wish to use it for things-in-themselves. It soon becomes clear that it is not a theoretical purpose, but a **practical intention**, which makes this a necessity for us. Even if we succeeded in using it for **speculation** <term>speculation: theoretical thinking that does not rely on physical evidence</term>, <insert>[55]</insert> we would not make any true gain in our knowledge of nature or in regard to any objects that might be given <margin>40</margin> to us. At most, we would take a long step away from what is sensory-conditioned <note>that is, things we can actually see, touch, or measure</note> (where we ought to stay and...

He distinguishes between using reason to understand the physical world (theory) and using it to guide our actions (practice).</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the argument regarding the leap from sensible experience to the supersensible. It shifts focus from how we know things (theoretical reason) to how we act (practical reason), specifically how the concept of a "cause" applies to the human will.</meta>

### First Part, Book I, Chapter 1. II.

(...since we already have enough to do diligently tracing the chain of causes) to the supersensible, in order to <note>original: "a) und", noting a variation in early editions where "and" was used</note> complete and limit our knowledge from the side of reasons. However, an infinite gap would always remain unfilled between that limit and what we know, and we would have listened more to a vain curiosity than to a thorough desire for knowledge.

But besides the relationship in which the understanding stands to objects (in theoretical knowledge), it also has a relationship to the faculty of desire, which is therefore called the **will**. This is called a **pure will** insofar as the pure understanding (which in such a case is called **reason**) is practical through the mere representation of a law. The objective reality of a pure will or—what is the same thing—of a pure practical reason, is <margin>10</margin> given as it were through a fact in the moral law **a priori** <term>a priori: knowledge that is independent of experience, such as "2+2=4" or moral principles</term>; for one can call a determination of the will "a fact" when it is unavoidable, even though it does not rest on empirical <note>empirical: based on observation or sensory experience</note> principles. In the concept of a will, however, the concept of **causality** <term>causality: the relationship between a cause and its effect</term> is already contained. Consequently, in the concept of a pure will, there is the concept of a causality with **freedom**—that is, a causality that cannot be determined according to the laws of nature. This freedom is therefore not capable of being proven by any empirical intuition <margin>20</margin> <note>original: "b) Realität", referring specifically to the reality of the pure, free will</note> as evidence of its reality. Nevertheless, the pure practical law *a priori* perfectly justifies its objective reality, though (as is easy to see) not for the purpose of the theoretical, but merely for the practical use of reason. 

Now, the concept of a being that has a free will is the concept of a **cause in the realm of the noumenon** <note>original: "causa noumenon", a cause that exists as a thing-in-itself rather than a physical appearance</note>. One is already assured that this concept does not contradict itself by the fact that the concept of a cause—originating entirely from the pure understanding—has its objective reality <margin>30</margin> <note>original: "c) in Ansehung", some versions like Hartenstein's read "reality according to"</note> secured in regard to objects in general through the **deduction** <term>deduction: the logical proof showing how our mental concepts can validly apply to the world</term>. Furthermore, because this concept is, by its very origin, independent of all sensory...

a) 1st and 4th–6th editions: "and".
b) namely, of the pure (free) will.
c) Hartenstein edition: "reality according to".

<summary>Kant explains that while we cannot "see" freedom in the physical world, the moral law acts as a "fact" of reason that proves our will can act as a cause independent of nature. He introduces the "causa noumenon" to describe a being that acts with free will.</summary>

# Expansion of Pure Reason in Practical Use

Because, however, no intuition—which can at all times only be sensory—can be placed underneath this application, the **noumenal cause** <term>noumenal cause <note>original: "causa noumenon"</note>: a cause that belongs to the world of things-as-they-are in themselves, rather than things as they appear to us</term> is, in respect to the theoretical use of reason, [56] although a possible, thinkable concept, nonetheless an empty one. Now, however, I do not demand by this to theoretically know the constitution of a being insofar as it has a **pure will**; it is enough for me to designate it as such, and thus only to connect the concept of **causality** with that of **freedom** (and what is inseparable from it, with the **moral law** as its determining ground); which authority certainly belongs to me by virtue of the pure, non-empirical origin of the concept of cause, in that I consider myself authorized to make no other use of it than in relation to the **moral law**, which determines its reality, i.e., to make only a **practical use**.

Had I, like Hume <note>David Hume (1711–1776): A Scottish philosopher who famously argued that we cannot actually see "cause and effect," only one thing happening after another, leading to skepticism about the laws of nature</note>, taken away objective reality from the concept of **causality** in theoretical <gloss>d) Kant wrote "practical"; corrected to "theoretical" by Schöndörffer.</gloss> use, not only in respect to things in themselves (the supersensible) but also in respect to the objects of the senses, then it would have lost all meaning and been declared a theoretically impossible concept as entirely unusable; and since one can make no use of nothing, the practical use of a theoretically-void concept would have been completely absurd. Now, however, the concept of an empirically unconditioned **causality** is indeed theoretically empty (without an intuition suiting it), but yet always remains possible and refers to an undetermined object; and instead of this, meaning is given to it in the **moral law**, and consequently in a practical relation, so—

<summary>Kant explains that while we cannot "see" or theoretically prove a cause that exists beyond the physical world, we are justified in using the concept of a "noumenal cause" to describe human freedom and the moral will. He distinguishes his view from David Hume’s skepticism, arguing that morality provides the practical reality that theoretical observation lacks.</summary>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous section where Kant justifies using concepts like "cause" for things we cannot see (the supersensible). He is explaining that while we can't use these concepts to "know" God or the soul in a scientific sense, we are required to use them to make sense of our moral lives.</meta>

### First Part, Book I, Chapter 1. II.

I indeed have no **intuition** <note>intuition (Anschauung): in Kant’s philosophy, this refers to direct sensory perception. We cannot "see" or "sense" the soul or God.</note> that could determine its **objective theoretical reality**, but it nonetheless has real application, which can be presented **in concrete form** in dispositions or maxims; that is, it has a **practical reality** that can be specified. This is sufficient to justify the concept, even in regard to **noumena** <term>noumena <note>original: "Noumenen"</note>: things as they exist in themselves, beyond the reach of our physical senses</term>.

But this objective reality of a **pure concept of the understanding** <note>concepts of the understanding: the basic mental categories (like cause and effect) that we use to process information</note>, once introduced into the field of the **supersensible**, now gives all the other **categories** objective reality as well—though always only insofar as they stand in necessary connection with the **determining ground of the pure will** (the **moral law**). However, this reality is nothing other than a merely **practically applicable reality**. Meanwhile, this has not the slightest influence on our **theoretical knowledge** of these objects—as an insight into their nature through pure reason—to expand that knowledge.

As we will find in the following sections, these categories always relate only to beings as **intelligences** <note>intelligences: rational beings capable of making moral choices</note>, and even then, they relate only to the <margin>[87]</margin> **relation of reason to the will**. Therefore, they always relate only to the **practical** and do not claim any further knowledge of these beings. Whatever other properties associated with them—belonging to the theoretical way of representing such supersensible things—might be brought in, these are collectively not to be counted as **knowledge**. Instead, they are counted only as the **right** (and in a practical intent, even the **necessity**) to assume and presuppose them. This holds true even where one **assumes** <note>original: "annimmt"</note> <gloss>a) "assumes," an addition by Hartenstein <note>Gustav Hartenstein (1808–1890): a philosopher and editor of Kant's collected works.</note></gloss> **supersensible beings** (such as **God**) according to an **analogy**—that is, according to the pure relation of reason, which we use practically in regard to sensory things. In this way, by applying reason to the supersensible (but only with a **practical intent**), we do not give the slightest encouragement for pure theoretical reason to **wander into the transcendent** <note>original: "Schwärmen ins Überschwengliche." This refers to "fanaticism" or "extravagance," where the mind tries to claim knowledge of things it cannot possibly prove.</note>.

<summary>Kant argues that while we cannot "know" the spiritual or supersensible world through science or logic, we are justified in "assuming" its reality to make sense of our moral duty. This application of logic to the supersensible is strictly for practical behavior, not for wild speculation.</summary>

<keywords>practical reality, noumena, categories, moral law, pure will, supersensible, God, Hartenstein, theoretical reason, analogy</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general application of "concepts of the understanding" (like cause and effect) to their specific role in ethics. Having established that we can use these concepts to think about the "supersensible" world of the will, Kant now defines what it actually means for something to be an "object" of our moral choices.</meta>

->On the Concept of an Object of Pure Practical Reason.<-

### Analytic of Practical Reason
## Chapter Two.
### On the Concept of an Object of Pure Practical Reason.

By a concept of an objectᵃ) of practical reason, I understand the representation of an object as an effect that is possible through **freedom**. To be an object of practical knowledge as such simply means the relationship of the <margin>10</margin> **will** to the action by which that object (or its opposite) would be made real. Therefore, judging whether something is an object of pure practical reason or not is merely a matter of distinguishing whether it is possible or impossible to **will** the action. This is the action through which—if we had the physical power (which must be judged by experience)—a certain object would become reality.

If we assume the object is the **determining ground** <term>determining ground <note>original: "Bestimmungsgrund"</note>: the underlying reason, motive, or "trigger" that moves the will to act</term> of our **faculty of desire** <note>faculty of desire: the part of our mind that seeks to make things happen or obtain things based on our wants</note>, then the physical <margin>20</margin> possibility of that object (through the free use of our own powers) must be decided *before* we can judge whether it is an "object of practical reason."

On the other hand, if the **law a priori** <note>a priori: a law that is known through reason alone, independent of any sensory experience</note> can be considered the determining ground of the action—meaning the action is determined by pure practical reason—then the judgment of whether something is an object of pure practical reason is entirely independent of any comparison with our physical power. [58] <margin>30</margin> In this case, the only question is whether we are **permitted** to will an action that aims at the existence of an object, provided it were in our power. Consequently, the **moral possibility** of the action must come first; for here, the object is not the motive...

---
ᵃ) The phrase "of an object" <note>original: "eines Gegenstandes"</note> is missing in Kant’s original text and was added by the editors; scholars v. Aster, Natorp, and Schöndörffer suggest it should read: "to the concept of an object."

<summary>Kant defines an "object of practical reason" as something we can bring about through our free will, arguing that in morality, we must first determine if an action is "morally possible" (permissible) before worrying about whether it is physically possible.</summary>

...but rather the law of the will is the determining ground of that action.

The sole objects of a practical reason are therefore those of the **Good** and the **Evil**. For by the former, one understands a necessary object of the **faculty of desire** <note>the capacity to want or seek something</note>, and by the latter, a necessary object of the **faculty of aversion** <note>the capacity to avoid or feel repulsed by something</note>; both, however, are judged according to a principle of reason.

If the concept of the Good is not to be derived from a pre-
<margin>10</margin> ceding practical law, but is instead to serve as the foundation for it, then it can only be the concept of something whose existence promises **pleasure** and thus determines the causality of the subject toward its production—that is, it determines the faculty of desire. Now, because it is impossible to see *a priori* <note>original: "a priori"; meaning to know something through reason alone, before having any sensory experience of it</note> which representation will be accompanied by pleasure and which, by contrast, will be accompanied by displeasure, it would depend entirely on experience to decide what is immediately good or evil. The property 
<margin>20</margin> of the subject, in relation to which alone this experience can be conducted, is the feeling of pleasure and displeasure, acting as a **receptivity** <note>the mind's capacity to be affected by sensations</note> belonging to the internal sense. Thus, the concept of what is immediately good would only refer to that with which the sensation of enjoyment is directly connected, and the concept of the "absolutely evil" would have to refer solely to that which directly excites pain. 

However, this is already contrary to common linguistic usage, which distinguishes the "pleasant" from the "good," and the
<margin>30</margin> "unpleasant" from the "evil." Language requires that Good and Evil be judged at all times by reason, and therefore by concepts that can be universally communicated, rather than by mere sensation, which is limited to individual subjects <insert>a)</insert> <note>original: "Subjekte"; the OCR notes Kant originally wrote "Objekte" (objects), but the editor Natorp corrected it to "Subjekte" (subjects) to match the meaning of individual people</note> and their specific susceptibility. Nevertheless, since a feeling of pleasure or displeasure cannot be immediately connected to any representation of an object *a priori* by itself, the philosopher who felt compelled 
<margin>40</margin> to base his practical judgment on a feeling of pleasure would call "good" what is a...

<meta>This page continues Kant’s investigation into the "Determining Ground" of the will. On the previous page, we saw that an action is moral only if it is determined by a law of reason rather than a desire for a physical object. Here, Kant begins to define the only two true "objects" of moral reasoning: Good and Evil.</meta>

->### On the Concept of an Object of Pure Practical Reason.<-

...means to what is pleasant, and **Evil**, which is the cause of unpleasantness and pain; for the assessment of the relationship between means and ends certainly belongs to **reason** <term>Vernunft; the human capacity to act according to principles rather than just instinct</term>. Although reason alone is capable of understanding the connection between means and their intended purposes (so that one could even define the **will** as the faculty of ends, since ends are always the determining grounds of the **faculty of desire** according to principles), [59] yet the practical **maxims** <note>personal rules of conduct</note> that followed from the above concept of the Good—merely as a <margin>10</margin> means—would never contain anything good in itself. Instead, they would only ever contain something that is "good for" some purpose as the object of the will. In this case, the Good would always be merely the **useful**, and that for which it is useful would always have to lie outside the will, within sensation. If this sensation, as a pleasant feeling, had to be distinguished from the concept of the Good, then there would be nothing immediately good at all; instead, the Good would only be sought in the means to something else—namely, some form <margin>20</margin> of pleasantness.

It is an old formula of the **Schools** <note>referring to Medieval Scholastic philosophy</note>: *we desire nothing except under the guise of the good, and we turn away from nothing except under the guise of the evil* <note>original Latin: "nihil appetimus nisi sub ratione boni, nihil aversamur nisi sub ratione mali"</note>; and it is often used correctly, but it is also often very disadvantageous to philosophy. This is because the expressions for *the good* <note>original Latin: "boni"</note> and *the evil* <note>original Latin: "mali"</note> contain an ambiguity for which the limitations of language are to blame. Because of these limitations, the words are capable of a double meaning, which inevitably <margin>30</margin> places practical laws on shaky ground <note>literally "on screws," meaning unstable or precarious</note>. Philosophy—which, in using these words, is well aware of the difference in concept despite using the same term, but still cannot find any specific expressions for them—is forced into subtle distinctions about which one cannot later agree, because the difference could not be directly designated by any appropriate expression.*)

***

*) Furthermore, the expression *under the guise of the good* <note>original Latin: "sub ratione boni"</note> is also ambiguous. For it can mean: we represent something to ourselves as good if and because we desire (will) it; but it can also mean: we desire something because we [represent it to ourselves as good...]

<summary>Kant argues that if "the Good" is defined only as a means to pleasure, then nothing is good in itself, only useful. He introduces a famous Latin philosophical maxim to show how the limitations of language often confuse sensory pleasure with moral goodness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kant’s analysis of how we define "Good" and "Evil." On the previous page, he introduced an old Latin formula regarding how we desire things. Here, he highlights a specific linguistic advantage of the German language that allows for a clearer distinction between moral worth and physical pleasure.</meta>

# First Part, Book I, Second Chapter.

The German language is fortunate to possess expressions which do not allow this difference to be overlooked. For that which the Latins name with a single word *the good* <note>original Latin: "bonum"</note>, German has two very different concepts and likewise very different expressions: for *bonum* it has **the Good** <term>das Gute; refers to moral excellence</term> and **Well-being** <term>das Wohl; refers to physical comfort or happiness</term>; for *the evil* <note>original Latin: "malum"</note> it has **the Evil** <term>das Böse; refers to moral wickedness</term> and **the Ill** <term>das Übel; refers to physical pain or misfortune</term> (or **Woe**). Consequently, <margin>[60]</margin> it involves two entirely different judgments whether we consider **the Good** and **Evil** of an action, <margin>10</margin> or our **Well-being** and **Woe** (**Ill**). From this it follows already that the aforementioned psychological proposition is at least still very uncertain if it is translated thus: "we desire nothing except with regard to our **well-being** or **woe**"; whereas, if it is presented thus: "we will nothing by the direction of **reason** <term>Vernunft; the capacity to act on principles</term> except insofar as we hold it to be **good** or **evil**," it is expressed with indubitable certainty and at the same time with total clarity.

**Well-being** or **Ill** always signifies only a <margin>20</margin> relation to our state of **pleasantness** or **unpleasantness**, of pleasure and pain; and if we therefore desire or detest an object, it happens only insofar as it is related to our **sensibility** <note>the capacity to receive impressions through the senses</note> and the feeling of pleasure and displeasure that it effects. **The Good** or **the Evil**, however, always signifies a relation to the **will** insofar as the will is determined by the **law of reason** to make something its object. For the will is never determined immediately by the object and its representation; rather, the will is <margin>20</margin> a faculty that makes a rule of reason into the motivating cause of an action (through which an object can become real). **The Good** or **the Evil** is thus properly applied to actions, not to the sensat-

---

...we represent it to ourselves as good, so that either the desire is the determining ground of the concept of the object as a good one, or the concept of the good is the determining ground of the desire (the will); in the former case, the phrase **under the guise of the good** <note>original Latin: "sub ratione boni"</note> would mean: we will something under the idea of the good; in the second: as a result of this idea, which must precede the willing as its determining ground.

<summary>Kant distinguishes between moral "Good/Evil" (related to reason and the will) and physical "Well-being/Ill" (related to pleasure and pain), arguing that German’s specific vocabulary prevents confusing these two concepts.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kant’s exploration of the distinction between moral "Good" and "Evil" versus physical "Well-being" and "Ill." ### On the Concept of an Object of Pure Practical Reason.

state of the person, and if something were to be considered or held to be **good** or **evil** absolutely (in every intention and without further condition), it would refer only to the way of acting, the **maxim** <term>a subjective principle or rule that a person follows</term> of the will, and therefore the acting **person** themselves as a good or evil person, but not to a **thing** that could be so named.

One might always laugh at the **Stoic** <term>a follower of the ancient Greek school of philosophy that taught that virtue is the only good and pain is not an evil</term> who, in the most intense pains of gout, cried out: "Pain, you may torture me as much as you like, I will never admit that you are something **evil**!" <note>original Greek: κακόν [kakon], Latin: malum</note>. He was, after all, right. It was an **ill** <term>das Übel; a physical misfortune or unpleasant state</term>; he felt that, and his cries betrayed it. But he had no reason at all to admit that any **evil** <term>das Böse; a moral wickedness</term> attached to him because of it; for the pain does not diminish <margin>ᵃ</margin> the value of his person in the least, but only the value of his state. A single lie of which he had been conscious would have had to cast down his courage; but the pain served only as an occasion to elevate it, when he was conscious that he had not deserved it <margin>ᵇ</margin> through any unjust action and had thereby made himself worthy of punishment.

What we are to call **good** must be, in the judgment of every rational human being, an object of the **faculty of desire**; and **evil** <margin>[61]</margin> must be, in everyone's eyes, an object of aversion. Consequently, besides the senses, this judgment requires **reason**. This is the case with **truthfulness** in contrast to lying, and with **justice** in contrast to violence, and so on. We can, however, call something an **ill** which everyone must nonetheless simultaneously declare to be **good**, sometimes indirectly, sometimes even directly <margin>ᶜ</margin>. One who undergoes a surgical operation feels it, without doubt, as an **ill**; but through reason, he and everyone else declares it to be **good**. If, however, someone who likes to tease and trouble peace-loving people,

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the distinction between the moral "good" (das Gute) and physical "well-being" (das Wohl). # First Part, Book I, Chapter 2.

...finally runs up <note>The context suggests a person who provokes or annoys others until they face consequences.</note> and is dispatched with a hearty dose of blows; this is certainly an **ill** <term>das Übel; a physical misfortune or pain</term>, but everyone gives their approval and considers it **good** in itself, even if nothing further were to come of it. Indeed, even the one who receives the blows must recognize in his **reason** <term>Vernunft; the faculty of principles</term> that justice has been done to him, because he sees the **proportion** between well-being and good conduct—which reason inevitably holds before him—brought precisely into practice here.

<margin>10</margin> To be sure, a great deal depends on our **well-being** and **woe** in the judgment of our practical reason, and, as far as our nature as sensible beings is concerned, everything depends on our **happiness** <term>Glückseligkeit; a state of lasting contentment and satisfaction with one’s condition</term>. This is true if happiness is judged—as reason especially demands—not by fleeting **sensation** <term>Empfindung</term>, but by the influence that this chance event has on our entire existence and our **satisfaction** with it. But not everything altogether depends on this. Man is a needy being
<margin>20</margin> insofar as he belongs to the **world of sense** <term>the physical world perceived through our senses</term>, and to that extent, his reason certainly has an undeniable mandate from the side of **sensibility** <term>Sinnlichkeit; our capacity for feelings, desires, and perceptions</term> to concern itself with those interests and to form practical **maxims** <term>subjective rules of conduct</term> also in view of the happiness of this life and, if possible, of a future one. 

But he is not so much an animal as to be indifferent to everything that reason says for itself, and to use reason merely as a **tool** for the satisfaction of his needs as a sensory being. 
<margin>30</margin> For his possession of reason does not raise him in value above mere **animality** <term>Tierheit</term> at all if it is only to serve him for the sake of what **instinct** performs for animals; reason would then be only a particular **manner** <term>Manier; a specific method or style</term> that nature used to equip man for the same **purpose** for which it destined animals, without destining him for any higher purpose. 

<margin>[62]</margin> According to this **natural arrangement** <term>Naturanstalt</term> once established for him, he certainly requires reason to consider his well-being and woe at all times;
<margin>40</margin> but he also possesses it for a higher **calling** <term>Beruf; a vocation or destination</term>, namely, to consider also that which is in itself good or evil, and about which pure reason—not at all interested in sensory matters—judges...

<summary>Kant argues that human reason is not merely a tool for achieving physical happiness or satisfying animal instincts, but has a higher "calling" to determine moral good and evil. He uses the example of a deserved punishment to show that physical pain (an ill) can be recognized as morally right (a good) through the "proportion" of conduct to well-being.</summary>
<keywords>practical reason, well-being vs woe, happiness, sensibility, animality, instinct, moral calling, Immanuel Kant, moral law</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into how reason judges the concepts of "good" and "evil." On the previous page, Kant noted that while man is a sensible being with needs (well-being), reason has a higher calling. This page clarifies that for a judgment to be truly moral, the "good" must be the supreme condition that overrides mere physical "well-being."</meta>

# On the Concept of an Object of Pure Practical Reason.

...to be able to judge for oneself, not only to take this into consideration, but to distinguish this assessment entirely from the other <note>meaning the assessment of physical well-being</note> and to make it the supreme condition of the <insert>good and evil</insert> <note>original Latin: "sc. des Guten und Bösen"; "sc." is an abbreviation for *scilicet*, meaning "namely" or "to wit"</note> latter.

In this assessment of what is **good** and **evil** in itself, as distinct from what can only be called such relatively—in terms of **well-being** <term>Wohl; physical comfort or happiness</term> or **woe** <term>Übel; physical pain or misfortune</term>—the following points are crucial. Either a **principle of reason** <term>Vernunftprinzip; a rule the mind follows</term> is already thought of in itself as the **determining ground** <term>Bestimmungsgrund; the actual motive or cause</term> of the **will** 

<margin>10</margin> without regard to possible objects of the **faculty of desire** <term>Begehrungsvermögen; our ability to want things</term> (that is, merely through the lawful form of the **maxim** <term>a personal rule of action</term>); in that case, that principle is a **practical law** *a priori* <term>prior to and independent of any physical experience</term> and **pure reason** is assumed to be practical of itself. The law then determines the will directly; the action according to it is **good in itself**. A will whose maxim always conforms to this law is **absolutely good** in every respect and is the supreme condition of all good. 

<margin>20</margin> Or, a determining ground of the faculty of desire precedes the maxim of the will, which presupposes an object of **pleasure** and **displeasure**, and consequently something that pleases or hurts. In this case, the maxim of reason—to promote the former and avoid the latter—determines the actions as they are good only **relatively** to our **inclination** <term>Neigung; a habitual desire or liking</term>. They are thus only **indirectly** good (regarded as a **means** to another end), and these maxims can then never be called **laws**, but may still be called **rational practical precepts**. 

<margin>30</margin> The **end** itself, the pleasure we seek, is in the latter case not a "good," but a "**well-being**"; it is not a concept of reason, but an **empirical concept** <term>a concept derived from experience or the senses</term> of an object of **sensation**. However, the use of the means to achieve it—that is, the action—is still called "good" (because rational reflection is required for it), but not absolutely. It is only good in relation to our **sensibility** <term>Sinnlichkeit; our capacity for physical feeling</term> regarding its feeling of pleasure and displeasure. But the will whose maxim is affected by this 

<margin>40</margin> is not a **pure will**, which is directed only toward that which...

<note type="footnote">a) namely, of the good and evil. Scholars Nolte and Natorp suggest the reading "of the" [instead of "of the latter"].<br>Kant, Critique of Practical Reason.</note>

<summary>Kant distinguishes between acting out of moral duty (the "good") and acting for the sake of physical pleasure ("well-being"). He argues that a truly moral will is determined by a rational law (a maxim) independently of our physical desires or feelings of pleasure and pain.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the will is determined. ...whereby **pure reason** can be practical for itself.

Now is the place to explain the **paradox of method** in a **Critique of Practical Reason**: <margin>[63]</margin> namely, that the **concept of good and evil** must not be determined *before* the **moral law** (to which it <note>the concept of good and evil</note> <insert>it</insert> <note>The original German "es" was corrected to "dem" (the/that) by the editor Hartenstein</note> would seemingly even have to serve as a foundation), but only (as occurs here) *after* the law and *through* the law. 

<margin>10</margin> Even if we did not know that the principle of **morality** <term>Sittlichkeit; the quality of being in accord with high standards of right and wrong</term> was a pure law determining the **will** *a priori* <term>independent of any personal experience or sensory input</term>, we would still—to avoid assuming principles without cause (*gratis*)—have to leave it undecided at first whether the **will** has merely empirical <note>based on experience or observation</note> or also pure *a priori* **determining grounds** <term>Bestimmungsgründe; the underlying reasons or motives that move the will to act</term>. For it is against all fundamental rules of **philosophical procedure** to assume the very thing we are meant to decide upon as having already been decided. 

<margin>20</margin> Suppose we wanted to begin with the **concept of the good** in order to derive the laws of the will from it. This concept of an **object** (as a "good" thing) would simultaneously identify that object as the sole **determining ground** of the will. Because this concept would have no **practical law** *a priori* as its guide <note>Hartenstein's second edition suggests "would have had" (hätte) here</note>, the **touchstone** <term>Probierstein; a standard or test for quality</term> of what is **good or evil** could be placed in nothing other than the agreement of the object with our feeling of **pleasure or displeasure**. 

<margin>30</margin> The use of **reason** could then only consist in two things: partly in determining this pleasure or displeasure in connection with all the sensations of my **existence** <term>Dasein; my state of being</term>, and partly in determining the means to acquire the object of that pleasure. Now, since what agrees with the feeling of pleasure can only be settled through **experience**, and yet the **practical law** is supposed to be based on this as a condition, the very possibility of practical laws *a priori* would be ruled out. This is because one would first find it necessary...

<summary>Kant explains his "paradox of method," arguing that the moral law must define what is "good," rather than a pre-existing idea of "the good" defining the law. If we started with "the good," morality would be reduced to a mere search for pleasure based on experience.</summary>
<keywords>paradox of method, moral law, good and evil, a priori, determining grounds, pleasure and displeasure, practical reason, Hartenstein</keywords>

### On the Concept of an Object of Pure Practical Reason

->6*<-

...thought they had to find an object for the **will** <term>Wille; the faculty of choice or the power of the mind to act according to principles</term>, the concept of which—as something "good"—would constitute the universal, though empirical <note>based on sensory experience rather than pure reason</note>, **determining ground** <term>Bestimmungsgrund; the underlying motive or reason that moves the will to act</term> of the will. However, it was first necessary to investigate whether there was not also a **determining ground** of the will *prior to experience* <note>original: "a priori"</note> (which would never have been found anywhere else than in a pure practical law, specifically insofar as this law prescribes the mere legal form to our **maxims** <term>Maximen; subjective rules of conduct that an individual chooses to follow</term> without regard to any specific object).

<margin>10</margin> Because, however, they had already placed an object—according to concepts of **good and evil**—at the foundation of every practical law, and because that object could only be thought of according to empirical concepts (without a preceding law), they had already deprived themselves in advance of the possibility of even thinking of a pure practical law. On the contrary, had they first analytically investigated the latter <note>the pure practical law</note>, they would have found <margin>[64]</margin> that it is not the concept of the **good** as an object that determines the **moral law**, but <margin>20</margin> conversely, the **moral law** that first determines and makes possible the concept of the **good**, insofar as it absolutely deserves this name.

This remark, which concerns only the method of the highest moral investigations, is of great importance. It explains all at once the primary reason for all the errors of philosophers regarding the supreme principle of **morality**. For they sought an object of the will in order to make it the matter and the ground of a law (which <margin>30</margin> was then supposed to be the determining ground of the will not immediately, but by means of that object being brought to a feeling of pleasure or displeasure). Instead, they should have first searched for a law that *prior to experience* <note>original: "a priori"</note> and immediately determined the will, and only then determined the object in accordance with that will. Now, whether they placed this object of pleasure—which was to provide the highest concept of the **good**—in **happiness**, in **perfection**, in **moral feeling** <margin>40</margin> <note>original footnote 'a': The author Kant originally wrote "Laws" (Gesezte); this was corrected to "Feeling" by the editor Hartenstein to match the context.</note>, or in the **will of God**, their...

<summary>Kant explains his "paradox of method," arguing that the moral law must be established first to define what is "good," rather than using a pre-existing idea of "goodness" to create the law. He suggests that previous philosophers failed because they tried to base morality on objects of desire like happiness or God's will instead of the law itself.</summary>

<keywords>moral law, the good, will, determining ground, a priori, maxims, happiness, perfection, moral feeling, methodology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kant’s analysis of the "paradox of method" in moral philosophy. It explains why starting with a "good object" instead of a "formal law" leads to errors in both ancient and modern ethics.</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>
<header>Part One, Book I, Chapter 2.</header>

...principle was always **heteronomy** <term>Heteronomie; when the will is guided by external factors like desires or objects, rather than by its own rational law</term>. They were inevitably forced to encounter empirical conditions for a moral law, because they could only label their object "good" or "evil" based on its immediate relation to **feeling** (which is always based on experience), by making that object the immediate **determining ground** <term>Bestimmungsgrund; the underlying motive or reason for an action</term> of the will. Only a **formal law**—that is, one which prescribes nothing more to reason than the form of its own universal **legislation** as the supreme condition of **maxims** <term>Maximen; the personal rules or principles we set for ourselves</term>—can be a determining ground of practical reason *prior to experience* <note>original: "a priori"</note>.

<margin>10</margin> The ancient philosophers, meanwhile, openly revealed this error by focusing their moral investigations entirely on defining the concept of the **highest good**. They intended to make this "highest good" an object that would serve as the determining ground of the will in the moral law. However, such an object can only be presented to a will much later—only after the moral law has first been proven on its own and justified as the immediate determining ground of the will. We intend to undertake the study of this object <margin>20</margin> (which is presented to a will already determined *prior to experience* according to its form) in the **Dialectic of Pure Practical Reason** <note>The "Dialectic" is a later section of this book where Kant addresses the apparent contradictions in our pursuit of the highest good</note>. 

The modern thinkers, among whom the question of the **highest good** seems to have fallen out of use or at least become a secondary matter, hide the aforementioned error behind vague words (as they do in many other cases). Nevertheless, one can see this error peeking through their systems, as it reveals <margin>30</margin> **heteronomy** of practical reason everywhere. From such a foundation, a moral law that commands universally and *prior to experience* <note>original: "a priori"</note> can never arise.

Since the concepts of **good and evil** (as consequences of the determination of the will *prior to experience*) also presuppose a pure practical principle—and thus a causality of pure reason—they do not originally refer to objects in the same way that the pure concepts of the understanding or **categories** <term>Kategorien; the fundamental concepts, like cause and effect, that our minds use to organize information</term> do. In theoretical reason, categories relate to the **synthetic unity** <note>the way the mind stitches together different pieces of sensory information</note> of the **manifold** of given **intuitions** <margin>40</margin> within a consciousness. Those theoretical concepts assume that objects are given to us; the concepts of good and evil, however, are instead **modes** <term>Modi; specific variations or forms of a general concept</term> of a single...

<summary>Kant argues that ancient and modern philosophers failed to find a universal moral law because they started with an object (the "highest good") rather than the form of the law itself. He asserts that the moral law must come before the definition of "good" to ensure it is based on reason rather than shifting human feelings.</summary>

...single category, namely that of **causality**, provided that the determining ground of this causality consists in the **rational representation** of its own law. This law, as a **law of freedom**, is one which reason gives to itself, thereby proving itself *prior to experience* <note>original: "a priori"</note> to be practical. Since, however, actions on the one hand fall under a law that is not a law of nature but a law of freedom—consequently belonging to the conduct of **intelligible beings** <term>intelligibeler Wesen; rational beings as they are in themselves, independent of physical constraints</term>—but on the other hand, as events <margin>10</margin> in the **world of sense**, also belong to **appearances**, the determinations of a practical reason can only occur in relation to the latter. Thus, they occur in accordance with the categories of the understanding, but not for the purpose of **theoretical use** (which seeks to bring the variety of sensory intuition under a single consciousness *prior to experience*). Instead, they serve only to subject the **manifold of desires** <term>Begehrungen; the various and often conflicting wants or impulses we feel</term> to the unity of consciousness of a practical reason that commands through the **moral law**, or to a **pure will** <margin>20</margin> *prior to experience*.

These **categories of freedom**—for so we shall name them to distinguish them from those theoretical concepts known as **categories of nature**—have an obvious advantage over the latter. While the categories of nature are merely **forms of thought** which use general concepts to designate objects in an indeterminate way for any possible intuition, these categories of freedom are directed toward the determination of a **free choice** <term>freie Willkür; the capacity to act or refrain from acting based on rational principles</term>. Although no intuition can be given that perfectly corresponds <margin>30</margin> to this choice, it is grounded in a pure practical law *prior to experience*. This is a unique situation that never occurs with concepts in the theoretical use of our **cognitive faculty**. As practical elementary concepts, instead of using the form of intuition (**space and time**) which does not reside in reason itself but must be taken from elsewhere (namely from **sensibility**), they have the **form of a pure will** as given within reason—and thus within the **faculty of thought** itself. Because of this, it happens that in all precepts of pure <margin>40</margin> practical reason, we are only concerned with the **determination of the will**, not with the **natural conditions** (of practical...

<summary>Kant introduces the "Categories of Freedom," which are the conceptual tools reason uses to organize human desires and choices under the moral law. Unlike the categories of nature (like space and time), these are rooted directly in the structure of the pure will itself.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Table of the Categories of Freedom" from page 86. Having listed the categories, Kant now explains how they function as a bridge between the abstract moral law and the physical actions we perform in the world of the senses.</meta>

<page-num>87</page-num>
<header>Of the Typic of Pure Practical Judgment.</header>
One will soon notice here that in this table [67] freedom—as a kind of **causality** <term>Kausalität; the relationship between cause and effect</term> that is not subject to **empirical determining grounds** <term>empirischen Bestimmungsgründen; factors based on experience or sensory desires</term>—is considered in regard to the actions possible through it as appearances in the **world of sense** <note>the physical world we perceive through our eyes, ears, and touch</note>. Consequently, freedom refers to the categories of their natural possibility. However, each category is taken so generally that the determining ground of that causality can also be assumed outside the world of sense, in freedom as a property of an **intelligible being** <margin>10</margin> <note>a being that exists in the realm of pure thought/reason, beyond the reach of the physical senses</note>. This continues until the categories of **modality** <note>the classification of things as possible, actual, or necessary</note> initiate the transition from practical principles in general to those of **morality** <term>Sittlichkeit; the system of ethical conduct</term>, though only tentatively <note>original: "problematisch"</note>; these can only later be presented **dogmatically** <note>original: "dogmatisch"; here meaning "as established, certain principles" rather than "stubbornly"</note> through the moral law.

I add nothing further here by way of explanation to the present table, because it is understandable enough on its own. This kind of division, composed according to principles, is very beneficial to every science, both for the sake of its <margin>20</margin> **thoroughness** as well as its **intelligibility**. For example, one knows immediately from the above table and its first entry where one must begin in practical considerations: with the **maxims** <term>Maximen; personal or subjective rules of conduct</term> which everyone bases on their **inclination** <term>Neigung; our desires, feelings, or biological urges</term>; then the prescriptions which apply to a class <note>original: "Gattung"; genus or kind</note> of rational beings insofar as they agree in certain inclinations; and finally the **law**, which applies to all regardless of their inclinations, and so forth. In this way, one oversees the entire plan of what must be achieved, including every question of practical philosophy that must be answered, <margin>30</margin> and at the same time the order that must be followed.

## First Part, Book I, Chapter 2.

...us <note>This "us" completes the sentence from the previous page regarding whether an action is possible for us.</note> in the realm of **sensibility** <term>Sinnlichkeit; our capacity to perceive things through the senses</term> as a possible **action**, whether it falls under the **rule** or not; for this, practical **judgment** <term>Urtheilskraft; the mental faculty of deciding whether something specific fits into a general rule</term> is required. Through this faculty, that which was stated in the **rule** generally (in the abstract <note>original: "in abstracto"</note>) is applied to an **action** in the concrete <note>original: "in concreto"</note>. However, a **practical rule** of pure **reason** firstly, as something practical, concerns the **existence** of an **object**; and secondly, as a practical **rule** of pure **reason**,
<margin>10</margin> it carries with it **necessity** regarding the **existence** of the **action**. It is, therefore, a practical **law** <margin>[68]</margin>—and specifically not a **law of nature** through empirical **determining grounds** <note>factors based on sensory experience or desires</note>, but rather a **law of freedom**. According to this law, the **will** is to be determinable independently of everything empirical (solely through the representation of a **law** in general and its **form**). Yet, all occurring **cases** of possible **actions** can only be **empirical**, that is, belonging to **experience** and **nature**. Therefore, it seems absurd to want to find a **case** in the **world of the senses** which,
<margin>20</margin> although it is always subject only to the **law of nature**, would nevertheless allow the **application** of a **law of freedom** to it, and to which the **supersensible idea of the morally** good—which is meant to be exhibited therein in the concrete—could be applied. Thus, the **judgment of pure practical reason** is subject to the very same **difficulties** as that of pure theoretical reason. The latter, however, had a **means** at hand to escape these: namely, since in its **theoretical use** it relied on **intuitions** <term>Anschauungen; the immediate way our minds receive sensory data</term>,
<margin>30</margin> to which pure **concepts of the understanding** could be applied. Such **intuitions** (even if only of **objects of the senses**) can nonetheless be given independently of experience <note>original: "a priori"</note>. Therefore, regarding the connection of the **manifold** <note>the various pieces of data received by the senses</note> within them, they can be provided in accordance with the pure **concepts of the understanding** independently of experience (as **schemata** <note>original: "Schemate"; a mental bridge that connects an abstract concept to a sensory image</note>). By contrast, the **morally good** is something **supersensible** <term>Übersinnliches; something existing beyond the reach of the physical senses</term> in terms of its **object**. Therefore, nothing **corresponding** to it can be found in any **sensory intuition**.
<margin>40</margin> Consequently, **judgment** under **laws of pure practical reason** seems to be subject to special **difficulties**, which are based on the fact that a **law of freedom** is to be applied to **actions** as events...

<summary>Kant explains the "problem of judgment": how can we apply an abstract, spiritual moral law (freedom) to physical, cause-and-effect actions in the material world (nature)? He notes that while theoretical reason uses "schemata" to bridge this gap, practical reason faces a harder task because moral goodness has no physical shape or sensory form.</summary>
