# Of the Typic of Pure Practical Judgment.

...events <note>This continues the sentence from page 88: "...nevertheless allow for the application of a law of freedom to events..."</note> that occur in the world of the senses and thus, to that extent, belong to nature.

However, here a favorable prospect opens up once again for pure practical judgment. In the **subsumption** <term>the process of categorizing a specific instance under a general rule</term> of an action possible for me in the world of the senses under a **pure practical law**, we are not concerned with the possibility of the action as an event in the world of the senses; for that belongs to the evaluation of the **theoretical use** of reason according to the law of **causality**—a pure concept of the understanding for which reason has a **schema** <term>a mental bridge or "map" that connects an abstract concept to a sensory image</term> in sensory intuition. Physical causality, or the condition under which it occurs, belongs among the concepts of nature, the schema of which is designed by the **transcendental imagination**.

But here, we are not dealing with the schema of a specific case according to laws, but rather with the schema (if this word is appropriate here) of a law itself; because the **determination of the will** (not the <insert>action</insert> <note>Kant originally used the masculine article "der"; corrected by the editor Hartenstein to the feminine "die" to agree with the noun "action".</note> <margin>69</margin> in relation to its success) through the law alone, without any other determining ground, binds the concept of causality to entirely different conditions than those which constitute the connection of nature.

A **schema**—that is, a universal procedure of the imagination (to present the pure concept of the understanding, which the law defines, to the senses **prior to experience** <note>original: "a priori"</note>)—must correspond to the **law of nature** as a law to which objects of sensory intuition are subject as such. But for the **law of freedom** (as a causality not conditioned by the senses at all), and thus also for the concept of the **unconditionally good**, no intuition and therefore no schema can be provided for the purpose of its application **in the concrete** <note>original: "in concreto"</note>. Consequently, the **moral law** has no cognitive faculty to mediate its application to objects of nature other than the **understanding** (not the imagination). The understanding can provide for an idea of reason not a schema of **sensibility**, but a law—yet one that can be presented **in the concrete** <note>original: "in concreto"</note> in objects of the senses, and thus a law of nature...

<summary>Kant explains that while physical actions follow laws of nature and use "schemas" of the imagination, the moral law (freedom) requires a different bridge. He suggests that the understanding provides a "law of nature" as a model to help apply abstract moral principles to real-world actions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Typic of Pure Practical Judgment" from page 89. Kant is explaining that because we cannot "see" freedom in the physical world, we must use the laws of nature as a "type" or template to test whether our moral choices (maxims) are consistent with the moral law.</meta>

<header>First Part, Book I, Second Chapter.</header>

law, but only according to its form, can be submitted as a law for the sake of the **power of judgment** <term>the ability to decide whether a specific case falls under a general rule</term>; and this we can therefore call the **type of the moral law**.

The rule of the **power of judgment** under laws of pure practical reason is this: Ask yourself whether the action you intend—if it were to occur according to a law of nature of which you yourself were a part—could you indeed regard it as possible through your 10 will? In fact, everyone judges actions by this rule to determine whether they are morally good or evil. Thus people say: "What if everyone, whenever they believed it to their advantage, allowed themselves to cheat; or felt authorized to shorten their own life as soon as a complete weariness of it <note>This refers to Kant's famous example of suicide as a violation of moral duty</note> befell them; or looked upon the needs of others with total indifference; and you belonged to such an order of things—would you indeed be there with the consent of your will?" Now, everyone knows well that if he secretly allows himself to cheat, it does not mean 20 everyone else will do so; or if he is uncharitable without being noticed, everyone would not immediately be so toward him; therefore, this comparison of the **maxim** <term>a personal rule of conduct</term> of his actions with a universal law of nature is not the determining ground of his will. But the latter is still a *type* for judging the former according to moral principles. If the maxim of the action is not such that it stands the test of the <margin>[70]</margin> form of a law of nature in general, then it is morally impossible. So judges even the 30 most common understanding; for the law of nature is always at the foundation of all its most ordinary judgments, even those of experience. It has this law, therefore, always at hand—except that in cases where **causality from freedom** is to be judged, it makes that law of nature merely a **type** of a law of freedom. It does this because, without having something at hand that it could use as an example in a case of experience, it could not provide the law of a pure practical reason with its use in application. 40

<summary>Kant explains the "Typic," a mental exercise where we test a moral action by imagining it as a universal law of nature to see if our will could still support it. This serves as a practical bridge between abstract moral laws and everyday decision-making.</summary>

### On the Typic of Pure Practical Judgment.

<meta>catchword: It</meta>

It is therefore also permitted to use the nature of the world of sense
as a **type** <term>a symbolic model or template</term> of an intelligible nature <term>the realm of reason and morality that we cannot see with our eyes</term>,
so long as I do not transfer the intuitions <note>sensory perceptions</note>,
and what depends on them, onto this intelligible nature, but 
merely relate the form of **lawfulness** in general (the 
concept of which is found even in the commonest <note>original: "reinsten" (purest); corrected to "gemeinsten" (commonest) by Hartenstein</note> use of
reason, but cannot be known as determined for any other purpose 
than merely for the pure practical use of reason *a priori* <term>independent of experience</term>). 10 
For laws as such are the same in this regard, regardless of where 
they take their determining grounds from.

Moreover, since absolutely nothing of the intelligible world can be 
known except (by means of the moral law) **freedom**, and even this 
only insofar as it is an inseparable presupposition of that law; and 
furthermore, since all intelligible objects to which reason might 
still lead us under the guidance of that law have, in turn, no 
further reality for us except for the sake of the same law and the 
use of pure practical reason; and since this reason is entitled and 20 
also required to use nature (according to its pure form of 
understanding) as a **type** for the power of judgment: therefore, 
the present remark serves to prevent that which belongs merely to 
the **typic** of concepts from being counted among the concepts 
themselves. This Typic, therefore, as the tool of the power of 
judgment, guards against the **empiricism** of practical reason, 
which places the practical concepts of good and evil merely 
in the consequences of experience (the so-called **happiness**), 30 
even though these consequences and the infinite useful results 
of a will determined by self-help—if this will simultaneously 
made itself into a universal law of nature—can certainly serve 
as a perfectly appropriate type for the morally good, but 
is nevertheless not identical with it. This same Typic 
also guards against the **mysticism** of practical reason, 
which <note>original: "welche"; corrected to "welcher" by Hartenstein to agree with "mysticism"</note> makes what served only as a symbol into a **schema** <term>a direct mental "blueprint" that would allow us to see or experience the invisible</term>, that is, real and yet not <margin>[71]</margin>

<summary>Kant explains that while we use the laws of nature as a mental model (type) to understand moral duty, we must not confuse this model with the moral law itself. This distinction prevents the errors of "empiricism" (basing morality on happiness) and "mysticism" (claiming to see the invisible world).</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the "Typic of Pure Practical Judgment." It contrasts the "Rationalism" of judgment—which uses natural law only as a formal model—with the dangers of "Mysticism" (which seeks invisible visions) and "Empiricism" (which reduces morality to feelings and desires). It argues that Empiricism is the far greater threat to human dignity.</meta>

# First Part, Book I, Chapter 2.

...substitutes sensory intuitions (of an invisible kingdom of God) for the application of moral concepts and wanders off into the transcendent. Only the **rationalism of judgment** is appropriate for the use of moral concepts; this rationalism takes nothing more from sensory nature than what pure reason can also think for itself—namely, **lawfulness**—and it carries nothing into the supersensible <term>the realm beyond physical experience</term> except what can actually be represented in the world of sense through actions according to the formal rule of a natural law in general. However, guarding against the **empiricism** <term>the theory that all knowledge and morality come from experience and feelings</term> of practical reason is much more important and advisable. This is because <margin>a) Kant: "with which"; corrected by Hartenstein.</margin> **mysticism** is still compatible with the purity and sublimity of the moral law; furthermore, it is not particularly natural or suited to the common way of thinking to strain one's imagination toward supersensible intuitions, and therefore the danger on that side is not so widespread. On the other hand, empiricism uproots morality in its very **dispositions** <term>the inner motivations or character</term> at the source. It is in these dispositions—and not merely in outward actions—that the high value consists which humanity can and should acquire through them. In place of duty, empiricism substitutes something entirely different: an empirical interest, through which the inclinations <note>desires or impulses</note> in general interact with one another. Moreover, for this very reason, empiricism is far more dangerous <margin>c) i.e., empiricism.</margin> than any **enthusiasm** <term>religious or mystical fanaticism</term> <note>original: "Schwärmerei"</note>, which can never constitute a lasting state for many people. This is because empiricism is so favorable to everyone's natural way of feeling, yet it allies itself with all those inclinations which (no matter what shape they take) degrade humanity when they are elevated to the dignity of a supreme practical principle. <margin>b) Adickes wants to delete "since they"; Natorp would rather write "which" instead of "since they."</margin>

---

b) Adickes wants to strike "since they"; Natorp would rather write "which" instead of "since they."
c) i.e., empiricism.

<summary>Kant concludes his discussion on the proper way to judge moral actions by warning that while mystical fanaticism is a risk, the greater danger to morality is empiricism, which reduces duty to mere personal desire and self-interest.</summary>

Von den Triebfedern der reinen prakt. Vernunft. 93

### Third Chapter.
## **Of the Incentives of Pure Practical Reason.**

The essential core of all moral value in actions depends on the fact that the moral law directly determines the will. If the determination of the will happens in accordance with the moral law, but only by means of a feeling—of whatever kind—which must be assumed so that the law becomes a sufficient determining <margin>10</margin> ground of the will, and therefore not for the sake of the law itself, then the action will indeed contain **legality** <term>acting in outward agreement with the law</term>, but not **morality** <term>acting out of respect for the law itself</term>. If <margin>[72]</margin> by **incentive** <term>the inner "spring" or driver of action</term> (<note>original: "elater animi"</note> <gloss>Latin: "driver of the soul"</gloss>) we understand the subjective determining ground of the will of a being whose reason does not already, by its very nature, necessarily conform to the objective law, then it follows first: that we cannot attribute any incentives at all to the divine will, but the incentive of the human will (and of every <margin>20</margin> rational being created by that will<sup>a)</sup>) can never be anything other than the moral law<sup>b)</sup>. Consequently, the objective determining ground must always and quite alone be, at the same time, the subjectively sufficient determining ground of the action, if the action is to fulfill not merely the letter of the law, without containing the spirit*) of it.

Since, therefore, for the sake of the moral law and in order to give it influence over the will, we must not seek any other incentive that might allow us to dispense with the moral <margin>30</margin> one—because that would produce nothing but hollow hypocrisy without substance—

---

> *) One can say of every lawful action that has nonetheless not happened for the sake of the law: it is morally good merely according to the letter, but not according to the spirit (the **disposition** <term>the internal attitude or mindset</term>).

---

a) Kant: "every"; corrected by Vorländer <note>Karl Vorländer was a prominent editor of Kant's works in the early 20th century.</note>.
b) Adickes: "ought to be" <note>Erich Adickes was a German philosopher known for his exhaustive editing of Kant’s handwritten remains.</note>.

<summary>This page defines the "incentive" of pure practical reason, arguing that a truly moral action must be driven by the law itself rather than by any underlying feeling. It establishes the famous distinction between "legality" (following the rules) and "morality" (acting out of duty).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 93 regarding the "Incentives of Pure Practical Reason." First Part, Book I, Chapter 3.

...would, and it is even dubious to allow any other incentives (such as those of personal advantage) to cooperate alongside the moral law. Thus, nothing remains but to carefully determine in what way the moral law becomes an **incentive** <term>a subjective "driver" or motive for action</term>, and what happens to the human **faculty of desire** <term>the capacity to pursue objects we desire</term> as an effect of that **determining ground** <term>the underlying reason why the will chooses a specific path</term> upon it. For how a law in itself and immediately can be the determining ground of the <margin>10</margin> **will** (which is the essence of all **morality**), is an insoluble problem for human reason and identical to the question: how a **free will** is possible. Therefore, we will have to show *a priori* <term>knowledge gained through reason alone, independent of sensory experience</term> not the ground from which the moral law provides an incentive in itself, but rather what it effects (or better, must effect) in the **mind** <note>original: "Gemüte"</note> insofar as it is such an incentive.

The essential point of all determination of the will through the **moral law** is this: that as a free will, <margin>20</margin> it is determined solely by the law—consequently, not merely without the cooperation of sensory impulses, but even with the rejection of all of them and the curtailment of all **inclinations** <term>our natural, often selfish, urges and desires</term>, insofar as they could be contrary to that law. To this extent, the effect of the moral law as an incentive is only negative, and as such, this incentive can be recognized *a priori*. For <margin>[73]</margin> all inclination and every sensory impulse is based on **feeling**, and the negative effect on feeling (through the curtailment of the inclinations) is itself a feeling. Consequently, we can see *a priori* that the moral law, as the determining ground of the will, must cause a feeling which can be called **pain** by thwarting all our inclinations. Here we have the first, and perhaps only case, where we can determine the relationship of a cognition (in this case, of a pure **practical reason**) to the feeling of **pleasure** or **displeasure** based on concepts *a priori*. All inclinations together (which can also be brought into a tolerable system, the satisfaction of which is then called one's own **happiness**) constitute **selfishness** (*solipsism* <note>original: "solipsismus"; here referring to self-centeredness rather than the theory that only the self exists</note>). This is either...

<summary>Kant argues that while we cannot explain how freedom allows the moral law to move us, we can observe its effect: it acts "negatively" by suppressing our selfish desires, which we experience psychologically as a form of pain.</summary>

# On the Incentives of Pure Practical Reason

...which consists of **self-love**, an all-surpassing **benevolence** toward oneself (**self-love** <note>original Greek: *philautia*</note>), or that of **satisfaction** with oneself (**arrogance** <note>original Latin: *arrogantia*</note>). The former is specifically called **selfishness** <note>original: *Eigenliebe*</note>, the latter **self-conceit** <note>original: *Eigendünkel*</note>. Pure practical reason merely **restricts** **selfishness**, by limiting it—as something natural and active within us even before the **moral law**—only to the condition of agreement with this law; in this case, it is then called **rational self-love**. But it completely **strikes down** 
<margin>10</margin>
**self-conceit**, because all claims of **self-esteem** that precede agreement with the **moral law** <term>the objective principle of duty</term> are **void** and without any authority. This is because the **certainty** of a **disposition** <term>the inner attitude or motivation behind an action; original: *Gesinnung*</term> that agrees with this law is the **first condition** of all **worth** of the **person** (as we will soon make clearer), and all **arrogance** prior to this is false and unlawful. Now, the **propensity** for **self-esteem** belongs to those **inclinations** which the **moral law restricts**, insofar as that esteem rests merely on 
<margin>20</margin>
**sensibility** <term>our sensory nature, including feelings and desires; original: *Sinnlichkeit*</term><sup>a)</sup>. Therefore, the **moral law strikes down self-conceit**. However, since this law is something **positive** in itself—namely, the form of an **intellectual causality**, that is, of **freedom**—it is simultaneously an object of **respect**. By **weakening self-conceit** in its **opposition** to the **subjective counterpart** (namely, the **inclinations** within us), and even **striking it down**—that is, **humiliating** it—it becomes an object of the greatest **respect**. Consequently, it is also the ground of a positive **feeling** that is not of 
<margin>30</margin>
**empirical origin** <term>based on sensory experience</term> and is known **a priori** <term>through reason alone, independent of experience</term>. Thus, **respect** for the **moral law** is a **feeling** produced by an **intellectual ground**, and this **feeling** is the only one we recognize completely **a priori**, and whose **necessity** we can understand.

We have seen in the previous chapter that <margin>[74]</margin> everything that presents itself as an **object** of the **will** before the **moral law** [is excluded] from the **determining grounds** <term>the underlying reasons why the will chooses a specific path</term>...

---

a) Kant: "Morality"; corrected to "Sensibility" by editors Adickes, Görland, Nolte, and Wille.

<summary>Kant distinguishes between rational self-love, which the moral law limits, and self-conceit, which the moral law destroys. This process of humbling the ego produces "respect," the only feeling known through pure reason.</summary>

### First Part, Book I, Chapter 3.

...of the will—under the name of the "unconditionally good"—is excluded by this law itself as the supreme condition of practical reason. Instead, the mere practical form, which consists in the suitability of **maxims** <term>subjective rules of conduct that an individual lives by</term> for universal legislation, is what first determines what is good in itself and absolutely. This forms the basis for the maxim of a **pure will**, which is the only thing good in every respect. Now, however, we find our nature as **sensible beings** <term>beings who perceive and are affected by the physical world through senses and feelings</term> to be so
<margin>10</margin>
constituted that the "matter" of the faculty of desire (objects of inclination, whether of hope or fear) first forces itself upon us. Our **pathologically determinable self** <note>In Kantian terms, "pathological" refers to being moved by feelings, needs, and sensory impulses rather than by reason</note>—even though it is entirely unsuitable through its maxims for universal legislation—nevertheless strives to assert its claims beforehand as the first and original ones, as if it constituted our entire self. <insert>is</insert> <note>The editor Vorländer suggests the word "is" should be here to complete the sense of the original German sentence.</note> One can call this tendency to make oneself the objective determining ground of the will (based on the subjective grounds of one's own choice) **self-love**. 
<margin>20</margin>
When this self-love makes itself legislative and the unconditional practical principle, it can be called **self-conceit** <note>original: *Eigendünkel*; a form of arrogance where one treats their own desires as if they were universal laws</note>. Now the **moral law**, which alone is truly (that is, in every respect) objective, completely excludes the influence of self-love from the supreme practical principle and does infinite harm to the self-conceit that prescribes the subjective conditions of the <insert>former</insert> <note>The editor Adickes corrects Kant's original "des" to imply the conditions of self-love</note> as laws. Now, whatever does harm to our self-conceit in
<margin>30</margin>
our own judgment, **humbles** us. Therefore, the moral law inevitably humbles every human being when they compare the sensory inclinations of their nature with that law. That which, when represented as a determining ground of our will, humbles us in our self-consciousness, awakens **respect** <note>original: *Achtung*</note> for itself insofar as it is positive and a determining ground. Thus, the moral law is also subjectively a ground of respect. Since everything found in self-love belongs to inclination,

# Of the Incentives of Pure Practical Reason.

belongs, but since all inclination rests on feelings, and consequently whatever strikes down all inclinations together in *self-love* necessarily has an influence on feeling, we can understand how it is possible to see *a priori* <term>prior to experience; through pure reason alone</term> that the *moral law*—by excluding inclinations and the tendency to make them the supreme practical condition (that is, *self-love*) from all participation in the supreme legislation—can exert an effect on feeling. This effect is, on the one hand, [75] merely *negative*, but on the other hand—specifically in regard to <margin>10</margin> the restricting ground of pure practical reason—it is *positive*. For this purpose, no special kind of feeling under the name of a "practical" or "*moral*" feeling may be assumed as preceding the *moral law* or serving as its basis.

The *negative effect* on <gloss>a) <note>Kant used "auf" (on) rather than "aufs" (on the).</note></gloss> feeling (the feeling of unpleasantness) is, like all influence upon it and like every feeling in general, *pathological* <note>In Kant's vocabulary, "pathological" refers to being moved by sensory desires or emotions rather than by logic or duty</note>. As an effect of the *consciousness of the moral law*, <margin>20</margin> and therefore in relation to an intelligible <term>a cause existing in the realm of thought rather than the physical world</term> cause—namely the subject of pure practical reason as the supreme legislator—this feeling of a rational subject who is affected by inclinations is indeed called *humiliation* (*intellectual contempt*). However, in relation to the positive ground of this same feeling <gloss>b) <note>A comma was first added after "the same" by the editor Hartenstein.</note></gloss>, the law, it is simultaneously called *respect* for that law. No feeling exists for this law itself; rather, in the judgment of reason, by clearing resistance out of the way, the removal of an obstacle is valued as equal <margin>30</margin> to a positive promotion of causality <term>the power to produce an effect; here, the law's power to move us to act</term>. Therefore, this feeling can now also be called a *feeling of respect for the moral law*, and for both reasons together, it can be called a *moral feeling*.

The moral law, therefore, just as it is the *formal determining ground* of action through pure practical reason, is also the *material*, though...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on "Respect" (Achtung) as the unique feeling generated by the moral law. Having established that this feeling is not "pathological" (based on physical sensation), Kant now explores its specific application to persons versus objects.</meta>

<page-num>99</page-num>
-><header>Of the Incentives of Pure Practical Reason.</header><-
<sig>7*</sig>

to a supreme being, or even to a being free from all sensibility—for whom sensibility could therefore not be an obstacle to practical reason—respect for the law cannot be attributed.

This feeling (under the name of the moral feeling) is thus produced solely by reason. It does not serve for the judging of actions, nor even for the founding of the objective <term>Moral Law; the universal principle of duty that commands us to act as if our actions could become universal laws</term> itself, but rather merely as an <term>Incentive; translated from 'Triebfeder,' literally a 'driving spring' or the internal motive force</term> to make this law into one's own <margin>10</margin> maxim. But with what name could one more suitably label this singular feeling, which cannot be compared to any pathological <note>In Kant's terminology, "pathological" refers to anything stemming from the physical senses or emotions, rather than from reason.</note> feeling? It is of such a peculiar kind that it seems to be at the disposal of reason alone, and specifically pure practical reason.

<term>Respect</term> always applies only to persons, never to things. The latter can awaken in us inclination, and if they are animals (e.g., horses, dogs, etc.) even love, or even fear, like the sea, a volcano, or a beast of prey; but never <margin>20</margin> respect. Something that comes closer to this feeling is admiration, and this, as an affect—namely astonishment—can also apply to things: for example, mountains reaching to the sky, the magnitude, number, and distance of the celestial bodies, the strength and speed of many animals, and so on. But all of this is not respect. A human being can also be an object of love, fear, or admiration, even to the point of astonishment, and yet not be an object of respect. Their humorous mood, their courage and strength, or their power due to the <margin>30</margin> rank they hold among others, can inspire such sensations in me, but there is still a lack of inner respect for them. Fontenelle^a) says: "Before a nobleman I bow, but my spirit does not bow." I can [77] add: Before a lowly, common man of the citizenry, in whom I perceive an uprightness of character in a certain measure—more than I am aware of in

---

a) Bernard le Bovier de Fontenelle (1657—1757), a widely read French popular philosopher and satirical writer of the time.

<summary>Kant distinguishes "Respect" as a unique moral feeling produced by reason that applies exclusively to persons, differentiating it from admiration of nature or fear of power. He illustrates this with a famous anecdote, noting that true respect is an internal recognition of a person's moral character, regardless of their social status.</summary>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of "Respect" (Achtung). On the previous page, Kant distinguished respect from mere admiration or fear, noting that respect is a feeling produced by reason and applies only to persons. This page explores how the moral examples of others act as a mirror, challenging our self-conceit and forcing us to acknowledge the authority of the moral law.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>
<header>First Part, Book I, Third Chapter</header>

...[even if] I am not conscious of [any superiority], yet when I perceive [a man of integrity], my spirit bows, whether I want it to or not, and no matter how high I hold my head, to prevent my superiority from overlooking him. Why is that? His example presents a law to me that strikes down my <term>self-conceit; translated from 'Eigendünkel,' the arrogant belief in one's own moral worth</term> when I compare it with my own conduct; and I see before me the proof that following this law is possible in practice—its <term>feasibility; translated from 'Tunlichkeit,' the quality of being able to be carried out</term>. Now, I may even be conscious of an equal degree of <term>integrity; translated from 'Rechtschaffenheit,' a state of being morally sound and honest</term> <margin>10</margin> in myself, and yet the respect remains. For since in a human being everything good is always deficient, the law—made vivid through an example—nonetheless always strikes down my pride. For this, the man I see before me provides a standard. His own <term>impurity; translated from 'Unlauterkeit,' a lack of pure moral intention</term>, which may still cling to him, is not as well known to me as my own is; he therefore appears to me in a purer light. Respect is a tribute that we cannot refuse to <term>merit; translated from 'Verdienst,' the quality of being particularly worthy or good</term>, whether we want to or not; we may indeed <margin>20</margin> withhold it outwardly, but we cannot help feeling it inwardly.

Respect is so far from being a feeling of pleasure that one yields to it regarding another person only reluctantly. We seek to find something that might lighten the burden of this respect—some fault or criticism—to compensate ourselves for the <term>humiliation; translated from 'Demütigung,' the lowering of one's pride</term> that we experience through such an example. Even the deceased are not always safe from this criticism, <margin>30</margin> especially when their example seems inimitable. Even the <term>moral law; the internal command of duty</term> itself, in its solemn majesty, is exposed to this attempt to resist the respect it demands. Do we really think it is due to any other cause that we would like to degrade it to the level of our familiar inclinations, and that we exert ourselves for any other reason to make it a popular precept of our own well-understood advantage, than to get rid of the terrifying respect that so sternly holds our own unworthiness <margin>40</margin> before us? Nevertheless, there is also so little displeasure in this that, once one has cast off self-conceit and [allowed] that respect—

<summary>Kant explains that respect is an involuntary internal tribute paid to moral merit because it humbles our self-conceit by proving that the moral law is achievable. He notes that humans often search for flaws in others or try to "soften" the moral law into simple self-interest to avoid the painful feeling of our own moral inadequacy.</summary>
<keywords>respect, self-conceit, integrity, moral law, humiliation, merit, Kantian ethics</keywords>

This page concludes that thought, noting how respect for the moral law—and the examples that represent it—serves as the primary internal motivator for ethical action.</meta>

<page-num>101</page-num>

-> ### On the Incentives of Pure Practical Reason ### <-

[Res]pect <note>The text begins with "tung," the second half of "Achtung" (Respect) from the previous page</note> has been allowed practical influence, one again cannot look enough at the glory of this law, and the soul believes it elevates itself in the same measure as it sees the holy law elevated above itself and its frail nature. To be sure, great talents and an <term>activity; translated from 'Tätigkeit,' referring to productive work or effort</term> proportioned [78] to them can also produce respect or a feeling analogous to it; it is also quite proper to dedicate it to them, and there it seems as if <margin>10</margin> admiration were the same as that sensation. However, if one looks more closely, one will notice that since it always remains uncertain how much of the skill is due to innate talent and how much to <term>culture; translated from 'Kultur,' meaning refinement through self-discipline and education</term> through one's own industry, reason presents the latter to us presumably as a fruit of culture, and thus as <term>merit; translated from 'Verdienst'</term>. This markedly lowers our <term>self-conceit; translated from 'Eigendünkel'</term> and either reproaches us for our lack of it or imposes on us the following of such an example in a way that is appropriate for us. It <margin>20</margin> is therefore not mere admiration, this respect which we show to such a person (actually to the law, which his<sup>a)</sup> example holds before us); this is also confirmed by the fact that the common crowd of admirers, if they believe they have learned elsewhere of the badness of character in such a man (such as <note>François-Marie Arouet, known as Voltaire (1694–1778), the French Enlightenment writer</note> Voltaire), give up all respect for him. The true scholar, however, still feels it at least from the viewpoint of his talents, because he himself is involved in a business and a calling which makes the imitation of those talents, to a certain extent, a <margin>30</margin> law for him.

Respect for the moral law is thus the sole and at the same time indubitable moral <term>incentive; translated from 'Triebfeder,' literally the 'mainspring' of a machine, used here to mean the internal drive for action</term>, just as this feeling is directed toward no object other than solely for this reason. <page-num>103</page-num>
<header>On the Incentives of Pure Practical Reason.</header>

[...inter]est, which is never attributed to any being unless it possesses reason, and signifies an <term>incentive; translated from 'Triebfeder,' meaning the driving force or "spring" that moves the will</term> of the will insofar as it is represented through reason. Since the law itself must be the incentive in a morally good will, <term>moral interest; the intellectual concern we take in the law</term> is a pure, non-sensory interest of mere practical reason. The concept of a <term>maxim; a personal rule or principle of action</term> is also based on this concept of an interest. A maxim is thus only morally genuine when it rests solely on the <margin>10</margin> interest one takes in following the law. However, all three concepts—that of an incentive, an interest, and a maxim—can only be applied to **finite beings**. For they all presuppose a limitation in the nature of a being, since the subjective constitution of its choice does not automatically agree with the objective law of practical reason; they imply a need to be driven to activity by something because an internal obstacle stands in the way. They cannot, therefore, be applied to the <margin>20</margin> **divine will**.

There is something so special in the limitless esteem for the pure moral law—stripped of all advantage—as practical <margin>[80]</margin> reason presents it to us for compliance, a voice that makes even the boldest sinner tremble and forces him to hide from its sight, that one should not be surprised to find this influence of a purely intellectual idea on feeling to be unfathomable for speculative reason. One must be content with seeing <margin>30</margin> <term>a priori; from first principles, independent of experience</term> at least this much: that such a feeling is inseparably connected with the representation of the moral law in every finite rational being. <page-num>104</page-num>
<header>Part One, Book I, Chapter 3.</header>

...ual idea <note>continuing from the previous page: "purely intellectual idea"</note> indeed attaches to the representation of a law merely according to its form, not for the sake of any object of that law; therefore, it cannot be counted as either pleasure or pain, and yet it produces an interest in following it, which we call the **moral** interest; just as the capacity to take such an interest in the law (or respect for the moral law itself) is actually what **moral feeling** is.

<margin>10</margin> The consciousness of a free submission of the will to the law—yet as being combined with an unavoidable constraint exercised on all inclinations, but only through one’s own reason—is now **respect for the law**. The law that demands this respect and also inspires it is, as one can see, none other than the moral law (for no other law excludes all inclinations from the immediacy of their influence on the will). The action that is objectively practical according to this law, with the exclusion of all de-
<margin>20</margin> termining grounds from inclination, is called **duty**, which, for the sake of this exclusion, contains in its concept **practical compulsion**, i.e., determination to actions, however reluctantly they may be performed. The feeling that arises from the consciousness of this compulsion is not <term>pathological; in Kant's vocabulary, this does not mean "sickly," but rather a feeling caused by the physical senses or emotions rather than by reason</term>, as something that would be produced by an object of the senses, but is solely **practical**, i.e., possible through a prior (objective) determination of the will and the causality of reason.
<margin>30</margin> It therefore contains, as submission to a law, i.e., as a command (which announces constraint for the sensorily affected subject), no pleasure, but rather, to that extent, displeasure in the action itself. On the other hand, however, since this constraint is exercised merely by the legislation of one's own reason, it also contains
<insert>[81]</insert> **elevation**, and the subjective effect on feeling, insofar as pure practical reason is the sole cause of it, can thus be called **self-approval** in re-
<margin>40</margin> gard to the latter, in that one recognizes oneself as determined to it without any [outside] interest, merely through the law, and now becomes aware of a completely different interest produced subjectively through it, which is purely...

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues Kant’s exploration of "respect" (Achtung) as the subjective bridge between the abstract moral law and human action. It clarifies the crucial distinction between acting merely in outward accordance with the law (legality) and acting because of the law (morality).</meta>

<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>On the Incentives of Pure Practical Reason. 105</header>

is practical and free, which reason—through the practical law—absolutely commands and actually produces, without any inclination <note>Inclination refers to our natural desires, feelings, or moods</note> suggesting we take a liking to a duty-bound action. Because of this, it carries a very peculiar name, namely that of **respect**.

The concept of **duty** thus requires, regarding the action, objective agreement with the law; however, regarding the **maxim** <term>a "maxim" is the personal rule or principle that an individual follows when they decide to act</term> of that action, it requires subjective **respect** for the law as the sole way the will is determined by it. And upon this rests the difference <margin>10</margin> between the consciousness of having acted *in accordance with duty* and *from duty* (i.e., out of respect for the law). The first (which we call **legality**) is possible even if inclinations alone were the determining grounds of the will; but the second (**morality**), the true moral worth, must be placed solely in the fact that the action happens *from duty*—that is, purely for the sake of the law.*)

It is of the greatest importance in all moral <margin>20</margin> assessments to pay the most precise attention to the subjective principle of all maxims, so that all morality of actions is placed in the necessity of acting from duty and from respect for the law, not from love or a liking for what the actions are intended to produce. For humans and all created rational beings, moral necessity is a form of "necessitation"—that is, **obligation**. Every action based on this must be regarded as duty, and not as a way of behaving that we already <margin>30</margin> liked or could naturally come to like on our own.

---

*) If one carefully considers the concept of **respect for persons**, as it was previously explained, one realizes that it always rests on the consciousness of a duty that sets an example for us. Therefore, respect can never have anything other than a moral ground. Even from a psychological perspective—which is very useful for "the knowledge of man"—it is good to notice how, whenever we use this expression, we are paying a secret and wonderful, yet frequently occurring, regard to the moral law in our judgments.

<summary>Kant defines "duty" as requiring both outward obedience to the law and an inner motive of "respect" for that law. He distinguishes between "legality" (doing the right thing for any reason) and "morality" (doing the right thing specifically because it is a duty).</summary>

<page-num>106</page-num>
<header>First Part, Book I, Chapter 3.</header>

way of representing it. It is as if we could ever reach the point <margin>[82]</margin> where—without respect for the law, which is always connected with fear or at least anxiety regarding its transgression—we could, like the Deity who is exalted above all dependence, come into the possession of a **holiness of the will**. This would happen through a spontaneous agreement of the will with the pure moral law, which had become like a second nature to us and could never be shaken. In such a state, because we could never be tempted <insert>a)</insert> to become unfaithful to the law, <margin>10</margin> it would eventually even cease to be a "command" for us.

For the will of an all-perfect being, the moral law is indeed a law of **holiness**; but for the will of every finite rational being, it is a law of **duty**, of moral necessitation <term>"necessitation" refers to the way the law forces or constrains a being with natural desires</term>, and of the determination of its actions through respect for the law and out of reverence for its duty. No other subjective principle must be accepted as an **incentive**; for otherwise, while the action may turn out exactly as the law prescribes, and thus be **in accordance with duty**, it does not happen **from duty**. In that case, the disposition <margin>20</margin> toward the action is not moral, and it is the disposition that truly matters in this legislation.

It is very beautiful to do good to people out of love and sympathetic benevolence, or to be just out of a love for order; but this is not yet the genuine moral **maxim** of our conduct. It is not appropriate to our station as humans among rational beings if we presume, like "volunteers" so to speak, to proudly imagine ourselves above the thought of duty. We should not <margin>30</margin> pretend that, as if we were independent of any command, we want to do merely out of our own pleasure what we think requires no command <insert>b)</insert>. We stand under a **discipline of reason**, and in all our maxims we must not forget our submission to it; we must take nothing away from it, nor allow the authority of the law (even though

<margin>10</margin>
...[which] our own reason gives) to shortchange <insert>a)</insert> <note>original: "abkürzen". Some editors, like Kehrbach and Natorp, suggest "abzukürzen" for grammatical consistency.</note> the law through a self-loving delusion. This happens when we place the determining ground of our will—even if the action matches the law—in something other than the law itself and the **respect** for this law. **Duty** and **obligation** are the only names that we must give to our relationship to the moral law. We are indeed legislative members of a **kingdom of morals** <term>Kant’s "Kingdom of Morals" (Reich der Sitten) is an ideal community where every person is treated as an end in themselves, never merely as a tool.</term> made possible through freedom and presented to us for our respect by practical reason; yet we are at the same time its **subjects**, not its sovereign head. To fail to recognize our lower status as created beings, and to allow our **self-conceit** to rebel against the authority of the holy law, is already a spiritual departure from <margin>[83]</margin> that law, even if its literal requirements were fulfilled.

<margin>20, 30</margin>
However, the possibility of a command such as: **Love God above all, and your neighbor as yourself**<sup>*)</sup>, agrees quite well with this. For as a command, it requires respect for a law that *commands* love, rather than leaving it to one's arbitrary choice to make love a principle. But **love for God** as an inclination (pathological love <term>"Pathological" here does not mean "diseased," but rather "pertaining to the feelings or senses" (from the Greek *pathos*).</term>) is impossible; for He is not an object of the senses. This same love toward other humans is indeed possible, but it cannot be **commanded**; for it is not in any human's power to love someone merely on command. Therefore, it is only **practical love** that is meant in that core of all laws. In this sense, to love God means: *to gladly do His commandments*; to love one's neighbor means: *to gladly practice every duty toward him*. But the command that makes this a rule cannot order us to *have* this feeling in our dutiful actions, but rather merely to **strive** toward it.

*) The principle of one's own **happiness**, which some wish to make the supreme principle of morality, forms a strange contrast with this law. That principle would read: **Love yourself above all, but love God and your neighbor for your own sake.**

<page-num>109</page-num>

### On the Incentives of Pure Practical Reason.

...but to make this latter, namely the mere love of the law (since it would then cease to be a command, and morality—which would then subjectively pass over into **holiness**—would cease to be **virtue**), the constant though unattainable goal of one's efforts. For in that which we highly esteem, but yet (on account of the consciousness of our weaknesses) dread <note>original: "scheuen"; implies a respectful distance or awe rather than simple fear.</note>, the respectful dread transforms into affection, and respect into love, through the increasing ease of satisfying it; at the very least, it would be the perfection of a disposition dedicated to the law if it were ever possible for a creature to reach it.

This reflection is intended here not so much to bring the cited Gospel command to clear concepts in order to prevent religious fanaticism <term>original: "Religionsschwärmerei"; refers to a state of religious "delirium" or "enthusiasm" where one believes they have a direct emotional or mystical connection to God that bypasses moral law.</term> regarding the love of God, but rather to precisely determine the moral disposition even directly regarding duties toward men, and <gloss>a) to <note>The editor Paul Natorp suggests adding "um" (to) here for grammatical clarity.</note></gloss> to check or if possible prevent a mere **moral fanaticism**, which infects many minds. The moral stage upon which man (and, according to all our insight, every rational creature) stands is **respect for the moral law**. The disposition that is incumbent upon him to follow this law is: to follow it from **duty**, not from voluntary inclination or even from a self-initiated, uncommanded effort gladly undertaken. His moral state, in which he can always be, is **virtue**—that is, a moral disposition *in struggle*, and not **holiness** in the presumed possession of a complete purity of the dispositions of the will. It is pure moral fanaticism and an increase in **self-conceit** <term>original: "Eigendünkel"; the arrogant belief that one's own character is already naturally perfect.</term> when one tunes minds toward actions as being "nobler," "sublimer," and "more magnanimous" through encouragement, thereby placing them in the delusion <margin>[85]</margin> as if it were not duty—that is, respect for the law, whose yoke (which is nonetheless gentle, because reason itself imposes it upon us) they must bear, even if reluctantly, which constitutes the determining ground of their ac...

<summary>Kant distinguishes between "virtue," which is the moral struggle of a human being, and "holiness," which is the unattainable ideal of a will that naturally loves the good. He warns against "moral fanaticism," where people imagine they act out of noble feelings rather than the humble requirement of duty.</summary>

<keywords>holiness, virtue, respect for the law, moral fanaticism, duty, self-conceit, practical reason, Natorp</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues Kant’s critique of "moral fanaticism" from page 109. He is warning against the idea that humans can reach a state where they do good naturally out of "love" or "inclination" without the need for the concept of duty. The text picks up mid-sentence, concluding a thought about how moral fanaticism misrepresents our relationship to the moral law.</meta>

<page-num>110</page-num>

<header>First Part, Book I, Chapter 3.</header>

...constituted their actions, and which still humbles them while they follow (obey) it; but rather as if those actions were expected of them not out of duty, but as mere merit.ª) For not only would they have failed to satisfy the spirit of the law in the least by imitating such deeds—namely, from such a principle—which consists in the disposition that submits to the law, not in the legality of the action (whatever the principleᵇ) might be) and places the incentive pathologically <term>original: "pathologisch"; in Kant's philosophy, this refers to being driven by feelings, emotions, or physical desires rather than by reason or the moral law.</term> (in sympathy or even self-love <term>original: "Philautie"; from the Greek 'philautia', meaning self-love or vanity.</term>), not morally (in the law): so they produce in this way a windy, flighty, fantastical way of thinking, flattering themselves with a voluntary kindness of their mind <note>original: "Gutartigkeit ihres Gemüts"; a natural good-heartedness that doesn't feel the need for rules.</note> that needs neither spur nor bridle, for which a command is not even necessary, and thereby forgetting their obligation, which they should think of rather than merit. Actions of others that were performed with great sacrifice and indeed solely for the sake of duty may well be praised under the name of noble and sublime deeds, and yet even then only insofar as there are traces that suggest they were done entirely out of respect for duty, not from outbursts of the heart <note>original: "Herzensaufwallungen"; temporary surges of emotional feeling that might lead to good deeds but lack the stable foundation of moral law.</note>. But if one wants to present them to someone as examples to follow, then respect for duty (as the only genuine moral feeling) must absolutely be used as the incentive: thisᶜ) serious, holy prescription that does not leave it to our vain self-love to trifle with pathological impulses (so long as they are analogous to morality) and to pride ourselves on meritorious worth. If we only search well enough, we will find for all actions that are worthy of praise a law of duty that commands and does not leave it to our discretion what might be pleasing to our inclination.

ª) <note>original: "würde"</note> Kant wrote "would"; corrected to "were" by the editor Hartenstein.
ᵇ) <note>original: "es"</note> The word "it" was added by editors Rosenkranz and Hartenstein for clarity.
ᶜ) The second and later editions use "the" <note>original: "die"</note> here.

<summary>Kant argues against "moral fanaticism," asserting that human morality must be based on respect for the law and duty rather than spontaneous emotional impulses or a desire for personal merit. He warns that acting out of a presumed "goodness of heart" leads to a flighty, arrogant mindset that ignores the reality of moral obligation.</summary>

<keywords>Immanuel Kant, moral fanaticism, duty, respect for law, pathological incentives, self-love, merit, moral disposition, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 110 regarding "moral fanaticism" (Schwärmerei). Kant is concluding his argument that morality must be based on the objective law of duty and respect, rather than subjective feelings or the prideful belief that one does good out of natural inclination or "heroic" virtue.</meta>

<page-num>111</page-num>

<header>### On the Incentives of Pure Practical Reason.</header>

This is the only mode of presentation that morally forms the soul, because it alone is capable of firm and precisely determined principles.

If **fanaticism** <note>original: "Schwärmerei"; in the 18th century, this term referred to a state of being deluded by one's own imagination or irrational religious fervor. Kant uses it to describe the mistake of thinking we can be "above" the need for rules.</note> in its most general meaning is an exceeding of the limits of human reason undertaken according to principles, then **moral fanaticism** is this exceeding of the limits that pure practical reason sets for humanity. By this, reason forbids us from placing the subjective **determining ground** <term>original: "Bestimmungsgrund"; the underlying reason or motive that triggers a choice.</term> of actions performed according to duty—that is, their moral **incentive** <term>original: "Triebfeder"; literally a "drive-spring," the internal force that moves a person to act.</term>—in [86] anything other than the law itself. It also forbids placing the disposition, 10 which is thereby brought into one's **maxims** <term>original: "Maximen"; the personal rules or principles of conduct that an individual sets for themselves.</term>, anywhere else than in **respect** for this law. Therefore, reason commands us to make the thought of duty—which strikes down all arrogance as well as vain **self-love** <term>original: "Philautie"; from the Greek 'philautia', meaning vanity or self-conceit.</term>—the supreme life-principle of all morality in man.

If this is so, then not only writers of romance or sentimental educators (though they may zealously rail against sentimentality) 20 but sometimes even philosophers—indeed, the strictest of all, the **Stoics**—have introduced moral fanaticism instead of a sober but wise discipline of morals. However, the fanaticism of the latter was more heroic, while that of the former was of a shallow and melting quality. One can say of the moral teaching of the **Gospel** <note>original: "Evangelii"; the Latin genitive form of Evangelium.</note> with all truth and without hypocrisy: that it was first, through the purity of the moral principle and its appropriateness to the limits of 30 finite beings, to subject all human conduct to the discipline of a duty laid before their eyes. This duty does not let them wander fanatically among dreamed-of moral perfections, but instead sets boundaries of **humility** (that is, **self-knowledge**) against both conceit and self-love, both of which are prone to mistake their own limits.

**Duty!** You sublime, great name, you who contain nothing lovable that involves flattery, but instead demand 40 **submission**; yet you also threaten nothing that awakens natural aversion in the mind...

<summary>Kant defines moral fanaticism as the dangerous attempt to bypass the law of duty in favor of personal feelings or pride. He contrasts "heroic" Stoicism and "sentimental" education with the humble, realistic discipline of duty found in the Gospel.</summary>

<keywords>moral fanaticism, duty, incentive, maxims, respect, self-love, Stoics, Gospel, humility, self-knowledge</keywords>

<header>Part One, Book I, Chapter 3.</header>

...aroused and terrified in order to move the will, but merely sets forth a law which of itself<margin>a)</margin> finds entrance into the mind, and yet gains reverence <note>original: "Verehrung"; a deep sense of awe or veneration.</note> for itself against one's will (even if not always obedience); a law before which all inclinations fall silent, even if they secretly work against it. What origin is worthy of you, and where is the root of your noble ancestry to be found, which proudly rejects all kinship with the inclinations? From what root must one descend as the indispensable condition of that value which human beings alone can give themselves?

It can be nothing less than that which raises the human being above himself (as a part of the **world of sense** <term>original: "Sinnenwelt"; the physical world we experience through our five senses, governed by cause and effect.</term>), which links him to an order of things that only the understanding can think. This order has under it the entire world of sense, and with it the empirically determinable existence of the human being in time, as well as the whole of all ends (which alone is appropriate to such unconditional practical laws as the moral law). It is nothing other than **personality**, i.e., freedom and independence from the mechanism of all nature. Yet this is viewed at the same time as the capacity of a being who is subject to special laws—namely, pure practical laws given by his own reason. Thus the **person**, as belonging to the world of sense, is subject to his own **personality** <note>In Kant's philosophy, "personality" is not one's character, but the capacity to act according to moral laws one gives to oneself.</note> insofar as he also belongs to the **intelligible world** <term>original: "intelligibelen Welt"; the world of pure reason and freedom, beyond physical perception.</term>. Therefore, it is not surprising that the human being, belonging to both worlds, must regard his own being in relation to his second and highest **vocation** <note>original: "Bestimmung"; often translated as "destination" or "calling."</note> with nothing but reverence, and the laws of that vocation with the highest respect.

Many expressions that designate the value of objects according to moral ideas are based on this origin. The moral law is **holy** (inviolable). While a human being is unholy enough, the **humanity** in his person must be holy to him. In all of creation, everything one wants, and over which one has power, can also be used merely as a **means**; only the—

<summary>Kant identifies "personality"—the human capacity for rational freedom—as the source of moral duty, arguing that humans belong to both a physical world of necessity and a rational world of freedom. Because of this dual nature, we must treat the "humanity" within ourselves and others as holy and as an end in itself, never merely as a tool.</summary>

<page-num>113</page-num>

# Of the Incentives of Pure Practical Reason.

The human being, and with him every rational creature, is an **end in himself** <term>original: "Zweck an sich selbst"; the idea that a person has absolute value and should never be used as a tool for another's gain.</term>. He is, in fact, the subject of the moral law, which is holy, by virtue of the autonomy of his freedom. For the sake of this freedom, every will—even a person's own will directed toward themselves—is restricted by the condition of agreement with the autonomy of the rational being. This means that a person must never be subjected to any purpose that is not possible according to a law which could <margin>10</margin> arise from the will of the passive subject <note>The "passive subject" refers to the person being acted upon; Kant argues we can only do things to others that they could rationally agree to.</note> himself; thus, one should never use a person merely as a **means**, but always simultaneously as an **end**. We rightly attribute this condition even to the Divine Will regarding the rational beings in the world as His creatures, since it rests on their **personality**, through which alone they are ends in themselves.

This respect-inspiring idea of personality, which sets before our eyes the sublimity of our nature (according to its vocation), while <margin>20</margin> simultaneously letting us notice the lack of fitness in our behavior regarding it—and thereby striking down **self-conceit**—is natural and easily noticeable even to the most common human reason. Has not every even moderately honest man sometimes found that he refrained from an otherwise harmless lie—by which he could have either pulled himself out of a [88] troublesome affair or perhaps even provided a benefit to a beloved and deserving friend—merely so that <margin>30</margin> he would not be forced to despise himself secretly in his own eyes? Does not the consciousness that he has maintained and honored the dignity of humanity in his own person still uphold a righteous man in the greatest misfortunes of life, which he could have avoided if only he could have disregarded his duty? Does it not keep him from having cause to be ashamed of himself or to dread the inner gaze of self-examination? This comfort is not **happiness** <term>original: "Glückseligkeit"; a state of total well-being or pleasure, which Kant distinguishes from moral contentment.</term>, nor even the smallest part of <margin>40</margin> it. For no one would wish for the opportunity to experience this, nor perhaps even for a life in such

->Kant, *Critique of Practical Reason*. 8<-

<vocab>Human being, rational creature, end in itself, moral law, autonomy, freedom, personality, respect, sublimity, duty, dignity, self-examination, happiness</vocab>

<summary>Kant argues that human beings must always be treated as ends in themselves, never merely as tools, because of their capacity for moral self-governance. He illustrates this by showing how even an ordinary person feels a sense of dignity and self-respect that is more valuable than mere happiness or avoiding trouble.</summary>

# Part One, Book I, Chapter 3.

<margin>10</margin> [to be in those] circumstances. But he lives, and he cannot endure to be unworthy of life in his own eyes. This inner peace is therefore merely negative in respect to everything that might make life pleasant; namely, it is the prevention of the danger of sinking in personal worth, after the worth of his condition <note>Kant distinguishes between our "condition" (our happiness or external situation) and our "personal worth" (our moral character).</note> has already been completely given up by him. It is the effect of a respect for something quite different from life, in comparison and contrast to which life, with all its agreeableness, has no value at all. <margin>20</margin> He continues to live only out of **duty**, not because he finds the least taste for life.

Such is the nature of the true **incentive** <term>original: "Triebfeder"; the internal motivation that drives a person to act.</term> of pure practical **reason**; it is none other than the pure moral **law** itself, insofar as it lets us feel the **sublimity** of our own supersensible **existence** and subjectively works **respect** for their higher vocation in human beings—who are simultaneously aware of their sensible existence and the <margin>30</margin> dependent nature connected with it (which is, in this regard, pathologically affected <note>In Kant’s vocabulary, "pathological" does not mean diseased, but rather "moved by feelings or sensory impulses" instead of by reason.</note>). Now, many charms and comforts of life can well be connected with this incentive, such that for their sake alone, even the wisest choice of a rational **Epicurean** <term>Epicurean: a follower of Epicurus; here referring to someone who logically calculates how to achieve the most pleasure and least pain in life.</term>—one who reflects on the greatest well-being of life—would declare itself in favor of moral **good conduct**; <margin>[89]</margin> <margin>40</margin> and it may even be advisable to combine this prospect of a cheerful enjoyment of life with that supreme and already self-sufficiently determining motive; but only to provide a counterweight to the allurements that vice does not fail to present on the opposite side, not to place the actual moving force here, even in the slightest part, when it is a matter of duty. For that would be equivalent to wanting to contaminate the **moral disposition** at its source. The dignity of duty has nothing to do with the **enjoyment of life**; it has its own unique law and its own unique court; and even if one wanted to shake them both together as much as possible, to hand them over mixed, as it were...

<summary>Kant explains that the moral law serves as an "incentive" that allows humans to recognize their own dignity, which is independent of physical happiness or pleasure. He argues that while being moral might lead to a pleasant life, we must act out of duty alone to keep our moral character pure.</summary>

## Critical Examination of the Analytic of Pure Practical Reason.

By the **critical examination** <term>original: "Kritische Beleuchtung"; literally a "critical illumination" or lighting up of the subject to see its structure clearly.</term> of a science, or of a section of it that constitutes a system on its own, I mean the investigation and justification of why it must have precisely this and no other systematic form when compared with another system that is based on a similar faculty of knowledge. Now, **practical reason** <term>the capacity to determine the will and choose how to act</term> is based on the same faculty of knowledge as **speculative reason** <term>also called theoretical reason; the capacity to understand how the world is, rather than how it ought to be</term>, insofar as both are **pure reason**. Thus, the difference in the systematic form of the one compared to the other must be determined through a comparison of both, and the reason for it must be provided.

The **Analytic** <term>the branch of the critique that breaks down the faculty of reason into its basic elements</term> of pure theoretical reason was concerned with the knowledge of objects that may be given to the **understanding**. It therefore had to begin with **intuition** <term>original: "Anschauung"; the direct, sensory perception of an object</term>, and thus (since this is always sensory) with **sensibility**. From there, it had to proceed first to **concepts** (of the objects of this intuition) and could only conclude with **fundamental principles** after both of these had been established. By contrast, because practical reason is not concerned with knowing objects, but with its own faculty of making those objects real (according to a knowledge of them)—that is to say, it is concerned with a **will**, which is a form of **causality** insofar as reason contains its **determining ground** <term>the "reason why" a will decides to act</term>—it consequently contains no object of intuition. Instead, as a practical reason, it must proceed differently, because the concept of causality always involves a relationship to a **law** which determines the existence of a manifold <note>A "manifold" refers to the variety of sensory data or elements that need to be organized by the mind.</note> in relation to one another.

<summary>Kant explains that a "critical examination" justifies the specific structure of a science. He notes that while theoretical reason begins with sensory intuition and moves toward principles, practical reason must follow a different path because it deals with the will and causality rather than observing objects.</summary>

<keywords>critical examination, analytic, practical reason, theoretical reason, intuition, sensibility, causality, determining ground, systematic form</keywords>

<page-num>116</page-num>
<header>First Part, Book I, Chapter 3.</header>

reason has only one law to provide: thus a critique of its Analytic, insofar as it is to be a **practical reason** (which is the actual task ᵃ)), must begin from the possibility of **practical principles** *a priori* <note>original: "a priori"; meaning principles that are known independently of any sensory experience.</note>. From 
<margin>[90]</margin> there, it could only then proceed to concepts of the objects of a practical reason—namely, those of the absolutely **Good** and **Evil**—in order to define them for the first time in accordance with those principles (for it is impossible to identify Good and Evil through any faculty of knowledge prior to those principles). Only then could the final chapter conclude this part: the chapter regarding the relationship of pure practical reason to **sensibility** <term>our capacity for feeling and desire</term> and its necessary influence on the same, which must be known *a priori*—that is, the **moral feeling**.

Thus, the Analytic of pure practical reason divided the entire range of all conditions of its use quite analogously to the theoretical reason, but in **reverse order**. The Analytic of pure theoretical reason was divided into **Transcendental Aesthetic** and **Transcendental Logic**; that of the practical reason is, conversely, divided into **Logic** and **Aesthetic** of pure practical reason (if I may be permitted to use these otherwise unsuitable terms here merely for the sake of the analogy). Furthermore, the Logic was there divided into the **Analytic of concepts** and that of **principles**; here, it is divided into the Analytic of **principles** and then of **concepts**. 

The Aesthetic in the first critique <note>the *Critique of Pure Reason*</note> had two parts, because of the twofold nature of sensory **intuition** <term>original: "Anschauung"; the way our mind directly perceives space and time</term>; here, however, sensibility is not considered as a capacity for intuition at all, but merely as **feeling** (which can be a subjective ground of desire), and in regard to this, pure practical reason permits no further division.

Namely, it proceeds from the universal in the **major premise** (the moral 
<margin>10</margin> principle) through a **subsumption** <term>the act of categorizing a specific case under a general rule</term> of possible actions (as good or evil) performed in the **minor premise**, toward the **conclusion**—namely, the subjective determination of the will (an interest in the practically possible good and the **maxim** <term>a personal rule or principle of action</term> based upon it). For anyone who has been [91] convinced by the propositions appearing in the Analytic, such comparisons will bring pleasure; for they rightly justify the expectation that we might one day reach an insight into the **unity** of the entire faculty of pure reason (both 
<margin>20</margin> theoretical and practical) and be able to derive everything from a single principle. This is the unavoidable need of human reason, which finds complete satisfaction only in a fully systematic unity of its knowledge.

But if we now also consider the content of the knowledge we can have from and through a pure practical reason, as the Analytic sets it out, we find remarkable differences alongside a striking analogy between it and theoretical reason. Regarding the theoretical side, the faculty 
<margin>30</margin> of pure rational knowledge *a priori* could <gloss>Kant wrote "could" (konnte); corrected by Grillo</gloss> be proven quite easily and clearly through examples from the sciences. ...ground of determination is also practical in itself: one had to be able to demonstrate this from the most common use of practical reason, by authenticating the supreme practical principle as one that every natural human reason recognizes as completely *a priori* <note>original: "a priori"; meaning known through reason alone, independent of sensory experience</note>, independent of any sensory data <note>original: "Datis"</note>, as the supreme law of its will. One had to first prove and justify it, according to the purity of its origin, even in the judgment of this common reason, before science could take hold of it to make use of it—treating it, as it were, as a **fact** <term>Kant's famous "Fact of Reason" (Factum der Vernunft): the idea that our consciousness of the moral law is an undeniable reality that requires no further proof</term> that precedes all hair-splitting about its possibility and all consequences that might be drawn from it. But this circumstance can also be well explained from what was cited just before; because pure practical reason must necessarily begin from principles, which must therefore be laid as the foundation for all science as primary data and cannot first arise from it. This justification of moral principles as principles of a pure reason could also be conducted quite well and with sufficient certainty through a mere appeal to the judgment of common human understanding, because everything empirical <note>experience-based</note> that might sneak into our
<margin>[92]</margin> **maxims** <term>personal rules of action</term> as a determining ground of the will immediately makes itself known through the feeling of pleasure or pain that necessarily attaches to it (insofar as it arouses desire); but pure practical reason directly resists admitting this into its principle as a condition. The lack of similarity between the grounds of determination (the empirical and the rational) is made known by this resistance of a practically legislative reason against all interfering inclinations through a peculiar kind of sensation. This sensation, however, does not precede the legislation of practical reason, but is rather produced by it alone, and indeed as a compulsion—namely, through the feeling of **respect** <term>Achtung; a unique moral emotion that is not a drive for pleasure, but a recognition of the law's authority</term>, the like of which no human has for inclinations, of whatever kind they may be, but only for the law; it is thus made so recognizable and...

<summary>Kant argues that the supreme moral law is a "fact of reason" recognized even by common people without scientific training. He explains that we distinguish moral duty from selfish desires through the unique feeling of "respect" for the law, which resists our natural inclinations.</summary>

<page-num>119</page-num>
<header>Critical Elucidation of the Analytic.</header>

...so elevated and prominent that no one, even the most common human understanding, should fail to realize instantly in a presented example that while empirical grounds of volition <note>Experience-based reasons for wanting something</note> may advise him to follow their allurements, he can never be expected to obey anything other than the merely pure practical law of reason.

The distinction between the **doctrine of happiness** and the **doctrine of morals**, in the first of which empirical principles form the entire foundation, while in the second they do not constitute even the slightest addition, is now the first and most important occupation incumbent upon the **Analytic of Pure Practical Reason** <note>The section of the book that breaks down how reason works in moral decision-making</note>; in this, it must proceed as punctually—and if it were required, as painstakingly—as any geometer in his work. However, the philosopher who has to struggle with greater difficulty here (as always in rational knowledge through mere concepts, without the construction of them) because he cannot rely on any **intuition** <term>original: "Anschauung"; a direct sensory perception or mental "looking" at an object</term> (<insert>to a pure</insert> <term>Noumenon: a thing as it is in itself, accessible only to the mind and not the senses</term>) as a basis, is nevertheless aided by the fact that he can, almost like the **chemist**, at any time conduct an experiment with every person's practical reason to distinguish the moral (pure) ground of determination from the empirical one. This is done by adding the moral law (as the ground of determination) to the empirically affected will (for example, of someone who would like to lie because he can gain something by it). It is as if the analytical chemist adds alkali to a solution of lime in hydrochloric acid <note>original: "Salzgeist"; literally "spirit of salt," an archaic term for hydrochloric acid</note>; the acid immediately leaves the lime, unites with the alkali, and the lime is precipitated to the bottom. Similarly, present the moral law to someone who is otherwise an honest man (or who at least places himself in the position of an honest man in thought), by which [93] he recognizes the worthlessness of a liar—immediately his practical reason (in judging what ought to be done by him) abandons the advantage and

<summary>Kant explains that the distinction between moral duty and the pursuit of happiness is so clear that even an ordinary person can see it. He uses a chemical analogy to show how the moral law "precipitates" out pure duty from selfish interests when we test our motivations.</summary>
