But this distinction between the **principle of happiness** and that of **morality** is not for that reason an immediate opposition of the two, and pure practical reason does not require that one should give up claims to happiness, but only that, as soon as duty is at issue, one should take no account of it at all. It can even be a duty in a certain respect to care for one's happiness; partly because it (to which skill, health, and wealth belong) contains means <margin>20</margin> for the fulfillment of one's duty, and partly because the lack of it (e.g., poverty) contains temptations to transgress one's duty. However, to promote one's happiness can never directly be a duty, much less a principle of all duty. Now, since all **determining grounds** <note>the underlying reasons or motives that move the will to act</note> of the will, except for the single pure practical law of reason (the moral law), are altogether empirical <note>based on sensory experience or personal desires</note>, and as such belong to the principle of happiness, they must all be separated from the supreme moral principle and never incorporated into it as a <margin>30</margin> condition. To do so would destroy all moral value, just as an empirical mixture added to geometrical principles would destroy all mathematical evidence—the most excellent thing that (according to **Plato's** judgment) mathematics possesses in itself, and which even precedes all its utility.

Instead of the **deduction** <term>original: "Deduktion"; in Kantian philosophy, a formal justification or proof of the validity of a concept</term> of the supreme principle of pure practical reason—that is, the explanation of the possibility of such a cognition **from first principles** <term>original: "a priori"; knowledge that is independent of experience</term>—nothing further could be cited<gloss>a)</gloss> than that <margin>40</margin> if one considers the possibility of the freedom of an acting...

<meta>This page continues the "Critical Elucidation of the Analytic," shifting focus from the isolation of moral motives to the intrinsic link between the moral law and the concept of freedom.</meta>

<page-num>121</page-num>
->### Critical Elucidation of the Analytic.<-

If one could perceive the cause, one would see not merely the possibility, but even the necessity of the **moral law** as the supreme practical law for rational beings, to whom one attributes **freedom** of the causality of their will. This is because both concepts are so inseparably connected that one could define practical freedom simply as the independence of the will [94] from every other law except the moral law alone. However, the freedom of an <margin>10</margin> acting cause, particularly in the **sensory world** <note>the world as we experience it through our five senses, governed by the laws of physics</note>, can in no way be understood in terms of its possibility. We are fortunate enough if we can simply be sufficiently assured that no proof of its impossibility exists; being compelled by the moral law, which postulates <note>assumes as a necessary condition</note> freedom, we are thereby justified in assuming it. 

Yet, there are still many who believe they can explain this freedom according to empirical principles <note>theories based only on observation and experience</note>, just like any other natural faculty. They view it as a **psychological property** <margin>20</margin>—the explanation of which would merely require a deeper study of the nature of the soul and the "springs" <note>motivating forces</note> of the will—rather than as a **transcendental predicate** <note>a fundamental characteristic that goes beyond sensory experience</note> of the causality of a being that belongs to the sensory world (which is the only thing that truly matters here). By doing so, they suppress the magnificent discovery that comes to us through pure practical reason by means of the moral law: namely, the discovery of an **intelligible world** <note>a realm of reality that can be understood by the mind but not perceived by the senses</note> through the realization of the otherwise transcendent <margin>30</margin> concept of freedom. In doing so, they also abolish the moral law itself, which accepts absolutely no empirical determining ground. It is therefore necessary to provide a defense here against this delusion and to expose **empiricism** in all the nakedness of its shallowness.

The concept of causality as **natural necessity**, in contrast to causality as **freedom**, concerns only the existence of things insofar as they are determinable in time—consequently, as **appearances**—as <margin>40</margin> opposed to their causality as **things in themselves** <note>the way things exist independently of our human perception of them</note>. If one takes the determinations of the existence of things in time for determinations of things in themselves...

<summary>Kant explains that freedom and the moral law are two sides of the same coin, arguing that freedom cannot be explained by psychology or science because it belongs to a realm beyond sensory experience.</summary>
<keywords>freedom, moral law, causality, sensory world, intelligible world, empiricism, things in themselves, appearances</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 121, where the author began to argue that if we view ourselves only as part of the natural world (things as they appear in time), freedom is impossible because every action would be determined by previous events.</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>
<header>First Part, Book 1, Main Section 3.</header>

itself (which is the most common way of thinking), then the necessity in the **causal relationship** <note>the link between a cause and its effect</note> cannot in any way be reconciled with **freedom**; rather, they are contradictorily opposed to one another. For from the former it follows that every event, and consequently every action that occurs at a point in time, is necessary under the condition of what was in the preceding time. Since past time is no longer in my power <margin>10</margin>, every action I perform must be necessary due to determining grounds that are not in my power; that is, at the point in time in which I act, I am never free. Indeed, even if I assumed my entire existence as independent of any <margin>[95]</margin> foreign cause (such as from God), so that the determining grounds of my causality—even of my entire existence—were not outside of me at all, this would not in the least transform that **natural necessity** into freedom. For at every point in time <margin>20</margin> I still stand under the necessity of being determined to act by that which is not in my power, and the infinite series of events *from the preceding part* <note>original: "a parte priori"; referring to the chain of causes stretching back into the past</note>, which I would always only continue according to an already predetermined order and never begin of myself, would be a continuous chain of nature, and my causality therefore never freedom.
    If, therefore, one wishes to attribute freedom to a being whose existence is determined in time, one cannot at least exempt it from the law of natural necessity <margin>30</margin> of all events in its existence, and thus also its actions; for that would be the same as handing it over to blind chance. But since this law inevitably affects all causality of things insofar as their existence is determinable in time, if this were the way in which one also had to represent the **existence of these things in themselves** <note>the reality of things as they are, independent of our sensory perception</note>, then freedom would have to be rejected as a void and impossible concept. Consequently, if one still wishes to save it, <margin>40</margin> no way remains but [to consider] the existence of a thing,

<margin>20</margin> With this, the judicial verdicts of that wondrous faculty within us which we call **conscience** also perfectly agree. A person may craft as many excuses as he wants to paint an unlawful behavior he remembers as an unintentional slip, a mere oversight that can never be entirely avoided—consequently as something in which he was swept away by the current of **natural necessity**—and thus declare himself free of guilt. Yet he finds that the advocate who speaks in his favor can by no means silence the accuser within him, if he is aware that at the time he committed the wrong, he was in his right mind—that is, in the use of his **freedom**. And nevertheless, he explains his transgression to himself as a certain bad habit, brought on by the gradual neglect of attention to himself, to such a degree that he can regard it as a natural consequence of that habit; however, this does not protect him against **self-reproach**...

Reason then always morally links the same feeling with the deed, whether it is happening now or happened long ago. # Critical Examination of the Analytic.
<header>Critical Examination of the Analytic.</header>

...only make them all the more reprehensible and deserving of punishment.

But a difficulty still faces **freedom**, insofar as it is to be combined with the **mechanism of nature** in a being that belongs to the **world of sense** <note>The physical world we experience through our senses, which Kant believes operates like a predictable machine.</note>: a difficulty which, even after everything previously mentioned has been granted, still threatens freedom with complete destruction. Yet, despite this danger, one circumstance offers hope for a <margin>10</margin> successful outcome for the defense of freedom. Specifically, this same difficulty weighs much more heavily (in fact, as we shall soon see, exclusively) on the system that views existence in time and space as the existence of **things-in-themselves** <note>Objects as they exist independently of our perception. Kant argues that time and space are just human "filters," not properties of things-in-themselves.</note>. Therefore, it does not force us to abandon our primary assumption of the **ideality of time** as a mere form of sensory intuition—consequently as a mere mode of representation belonging to the subject as part of the world of sense <note>original: "abzugehen" (to depart). Scholars like Hartenstein and Natorp suggest "aufzugeben" (to abandon) fits the context of sticking to the theory that time is mental rather than physical.</note>—and <margin>20</margin> only requires us to reconcile freedom with this idea.

For even if it is granted to us that the <term>intelligible subject</term> <note>The "true self" that exists beyond time and space, and therefore isn't bound by physical laws.</note> can still be free regarding a given action (even though, as a subject belonging to the world of sense, it is mechanically determined), it still seems that as soon as one assumes God, as the universal **primordial being**, is also the cause of the existence of **substance**, one must also grant a certain conclusion. (This is a proposition that must never be abandoned without also abandoning the concept of God as the "Being of all beings" and His **all-sufficiency**, <margin>30</margin> upon which everything in theology depends). One must also grant <note>The word "also" is missing in the second edition of the original book.</note> that man's actions have their determining ground in something that is entirely [101] beyond his power—namely, in the **causality** of a **Supreme Being** distinct from him, upon whom his existence and the entire determination of his causality absolutely depend. In fact, if the actions of man, as...

<summary>Kant explores a major threat to human freedom: if God created human beings as substances, then God might be the ultimate cause of our actions, potentially making us puppets. He suggests that his theory—that time and space are only human perceptions—is the only way to resolve this conflict and preserve free will.</summary>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 129, where Kant addresses a major objection to his theory of freedom: if God created human beings, and God is the cause of everything, how can humans be responsible for their own actions? He is currently explaining the dire consequences for freedom if we do not accept his "ideality of time" (the idea that time is a human perception rather than a property of the world as it truly is).</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>
<header>First Part, Book I, Chapter 3.</header>
<meta>catchword: Hin-</meta>

...belong to its determinations in time—not merely as determinations of the being as an appearance, but as a **thing-in-itself** <note>The object as it exists independently of our senses.</note>—then **freedom** could not be saved. Man would be a puppet or a **Vaucansonian**<sup>a</sup> <note>Jacques de Vaucanson (1709–1782) was a famous French inventor of complex mechanical automatons.</note> **automaton**, constructed and wound up by the supreme Master of all artworks; and while self-consciousness would indeed make him a **thinking automaton**, the consciousness of his **spontaneity** <note>The capacity to act independently.</note> (if it were mistaken for freedom) would be a mere illusion. It would only deserve to be called "freedom" in a comparative sense, because although the immediate causes of his movement—and a long series of causes leading up to them—are internal, the final and highest cause is nonetheless found entirely in a **foreign hand**. Therefore, I do not see how those who still persist in viewing **time** and **space** as determinations belonging to the existence of things-in-themselves can hope to avoid the **fatalism** <note>The belief that all events are predetermined and inevitable.</note> of <margin>20</margin> actions here. If they simply admit (as the otherwise sharp-witted **Mendelssohn**<sup>b</sup> <note>Moses Mendelssohn (1729–1786) was a leading Jewish-German philosopher of the Enlightenment.</note> did) that both time and space are conditions necessarily belonging only to the existence of finite and derived beings, but not to the existence of the infinite **Original Being**, I fail to see how they justify the authority to make such a distinction. I also do not see how they expect to avoid the contradiction they commit when they view existence in time as a determination necessarily attached to finite things-in-themselves. For if God is the cause of this existence, yet He cannot be the cause of **time** (or **space**) itself—because these must be presupposed as a necessary condition prior to experience <note>original: "a priori"</note> for the existence of things—then His **causality** regarding the existence of these things must itself be conditioned by time. This would inevitably lead to all the contradictions against the concepts of His infinity and independence. To-

b) In his *Morning Hours, or On the Existence of God* (1785).

On the other hand, it is quite easy for us to distinguish the determination of the **divine existence** as independent of all **conditions of time**, in contrast to that of a being of the **sensory world** <note>The world as we experience it through our senses.</note>, as the existence of a **being in itself** from that of a **thing in appearance**. Therefore, if one does not accept that ideality of time and space, then only **Spinozism** <note>The philosophy of Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677), who argued that there is only one substance—God—and everything else is just a mode or property of God.</note> remains, in which [102]
<margin>10</margin> space and time are essential determinations of the **Primal Being** itself, while the things dependent on Him (including ourselves) are not **substances**, but merely **accidents** <note>In traditional philosophy, a "substance" exists on its own, while an "accident" (like a color or a thought) can only exist by belonging to a substance.</note> inhering in Him. This is because, if these things exist merely as His effects in **time**—which would be the condition of their existence in themselves—then the actions of these beings would also have to be merely His own actions, which He performed somewhere and at some time. Therefore, Spinozism, despite the absurdity of its fundamental idea, still concludes much more
<margin>20</margin> consistently than is possible according to the **theory of creation**, if substances—beings assumed to exist in themselves in time—are viewed as<sup>a)</sup> **effects** of a supreme cause, and yet not at the same time belonging to Him and His action,<sup>b)</sup> but rather as substances in their own right.

The resolution of the aforementioned difficulty occurs briefly and clearly in the following way: If existence in time is a merely **sensory way of representing things** <note>A way our minds organize information, rather than a property of reality itself.</note> used by thinking beings in the world,
<margin>30</margin> and consequently does not apply to them as **things in themselves**, then the **creation** of these beings is a creation of **things in themselves**. This is because the concept of creation does not belong to the sensory way of representing existence or to **causality**, but can only be referred to **noumena** <note>Reality as it exists independently of our senses; things as they are in themselves.</note>. Consequently, when I say of beings in the sensory world that they are **created**, I am considering them insofar as they are noumena. Just as it would be a contradiction to say that God is a creator of **appearances**, it is also a contradiction to say that He, as Creator, is the cause of actions in the

b) "belonging" [gehörig] addition by Hartenstein.

<summary>Kant argues that unless time is viewed as a human perception (ideality of time), we must logically accept Spinozism, where humans are just "accidents" of God. He resolves this by stating that God creates humans as "noumena" (things-in-themselves) outside of time, thus allowing for human freedom.</summary>

Since it is actually the concept of **freedom** which, among all the ideas of pure speculative reason, alone provides such a great extension in the field of the **supersensible** <note>The realm of reality that lies beyond sensory experience.</note>—even if only in regard to **practical knowledge** <note>Knowledge concerning how one ought to act, rather than how things are in nature.</note>—I ask myself: why has such a great fertility been granted to it exclusively, while the others indeed mark the empty space for pure possible **beings of the understanding** <note>original: "Verstandeswesen"; entities that can be thought but not perceived by senses, also called noumena.</note>, yet cannot determine the concept of them through anything? I soon realize that, since I can think nothing without a **category** <note>The fundamental "building blocks" of human thought, such as cause and effect.</note>, this category must first be sought even in the idea of reason concerning freedom, which I am dealing with here; in this case, it is the category of **causality**. I also realize that<sup>a)</sup>, even though no corresponding **intuition** <note>original: "Anschauung"; the direct mental representation of an object through the senses.</note> can be provided for the concept of freedom (since it is a **transcendent** <note>original: "überschwenglich"; a concept that exceeds the bounds of all possible experience.</note> concept), a sensory intuition must nevertheless first be given for the concept of the understanding (causality). It is for the **synthesis** <note>The act of combining different representations into one unified piece of knowledge.</note> of this causality that freedom demands the **unconditioned** <note>The ultimate starting point or cause that is not itself caused by anything else.</note>, and through this sensory intuition, its **objective reality** [104] is first secured. Now, all categories are divided into two classes: the **mathematical** ones, which concern merely the unity of synthesis in the representation of objects, and the **dynamic** ones, which concern the unity in the representation of the existence of objects. The former (those of magnitude and quality) always contain a synthesis of the **homogeneous** <note>Things of the same kind or nature.</note>. In this synthesis, the unconditioned cannot be found at all in relation to the conditioned given in sensory intuition in space and time, because the unconditioned itself would have to belong to space and time and would therefore always have to be **conditioned**<sup>b)</sup> again<sup>c)</sup>; therefore, also in the Dia—

...dialectics <note>original: "Dialektik"; here referring to the "Antinomies," where reason seems to contradict itself when thinking about the universe.</note> of pure theoretical reason, the two opposing ways of finding the **unconditioned** <term>Unconditioned: that which is not dependent on any prior cause or condition; the ultimate starting point.</term> and the **totality of conditions** for it were both false. The categories of the second class (those of **causality** and the **necessity** of a thing) did not require this homogeneity—this sameness of nature between the conditioned thing and its condition within the **synthesis** <note>The mental act of combining different representations into one piece of knowledge.</note>—at all. This is because we were not considering how an **intuition** <note>The direct sensory perception of an object.</note> is composed from a variety of parts within it. Instead, we were only considering how the existence 
<margin>10</margin>
of the corresponding conditioned object is added to the existence of the condition (as linked within the understanding)<sup>a)</sup>. In that case, it was permissible to place the **unconditioned**—even if otherwise left undefined—within the **intelligible world** <note>The world as it is in itself, accessible only to thought and not the senses.</note>, to account for what is entirely **conditioned** in the **sensible world** <note>The physical world we experience through our senses.</note> (regarding both the chain of cause and effect and the accidental existence of things themselves). This made the synthesis **transcendent** <note>Going beyond the limits of sensory experience.</note>. Therefore, in the Dialectic of pure speculative reason, it was found that the two seemingly 
<margin>20</margin>
opposite ways of finding the unconditioned for the conditioned did not, in fact, contradict each other. For example, in the synthesis of **causality**, one can think of a causality that is no longer conditioned by the senses (a first cause) to account for the conditioned series of causes and effects in the world of the senses. The very same action which, as part of the sensible world, is always sensibly conditioned—and therefore **mechanically necessary**—can nonetheless also have a foundation in a causality that is unconditioned by the senses. This is possible if the acting being is considered as belonging<sup>b)</sup> to the **intelligible world** 
<margin>30</margin>
and thus its action can be thought of as **free**. Now, the only thing that mattered was that this "possibility" be transformed into a "being." That is, one must be able to prove in an actual case, as if through a **fact** <note>original: "Faktum"; specifically the "Fact of Reason," which is the presence of the moral law in our minds.</note>, that certain actions presuppose such a causality (one that is intellectual and unconditioned by the senses), whether these actions are actual or merely commanded—that is, **objectively and practically necessary**. <meta>This page continues the "Critical Examination of the Analytic." Here, he explains how the moral law provides the proof for this, specifically regarding human freedom.</meta>

<page-num>135</page-num>
# Critical Examination of the Analytic

In the world of the senses, we could not hope to find this connection [105] because **causality through freedom** <note>The idea that a person can initiate an action spontaneously, rather than being pushed by a prior physical cause like a falling domino.</note> must always be sought outside the world of the senses, in the **intelligible** realm <note>The "intelligible world" refers to reality as it is in itself, which we can grasp through logic and reason even if we cannot see or touch it.</note>. However, no beings other than sensory ones are given to us for perception and observation. Therefore, nothing remained but to find an undeniable and objective principle of causality that excludes all sensory conditions from its determination—that is, a principle in <margin>10</margin> which reason no longer appeals to something else as a **determining ground** <term>Determining ground: the fundamental reason or motive that triggers a choice or action.</term> regarding causality, but rather contains that ground within itself through that very principle. In such a case, reason is **practical** as **pure reason** <note>"Pure reason" refers to our capacity to think and act based on logic alone, independent of our physical desires or environment.</note> itself. This principle, however, requires no searching or invention; it has long existed in the reason of all human beings and is woven into their very essence: it is the **principle of morality**.

Thus, that unconditioned causality and the power it represents—**freedom**—is established. Along with it, a being (myself) which belongs to the world of the senses is <margin>20</margin> simultaneously thought of as belonging to the intelligible world. This is not merely a vague or possible thought (which speculative reason had already determined was feasible), but is recognized **assertorically** <term>Assertoric: a statement of fact or reality, rather than just a possibility.</term> in relation to the law of its causality. Thus, the reality of the intelligible world is given to us, specifically from a **practical** <note>In Kant's philosophy, "practical" refers to the realm of action, ethics, and "what we ought to do."</note> perspective. This determination, which would be **transcendent** <margin>30</margin> <term>Transcendent: "overstepping" the limits of what we can actually experience or prove through science.</term> (extravagant) from a theoretical standpoint, is **immanent** <term>Immanent: "remaining within"; here meaning it is a valid and necessary part of our actual lived experience and moral choices.</term> from a practical one.

However, we could not take a similar step regarding the second **dynamical idea** <note>Ideas concerning the existence of things, specifically the relationship between cause and effect or necessity.</note>, namely that of a **necessary being** <note>A reference to God as the ultimate, necessary cause of the universe.</note>. We could not ascend to it from the world of the senses without the mediation of the first dynamical idea <note>Kant argues we can only find a path to the idea of God through the starting point of human freedom and morality.</note>. For if we had tried to do so, we would have had to risk the "leap" of leaving behind everything given to us and soaring toward that of which nothing is given to us, through <margin>40</margin> which we might have bridged the connection of such an intelligible being with the world of the senses (since the necessary being was to be recognized as existing outside of us);

<summary>Kant explains that while science cannot prove freedom, our internal sense of the "moral law" proves we belong to an intelligible world beyond mere physics. He argues that this moral freedom is the only bridge we have to understand higher concepts like a necessary being or God.</summary>

...which, on the other hand, is quite possible in regard to our own **subject**, insofar as it determines itself through the ***moral law*** on one hand as an ***intelligible being*** <note>A being that exists in the realm of pure thought or reason, rather than just as a biological machine.</note> (by virtue of ***freedom***), while on the other hand it recognizes itself as acting in the ***world of the senses*** according to this determination, as is now clearly evident. The single ***concept of freedom*** allows us to avoid having to go outside of ourselves to find the ***unconditioned*** <note>The "unconditioned" is the ultimate starting point or cause that doesn't depend on anything else.</note> and ***intelligible*** to accompany the ***conditioned*** and ***sensory***. 

<margin>[106] 10</margin> For it is our ***reason*** itself which, through the ***highest and unconditioned practical law***, recognizes itself—and the ***being*** that is conscious of this law (***our own person***)—as belonging to the ***pure world of the understanding***. It even does so by defining the specific way it can act as such a being. Thus, it becomes clear why, in the entire faculty of reason, only the ***practical*** use can be the one that helps us beyond the ***world of the senses*** and provides us with ***knowledge*** of a ***supersensible order*** and ***connection***. However, this knowledge can only be extended as far as is strictly necessary for ***pure practical purposes*** <note>Meaning we only know about this "higher world" enough to act morally, not enough to describe exactly how it looks or works like a scientist would describe a plant.</note>.

<margin>20</margin> Allow me to draw attention to just one more thing on this occasion: namely, that every step taken with ***pure reason***, even in the practical field where one does not consider subtle speculation at all, nevertheless connects so precisely and automatically to all aspects of the ***Critique of theoretical reason*** <note>Kant's earlier work, the *Critique of Pure Reason*, which dealt with what we can know about the physical world.</note>, as if each step had been thought out with deliberate caution merely to provide confirmation for it. 

<margin>30</margin> Such an exact agreement of the most important principles of ***practical reason*** with the often seemingly too subtle and unnecessary remarks of the ***Critique of speculative reason*** is surprising and fills one with wonder. This agreement was in no way sought out, but (as one can convince oneself by pursuing ***moral inquiries*** to their ***principles***) is found naturally. It reinforces the ***maxim*** <term>Maxim: a personal rule or principle that a person follows when they act.</term>, already recognized and praised by others, to continue one's path undisturbed in every scientific investigation with all possible accuracy and openness, without...
<margin>40</margin>

<meta>This page concludes a specific methodological reflection within the "Critical Examination of the Analytic." Kant is explaining his "isolation" method: focusing entirely on the internal logic of a philosophical problem before worrying about how it fits with other established systems. He argues that if the internal logic is sound, it will eventually harmonize with external truths on its own.</meta>

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>Critical Examination of the Analytic.</header>

<warning>Heavy bleed-through from the reverse side of the page and transparency from the following page.</warning>

...to concern oneself with what it might conflict with outside its own field, but rather to carry it out for itself alone, as truthfully and completely as one can. Frequent observation has convinced me that, once one has brought this <gloss>this <note>The original German manuscript used the word "diese"; the editor Friedrich Grillo corrected this to "dieses" to properly agree with the gender of the noun for "business" or "task."</note></gloss> task to a conclusion, things that halfway through seemed very doubtful in light of other external doctrines eventually—if I simply set aside those doubts and focused only on my own task until its completion—perfectly coincided in an unexpected way with <margin>10</margin> what had been found on its own, without the slightest regard for those other doctrines, and without any bias or preference for them. Writers would save themselves many errors and much wasted effort (because such effort was based on mere illusion <note>original: "Blendwerk," referring to a deceptive appearance or a "smoke and mirrors" effect.</note>) if they could only resolve to proceed with a bit more openness.

<summary>Kant defends his rigorous philosophical method, arguing that researchers should focus on the internal consistency of their work rather than worrying about external contradictions, as truth will eventually harmonize with itself. He suggests that many intellectual errors arise from trying to force a harmony between theories too early in the process.</summary>

<keywords>methodology, philosophical consistency, Friedrich Grillo, Immanuel Kant, intellectual honesty, illusion, Critique of Practical Reason</keywords>

# Dialectic of Pure Practical Reason.
---
## First Chapter.
### Concerning a Dialectic of Pure Practical Reason in General.

Pure reason always has its dialectic <note>In Kant’s philosophy, a "dialectic" is not just a conversation, but a "logic of illusion"—a study of the natural ways human reason gets tripped up by its own structure.</note>, whether one considers it in its speculative or its practical use; for it demands the absolute <margin>10</margin> totality of conditions for a given conditioned <term>conditioned: something that depends on something else to exist or be true</term> thing, and this totality can absolutely only be found in things as they are in themselves. However, since all concepts of things must be related to intuitions <note>original: "Anschauungen," referring to the way our minds directly receive sensory information.</note>, which for us humans can never be anything but sensory, and which therefore allow us to know objects not as things in themselves but merely as appearances, in whose series of the conditioned and conditions the unconditioned <term>unconditioned: the ultimate or absolute reality that doesn't depend on anything else</term> can never be found: thus an unavoidable illusion <margin>20</margin> arises from the application of this idea of reason—this totality of conditions (and thus the unconditioned)—to appearances as if they were things in themselves (for they are always taken as such in the absence of a cautionary critique <note>Kant's "Critique" is his method of testing the limits and boundaries of what the human mind can actually know.</note>). This illusion, however, would never be noticed as deceptive if it did not betray itself through a conflict of reason with itself in the application of its own principle—to assume the unconditioned for everything conditioned—to appearances themselves.<sup>a)</sup> By this, however, reason is forced to...

---

a) Kant: "betrayed" [plural]; corrected by Rosenkranz and Hartenstein <note>original: "verrieten"; corrected to "verriete" [singular] to grammatically match the subject "illusion." Editors Rosenkranz and Hartenstein were major 19th-century scholars who standardized Kant's texts.</note>.

<note>Vocabulary: Dialectic, Reason, Totality, Conditioned, Unconditioned, Appearances, Critique, Kant, Rosenkranz, Hartenstein</note>

<summary>Kant introduces the "Dialectic," explaining that human reason naturally but mistakenly tries to find absolute, unconditioned truths within the world of sensory appearances, leading to internal logical conflicts. He argues that this "illusion" is inevitable unless corrected by a critical examination of how we think.</summary>
<keywords>pure reason, dialectic, appearances, the unconditioned, things-in-themselves, philosophical illusion, totality of conditions</keywords>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the "Dialectic of Pure Practical Reason." It transitions from describing the "antinomy" (a logical conflict) as a "beneficial error" to defining the "Highest Good" as the ultimate goal of practical reason. It also begins to redefine "philosophy" in its ancient sense as the science of wisdom and the highest good.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>Of a Dialectic of Pure Practical Reason in General.</header>

...to track down this illusion, to see where it arises and how it can be cleared away. This can only be achieved through a complete critique of the entire capacity for pure reason. In fact, the **antinomy** <term>antinomy: a conflict between two laws or conclusions that both seem equally logical</term> of pure reason, which is revealed in its dialectic, is actually the most beneficial error that human reason could have stumbled into. It eventually forces us to look for the key to escape this labyrinth. When found, this key <margin>10</margin> also uncovers what we were not searching for but still require: a vista into a higher, unchanging order of things. We are already part of this order, and we can now be given specific instructions on how to continue our existence there in accordance with the highest purpose of reason. [108]

How that natural dialectic is to be resolved in the **speculative use** of pure reason, and how error—arising from an otherwise natural illusion—can be prevented <margin>20</margin>, can be found in detail in the *Critique* of that faculty <note>Kant is referring to his previous work, the *Critique of Pure Reason*, which focused on theoretical knowledge.</note>. But reason fares no better in its **practical use**. As pure practical reason, it also seeks the **unconditioned** <term>unconditioned: the absolute or ultimate reality that does not depend on anything else</term> for the "practically-conditioned" (which rests on our inclinations and natural needs). It does not seek this as the determining ground of the will—for that has already been provided in the **moral law**—but rather as the absolute totality of the object of pure practical reason, known as the **highest good**. <margin>30</margin>

Defining this idea "practically"—that is, in a way sufficient for the **maxims** <term>maxims: the personal rules or principles we use to make decisions</term> of our rational behavior—constitutes the **doctrine of wisdom**. This, in turn, as a science, is **philosophy** in the sense the ancients understood the word: as an instruction in the concept of where the highest good is to be placed, and the behavior by which it is to be attained. It would be beneficial if we left this word with its ancient meaning: a doctrine <margin>40</margin> of the highest good, insofar as reason strives to turn it into a science. For on the one hand, the restrictive...

<summary>Kant explains that the contradictions of reason (antinomies) are beneficial because they force us to discover a higher order of reality. He defines the "highest good" as the ultimate object of practical reason and argues for returning to the ancient definition of philosophy as the study of this good.</summary>

<margin>10</margin>
...condition would be appropriate to the Greek expression <note>original: "griechischen Ausdrucke", referring to *philosophia* (φιλοσοφία)</note> (which means **love of wisdom**) and yet at the same time be sufficient to include the love of science—and thus all speculative knowledge of reason, insofar as it serves reason both for that concept and for the practical determining ground—under the name of **philosophy**, while never losing sight of the main purpose for whose sake alone it can be called the doctrine of wisdom.

<margin>[80]</margin>
On the other hand, it would also not be a bad thing to deter the self-conceit of anyone who dared to claim the title of **philosopher** for themselves, if one were to hold up to him, through the very definition, a standard of self-estimation that would greatly lower his pretensions. For to be a **teacher of wisdom** <margin>20</margin> would likely mean something more than being a student who has still not come far enough to lead himself, much less others, with certain expectation of such a high goal. It would mean a master in the knowledge of wisdom, which says more than a modest man would ever claim for himself. 

<margin>[109]</margin>
Thus, philosophy, like wisdom itself, would still remain an ideal which objectively is represented completely in reason alone; subjectively, however, for the person, it is only the goal of his unceasing efforts. To be in possession of this under the assumed name of a philosopher is something only he is entitled to claim who can also exhibit its infallible effect <margin>30</margin> in his own person as an example (in the mastery of himself and the unquestioned interest that he takes preeminently in the **common good**). This is what the ancients also demanded in order to be able to deserve that title of honor.

Regarding the **Dialectic** <term>dialectic: here, the investigation into the contradictions that arise when reason tries to understand the absolute</term> of pure practical reason in the point of determining the concept of the **highest good** <term>highest good: (Latin: *summum bonum*) the ultimate end of moral action, which Kant argues must involve both perfect virtue and the happiness that follows it</term> (which, if its resolution is successful, allows us to expect a most beneficial effect, just like the dialectic of theoretical reason; this is because the sincerely conducted and unhidden contradictions of pure practical reason <margin>40</margin> with itself lead to a complete critique of its own faculty)...

## Second Main Chapter.

### On the Dialectic of Pure Reason in Determining
### the Concept of the Highest Good.

The concept of the "highest" already contains an ambiguity that, if one does not pay attention to it, can cause unnecessary disputes. The "highest" can mean either the **supreme** <note>original: "supremum"</note> or the **complete** <note>original: "consummatum"</note>. The first is that condition which is itself unconditioned—that is, it is not subordinate to any other <margin>10</margin> condition <note>original: "originarium", meaning primary or original</note>. The second is that whole which is not a part of an even larger whole of the same kind <note>original: "perfectissimum", meaning most perfect or most complete</note>. It was proven in the **Analytic** <note>The "Analytic" refers to the first major section of this book, where Kant laid out the basic rules of morality</note> that **virtue** (as the worthiness to be happy) is the supreme condition of everything that might seem desirable to us—and thus of all our pursuit of happiness—and is therefore the **supreme good**. However, it is not yet the entire and **complete good** as the object of the desire of rational, finite <margin>20</margin> beings. For to be that, **happiness** is also required, and not just in the biased eyes of a person who makes themselves their own end, but even in the judgment of an impartial reason, which regards happiness in the world generally as an end in itself. For to be in need of happiness, and also worthy of it, but nevertheless not to participate in it, cannot coexist at all with the perfect will of a rational being who simultaneously has all power—even if we only think of <margin>30</margin> such a being for the sake of an experiment. Now, virtue and happiness together constitute the possession of the highest good in a person. But in this context, happiness must also be distributed exactly in proportion to **morality** (as the value of the person and their worthiness to be happy). This constitutes the highest good of a possible world. <margin>[111]</margin> This signifies the whole, the complete good, in which virtue is always the supreme good as the condition, because it has no further condition above it, while happiness...

<summary>Kant distinguishes between the "supreme" good (virtue) and the "complete" good (virtue combined with happiness), arguing that a perfect world requires happiness to be distributed according to moral worth.</summary>

<keywords>Highest Good, virtue, happiness, supreme good, complete good, morality, worthiness to be happy, Analytic of Pure Practical Reason</keywords>

--- Page 197 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Highest Good" from page 142, focusing on the relationship between virtue and happiness. The previous page established that while virtue is the supreme condition, the complete good requires both virtue and happiness.</meta>

<page-num>143</page-num>
<header>Dialectic of Pure Reason in the Determination of the Concept of the Highest Good.</header>

...always something that is indeed pleasant to the one who possesses it, but is not good in itself absolutely and in every respect, but rather always presupposes moral conduct according to the law as its condition.

Two determinations necessarily combined in one concept must be linked as ground and consequence, and specifically either in such a way that this unity is considered **analytic** <note>A logical connection where one concept is already contained within the other.</note> (a logical connection) or **synthetic** <note>A real connection where two distinct concepts are joined together, often through cause and effect.</note> (a real connection); the former following the law of identity, the latter following the law of causality. <margin>10</margin> The connection of **virtue** with **happiness** can therefore be understood in two ways: either the effort to be virtuous and the rational pursuit of happiness are not two different actions, but entirely identical ones—in which case the former would require no other maxim than the one underlying the latter <note>A textual variant by Kellermann suggests reading "than that of the latter" <insert>original: "als der letzteren"</insert> here.</note>; or that connection is based on virtue producing happiness as something distinct from the consciousness of virtue, just as a cause <margin>20</margin> produces an effect.

Of the ancient Greek schools, there were actually only two that followed the same method in determining the concept of the highest good, insofar as they did not treat virtue and happiness as two different elements of the highest good, and thus sought the unity of the principle according to the rule of identity; however, they differed in which of the two they chose as the fundamental concept. The **Epicurean** <note>Followers of Epicurus (341–270 BCE) who argued that the goal of life is to attain a state of tranquility and freedom from fear/pain.</note> said: to be conscious of one's maxim leading to happiness is virtue. The **Stoic** <note>Followers of a school founded by Zeno of Citium (c. 300 BCE) which taught that virtue is the only good and is sufficient for happiness.</note> said: to be conscious of one's virtue is happiness. To the former, **prudence** <note>Practical wisdom in managing one's own affairs for the sake of happiness.</note> was the same as morality; to the second, who chose a higher name for virtue, **morality** alone was true wisdom.

It is regrettable that the sharp-wittedness of these men (whom one must nonetheless admire for attempting all conceivable paths of philosophical exploration at such an early time) was <margin>40</margin> unfortunately applied to finding identity between extremely dissimilar concepts—that of happiness and that of...

<summary>This page analyzes the logical relationship between virtue and happiness, contrasting the Epicurean and Stoic views which both incorrectly attempted to define one concept as being identical to the other.</summary>
<keywords>Virtue, Happiness, Highest Good, Analytic, Synthetic, Epicureans, Stoics, Causality, Prudence, Morality</keywords>

--- Page 198 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of how the ancient Greek schools, specifically the Stoics and the Epicureans, attempted to solve the problem of the "highest good" by treating virtue and happiness as identical rather than distinct.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>
<header>Part One, Book II, Main Chapter 2.</header>

...virtue, to speculate on their identity. However, it was consistent with the dialectical <note>Referring here to the art of logical debate and the tendency to resolve contradictions through reasoning</note> spirit of their times—which even now sometimes misleads subtle minds—to resolve essential and never-to-be-reconciled differences in principles by attempting to turn them into a mere dispute over words. In this way, they artificially created an appearance of conceptual unity [112] merely under different names. This commonly occurs in cases where the unification of dissimilar grounds lies so deep or high, or would require such a complete overhaul of the doctrines otherwise accepted in the philosophical system, that one shrinks from engaging deeply with the real difference and prefers to treat it as a disagreement over mere formalities.

Indem both schools sought to speculate on the sameness of the practical principles of virtue and happiness, they were not in agreement about how they wanted to force this identity. Instead, they separated from one another by infinite distances. One placed its principle on the aesthetic <note>Used here in its original sense meaning "relating to sensory feeling or perception" rather than the study of beauty</note> side, the other on the logical side; the former in the consciousness of sensory needs, the other in the independence of practical reason from all sensory determining grounds. The concept of virtue was, according to the Epicurean, already contained in the maxim <note>A "maxim" is a personal rule or principle that a person chooses to follow in their actions</note> to promote one’s own happiness; on the contrary, according to the Stoic, the feeling of happiness was already contained in the consciousness of one’s virtue. What is contained in another concept, however, is indeed identical with a part of the containing whole, but not with the whole itself. Furthermore, two wholes can be specifically different from one another even if they consist of the exact same material, provided that the parts in both are combined into a whole in completely different ways. The Stoic maintained that virtue was the entire highest good, and happiness was merely the consciousness of possessing it as belonging to the state of the subject. The Epicurean maintained that happiness was the entire highest good, and virtue was merely the form of the maxim for pursuing it—namely, in the rational use of the means to achieve it.

<summary>Kant explains how the Stoics and Epicureans incorrectly tried to resolve the tension between virtue and happiness by claiming they were logically identical, though they disagreed on which one was the primary cause.</summary>

<keywords>virtue, happiness, highest good, Stoicism, Epicureanism, maxims, practical reason, dialectic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "highest good" (summum bonum), transitioning from the historical views of the Epicureans and Stoics to Kant's own explanation of why virtue and happiness are fundamentally different concepts that cannot be easily reconciled.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>The Antinomy of Practical Reason.</header>

Now, however, it is clear from the <term>Analytic</term> <note>The section of the book that examines the basic elements of moral reasoning</note> that the <term>maxims of virtue</term> and those of one’s own <term>happiness</term> are entirely different in kind with respect to their highest practical principle. Far from being in harmony—even though they both belong to a <term>highest good</term> to make the latter possible—they actually restrict and interfere with one another quite significantly within the same subject <note>In philosophy, the "subject" refers to the individual person or thinking agent</note>. Thus, the question: "How is the <term>highest good</term> practically possible?" remains an insoluble task despite all previous <term>attempts at coalition</term> <note>Historical attempts by philosophers to merge the concepts of virtue and happiness into one</note>. That which makes it a difficult task is the fact that <term>happiness</term> and morality are two specifically distinct elements of the <term>highest good</term>, and their connection therefore cannot be recognized analytically. (It is not the case that someone seeking their [113] <term>happiness</term> will find themselves virtuous simply by dissecting their concepts of behavior; nor that someone who follows <term>virtue</term> will, in the consciousness of such behavior, <term>by that very fact</term> <note>original: "ipso facto"</note> already find themselves happy). Instead, this connection must be a <term>synthesis of concepts</term> <note>A "synthesis" means combining two different ideas together rather than finding one already hidden inside the other</note>. Because this connection is recognized as <term>independent of experience</term> <note>original: "a priori"</note>—and thus practically necessary and not derived from experience—the possibility of the <term>highest good</term> does not rest on any empirical principles. Therefore, the deduction of this concept must be <term>transcendental</term> <note>A "transcendental" investigation looks at the underlying conditions that make our knowledge or experience possible</note>. It is <term>independent of experience</term> (morally) necessary to bring about the <term>highest good</term> through <term>freedom of the will</term>; the condition of its possibility must therefore also rest solely on grounds of knowledge that are <term>independent of experience</term>.

# I.

## The Antinomy of Practical Reason.

In the highest good that is practical for us—that is, the good to be made real through our will—<term>virtue</term> and <term>happiness</term> are thought of as necessarily connected, so that the one cannot be accepted by <term>practical reason</term> without the other also belonging to it. Now, this connection (like any connection in general) is either analytical or 
Kant, Critique of Practical Reason. 10

<summary>Kant argues that virtue and happiness are distinct concepts that do not naturally imply one another, creating a philosophical "antinomy" or contradiction that must be resolved. He concludes that their connection is not found through simple logic (analysis) but must be established through a transcendental synthesis based on moral necessity.</summary>
<keywords>Antinomy of Practical Reason, Highest Good, Virtue, Happiness, Synthesis, A Priori, Transcendental Deduction, Freedom of the Will</keywords>

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical breakdown of the "Highest Good." On the previous page, Kant established that virtue and happiness are not the same thing. Here, he explores the two ways they might be linked—either happiness causes virtue, or virtue causes happiness—and explains why both options seem impossible at first glance.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>
<header>Part One, Book II, Second Main Chapter. I, II.</header>

<term>synthetic</term> <note>A "synthetic" connection is one where two different concepts are joined together, like "heat" and "expansion," rather than one being a definition of the other.</note>. Since this given connection cannot be <term>analytic</term> <note>An "analytic" connection is one found within the definition itself, like "a triangle has three sides."</note>, as was just shown previously, it must be thought of as <term>synthetic</term> and specifically as the connection of a cause with its effect; because it concerns a practical good, that is, what is possible through action. It must therefore be either that the desire for <term>happiness</term> is the motivating cause for <term>maxims</term> <note>A "maxim" is a personal rule or principle that someone follows when they act.</note> of virtue, or the <term>maxim</term> of virtue must be the <margin>10</margin> efficient cause of <term>happiness</term>. The first is absolutely impossible; because (as was proven in the <term>Analytic</term> <note>The earlier section of this book where Kant analyzed the nature of duty.</note>) <term>maxims</term> that place the <term>determining ground</term> <note>The fundamental reason or motivation for a choice.</note> of the will in the desire for one's own happiness are not moral at all and can ground no virtue. But the second is also impossible, because every practical connection of causes and effects in the world—as a result of the determination of the will—does not depend on the moral intentions of the will, but rather on the knowledge of the <term>laws of nature</term> and the physical power to use them <margin>20</margin> for one’s own purposes. Consequently, no necessary connection between <term>happiness</term> and virtue, sufficient for the <term>highest good</term>, can be expected in the [114] world, even by the most punctual observation of the moral laws. Now, since the promotion of the <term>highest good</term>—which contains this connection in its very concept—is an <term>a priori</term> <note>Something known through reason alone, independent of experience.</note> necessary object of our will and is inseparably linked with the moral law, the impossibility of the former must also prove the falsehood <margin>30</margin> of the latter. Therefore, if the <term>highest good</term> is impossible according to practical rules, then the moral law, which commands us to promote it, must also be <term>fantastic</term> <note>In this context, "fantastic" means an empty fantasy or a delusional idea.</note> and directed toward empty, imagined ends, and therefore must be false in itself.

### II.

## Critical Resolution of the Antinomy of Practical Reason.

In the <term>antinomy</term> <note>A paradox or a conflict between two laws or principles that both seem equally logical.</note> of pure <term>speculative reason</term> <note>The use of reason to understand the world as it is, rather than how we ought to act.</note> there is a similar conflict between natural <margin>40</margin> necessity and freedom in the causality of...

<summary>Kant presents a paradox: for the "highest good" to exist, virtue must lead to happiness, yet in our physical world, nature does not reward good intentions with guaranteed joy. If this connection is impossible, Kant warns that the moral law itself might be a mere delusion.</summary>

<keywords>synthetic connection, analytic connection, happiness, virtue, maxims, highest good, a priori, moral law, antinomy, causality</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues the resolution of the "Antinomy," or the logical contradiction, regarding the Highest Good. On the previous page, Kant noted that virtue does not seem to naturally cause happiness in this world, and happiness certainly does not cause virtue. Here, he explains how these two can be reconciled by looking at human existence from two different perspectives.</meta>

<page-num>147</page-num>
<header>Resolution of the Antinomy of Practical Reason.</header>
<sig>10*</sig>

...events in the world. It <note>Referring to the logical conflict mentioned on the previous page.</note> was resolved by proving that there is no true conflict if one considers the events and even the world in which they occur (as one should) merely as appearances; for one and the same acting being, as an appearance (even to its own inner sense), has a causality <note>The relationship between cause and effect.</note> in the sensory world that is always in accordance with the mechanism of nature. However, regarding that same event, insofar as the acting person also views themselves as a <term>noumenon</term> <note>original: "Noumenon." A term for a thing as it exists in itself, independent of our sensory perception; the "intelligible" reality behind the appearance.</note> (as a pure intelligence in an existence that cannot be determined by time), they can contain a basis for determining that causality according to natural laws, which is itself free from all laws of nature.

It is now the same with the present antinomy of pure practical reason. The first of the two propositions—that the pursuit of happiness produces a ground for a virtuous disposition—is absolutely false; the second, however—that a <term>virtuous disposition</term> <note>original: "Tugendgesinnung." The internal moral character or mindset of a person.</note> necessarily produces happiness—is not absolutely false, but only conditionally so. It is false only if virtue is viewed as the form of causality in the sensory world, and therefore, if I assume existence in that world to be the only kind of existence for a rational being. But I am not only authorized to think of my existence also as a <term>noumenon</term> in a world of the understanding, but I even have a purely intellectual determining ground of my causality (in the sensory world) provided by the moral law.

Thus, it is not impossible that the morality of one’s disposition has a necessary connection—if not a direct one, then an indirect one (mediated by an intelligible Author of nature)—as a cause to happiness as an effect in the sensory world. In a nature that is merely an object of the senses, such a connection could never occur except by chance, and would not be sufficient for the highest good.

Therefore, despite this apparent conflict of practical reason with itself, the highest good—the necessary highest goal of a morally determined will—is a true object of the...

<summary>Kant resolves the contradiction between virtue and happiness by arguing that while they aren't linked by the laws of physics, they can be linked if we view ourselves as spiritual or "noumenal" beings. He suggests that a higher power (an "Author of nature") could bridge the gap between being a good person and being a happy one.</summary>

This reality was initially challenged by that <term>Antinomy</term> <note>A logical contradiction between two principles that both seem true. 10 When we find ourselves forced to seek the possibility of the "highest good"—that goal of all moral desires set by reason for all rational beings—at such a distance, namely in connection with an <term>intelligible world</term> <note>The "noumenal" realm; a world of pure thought or spiritual reality that exists beyond what our physical senses can perceive.</note>, it must seem strange that philosophers of both ancient and modern times have nonetheless been able to find, or have been able to persuade themselves they were conscious of, happiness and <term>virtue</term> <note>The moral strength of a person's will in following their duty.</note> in quite proper proportion even in this life (in the sensory world).

The former, Epicurus, was not so low-minded in his practical instructions as one might conclude from the principles of his theory (which he used for explanation rather than for action), or as many interpreted him, being misled by his use of the term "voluptuousness" instead of "contentment." On the contrary, he included the most selfless practice of the good among the types of enjoyment providing the most heartfelt joy; 30 and the self-sufficiency and the curbing of inclinations, just as any of the strictest <term>moral philosophers</term> might demand, belonged to his plan for pleasure (by which he understood a constantly cheerful heart). In this, he differed from the <term>Stoics</term> primarily in that he placed the motive for action in this pleasure, which the latter—and indeed rightly—refused to do. <margin>[116]</margin> For on the one hand, the virtuous <term>Epicurus</term>, like many morally well-meaning men even now who do not think deeply enough about their principles, fell into the error of already assuming a virtuous <term>disposition</term> <note>original: "Gesinnung." The internal moral character or fundamental orientation of a person's will.</note> in the persons for whom he first wanted to provide the motivation for virtue.

(and in fact, the person of integrity cannot find themselves happy if they are not first aware of their integrity; for with such a <term>disposition</term> <note>original: "Gesinnung." <meta>This page continues the discussion of the "fallacy of subreption," where a person mistakes the moral satisfaction of doing their duty for a physical or sensory pleasure. It explores the sublime nature of the human will being moved by pure reason rather than by the promise of happiness.</meta>

# First Part, Book II, Second Main Chapter. II.

...a feeling of pleasantness that is expected from the desired action would have done. Thus, we easily mistake what we ourselves *do* for something that we merely passively *feel*. We take the **moral incentive** <note>original: "moralische Triebfeder." The internal "spring" or motive that pushes a person to act according to morality.</note> for a **sensory impulse**, just as usually happens in the so-called "illusion of the senses"—in this case, the internal sense. 

<margin>[117]</margin>

There is something very **sublime** in human nature: to be directly determined to act by a pure **law of reason**. Even the illusion of taking the subjective side of this intellectual determination of the **will** for something "aesthetic" <note>In this context, "aesthetic" refers to feelings and sensations rather than the study of beauty.</note> and the effect of a special sensory feeling (since an "intellectual feeling" would be a contradiction) is remarkable. It is also of great importance to draw attention to this quality of our **personality** and to cultivate the effect of reason on this feeling as much as possible. 

However, one must also be careful not to degrade and disfigure the genuine, true incentive—the law itself—as if with a false veneer. This happens through "insincere praises" of this moral ground of determination, by treating feelings of special joy as the *reasons* for acting (when they are actually only the *consequences*). Therefore, **respect**, and not pleasure or the enjoyment of **happiness**, is something for which no prior feeling based on reason is possible (since such a feeling would always be aesthetic and **pathological** <note>original: "pathologisch." In Kantian philosophy, this refers to feelings or desires caused by our physical nature and needs, rather than our rational will.</note>). 

Instead, the consciousness of the direct **necessitation of the will** by the law is hardly an **analogue of the feeling of pleasure**, even though it does the same thing in relation to our **faculty of desire**, but from different sources. Only through this way of thinking can we achieve what we seek: namely, that actions happen not just "in accordance with duty" (due to pleasant feelings), but "from duty," which must be the true goal of all **moral education**.

<meta>This page continues the resolution of the "Antinomy of Practical Reason." Having established that morality and happiness are not directly linked by cause and effect in the physical world, Kant now explores the specific kind of "satisfaction" a person feels when they act according to duty—a feeling distinct from sensory pleasure.</meta>

### Resolution of the Antinomy of Practical Reason.

...enjoyment, such as that of **happiness**, but nevertheless indicated a satisfaction with one’s existence, an analogue of happiness <note>original: "Analogon der Glückseligkeit." A state that resembles happiness but originates from a different source (reason rather than senses).</note> which must necessarily accompany the consciousness of **virtue**? Yes! This word is **self-satisfaction**, which in its proper meaning always indicates only a *negative* satisfaction with one’s existence, in which one is conscious of needing nothing. **Freedom**, and the consciousness of it as a capacity to follow the **moral law** with a predominant **disposition**, is independence from inclinations <note>original: "Neigungen." These are our sensory desires, cravings, and natural impulses.</note>—at least as *determining* causes (even if not as *affecting* causes) of our desire. 

<margin>10</margin>
<margin>[118]</margin>

Insofar as I am conscious of this freedom while following my **moral maxims** <note>A "maxim" is a personal rule or principle that someone chooses to live by.</note>, it is the only source of an unchanging satisfaction that is necessarily connected to it and rests on no special feeling; this may be called **intellectual** satisfaction. The **aesthetic** satisfaction (which is improperly so named), resting on the gratification of inclinations—no matter how subtly they may be refined—can never be adequate to what one thinks about it. For inclinations change, growing with the indulgence one allows them, and they always leave behind an even greater void than one had intended to fill. Therefore, they are always burdensome to a **rational being**; even if he cannot lay them aside, they still wring from him the wish to be rid of them. 

<margin>20</margin>
<margin>30</margin>

Even an inclination toward what is dutiful (for example, toward **beneficence**) can indeed greatly facilitate the effectiveness of moral maxims, but it cannot produce any. For in the maxim, everything must be directed toward the **representation of the law** as the **determining ground**, if the action is to contain not merely **legality** but also **morality**. 

<margin>40</margin>

**Inclination** is blind and slavish, whether it be good-natured or not; and **reason**, where **morality** is at stake, must not merely represent the role of a guardian to inclination, but must, without regarding it at all, look after its own interest entirely alone as **pure practical reason**. Even this feeling of compassion and tender-hearted sympathy...

<summary>Kant defines "self-satisfaction" as an intellectual contentment arising from the consciousness of one's freedom and independence from sensory desires. He argues that true morality must be determined by the moral law of reason alone, rather than by "slavish" inclinations, even benevolent ones.</summary>
<keywords>self-satisfaction, moral law, freedom, inclinations, maxims, legality vs. morality, pure practical reason, rational being</keywords>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page continues the resolution of the "Antinomy of Practical Reason." Having established that morality and happiness are not directly linked by cause and effect in the physical world, Kant now explores the specific kind of "satisfaction" a person feels when they act according to duty—a feeling distinct from sensory pleasure.</meta>

# First Part, Book II, Chapter 2. II.

...inclination, when it precedes the deliberation of what duty is and becomes the basis for determining our actions, is burdensome to right-thinking persons. It confuses their carefully considered **maxims** <note>A "maxim" is a personal rule or principle that someone chooses to live by.</note> and causes them to wish to be rid of a) <note>original: "seiner" (of it). The scholar Karl Vorländer suggests this refers to the impulse or inclination itself.</note> such impulses and to be subject solely to the law-giving power of **Reason**.

From this, we can understand how the awareness of this capacity of pure practical reason through action (**virtue**) can produce a consciousness of mastery <margin>10</margin> over one’s inclinations. This awareness brings independence from those desires and, consequently, from the dissatisfaction that always follows them. This results in a "negative" satisfaction with one’s state—that is, a sense of contentment—which at its source is satisfaction with one’s own person. In this way, **freedom** itself becomes capable of a kind of enjoyment. This cannot be called **happiness** <note>original: "Glückseligkeit." In Kant's view, happiness is based on the fulfillment of sensory desires.</note>, because it does not depend on a positive contribution of feeling; nor, strictly speaking, can it be called **bliss** <margin>20</margin> <note>original: "Seligkeit." Often used to describe the divine state of perfect, holy happiness.</note>, because it does not entail complete independence from all inclinations and needs. However, it is similar to the latter, insofar as the determination of the will can at least keep itself free from the influence of those desires. Thus, in its origin at least, it is analogous to the **self-sufficiency** <note>original: "Selbstgenügsamkeit."</note> that can only be attributed to the Supreme Being.

<margin>[119]</margin> From this resolution of the **Antinomy** <note>An "antinomy" is a logical contradiction between two principles that both seem true.</note> of pure practical reason, it follows that in practical principles, we can think of a natural and necessary connection <margin>30</margin> between the consciousness of **morality** and the expectation of a proportionate happiness as its consequence. We can at least think of this as *possible* (though we cannot, of course, fully know or perceive it as a fact). On the other hand, it is impossible for the pursuit of happiness to produce morality. Therefore, morality is the supreme good (as the first condition of the **highest good**), while happiness constitutes its second element—but only in such a way that happiness is the morally <margin>40</margin> conditioned, yet necessary, consequence of the former.

He establishes that morality is the primary requirement for the "highest good," with happiness being its necessary consequence rather than its cause.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on how the "Highest Good" (the union of virtue and happiness) must be viewed as a goal of reason. It then transitions into a new section (Chapter III) exploring the relationship between our "speculative" reason—the part of our mind that seeks to understand how the world works—and our "practical" reason—the part that tells us how we ought to act.</meta>

# Primacy of Pure Practical Reason.
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

In this subordination <note>referring to the subordination of the desire for happiness to the requirements of morality</note> alone is the **Highest Good** <term>Highest Good: In German, "das höchste Gut" (Latin: summum bonum); the perfect union of moral virtue and the happiness that should follow it.</term> the entire object of pure practical reason. Reason must necessarily represent the Highest Good as possible, because it is a command of that same reason to contribute everything possible to its production. However, the possibility of such a connection between the "conditioned" (happiness) and its "condition" (virtue) belongs entirely to the **supersensible** <note>original: "übersinnlichen"; that which exists beyond the reach of our physical senses or the laws of nature.</note> relationship of things and cannot be given according to the laws of the sensory world. 
<margin>10</margin>Nevertheless, the practical consequences of this idea—namely, the actions aimed at making the Highest Good a reality—do belong to the sensory world. Therefore, we will seek to represent the grounds of that possibility: first, regarding what is directly within our power; and second, regarding what Reason offers us (as necessarily required by practical principles) as a supplement to our own inability to achieve the Highest Good, which is not within our power.

### III.

<margin>20</margin>On the **Primacy** of Pure Practical Reason
->in its connection with Speculative Reason.<-

By "primacy" between two or more things connected by reason, I mean the priority of one to be the first determining ground of the connection with all the others. In a narrower practical sense, it signifies the priority of the interest of one insofar as the interests of the others are subordinated to it (an interest which cannot be placed second to any other). To every faculty of the mind, one can attribute an "interest"—that is, a principle that contains the condition under which alone the exercise of that faculty is promoted. 
<margin>30</margin>Reason, as the faculty of principles, determines the interest of all the powers of the mind, but it determines its own interest for itself. The interest of its **speculative use** <note>Speculative reason is our capacity to seek knowledge and theoretical understanding of the world.</note> consists in the knowledge of the object up to the highest **a priori** principles <note>original: "Prinzipien a priori"; principles that are known through pure reason alone, independent of sensory experience.</note>. The interest of its **practical use** consists in the determination of the **will** regarding the final and complete goal.

<summary>Kant argues that because reason commands us to pursue the "Highest Good," we must assume it is possible, even if it requires a power beyond our own. He then defines "Primacy" to explain why our moral requirements (practical reason) take precedence over our theoretical knowledge (speculative reason).</summary>
<keywords>Highest Good, Practical Reason, Speculative Reason, Primacy, Will, Supersensible, A Priori</keywords>
