# Part One, Book II, Main Section 2. III.

<meta>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing the word "erforder-lichkeit" (requirement/necessity) from the previous page.</meta>
-ness required for any use of reason whatsoever—namely, that its principles and assertions must not contradict one another—does not constitute a part of its interest, but is rather the condition of having reason at all; only the **extension** of reason, not mere consistency with itself, is counted as its interest.

If practical reason may not assume and think of as given anything further than what speculative reason <note>Speculative reason refers to our theoretical faculty that seeks to understand the world through logic and sensory data.</note> could offer it from its own insight, then speculative reason holds the **primacy**. But suppose practical reason had original principles *a priori* <term>a priori: Principles that are known through reason alone, independent of and prior to any sensory experience.</term> of its own, with which certain theoretical positions were inseparably connected, yet which were nevertheless beyond the reach of any possible insight of speculative reason (even though they must not contradict it). Then the question is: which interest is the supreme one? (It is not a question of which must give way, for one does not necessarily conflict with the other.) Must speculative reason—which knows nothingᵃ) of all that which practical reason offers for its acceptance—take up these propositions and try to unite them with its own concepts as a foreign possession transferred to it, even though they are transcendent <term>transcendent: In German, "überschwenglich"; refers to ideas that go beyond the limits of what can be proven by physical experience.</term> for it? Or is it entitled to stubbornly follow its own separate interest and, according to the **Canon of Epicurus** <note>Epicurus (341–270 BCE) was an ancient Greek philosopher who argued that all knowledge comes from the senses; his "Canon" refers to his rules for judging truth based on physical evidence.</note>, reject everything as empty sophistry that cannot authenticate its objective reality through obvious examples to be found in experience? This would be the case even if these ideas were woven ever so closely into the interest of the practical (pure) use of reason, and were not in themselves contradictory to theoretical reason, simply because they actually impair the interest of speculative reason by dissolving the boundaries it has set for itself and exposing it to all the nonsense or madness of the imagination.

In fact, if practical reason were grounded as **pathologically conditioned** <term>pathologically conditioned: In Kant's vocabulary, "pathological" does not mean "sick," but rather "determined by feelings, emotions, or physical desires" (from the Greek 'pathos') rather than by the moral law.</term>—that is, merely managing the interest of the inclinations under the sensory principle of happiness—then it would...

<meta>This page continues the investigation into whether our moral needs (practical reason) can demand that our logical mind (speculative reason) accept ideas like God or the soul, even if they cannot be proven by science. ### Primacy of Pure Practical Reason

—not make this demand on speculative reason <note>Speculative reason refers to our theoretical faculty that seeks to understand the world through logic and sensory data.</note> at all. Muhammad's paradise, or the "melting union" with the deity sought by theosophists and mystics—wherever anyone’s whim might lead—would force their monstrosities upon reason. It would be just as well to have no reason at all as to surrender it in such a way to every daydream. [121] But if pure reason can be and actually is practical of itself—as the consciousness of the **moral law** proves—it is still only one and the same reason <margin>10</margin> that judges according to *a priori* <term>a priori: Principles known through reason alone, independent of and prior to sensory experience.</term> principles, whether for theoretical or practical purposes. It is clear, then, that even if its capacity is not sufficient to establish certain propositions affirmatively in the former [theoretical] use—though these propositions do not contradict it—it <insert>the reason</insert>ᵃ) must still accept these very propositions as soon as they belong inseparably to the **practical interest** of pure reason. Although these ideas are like a foreign offer not grown on its own soil, they are sufficiently certified. Speculative reason must try to compare <margin>20</margin> and connect them with everything it has in its power. It must do so while modestly acknowledging that these are not its own insights, but rather **extensions** of its use for a different, namely practical, purpose. This is not at all contrary to its interest, which consists in the restriction of speculative mischief <note>Kant uses "mischief" or "impudence" (*Frevel*) to describe the mind's tendency to make wild claims beyond the limits of possible experience.</note>.

In the union, therefore, of pure speculative with pure practical reason into one single cognition, the latter holds the **primacy**. This is provided, <margin>30</margin> of course, that this union is not accidental or arbitrary, but is based *a priori* on reason itself and is therefore necessary. For without this subordination, a conflict of reason with itself would arise. If they were merely coordinated (equal in rank), the former [speculative reason] would close its boundaries tightly and admit nothing from the latter [practical reason] into its domain, while the latter would extend its boundaries over everything and, where its needs require, seek to include the former within its own— <margin>40</margin>

<note>original: "a) 'sie', ergänzt durch den Herausgeber; Kellermann will statt dessen Zeile 20 setzen: 'sie annehmen und'."</note>

<summary>Kant argues that because morality is a necessary part of human reason, our logical/theoretical mind must accept certain moral "postulates" (like God or immortality) as long as they don't lead to logical contradictions. This establishes the "primacy" of practical (moral) reason over theoretical logic.</summary>

First Part, Book II, Second Chapter. IV.

...seek to engage with. However, one cannot expect pure practical reason to be subordinate to speculative reason and thus reverse the order of priority, because all interest is ultimately practical. Even the interest of speculative reason <note>Speculative reason refers to our capacity for logical thought and scientific inquiry about the physical world.</note> is only conditional and is only complete when used for practical purposes.

### IV.

#### The Immortality of the Soul as a Postulate of Pure Practical Reason.

<margin>10</margin> Achieving the highest good <note>The "highest good" (*summum bonum*) for Kant is the perfect harmony of moral virtue and happiness.</note> in the world is the necessary object of a will that is determined by the moral law. In such a will, however, the complete **fitness** <note>original: *Angemessenheit*; this refers to a perfect, internal harmony between a person's character and the requirements of morality.</note> of one’s inner disposition to the moral law is the supreme condition of the highest good. This fitness must therefore be just as possible as its object, because the command to promote that object is contained within the same moral law. However, the complete **fitness** of the will to the moral law is **holiness** <note>In Kant’s vocabulary, "holiness" is a state where a will is naturally and perfectly in step with moral laws without any struggle.</note>, a **perfection** of which no rational <margin>20</margin> being in the world of the senses is capable at any point in its existence. Yet, because this perfection is nonetheless required as practically necessary, it can only be found in a **progress** <note>original: *Progressus*</note> toward that complete fitness that continues into infinity. According to the principles of pure practical reason, it is necessary to assume such a practical progression as the real object of our will.

This infinite **progress** is only possible, however, under the assumption of an existence and personality of that same rational being that continues into infinity (which is called the **immortality of the soul**). <margin>30</margin> Therefore, the highest good is practically possible only under the assumption of the **immortality of the soul**. Consequently, this immortality—being inseparably connected with the moral law—is a **postulate** <term>postulate: A theoretical proposition that cannot be proven by science or logic but must be assumed as a necessary condition for moral action.</term> of pure practical reason. (By this, I mean a theoretical proposition that is not provable as such, but which is inseparably attached to a practical law that holds unconditionally **from reason alone** <term>from reason alone: translated from the Latin "a priori," referring to knowledge independent of sensory experience.</term>).

<summary>Kant argues that since the moral law commands us to be "holy" (perfectly moral), and such perfection is impossible in a finite human life, we must assume the soul is immortal to allow for an infinite progression toward that goal.</summary>
<keywords>immortality of the soul, highest good, holiness, moral law, postulate of practical reason, infinite progress</keywords>

--- Page 211 ---
<meta>This page continues Kant’s discussion on why we must assume the soul is immortal. On the previous page, he established that morality requires "holiness" (perfect alignment with the moral law), but since no human can achieve this in a finite lifetime, he argues that our existence must extend infinitely to allow for endless progress toward that goal.</meta>

# The Immortality of the Soul as a Postulate, etc.

The principle of the moral destiny of our nature—the idea that we can reach complete **harmony** <note>original: *Angemessenheit*; a state where our inner character perfectly matches the demands of morality.</note> with the moral law only through a progress that extends into infinity—is of the greatest benefit. This is true not only regarding the current remedy for the incapacity of speculative reason <note>Meaning: logic alone cannot prove we are immortal, so our moral needs must "fill the gap."</note>, but also concerning religion. 

Without this principle, the moral law is either completely stripped of its holiness by being falsely depicted as **indulgent** <note>original: *indulgent*; lenient or permissive.</note> and thus adapted to our personal comfort; or, one stretches their calling and expectation toward an unattainable destiny—namely, the hoped-for complete acquisition of holiness of the will <gloss>[123]</gloss>—and loses oneself in fanatical <note>original: *schwärmende*; a term Kant used for "enthusiastic" or mystical delusions that lack a basis in reason.</note> theosophical dreams that completely contradict self-knowledge. Both of these paths only hinder the constant striving for punctual and thorough obedience to a strict, uncompromising, yet not merely ideal, but true command of reason. 

For a rational but finite being, only a **progression into infinity** from lower to higher stages of moral perfection is possible. The Infinite One <note>God.</note>, for whom the condition of time is nothing, sees in this series—which is endless for us—the whole of this harmony with the moral law. The holiness which His command relentlessly demands (to be consistent with His justice in the share of the highest good He assigns to everyone) is fully present in a single **intellectual intuition** <term>intellectual intuition: a direct, non-sensory way of knowing an object's essence entirely and all at once, which Kant believes is unique to God.</term> of the existence of rational beings. 

What the creature alone can attain regarding the hope of this share would be the consciousness of its proven **disposition** <term>disposition (Gesinnung): the fundamental orientation of a person's will; their underlying moral character.</term>. From its progress thus far from the worse to the morally better, and from the resulting awareness of its own unchanging resolution, it may hope for a further uninterrupted continuation of this progress, however long its existence may last, even beyond this life*), and 

---

*) The conviction of the unchangeability of one's <gloss>[123]</gloss> disposition in progressing toward the good seems nevertheless...

<summary>Kant argues that believing in the soul's immortality prevents two moral errors: lowering the standards of morality to suit human weakness, or falling into mystical delusions of achieving perfection instantly. He explains that while humans must progress forever, God sees this infinite journey as a completed whole.</summary>
<keywords>immortality, moral law, holiness, progress into infinity, God, intellectual intuition, disposition, theosophy</keywords>

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument for the immortality of the soul and begins Section V, which argues for the existence of God. On the previous page, Kant explained that since "holiness" (perfect moral character) is required by the moral law but impossible for humans to achieve in a finite time, we must assume our souls live on forever to make endless progress toward that goal.</meta>

<margin>[124]</margin>—so that never here, nor at any foreseeable future point of his existence, but only in the **infinity** of his continued duration (which God alone can survey), can he be fully adequate to the Will of God. This must happen without leniency or remission, as these would not be consistent with **justice**.

## V.
## The Existence of God as a Postulate of Pure Practical Reason.

The **moral law** led, in the preceding analysis, to a practical task that is prescribed solely by pure reason, without any intervention from sensory motives. This task is the necessary completion of the first and most important part of the **highest good** <note>The "highest good" (*summum bonum*) is the ultimate goal of human life: a state where people are both perfectly virtuous and perfectly happy.</note>—namely, **morality**. Because this task can only be fully resolved within an eternity, it led to the **postulate of immortality** <note>A "postulate" is an assumption that cannot be proven by logic but must be accepted as true if we are to make sense of our moral obligations.</note>. This same law [leads us to find] <note>The original German text here has a slight grammatical break or missing clause in the transcription: "Ebendieses Gesetz, wenn es nicht..."</note> it to be impossible for a creature to achieve this by itself. For this reason, Christian religious doctrine derives it solely from that same Spirit who works **sanctification** <note>original: *Heiligung*; the process of being made holy or spiritually pure.</note>—that is, this firm resolve and, with it, the consciousness of persistence in **moral progress**. 

But naturally, one who is conscious of having maintained progress toward the better from genuine moral motives for a long part of their life, right until its end, may indeed harbor the comforting hope (though not certainty) that they will persist in these principles even in an existence continued beyond this life. Although such a person is never justified in their own eyes here, and cannot hope to be so even with the expected future growth of their natural perfection (and with it, their duties), they may nevertheless have a prospect of a **blessed future** <note>original: *selige Zukunft*; a state of spiritual happiness or salvation.</note> within this progress. Although this progress concerns a goal pushed into infinity, it nevertheless counts as a "possession" in the eyes of God. For this is the expression reason uses to designate a complete well-being independent of all accidental causes in the world. Like **holiness**, this is an **idea** <term>Idea: In Kant's philosophy, a concept produced by reason that goes beyond anything we can experience with our senses.</term> that can only be contained in an infinite progress and its totality; thus, it is never fully reached by any creature.

<summary>Kant concludes that since humans can never reach moral perfection in a finite life, we must assume immortality to allow for eternal progress. He then begins to argue that the existence of God is a necessary assumption to bridge the gap between our moral efforts and the ultimate goal of the "highest good."</summary>
<keywords>God, Infinity, Justice, Postulate of Pure Practical Reason, Moral Law, Highest Good, Morality, Immortality, Sanctification, Moral Progress, Blessedness</keywords>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "highest good" from the previous page. Having established that the first part of the highest good—perfect morality—requires the postulate of the soul's immortality, Kant now argues that the second part—happiness—requires the postulate of God's existence.</meta>

### The Existence of God as a Postulate etc.

[The moral] law must also lead to the possibility of the second element of the **highest good** <note>The "highest good" (*summum bonum*) is the ideal state where people are both perfectly virtuous and perfectly happy.</note>, namely **happiness** proportioned to that **morality**. It must do so just as disinterestedly as before, from mere impartial reason; that is, it must lead to the assumption of the existence of a cause adequate to this effect. In other words, it must **postulate** <note>A "postulate" is an assumption that we cannot prove through logic but must accept as true to make sense of our moral obligations.</note> the existence of God as necessarily belonging to the possibility of the highest good (which is an object of our will necessarily connected with <margin>10</margin> the moral legislation of pure reason). We wish to present this connection convincingly.

**Happiness** is the state of a rational being in the world for whom, in the whole of his existence, everything goes according to his wish and will. It rests, therefore, on the harmony of nature with his whole purpose, as well as with the essential **determining ground** <note>original: *Bestimmungsgrund*; the underlying reason or motive that triggers a choice.</note> of his will. Now, the moral law, as a law of freedom, commands through determining grounds that are to be <margin>20</margin> entirely independent of nature and the harmony of nature with our **faculty of desire** (acting as motives). However, the acting rational being in the world is not simultaneously the cause of the world and of nature itself. Therefore, there is not the slightest ground in the moral law for a necessary connection between morality and the proportionate happiness of a being that belongs to the world as a part and is therefore dependent upon it. For this very reason, such a being <margin>30</margin> cannot be the cause of this nature through his will, nor can he, as far as his happiness is concerned, bring nature into thorough agreement with his practical principles by his own powers.

Nevertheless, in the **practical task** <insert>[125]</insert> of pure reason—that is, the necessary labor toward the highest good—such a connection is postulated as necessary: we ought to seek to promote the highest good (which must therefore be possible). Thus, the existence of a cause of all nature, distinct from nature itself, is also <margin>40</margin> **postulated**—a cause which contains the ground of this connection, namely, the exact harmony of happiness with morality. This supreme cause, however...

<summary>Kant argues that since humans cannot control nature to ensure that being good leads to being happy, we must assume (postulate) the existence of God to guarantee that these two eventually align.</summary>
<keywords>God, happiness, morality, highest good, postulate, pure reason, laws of nature, freedom</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical deduction of the "highest good." Having previously established that the moral law requires us to pursue a world where virtue and happiness are aligned, Kant now argues that such a world is only possible if we assume the existence of a supreme being who can bring nature into harmony with morality.</meta>

### Part One, Book II, Second Chapter. V.

...[the cause must] contain the ground of the agreement of nature not only with a law of the will of rational beings, but with the **representation** <note>original: *Vorstellung*; the mental concept or idea of the law that guides an individual's choices.</note> of this law, insofar as they make it the supreme **determining ground** <note>The underlying motive or reason that triggers the will to act.</note> of the will. Thus, it must agree not only with the outward form of morals, but also with their morality as their motivating ground—that is, with their **moral disposition** <note>original: *moralischen Gesinnung*; the internal heart or sincere intention behind an action.</note>. Therefore, the **highest good** in the world is only possible if we assume a supreme **cause** <margin>a) "Cause" was added by Grillo <note>Friedrich Gottlieb Grillo (1766–1817) was a contemporary of Kant and an editor of his texts.</note>.</margin> of nature that possesses a **causality** consistent with the moral disposition. Now, a being capable of acting according to the representation of laws is an **intelligence** (a rational being), and the causality of such a being according to this representation of laws is its **will**. Therefore, the supreme cause of nature, insofar as it must be presupposed for the highest good, is a being that is the cause (and consequently the **author**) of nature through understanding and will—that is, **God**.

Consequently, the **postulate** <note>A "postulate" is an assumption that cannot be proven by science or logic, but is required to make sense of our moral lives.</note> of the possibility of the highest **derived** good (the best world) is at the same time the postulate of the **reality** of a highest **original** good, namely the **existence of God**. Now, it was our duty to promote the highest good; therefore, it is not just a right, but a **necessity** connected with duty as a **need** to presuppose the possibility of this highest good. Since this possibility only exists under the condition of God’s existence, the presupposition of God is inseparably connected with duty. That is, it is **morally necessary** to assume the existence of God.

It is important to note here that this moral necessity is **subjective**—that is, it is a **need**—and not **objective**—that is, it is not a duty in itself. For there can be no duty to assume the existence of any thing (because that concerns only the **theoretical use of reason** <note>Theoretical reason deals with facts and what can be proven, whereas practical reason deals with action and ethics.</note>). Furthermore, this does not mean that assuming the existence of God is necessary as a ground of all obligation in general (for this ground rests, as has...

In this context, duty consists only in the labor toward the production and promotion of the **highest good** in the world, the possibility of which can therefore be **postulated**. However, our reason finds this possibility thinkable only under the presupposition of a supreme **intelligence** <note>In Kant's vocabulary, an "intelligence" refers to a rational being endowed with understanding and will.</note>, the assumption of whose existence is thus connected with the consciousness of our duty. This assumption itself belongs to **theoretical reason**; 
<margin>10</margin> viewed from that perspective alone, it is an **explanatory ground** regarded as a **hypothesis**. However, in relation to the intelligibility of an object given to us through the **moral law** (the highest good), it represents a need for a practical purpose. In this sense, it can be called **faith**, and specifically a **pure rational faith** <note>original: *reiner Vernunftglaube*; a belief that is not based on divine revelation, but arises necessarily from the requirements of human reason.</note>, because pure reason alone (in both its theoretical and practical use) is the source from which it springs.

From this deduction, it now becomes 
<margin>20</margin> understandable why the **Greek schools** could never reach the solution to their problem regarding the practical possibility of the highest good. This is because they always made the rule of the use that the human **will** makes of its **freedom** the sole and self-sufficient ground of that good, without—in their view—needing the existence of God for that purpose. They were indeed right to establish the **principle of morals** for itself, independently of this postulate, based solely on the relationship of reason 
<margin>30</margin> to the will, and thus to make it the supreme practical condition of the highest good; however, this principle was not the *entire* condition of its possibility. The **Epicureans** <term>Epicureans: Followers of Epicurus who believed that pleasure and the avoidance of pain were the primary goals of life.</term> had indeed adopted a completely false principle of morals as their supreme one, namely that of **happiness** <note>original: *Glückseligkeit*; often translated as "happiness" or "bliss," referring here to the satisfaction of all our inclinations.</note>, and had substituted a **maxim** of arbitrary choice based on each individual's inclination in place of a law. Yet, they proceeded consistently enough in that they 
<margin>40</margin> degraded their highest good in the same way—that is, in proportion to the baseness of their principle—and expected no greater happiness than what could be achieved through human prudence (which includes temperance and...

# The Existence of God as a Postulate, etc.

...not yet considered as a doctrine of religion, gives in this part a concept of the **highest good** (of the kingdom of God), which alone satisfies the strictest demands of practical reason. Previously, philosophers had regarded the incentives of the senses as something that only held power through a "weakness of the soul." <note>Kant refers to the Greek view that giving in to desire was a failure of will, rather than an inherent part of human nature that must be reconciled with virtue.</note> Virtue was therefore, among them, a certain **heroism** of the **Sage** rising above the <unclear>animal</unclear> <note>original: "die" - addition by the editor Hartenstein</note> nature of man, who is sufficient unto himself; one who indeed prescribes duties to others, but is himself <unclear>exalted</unclear> <note>original: "erhaben" or "erhoben" in different editions</note> above them and subject to no temptation to transgress the moral law. However, they could not have done all this if they had imagined this law in the same purity and strictness as the precept of the **Gospel** <note>original: "Evangelii"</note> does. If I understand an **idea** to be a perfection to which nothing in experience can be adequately given, the moral ideas are not on that account something "extravagant"—that is, something of which we could not even sufficiently determine the concept, or of which it is uncertain whether an object corresponds to it at all (like the ideas of <term>speculative reason</term> <note>Purely theoretical thinking that tries to go beyond what we can experience, such as debating the nature of the universe's beginning.</note>). Instead, they serve as **archetypes** of practical perfection, providing an indispensable guide for moral conduct and, at the same time, a standard of comparison. 

If I now consider **Christian morality** from its philosophical side, it would appear as follows when compared with the ideas of the Greek schools: the ideas of the <term>Cynics</term>, the <term>Epicureans</term>, the <term>Stoics</term>, and the <term>Christians</term> are **simplicity of nature**, **prudence**, **wisdom**, and **holiness**. [128] Regarding the way to attain these, the Greek philosophers differed from one another in that the <term>Cynics</term> <note>An ancient school that advocated for living a simple life according to nature, often rejecting social norms.</note> found common human understanding sufficient, while the others found only the path of science sufficient; both, however, considered the mere use of **natural powers** to be adequate for the task. Christian morality, because it sets its precept so purely and unyieldingly (as it must be), deprives man of the confidence of being fully adequate to it, at least here in this life. Yet, it also encourages him by suggesting that if we act as well as is in our power, we can hope that what is not in our power will come to our aid from elsewhere, whether we know the manner of it or not. **Aristotle** and **Plato** differed only regarding the origin of our moral concepts.

God), who alone satisfies the strictest demand of <term>practical reason</term> <note>The faculty of the mind that deals with how we ought to act, rather than just how the world is.</note>. The moral law is holy (inflexible) and demands holiness of morals, although all moral perfection that a human being can attain is always only **virtue**, which is to say: a disposition toward the law out of respect for the law. But the moral law in itself does not promise any happiness; for happiness, according to the concepts of a natural order in general, is not necessarily connected with following the law. Christian ethics now supplements this deficiency (of the second indispensable component of the <term>highest good</term> <note>The ideal state where a person is both perfectly virtuous and perfectly happy.</note>) by representing the world as a **Kingdom of God**. In this realm, rational beings 
<margin>30</margin> 
dedicate themselves to the moral law with their whole soul. Here, nature and morals come into a harmony—one that is foreign to each of them on their own—through a holy Author who makes the "derived highest good" possible.

The holiness of morals is assigned to them in this life as a guideline; however, the well-being proportioned to it, namely **blessedness**, is presented as attainable only in an **eternity**. [129] This is because holiness must always be the archetype of their behavior in every state, and 
<margin>40</margin> 
progressing toward it is already possible and necessary in this life. Happiness, however, under the name of earthly "happiness," cannot be reached at all in this world (at least as far as our own power is concerned).

<summary>Kant argues that human morality is a perpetual progress toward an ideal of holiness that we can never fully reach in this life, necessitating the hope for an eternal life. Regardless of this, the Christian principle of morality itself is not theological (and thus not <term>heteronomy</term> <note>Heteronomy refers to a state where the will is determined by something outside itself, such as fear of punishment or a desire for a specific reward.</note>), but rather the <term>autonomy</term> <note>Autonomy is the capacity of a rational being to give themselves the moral law, independent of external desires or pressures.</note> of pure practical reason for itself. In such a way, the moral law leads—through the concept of the highest good as the object and final end of pure practical reason—to **religion**, that is, to the **recognition of all duties as divine commands**. These are not to be understood as sanctions—that is, as arbitrary <note>Original: "willkürliche... zufällige" (arbitrary... accidental); these terms were corrected by the scholar Hartenstein.</note> and inherently accidental <margin>20</margin> decrees of a foreign will—but as essential laws of every free will for itself. Even here, <margin>30</margin> everything remains disinterested and based solely on duty; fear or hope must not be used as the underlying motives. If fear or hope were to become our principles of action, they would destroy the entire moral value of our deeds. The moral law commands me to make the highest possible good in a world the ultimate object of all my conduct. Even if <margin>[130]</margin> my own happiness is included within this, it is not happiness, but rather the **moral law** (which instead strictly limits my unlimited desire for happiness to specific conditions) that is the **determining ground** <note>The "determining ground" (*Bestimmungsgrund*) is the fundamental reason or motive that moves the will to act.</note> of the will, which is directed toward the promotion of the **highest good**. <margin>10</margin> Therefore, morality is not properly the doctrine of how we make ourselves happy, but how we are to become **worthy** of happiness. Now it follows from this: that morality in itself must never be treated as a **doctrine of happiness**, that is, as an instruction on how to become a participant in happiness; for it deals solely with the rational condition <note>original: "*conditio sine qua non*"; an indispensable condition without which something cannot occur.</note> of <margin>30</margin> the latter, and not with the means of acquiring it. However, when morality (which merely imposes duties and does not provide recipes for selfish wishes) has been fully presented, then for the first time—after the moral wish to promote the highest good (to bring the **Kingdom of God** to us) has been awakened, a wish based on a law which could not previously arise in any selfish soul—and after the step toward **religion** has been taken for its sake, then this moral theory can also be called a doctrine of happiness, because the hope for it only <margin>40</margin> begins with religion.

First Part, Book II, Second Chapter. VI.

If the latter makes Him (to speak in a human way) lovable, then through the former He is an object of **adoration** <note>original: *Adoration*</note>. Even human beings can gain love through beneficence, but through that alone <margin>10</margin> they can never acquire respect; thus, the greatest benevolence only does them honor if it is exercised according to their worthiness.
It follows naturally that in the order of ends, the human being (and with him every rational creature) is an **end in itself**. That is, he can never be used merely as a means by anyone (not even by God) without at the same time being an end himself. Thus, humanity in our person must be holy to ourselves. This is because the human being is <margin>20</margin> the subject of the moral law, and therefore the subject of <margin>[132]</margin> that which is holy in itself—for the sake of which, and in agreement with which, anything at all can be called holy. For this moral law is grounded on the **autonomy** <term>Autonomy: the capacity of a rational will to be a law unto itself, independent of outside influences.</term> of his will as a free will, which, according to its universal laws, must necessarily be able to agree with that very law to which it is to subject itself.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how practical reason (our moral capacity) provides answers to the deep questions—God, freedom, and immortality—that pure logic alone could never prove. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page's introduction of "postulates" (necessary assumptions).</meta>

# On the Postulates of Pure Practical Reason in General.

[They do] not <note>original: *nicht*. This word was missing in the original printed text but was added by Kant in his personal copy for clarity.</note> provide speculative knowledge <note>Speculative knowledge refers to theoretical "book learning" or logical proofs about the nature of the universe.</note>, but they do give objective reality to the ideas of speculative reason in general (by means of their relationship to the practical). They authorize reason to use concepts that it otherwise could not even venture to claim were possible.

These postulates are those of **immortality**, of **freedom**—viewed positively (as the causality of a being insofar as it belongs to the intelligible world) <term>Intelligible world: a realm of reality understood by the intellect rather than the physical senses.</term>—and of the **existence of God**. 

The first <margin>10</margin> flows from the practically necessary condition that our existence must last long enough to fully satisfy the moral law. The second flows from the necessary assumption that we are independent of the sensory world and have the power to determine our own will according to the laws of an intellectual world—that is, our freedom. The third flows from the necessity of a condition that allows such an intellectual world to be the "highest good," which requires the assumption of a highest, independent good—that is, the existence <margin>20</margin> of God.

The intention to achieve the highest good, which is made necessary by our respect for the moral law, leads us to assume its objective reality. This leads, through these "postulates" of practical reason, to concepts that speculative reason could propose as problems but could never solve. 

1) It leads to a solution for the very problem where speculative reason could only <insert>[133]</insert> commit **paralogisms** <term>Paralogism: a logical fallacy; in this context, the mistake of trying to prove the soul is a permanent "substance" through logic alone.</term> (namely, the question of **immortality**). <margin>30</margin> Pure logic lacked the evidence of "permanence" needed to turn the psychological concept of a "final subject"—which we feel in our own self-consciousness—into a real proof of a lasting soul. Practical reason achieves this through the postulate of a duration required to meet the demands of the moral law. 

2) It leads to that which speculative reason could see as nothing but **antinomy** <note>original: *Antinomie*. An antinomy is a "conflict of laws" or a paradox where two contradictory conclusions both seem equally logical.</note>—

<summary>Kant defines the three postulates of practical reason—immortality, freedom, and God—as necessary assumptions required to make sense of our moral lives. He argues that while pure logic cannot prove these ideas, our moral duty gives them "objective reality."</summary>
<keywords>postulates, immortality, freedom, existence of God, moral law, highest good, paralogisms, antinomy, practical reason</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how practical reason (the moral faculty) provides objective reality to ideas that speculative reason (pure logic) could only think about as possibilities. It specifically addresses how the "postulate of freedom" allows us to bridge the gap between our physical existence and an intellectual realm.</meta>

# First Part, Book II, Main Section 2. VI.

[...reason] held, the resolution of which it could only base on a concept that was "problematically" <note>In philosophy, "problematic" refers to something that can be thought of without contradiction, but cannot be proven to exist.</note> thinkable, yet whose objective reality it could neither prove nor define. This concept is the **cosmological idea** of an intelligible world <term>Intelligible world: a reality beyond our physical senses that is understood by the intellect.</term> and the consciousness of our existence within it, by means of the **postulate of freedom**. (Reason demonstrates the reality of freedom through the moral law, and along with it, the law of an intelligible world—something speculative reason could only point toward, <margin>10</margin> but whose concept it could not determine.) 3) It gives meaning to the theological concept of the **Original Being**, which speculative reason could think of but had to leave undefined as a mere **transcendental ideal**. This meaning is provided for a practical purpose—that is, as a necessary condition for achieving the goal of a will guided by the moral law. This Being is understood as the supreme principle of the highest good in an intelligible world, through the authority of moral legislation within that world.

<margin>20</margin> But is our knowledge really expanded in this way by pure practical reason? Is that which was "transcendent" <note>Transcendent: lying beyond the limits of all possible human experience.</note> for speculative reason now "immanent" <note>Immanent: remaining within or operating within the boundaries of our actual use or experience.</note> in practical reason? Certainly, but only **for a practical purpose**. For while we do not gain insight into the nature of our soul, or the intelligible world, or the Highest Being as they are in themselves, we have nevertheless united these concepts in the practical concept of the **highest good**. This is the object of our will, and we arrive at it completely a priori <note>A priori: knowledge that is independent of any sensory experience, derived from pure reason alone.</note> through pure reason. However, we do so only <margin>30</margin> by means of the moral law and solely in relation to it, regarding the object that the law commands.

Yet, we still do not understand *how* freedom is even possible, or how we are to represent this kind of causality <term>Causality: the relationship between a cause and its effect; here, the ability of the will to cause actions.</term> theoretically and positively. Instead, we only understand *that* such a freedom exists, because it is required by the moral law and for its sake. The same applies to the other ideas. No human understanding will ever <margin>[134] 40</margin> fathom how they are possible; however, no clever argument will ever strip away the conviction—even from the most ordinary person—that they are true and valid concepts.

<summary>Kant explains that while we cannot scientifically prove the existence of God or freedom, our moral duty makes it necessary to assume they are real for practical purposes. This "moral law" gives substance to ideas that would otherwise remain empty philosophical theories.</summary>

<header>How an extension of pure reason, etc.</header>

## VII.

### How is it possible to conceive of an extension of pure reason for a practical purpose, without simultaneously extending its knowledge as speculative?

<margin>10</margin>
To avoid becoming too abstract, we will answer this question immediately by applying it to the case at hand. — To expand a pure piece of knowledge practically, a purpose must be given *a priori* <note>a priori: knowledge that exists in the mind independent of sensory experience.</note>, that is, a goal as an object (of the will). Through a (categorical) imperative <term>Categorical Imperative: the absolute moral law that commands us to act according to principles that could become universal laws.</term> that directly determines the will, this goal is presented as practically necessary; in this context, that goal is the **highest good**. However, this is not possible without presupposing three theoretical concepts (for which no corresponding intuition <note>Intuition: the direct sensory perception or mental "looking at" an object. Kant argues we can't "see" God or the soul, so they lack theoretical intuition.</note> can be found, and therefore no objective reality can be established through theoretical means): namely **freedom**, **immortality**, and **God**. Therefore, through the
<margin>20</margin>
practical law, which commands the existence of the highest good possible in a world, the possibility of those objects of pure speculative reason—the objective reality which speculation could not guarantee them—is **postulated**. Through this, the theoretical knowledge of pure reason certainly receives an increase. However, this increase consists merely in the fact that those concepts, which were otherwise **problematic** <note>In philosophy, a "problematic" concept is one that can be thought of without contradiction but whose reality cannot be proven.</note> (merely thinkable) for it, are now declared **assertorically** <note>Assertoric: stated as an actual fact or reality, rather than just a possibility.</note> to be concepts to which objects actually belong. This is because practical reason
<margin>30</margin>
unavoidably requires their existence for the possibility of its own object—the practically and absolutely necessary "highest good"—and theoretical reason is thereby justified in presupposing them. This extension of theoretical reason, however, is not an extension of **speculation**; that is, it does not mean we can now make a positive use of these concepts for a theoretical purpose.

a) Kant: "theological," corrected by Hartenstein; Grillo: "teleological." <note>This refers to a famous textual dispute between editors regarding whether Kant originally meant "theological principles" or "teleological" (purpose-driven) principles.</note>

<summary>Kant explains that while we cannot scientifically prove the existence of God, freedom, or immortality, we are justified in "postulating" them as real because they are necessary requirements for morality to make sense.</summary>

For since nothing more has been accomplished by practical reason here than that those concepts are real and actually have their (possible) objects, but nothing of intuitions <note>Intuition (Anschauung): In Kant's philosophy, this refers to the direct sensory data or "looking at" an object that is required for actual knowledge. Since we cannot "see" God or the soul, we lack these intuitions.</note> of them is given to us (which cannot be demanded anyway), no synthetic proposition <note>Synthetic proposition: A statement that adds new information to a subject, rather than just defining it. For example, "The car is blue" is synthetic, whereas "A car is a vehicle" is analytic.</note> is possible through this granted reality of them. 

Consequently, this opening does not help us in the least for speculative 
<margin>[135]</margin> purposes, but it does in regard to the practical 
<margin>10</margin> use of pure reason for the extension of this knowledge of ours. The above three ideas <note>The three ideas are God, freedom, and immortality.</note> of speculative reason are in themselves not yet pieces of knowledge; yet they are (transcendent) thoughts in which there is nothing impossible. Now, they receive objective reality through an apodictic <note>Apodictic: Expressing something that is necessary or absolutely certain.</note> practical law as necessary conditions for the possibility of what this law commands to be made an object; that is, we are directed by that law to accept that they have objects, without yet being able to 
<margin>20</margin> show how their concept relates to an object. And even this is still not knowledge of these objects; for one can thereby judge nothing synthetically about them, nor determine their application theoretically, and thus one can make no theoretical use of reason regarding them, which is what all speculative knowledge actually consists of. 

But nevertheless, theoretical knowledge—not indeed of these objects, but of reason in general—was thereby extended insofar as, through the 
<margin>30</margin> practical postulates <term>Postulates: Assumptions that cannot be proven by logic alone but must be accepted as true for a system (in this case, morality) to function.</term>, objects were given to those ideas, since a merely problematic thought thereby first received objective reality. Thus, it was no extension of the knowledge of given **supersensible** <note>Supersensible (Übersinnliches): Things that exist beyond the reach of our five senses, such as the soul or God.</note> objects, but yet an extension of theoretical reason and its knowledge in regard to the supersensible in general, insofar as reason was forced to concede that there are such objects, without 
<margin>40</margin> being able to define them more closely. Consequently, reason was not able to extend this knowledge of the objects themselves (which have now been given to it on practical grounds and only for practical use), even though...

# How an extension of pure reason, etc.

...is therefore owed solely to its pure practical capacity. For pure theoretical reason, all those ideas were <term>transcendent</term> <note>Meaning "beyond experience." These are concepts we can think about but can never physically see or prove through scientific observation.</note> and without an object; here, however, they become <term>immanent</term> <note>Meaning they are "built-in" or internal to the way our practical reason operates.</note> and **constitutive**, as they are the grounds of the possibility of making the necessary object of pure practical reason (the **highest good**) real. Without this, they are merely **regulative principles** of speculative reason, which do not require it to assume a new object beyond experience, but only <margin>10</margin> to bring its use within experience closer to completeness. 

But once reason possesses this increase, it will act as speculative reason (strictly to secure its practical use) in a negative way—that is, not by expanding, but by purifying those ideas. It does this, on the one hand, to ward off **anthropomorphism** <note>The tendency to imagine God as having human-like qualities or physical traits.</note> as the source of **superstition**, which is the illusory expansion of those [136] concepts through supposed experience. On the other hand, it wards off **fanaticism**, which promises such an expansion <margin>20</margin> <gloss>namely, the expansion</gloss> through "supersensible" intuition or similar feelings. All of these are obstacles to the practical use of pure reason. Therefore, defending against them certainly belongs to the expansion of our knowledge in its practical purpose, without <note>Kant originally wrote "or," but this was corrected by the editor Hartenstein to "without."</note> this contradicting the admission that reason has gained nothing in the least for its speculative (theoretical) purpose.

For every use of reason regarding an object, <term>pure concepts of understanding</term> <margin>30</margin> (**categories**) are required, without which no object can be thought. For the theoretical use of reason—that is, for actual knowledge—these can only be applied if an **intuition** <note>In Kant’s philosophy, "intuition" refers to the raw sensory data we get from looking at or touching things.</note> (which is always sensory) is provided for them, and thus they serve only to represent an object of possible experience. But here we are dealing with ideas of reason that cannot be given in any experience...

<summary>Kant explains that while morality allows us to treat God and immortality as real (practical extension), we still lack sensory proof of them. Reason's job is to protect these ideas from superstition and fanatical "feelings" while admitting they aren't scientific facts.</summary>

# How an extension of pure reason, etc.

...can be—specifically, that which I would have to think through <term>categories</term> <note>Kant’s fundamental concepts of the mind (like cause and effect) that we use to organize our experiences.</note> in order to know it. However, we are not concerned here with the theoretical knowledge of the objects of these Ideas, but only with the fact that they have objects at all. This reality is provided by pure practical reason <note>The capacity of our reason to determine the will and guide moral action.</note>. In this process, theoretical reason has nothing more to do than simply to *think* those objects through categories. As we have clearly shown elsewhere, this is quite possible without needing <term>intuition</term> <note>German: *Anschauung*. This refers to the direct sensory perception of an object. Kant argues we can have a concept of something even if we cannot "see" it with our senses or our mind's eye.</note> <margin>10</margin> (neither sensory nor supersensible). This is because the categories have their seat and origin in the **pure understanding** independently of and prior to all intuition, merely as the faculty of thinking; they always signify an object in general, in whatever way it may be given to us. 

Now, when these categories are to be applied to those Ideas, it is indeed impossible to provide them with an object in intuition. However, the fact that such an object is *real*—and thus that the category as a mere form of thought is not empty here but has meaning—is sufficiently secured by an object which practical reason offers beyond doubt in the concept of the **highest good**. <margin>20</margin> This reality is granted to the concepts that are necessary for the possibility of the highest good, without this addition causing even the slightest expansion of our knowledge according to theoretical principles.

<margin>[137]</margin> Furthermore, when these Ideas of **God**, of an **intelligible world** <note>A world that can only be understood by the intellect, not perceived by the senses.</note> (the Kingdom of God), and of **immortality** <margin>30</margin> are defined by <term>predicates</term> <note>Characteristics or qualities ascribed to a subject.</note> taken from our own nature, we must not view this definition as a "sensualization" of those pure Ideas of reason (**anthropomorphism**) <note>The error of imagining God as having human physical or emotional traits.</note>, nor as a "transcendent" knowledge of supersensible objects. For these predicates are nothing other than **understanding** and **will**, viewed in their relation to one another exactly as they must be thought within the **moral law**; <margin>40</margin> that is, only insofar as a pure practical use is made of them. Regarding everything else that

# How an extension of pure reason, etc.

...is psychologically attached to the concepts; that is, insofar as we observe these faculties of ours empirically in their exercise. For example, we observe <margin>10</margin> that the human **understanding** <term>Understanding: the faculty of the mind that organizes sensory data into thoughts and rules.</term> is **discursive** <note>Meaning it works through step-by-step logic and concepts, rather than seeing everything at once in a single flash of insight.</note>, meaning its representations are thoughts and not **intuitions** <note>original: "Anschauungen." The direct, immediate perception of an object. Humans have sensory intuition (seeing, touching), but Kant argues we lack "intellectual intuition" (seeing things as they truly are without senses).</note>; that these thoughts follow one another in time; and that the human **will** is always burdened by a dependence of satisfaction upon the existence of its object, and so on. Since these conditions cannot apply to the **Supreme Being**, they are then abstracted away. Thus, nothing remains of <margin>20</margin> the concepts through which we think of a pure being of understanding except what is strictly necessary for the possibility of thinking a **moral law**. Consequently, we do indeed arrive at a knowledge of God, but only in a **practical relation**.

If we then attempt to extend this to a **theoretical** knowledge, we find ourselves imagining a Divine Understanding that does not think but rather *intuits*, and a Will directed toward objects upon whose existence His satisfaction does not in the least depend. (I will not even mention the <margin>30</margin> **transcendental predicates** <note>Universal properties that apply to all things, such as "being" or "unity."</note>, such as a magnitude of existence—that is, duration—which, however, does not take place in **time**, our only possible means of representing existence as a magnitude). These are nothing but properties of which we can form no concept suitable for the knowledge of the object. We are thus taught that these concepts can never be used for a theory of **supersensible beings** <note>Beings that exist beyond the reach of our physical senses, like God or the soul.</note> and are entirely unable to ground speculative knowledge. Instead, their use is restricted solely to the exercise of the moral law.

This latter point is so evident and can be so clearly proven by the deed itself, that one can confidently challenge all alleged **natural theologians** (a strange name!)\*) to name even a single

Consequently, only a teacher of revealed theology <note>Theology based on scripture or history rather than pure logic.</note> can properly be called a "theologian" or "Divine scholar." However, if one wished to call someone who is in possession of the **sciences of reason** (mathematics and philosophy) a "scholar"—even though this would already contradict the literal meaning of the word...

<summary>Kant explains that we can only understand God's attributes (like His will or understanding) by stripping away human limitations, leaving us with a practical idea for moral guidance rather than a scientific theory. He also critiques the term "natural theologian," arguing that true scholarship is based on history and revelation, not pure reason alone.</summary>

# 176 First Part, Book II, Second Main Section. VII.

[138] ...any property that determines this object (beyond merely ontological predicates <note>Basic logical categories used to describe "being" in general, such as "existence" or "unity," which do not provide a specific description of a thing's character.</note>), such as **understanding** or **will**. If one strips away everything anthropomorphic <note>original: "Anthropomorphistische." The tendency to attribute human characteristics, emotions, or intentions to God.</note> from these properties, one could not unarguably demonstrate that anything more than a mere word remains. Without these human-like traits, we cannot connect even the slightest concept to the word, and thus we could never hope to expand our theoretical knowledge through it. Regarding what is **practical**, however, the properties of an understanding and a will still leave us with the concept of a **relationship**. The practical law (which determines this very relationship of the understanding to the will *a priori* <note>original: "a priori." Knowledge that comes from pure reason rather than from sensory experience.</note>) grants this relationship objective reality. 

<margin>10</margin> Once this has occurred, reality is also granted to the concept of the object of a morally determined will (the concept of the **highest good** <term>Highest Good: The ideal state where happiness is perfectly matched to how virtuous or "worthy of happiness" a person is.</term>), and with it, reality is granted to the conditions that make it possible: the ideas of **God**, **freedom**, and **immortality**. Yet, this reality is always granted only in relation to the practice of the <margin>20</margin> moral law (and never for any speculative purpose).

After these reminders, it is now easy to find the answer to the important question: is the concept of God one that belongs to **physics** (and thus also to metaphysics, which contains only the pure *a priori* principles of physics in a general sense), or is it a concept belonging to **morality**? To explain the arrangements of nature or their changes by taking refuge in God as the Author of all things <margin>30</margin> is, at the very least, not a physical explanation. It is always an admission that one's philosophy has come to an end, because one is forced to assume something of which one otherwise has no concept for oneself, in order to form an idea of the possibility of what one sees before one's eyes.

...meaning (since erudition <note>original: "Gelehrsamkeit." High-level scholarship or "learnedness."</note> always counts only that which must absolutely be taught, and which one cannot discover for oneself through reason), it would be contradictory: thus the philosopher, with his knowledge of God as a positive science, would make too poor a figure to let himself be called a scholar on that account.

<summary>Kant argues that while we cannot "prove" God's nature through science or logic (theoretical reason), the concept of God is a necessary "practical" requirement for morality to function. He concludes that God is a concept belonging to ethics rather than physics, and using God to explain natural phenomena is a sign of reached philosophical limits.</summary>

# How an Expansion of Pure Reason etc.
## Kant, Critique of Practical Reason.
### 12

...to be able to form a concept of what one sees before one's eyes.

However, it is impossible to reach a concept of God and a proof of his existence through certain inferences by starting from the knowledge of this world through **metaphysics**. This is because we would have to know this world as the most perfect possible whole—and to do that, we would have to know all possible worlds (to be able to compare them with this one). In short, we would have to be **all-knowing** [139] to say that this world was only possible through a God (as we must conceive of this concept).

<margin>10</margin> Moreover, it is absolutely impossible to recognize the existence of this being from mere concepts alone. Every **existential proposition**—that is, a statement that says a being of which I have a concept actually exists—is a **synthetic proposition** <note>A "synthetic proposition" is one where the predicate adds new information to the subject. For example, "all bachelors are unmarried" is certain by definition, but "this bachelor is tall" is synthetic because it adds a new observation not found in the definition of "bachelor."</note>. In such a statement, I go beyond the concept and say more about it than was already thought in the concept itself: namely, that a corresponding object exists outside the understanding for this concept within the understanding. It is clearly impossible to derive <margin>20</margin> this existence through any logical inference.

Therefore, only a single procedure remains for reason to reach this knowledge. As **pure reason**, it must start from the supreme principle of its **pure practical use** (since this use is directed at the existence of something as a result of reason anyway) and thereby determine its object. In this unavoidable task—namely, the necessary direction of the will toward the **highest good** <term>Highest Good: The ideal state where moral virtue and happiness are perfectly joined together.</term>—we find the necessity of assuming such an **original being** <note>original: "Urwesen." A foundational or primary being, identifying God as the source of all reality.</note> in relation to the <margin>30</margin> possibility of this good in the world. 

But more remarkably, this path provides something that was completely missing from the "natural path" of reason: a **precisely defined concept** of this original being. Because we only know a small part of this world and can even less compare it with all possible worlds, we can certainly use its order, **purposefulness**, and scale to infer a **wise, kind, and powerful** author of it. However, we cannot infer <margin>40</margin> his **omniscience, all-goodness, and omnipotence**. One might well admit...

<summary>Kant argues that while observing nature can suggest a creator, it cannot logically prove a perfect, all-powerful God. Only our moral need to achieve the "highest good" justifies the assumption of a precisely defined, omnipotent deity.</summary>

# First Part, Book II, Main Chapter 2. VII.

...that one is well-authorized to supplement this unavoidable deficiency with a permissible, entirely rational **hypothesis**: namely, that if wisdom, goodness, etc., shine forth in as many parts as present themselves to our closer knowledge, it will be the same in all the rest, and it is therefore rational to attribute all possible perfection to the **author of the world**; but these are not inferences through which we might pride ourselves on our insight: but only permissions <margin>10</margin> that can be granted to us, and yet still require a further recommendation in order to make use of them. The concept of God therefore remains, on the **empirical path** (that of physics), always a concept of the perfection of the first being that is not exactly determined, so as to consider it adequate for the concept of a Deity (while with **metaphysics**, however, in its transcendental part, nothing at all can be achieved).

<margin>[140]</margin>
I now try to hold this concept against the object of <margin>20</margin> **practical reason**, and there I find that the moral principle permits it<gloss>a)</gloss> only as possible under the presupposition of an author of the world of the highest perfection. He must be **all-knowing**, to recognize my behavior to the innermost part of my **disposition** <note>original: "Gesinnung"—this refers to the internal motivation or moral "spirit" behind an action, rather than just the outward deed.</note> in all possible cases and in all future; **all-powerful**, to grant it the appropriate consequences; likewise **omnipresent**, **eternal**, etc. Consequently, the **moral law**, through the concept of the **highest good** <term>Highest Good (Summum Bonum): The ideal state where moral virtue and happiness are perfectly joined together.</term>, as the object <margin>30</margin> of a pure practical reason, determines the concept of the original being as the highest being—something which the physical (and, continuing higher, the metaphysical) path, and thus the entire speculative path of reason, could not achieve. Thus, the concept of God is a concept belonging originally not to physics (that is, for speculative reason), but to **morals**; and the very same can also be said of the other concepts of reason, which we treated above as **postulates** <note>A "postulate" is a requirement of reason that cannot be proven by science but must be assumed to make sense of our moral experience.</note> of reason in its practical use. <margin>40</margin> When one [looks] into the history of the Greek...

<meta>This page continues Kant’s discussion on why speculative reason (pure logic and observation) fails to provide a complete concept of God. ### How an extension of pure reason, etc.

philosophy encounters no clear traces of a rational theology <note>original: "Vernunfttheologie" — a system of religious belief based on logic and reason rather than revelation.</note> beyond Anaxagoras <note>Anaxagoras (c. 500–428 BC) was a Pre-Socratic philosopher who was among the first to suggest that "Mind" (Nous) was the arranging force of the universe.</note>, the reason does not lie in the fact that the older philosophers lacked the understanding and insight to raise themselves to it through the path of speculation, at least with the help of a quite rational hypothesis; what could be easier, what more natural, than the thought—which offers itself to everyone—of assuming, instead of indeterminate degrees of perfection <margin>10</margin> in various world-causes, a single rational one that possesses all perfection? But the evils in the world seemed to them to be much too significant objections for them to consider themselves justified in such a hypothesis. Consequently, they showed precisely their understanding and insight by not allowing themselves that hypothesis and instead searching around among natural causes to see whether they might find among them the properties and powers required for <gloss>a)</gloss> an original being <note>original: "Urwesen" — the fundamental, first source of all existence.</note>. But after this ingenious people <note>Referring to the ancient Greeks.</note> had advanced so far in their investigations as to treat even moral objects <margin>20</margin> philosophically (about which other peoples have never done more than chatter), they then found, for the first time, a new need, namely a **practical** one. This need did not fail to provide them with the definite concept of the original being. In this process, speculative reason was a mere bystander, having at most the merit of decorating a concept that had not grown on its own soil, and promoting it with a series of confirmations from the observation of nature—which now emerged for the first time—not so much to bolster its authority <insert>[141]</insert> (which was already established) but rather <margin>30</margin> merely to promote a display of supposed theoretical rational insight.

From these reminders, the reader of the *Critique of Pure Speculative Reason* will be perfectly convinced of how highly necessary and beneficial for theology and morals that laborious **deduction of the categories** <term>Deduction of the Categories: Kant's complex proof in his first book showing how human concepts (like cause and effect) are necessarily applicable to everything we experience.</term> was. For only through this can it be prevented, when placing them in the pure understanding, from...

->Erster Teil, II. Buch, 2. Hauptstück. VIII.<-

to consider them innate with **Plato** and to ground upon them extravagant claims with theories of the supersensible <note>original: "Übersinnlichen" — things that exist beyond the reach of our physical senses, such as God or the soul.</note>, to which one sees no end, but thereby making theology into a magic lantern of brain-phantoms; but if one considers them acquired, to prevent one from—as with **Epicurus**—restricting each and every use of them, even that for a practical <note>In Kant's vocabulary, "practical" refers to ethics, morality, and how we choose to act.</note> purpose, merely to objects and motives of the senses. <margin>20</margin>for the definite thinking of the supersensible, though only insofar as this is defined merely by such predicates as necessarily belong to the pure, *prior* <note>original: "a priori"</note> given practical purpose and its possibility. Speculative limitation of pure reason and practical extension of the same first bring reason into that relationship of equality in which reason in general can be used purposefully; and this example proves better than any other that the path to wisdom, if it is to be secured and not become impassable or 
<margin>30</margin>misleading, must for us humans unavoidably pass through science <note>original: "Wissenschaft" — here meaning a systematic, rigorous body of knowledge or philosophy, rather than just the natural sciences.</note>, though one can only be convinced that this leads to that goal after its completion.

### On Assent from a Need of Pure Reason.

A need of pure reason in its speculative <note>Speculative reason is the use of logic to try and understand the nature of the world and the universe through theory alone.</note> use leads only to hypotheses, but that of pure practical reason leads to postulates;

<meta>This page continues Kant’s discussion on the limits of speculative reason. # On Holding Something to be True Based on a Need of Pure Reason.

...as high up in the series of grounds as I wish, and I require a **primary ground** <note>original: "Urgrund" — a fundamental first cause or ultimate source.</note><sup>a</sup>), not to give objective reality to those derived things (for example, the causal connection of things and changes in the world), but only to satisfy my inquiring reason completely in regard to them. Thus, I see order and purposiveness in nature before me and do not need to resort to speculation to assure myself of their reality;
<margin>10</margin>
rather, I only need to presuppose a **Deity** as their cause in order to explain them. Since an inference from an effect to a specific cause—especially one so precisely and completely defined as the God we must conceive—is always uncertain and precarious, such a presupposition cannot be carried further than the degree of the most reasonable opinion for us humans.*) On the other hand, a *need of pure practical reason* is grounded upon a *duty* to make something (the **highest good** <note>The "highest good" for Kant is the perfect harmony of morality/virtue and happiness.</note>) the object of my will, in order to promote
<margin>20</margin>
it with all my strength; however, in doing so, I must presuppose its possibility, and thus also the conditions for it—namely **God, freedom**, and **immortality**—because I cannot prove these through my speculative reason <note>Reason used to seek theoretical knowledge of the world.</note>, though I cannot refute them either. This duty is grounded on a principle that is, to be sure, quite [independent] of these latter presuppositions...

---

> *) But even here, we could not allege a need of reason if an uncertain <note>original: "problematischer" — in a philosophical sense, a concept that is possible but not yet proven.</note> but nonetheless unavoidable concept of reason were not before our eyes: namely, that of an absolutely necessary being. This concept now demands to be defined; and when the drive for expansion is added, this is the objective ground of a need of speculative reason: namely, to more closely define the concept of a necessary being that is to serve as a primary ground for others, and thus to make this ultimate being recognizable. Without such preceding necessary problems, there are no needs—at least not of the *pure reason*; the others are *needs of inclination* <note>original: "Neigung" — our natural desires, feelings, or preferences rather than rational duty.</note>.

---

a) 1st and 4th–6th editions: "non-ground" <note>original: "Ungrundes" — likely a printing error for "Urgrundes" (primary ground).</note>.

<summary>Kant distinguishes between the "needs" of theoretical reason, which only seeks to explain nature through a first cause, and the "needs" of practical reason, which requires the existence of God, freedom, and immortality to make moral duty possible.</summary>
<keywords>pure practical reason, speculative reason, highest good, primary ground, duty, God, freedom, immortality, inclination</keywords>

--- Page 236 ---
<meta>This page continues Kant’s argument regarding the "needs" of reason. On the previous page, he began explaining that the duty to promote the highest good is grounded on a principle that is "quite independent"—this page completes that thought, clarifying that the moral law requires no external theoretical proof to be binding.</meta>

->Part One, Book II, Chapter 2. VIII.<-

independent, apodictically <note>original: "apodiktisch" — meaning something that is absolutely certain and necessary, leaving no room for doubt.</note> certain in itself, namely the **moral law**; as such, it requires no
<margin>[143]</margin>
further support from theoretical opinions regarding the inner nature of things, the secret purposes of the world order, or a presiding ruler, in order to bind us perfectly to unconditionally lawful actions. But the subjective effect of this law—namely the disposition <note>original: "Gesinnung" — a person's underlying moral attitude or character.</note> appropriate to it and made necessary by it
<margin>10</margin>
to promote the practically possible **highest good**—presupposes at the very least that the latter is indeed possible. Otherwise, it would be practically impossible to strive for the object of a concept that was fundamentally empty and without a real object. Now, the aforementioned **postulates** <note>In Kant’s philosophy, a "postulate" is an assumption that cannot be proven by science but is necessary for moral action: God, freedom, and immortality.</note> concern only the physical or metaphysical conditions—in a word, those lying in the nature of things—for the possibility of the highest good. These are not assumed for the sake of some arbitrary speculative aim, but for a
<margin>20</margin>
practically necessary purpose of the pure rational will. Here, the will does not choose, but rather obeys an inexorable **command of reason** <note>original: "Vernunftgebot"</note>. This command has its objective ground in the nature of things as they must be universally judged by pure reason; it is not based on mere inclination. We are never justified in assuming means are possible, or an object is real, simply because we desire them for subjective reasons. Therefore, this is a need in an absolutely necessary
<margin>30</margin>
regard, and it justifies its assumption not merely as a permissible hypothesis, but as a **postulate in a practical sense**. Granted that the pure moral law inexorably binds everyone as a command (not as a mere rule of prudence), the righteous person <note>original: "Rechtschaffene"</note> may well say: I will that there be a **God**; that my existence in this world, even beyond its natural connections, be also an existence in a pure intelligible world; and finally, that my duration be endless. I
<margin>40</margin>
persist in this and will not let this faith be taken from me; for this is the only case where my interest—because I may not waive any part of it—inevitably determines my...

<summary>Kant argues that while the moral law is certain on its own, acting upon it requires us to "postulate" or assume the existence of God and immortality as necessary conditions for the "highest good" to be possible. This belief is described not as a scientific proof, but as a necessary moral commitment of a righteous person.</summary>
