# On Assent from a Need of Pure Reason.

judgment is inevitably determined, without paying attention to sophistries <note>original: "Vernünfteleien" — refers to trivial or overly subtle reasoning intended to confuse or mislead.</note>, no matter how little I might be able to answer them or counter them with more plausible arguments.*)

---

In order to prevent misunderstandings in the use of such an unusual <insert>[144]</insert> concept as that of a **pure practical rational belief**, allow me to add one more remark. — It might almost seem as if this rational belief were being announced here as a command itself, namely that

> *) In the *German Museum*, February 1787, there is a treatise by a very subtle and clear mind, the late Wizenmann <gloss>a) Referring to Thomas Wizenmann (1759–1787), a critic of Kant who argued that "needs" do not prove "reality."</gloss>, whose early death is to be regretted. In it, he contests the right to conclude the objective reality of an object based on a need, and illustrates his point with the example of a person in love. This lover, having become infatuated with an idea of beauty that is merely a figment of his imagination <note>original: "Hirngespinst" — literally "brain-spinning," a fantasy or delusion.</note>, might wish to conclude that such an object actually exists somewhere. I grant him complete right in this, in all cases where the need is based on inclination <note>original: "Neigung" — emotional desires or whims as opposed to moral duty.</note>. Such an inclination cannot necessarily postulate the existence of its object even for the one affected by it, much less contain a requirement valid for everyone; it is therefore a merely subjective ground for wishes. Here, however, we have a **need of reason** arising from an objective determining ground of the will, namely the **moral law**. This law necessarily binds every rational being; thus, one is justified *a priori* <note>A Latin term meaning "from the beginning" or "independent of experience."</note> in presupposing the conditions in nature that are appropriate to it, making the latter inseparable from the complete practical use of reason. It is a duty to make the **highest good** real according to our greatest ability; therefore, it must indeed be possible. Consequently, it is inevitable for every rational being in the world to presuppose whatever is necessary for its objective possibility. This presupposition is as necessary as the moral law itself, in relation to which alone it is valid.

> a) Regarding Wizenmann, compare my introduction to the Philosophical Library, Vol. 46 b, p. XXVIII, XXX, XXXV.

<summary>Kant defends his "rational belief" by distinguishing between subjective desires (which don't prove reality) and the objective "need of reason" (which requires us to assume the possibility of the highest good to fulfill our moral duty).</summary>

### Part One, Book II, Second Chapter. VIII.

...to assume the **highest good** <note>The "highest good" (summum bonum) is Kant’s term for a world where people are both perfectly moral and perfectly happy as a result of that morality.</note> as possible. However, a belief that is commanded is an absurdity <note>original: "Unding" — a conceptual impossibility or a "non-thing."</note>. If one recalls the previous discussion of what is required to be assumed in the concept of the highest good, one will <margin>10</margin> realize<sup>a)</sup> that assuming this possibility cannot be commanded at all. It requires no practical disposition to admit it; rather, **speculative reason** <note>Reason used to understand how the world *is* (science/logic), as opposed to "practical reason" which decides how we *ought* to act.</note> must grant it without being asked. For no one would want to claim that it is inherently impossible for rational beings in the world to be worthy of being happy, while simultaneously possessing a share of **happiness** proportioned to that worthiness. 

Now, regarding the first part of the highest good—namely, what concerns morality—the **moral law** gives us a mere command. To doubt the possibility of that component <margin>20</margin> would be the same as calling the moral law itself into doubt. But regarding the second part of that object—namely, happiness consistently proportioned to that worthiness—admitting its general possibility does not require a command at all, for theoretical reason has nothing against it. It is only the *way* in which we are to think of such a harmony between the laws of nature and the laws of <margin>[145]</margin> **freedom** that has something about it where we have a choice. This is because theoretical reason decides nothing here with **absolute certainty** <term>apodictic certainty: a truth that is necessarily or demonstrably true, leaving no room for doubt</term>. In <margin>30</margin> regard to this, there can be a **moral interest** that tips the scales.

Earlier, I had said that according to the mere course of nature in the world, happiness exactly proportioned to moral value is not to be expected and should be considered impossible. Therefore, the possibility of the highest good from this side can only be admitted under the presupposition of a **moral Author of the world** <note>Kant’s philosophical term for God, acting as the bridge between the physical laws of nature and the moral laws of human will.</note>. I intentionally <margin>40</margin> withheld the restriction of this judgment to the subjective conditions of our reason, in order to only then...

a) <gloss>Kant wrote "one will" (wird man); corrected by editors Rosenkranz and Hartenstein.</gloss>

<summary>Kant argues that while we cannot be "commanded" to believe, our reason naturally accepts that a world where virtue leads to happiness is possible. He suggests that because logic cannot prove how this happens, our moral duty gives us a valid reason to believe in a divine "Author of the world."</summary>

of **assent** <note>original: "Fürwahrhalten" — literally "holding-to-be-true." This faith is therefore not commanded; rather, as a voluntary determination of our judgment—conducive to the moral (commanded) purpose and moreover consistent with the theoretical need of reason—it consists in assuming that existence <note>Referring to the existence of God and the immortality of the soul.</note> and making it the further basis for the use of reason. It springs from the moral disposition itself; thus, even in the well-intentioned, it may
<margin>10</margin> occasionally waver, but it can never fall into unbelief.

### IX.

->**On the Proportion of Human Cognitive Faculties**<-
->**Wisely Adapted to Man’s Practical Vocation.**<-

If human nature is destined to strive for the **highest good** <term>Highest Good: A state where both perfect morality and perfect happiness exist together, which Kant argues we must assume is possible.</term>, then the measure of its cognitive faculties—especially their relationship to one another—must also be assumed to be suitable for this purpose. Now, however, the **Critique of Pure Speculative Reason** proves the greatest inadequacy of these faculties
<margin>20</margin> for solving the most important tasks presented to them in a way suited to that purpose. Although reason does not fail to recognize the natural and undeniable hints it receives, nor the great steps it can take to approach this great goal set before it, it can never reach it on its own, even with the help of the greatest knowledge of nature. Thus, nature seems
<margin>30</margin> here to have provided for us only in a **stepmotherly** <note>Kant uses "stiefmütterlich" (stepmotherly) to suggest that nature has been stingy or harsh by giving us the desire for answers without the mental tools to find them.</note> fashion with a faculty necessary for our purpose.

Suppose now that nature had been compliant with our wish in this matter and had granted us that capacity for insight or enlightenment which we would like to possess (or which some even imagine they actually do possess); what would likely be the consequence of this? Unless our whole nature were changed at the same time, the
<margin>40</margin> inclinations—which always have the first word—would...

<summary>Kant concludes that our belief in God is a "rational faith" born of moral character. He begins a new inquiry into why our intellect is limited, suggesting our lack of "proof" for the divine might be a deliberate, wise design.</summary>

<header>On the Practical Determination of the Human Being, etc.</header>

[would] have, first their satisfaction and, combined with rational reflection, their greatest possible and lasting satisfaction under the name of **happiness** <term>Glückseligkeit: A central Kantian term referring to the state of a rational being in the world for whom everything goes according to their wish and will.</term>; the moral law would speak afterward only to keep those desires within their proper limits and even to subject them all to a higher purpose that takes no account of any inclination. 

But instead of the struggle that the **moral disposition** <term>moralische Gesinnung: The internal attitude or motivation behind an action, which Kant believes is more important than the action itself.</term> must now wage with inclinations—in which, after some defeats, 
<margin>10</margin> the moral strength of the soul is gradually to be acquired—**God** and **eternity** with their terrifying majesty would stand unceasingly before our eyes (for what we can perfectly prove is as certain to us as what we see with our own eyes). The violation of the law would certainly be avoided, and what is commanded would be done; but because the disposition from which actions should occur cannot be instilled by any command, and the sting of 
<margin>20</margin> activity here would be immediately at hand and external, reason would therefore not have to work its way up first to gather strength for resistance against inclinations through a vivid representation of the dignity of the law. Thus, most lawful actions would occur out of fear, only a few out of hope, and none at all out of duty. However, a moral value of actions—on which alone the value of the person and even that of the world depends in the eyes of the highest wisdom—would not exist 
<margin>30</margin> at all. The behavior of human beings, as long as their nature remained as it is now, would therefore be transformed into a mere mechanism, where, as in a **puppet show** <note>original: "Marionettenspiel"</note>, everything would gesticulate well, but no life would be found in the figures.

Now, since our situation is quite different—since even with all the exertion of our reason we have only a very dim and ambiguous view into the future, and the Ruler of the world <note>original: "Weltregierer"</note> allows us only to surmise His existence and His majesty, not to 
<margin>40</margin> behold or clearly prove them—while on the other hand the moral law within us, without promising or threatening us anything with certainty,

<meta>This page concludes the final section of Book II, Chapter 2 of the "Critique of Practical Reason." It continues Kant's argument that our lack of direct knowledge regarding God and the afterlife is actually a necessary condition for true morality.</meta>

<page-num>188</page-num>
<header>Part One, Book II, Chapter 2. IX.</header>

[...the moral law] demands disinterested **respect** <term>Achtung: A feeling of reverence for the moral law that serves as the only legitimate motive for moral action.</term> from us, but otherwise, only when this respect has become active and dominant, does it allow—for the first time and only through it—glimpses into the realm of the **supersensible** <term>Übersinnlichen: That which exists beyond the reach of human senses or physical experience, such as God or the soul.</term>, though even then only with faint looks. In this way, a true moral **disposition** <term>Gesinnung: The internal attitude or fundamental orientation of a person's will toward the moral law.</term>, dedicated directly to the law, can exist, and the rational creature can become worthy of sharing in the **highest good** <term>höchstes Gut: The ideal state where perfect virtue is joined with perfect happiness.</term> in a way that is proportionate to the **moral worth** of their person and not merely their outward actions. 
<margin>[148] 10</margin> Thus, it may well be correct here, as the study of nature and of humanity otherwise sufficiently teaches us, that the **inscrutable wisdom** through which we exist is no less worthy of veneration in what it has **denied us** than in what it has granted us to share.

# **Doctrine of Method** <term>Methodenlehre: While the first part of the book established the rules of morality, this "Doctrine of Method" focuses on how these rules can be put into practice—specifically, how moral laws can gain influence over the human heart and mind through education.</term>
### of
## Pure Practical Reason. <term>reinen praktischen Vernunft: The capacity of human reason to determine what is right and wrong independently of our personal desires or physical impulses.</term>

---

<note>This page serves as a formal divider in the manuscript. Having completed his complex proof of how morality is possible, Kant now moves to the practical question of how we can teach people to be truly virtuous rather than just acting out of habit or fear of punishment.</note>

<summary>This page acts as the title page for the Second Part of the Critique, introducing the "Doctrine of Method." It signals a shift from the theoretical foundations of morality to the practical application and psychological influence of moral principles.</summary>

<keywords>Doctrine of Method, Pure Practical Reason, moral education, practical application</keywords>

--- Page 245 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page of the "Doctrine of Method," where Kant began to transition from the theory of moral laws to the practical application of how to instill those laws in the human mind.</meta>

<page-num>151</page-num>

By the **Doctrine of Method** <term>Methodenlehre: Here, Kant distinguishes between a "scientific" method (how we organize knowledge) and a "pedagogical" method (how we teach or internalize morality).</term> of pure practical reason, one cannot understand the way (either in reflection or in presentation) of proceeding with pure practical principles with a view toward a scientific knowledge of them—which is what is usually called "method" in the theoretical sphere (for popular knowledge requires a *manner*, but science requires a *method*, i.e., a procedure based on principles of reason, <margin>10</margin> by which alone the manifold of a body of knowledge can become a **system**). Rather, by this Doctrine of Method is understood the way in which one can provide the laws of pure practical reason access to the human mind and influence over its **maxims** <term>maxims: The personal rules or principles that an individual chooses to live by (e.g., "I will never tell a lie").</term>—that is, how one can make objectively practical reason also *subjectively* practical.

Now, it is indeed clear that those determining grounds of the will—which alone actually make maxims moral and give them a moral value—<margin>20</margin> must be presented as the actual **incentives** <term>Triebfedern: Literally "drive-springs"; the internal motivations that push us to act.</term> of actions; these are the immediate representation of the law and the objectively necessary observance of it as **duty**. Otherwise, while the **legality** of actions might be produced, the **morality** of dispositions <note>original: "Moralität der Gesinnungen." Kant distinguishes between "legality" (doing the right thing for any reason, like fear of jail) and "morality" (doing the right thing simply because it is right).</note> would not be achieved. However, it is not so clear—and instead must seem quite improbable to everyone at first glance—that subjectively, that presentation of pure **virtue** could have more power over the human mind and <margin>30</margin> provide a far stronger incentive even to effect that legality of actions, and to bring about more powerful resolutions to prefer the law out of pure respect for it over every other consideration, than all the allurements arising from illusions of pleasure...

<summary>Kant defines the "Doctrine of Method" as the art of making moral laws personally meaningful and influential to an individual's character. He argues that acting out of a sense of duty (morality) is a more powerful motivator than acting for the sake of reward or pleasure (legality).</summary>

<keywords>Doctrine of Method, pure practical reason, maxims, incentives, duty, legality vs morality, virtue, moral education</keywords>

--- Page 246 ---
<meta>This page continues Kant’s argument from the previous section regarding the "Doctrine of Method." He is explaining why the pure moral law itself must be the primary motivation for action, rather than external rewards or punishments, if one is to achieve true morality rather than mere legal compliance.</meta>

<page-num>192</page-num>
<header>Second Part.</header>

...pleasure and generally everything that might be counted toward happiness <note>original: "Glückseligkeit." The text here is missing a verb; editorial tradition suggests "entspringen" (to arise or result from).</note>, <margin>[152]</margin> or even any threats of pain and evils could ever achieve. Nevertheless, this is how things truly stand. If human nature were not constituted in this way, no way of presenting the law—no matter how many roundabout paths or recommending means were used—would ever produce a **morality of disposition**. Everything would be pure hypocrisy <term>Gleisnerei: An older German term for pretense or sham-holiness.</term>; the law would be hated or even despised, yet still followed <margin>10</margin> for the sake of one's own advantage. The letter of the law (**legality**) would be found in our actions, but the spirit of the law <note>original: "der Geist desselben." The OCR notes a correction from "derselben" to "desselben" to ensure the pronoun agrees with the gender of "Geist."</note> would not be found at all in our dispositions (**morality**). Since, despite all our efforts, we cannot entirely distance ourselves from reason in our judgment, we would inevitably have to appear in our own eyes as worthless, depraved human beings. This would be the case even if we tried to compensate ourselves for this insult before the **inner courtroom** <term>innerer Richterstuhl: Kant’s famous metaphor for the conscience acting as a judge that evaluates our internal motives.</term> <margin>20</margin> by delighting in the pleasures that—according to our delusion—some natural or divine law had attached to them. This delusion treats such laws like a mechanical police force that only cares about what one *does*, without bothering about the motivations *why* one does it.

To be sure, one cannot deny that in order to bring a mind that is either still unrefined or even wild onto the track of the **morally good**, some preparatory guidance is needed—luring it with its own advantage or frightening it with potential <margin>30</margin> harm. However, as soon as this mechanical machinery, this **leading-string** <term>Gängelband: Literally "walking strings" used to help children learn to walk; Kant uses it to describe external rewards and punishments that guide people before they develop internal autonomy.</term>, has had some effect, the pure moral motive must be brought to the soul. This motive is the only thing that establishes a **character** (a practical, consistent way of thinking according to unchanging **maxims**). Not only that, but because it teaches a human being to feel their own dignity, it gives the mind a power that is unexpected even to itself,

<summary>Kant argues that true morality requires acting out of respect for the law itself, rather than fear or desire for reward. He explains that while rewards and punishments can help "train" an unrefined mind, they are merely "leading-strings" that must eventually be replaced by a sense of duty and human dignity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kant’s discussion on how we evaluate moral character. On the previous page, he began explaining how people react when the moral character of an individual is being determined. He is arguing that even people who dislike abstract philosophy become deeply engaged when debating the motives and "moral worth" of others.</meta>

<page-num>194</page-num>

# Second Part.

...character of any person is to be determined. 
Those who otherwise find everything subtle and hair-splitting in theoretical questions dry and annoying, quickly join the conversation when it comes to determining the **moral worth** of an account of a good or bad action. In these cases, they are more precise, more analytical, and more subtle in thinking up everything that could diminish the purity of intention—and thus the degree of **virtue**—or even make it suspicious, than one would ever expect of them regarding any other object of speculation. 

In these assessments, one can often see the character of the persons judging others themselves shine through. Some seem particularly inclined, while exercising their office as judge (primarily over those who are deceased), to defend the good reported of this or that deed against all stinging objections of insincerity. They ultimately defend the entire **moral value** of the person against the charge of pretense and secret **malice**. 

<margin>[154]</margin> Others, however, focus more on accusations and charges to contest this value. Yet one cannot always attribute to these latter critics the intention of wanting to reason away virtue from all human examples in order to turn it into an empty name. Rather, it is often a well-intentioned rigor in determining the genuine moral content according to an unyielding law. When compared with this law—and not with other examples—**moral self-conceit** <term>Eigendünkel: An inflated sense of one's own moral goodness or merit, which Kant believes is deflated by the true moral law.</term> sinks greatly, and **humility** is not merely taught, but felt by everyone through sharp self-examination. 

Nonetheless, one can usually see that the defenders of the purity of intention in given examples would like to wipe away even the smallest blemish wherever there is a presumption of **integrity** <note>original: "Rechtschaffenheit"</note>. They do so from the motive that if the truthfulness of all examples were contested and the purity of all human virtue were denied, then virtue might finally be considered a mere **figment of the mind** <term>Hirngespinst: A "phantom" or "chimera"; something that exists only in the imagination without any reality.</term>. Consequently, all effort toward virtue would be looked down upon as vain posturing and deceptive self-conceit.

<summary>Kant observes that people are naturally drawn to judging the morality of others' motives, often displaying their own character in the process. He explains that while some are overly critical to maintain a high moral standard, others defend the possibility of pure virtue to prevent morality from being dismissed as a mere fantasy.</summary>

<keywords>moral worth, virtue, judgment of character, purity of intention, self-conceit, humility, integrity, moral law</keywords>

<page-num>195</page-num>

# Methodology of Pure Practical Reason.

I do not know why the educators of youth have not long ago made use of this natural inclination of reason—the tendency to enter with pleasure into even the most subtle examination of practical questions—and why, after first laying a purely moral catechism <note>A "catechism" in this context is a teaching tool using a question-and-answer format to instill core principles of ethics.</note> as a foundation, they did not search through the biographies of ancient and modern times. Their goal should be to have ready at hand examples of the duties they have presented. By
<margin>10</margin> comparing similar actions under different circumstances, they could set the judgment of their pupils into activity, allowing them to notice the lesser or greater moral content of these actions. They would find that even early youth, which is otherwise unready for any kind of abstract speculation, soon becomes very sharp-sighted and quite interested in this process, for they feel the progress of their own power of judgment. Most importantly, however, educators can hope with certainty that the frequent practice of recognizing and applauding good conduct in all its
<margin>20</margin> purity—while noticing even the slightest deviation from it with regret or contempt—will leave behind a lasting impression of high esteem on one side and horror on the other. Even if this is initially practiced only as<gloss>a)</gloss> <note>original: "als"</note> a game of judgment in which children can compete with one another, it would nonetheless constitute a good foundation for integrity in their future conduct of life. This is achieved through the mere habit of frequently looking upon such actions as worthy of either praise or
<margin>[155]</margin> blame. I only wish to spare them from examples of so-called "noble" (supererogatory) actions <term>überverdienstlich: These are "extra-credit" actions that go beyond what is strictly required by duty. Kant worries these distract from basic moral obligations.</term>, with
<margin>30</margin> which our sentimental writings make such a display. Instead, I wish to see everything based solely on duty and the value that a human being can and must give to themselves in their own eyes through the consciousness of not having transgressed it. For whatever ends in empty wishes and longings for an unachievable perfection produces merely "novel heroes."

...take great credit for something excessively grand, and in exchange, they absolve themselves from observing common and ordinary duty, which then seems insignificantly small to them.*)

But if one asks: what pure morality actually is, which serves as the touchstone <note>original: "Probemetall" — literally a "testing metal" used to determine the purity of gold or silver. In ethics, it refers to the standard used to judge the purity of an intention.</note> against which the moral content of every action must be tested, I must admit that only philosophers can make the answer to this question seem doubtful. <margin>10</margin> For in common human reason, this has long been decided—not through abstract general formulas, but through ordinary use—just as clearly as the difference between the right and left hand. We will therefore first demonstrate the test for pure virtue through an example. By imagining it presented to a ten-year-old boy for his judgment, we can see whether he would necessarily have to judge this way on his own, without being directed by a teacher. <margin>20</margin> Tell the story of an honest man whom people want to persuade to join the slanderers of an innocent, otherwise powerless person (such as Anne Boleyn <note>Anne Boleyn was the second wife of King Henry VIII of England; she was executed after being accused of adultery and treason. Kant uses her here as a historical example of a defenseless person facing the whim of a powerful monarch.</note> on the accusation of Henry VIII of England). They offer rewards—that is, great gifts or high rank—but he rejects them. <margin>[156]</margin> This will produce mere applause and approval in the...

---

*) It is quite advisable to praise actions that shine with a great, selfless, sympathetic disposition and humanity. But here, one must draw attention not so much to the "elevation of the soul" <note>original: "Seelenerhebung"</note>, which is very fleeting and temporary, but rather to the "submission of the heart" <note>original: "Herzensunterwerfung"</note> under duty, from which a more lasting impression can be expected because it carries principles with it (whereas the former are only emotional outbursts). One only needs to reflect a little, and one will always find some debt <note>original: "Schuld" — can mean both "debt" and "guilt."</note> he has incurred toward the human race (even if it is only that, due to the inequality of people in the civil constitution <term>civil constitution: the social and legal structure of a state</term>, he enjoys advantages for which others must do without all the more). This reflection prevents the self-loving imagination of "merit" from displacing the thought of duty.

# Doctrine of Method of Pure Practical Reason. 197

...affect the listener's soul, because it represents a gain. Now, however, one begins with the threat of loss. Among these slanderers are his best friends, who now renounce their friendship; close relatives, who threaten to disinherit him (as he is without property); powerful men, who can persecute and harm him in every place and condition; and a sovereign, who threatens him with the loss of freedom, and indeed life itself. But in order to make the measure of suffering full, so that he also feels the pain that only a <margin>10</margin> morally good heart can feel quite deeply, one may represent his family, threatened by extreme distress and poverty, beseeching him to yield. He himself—though upright, yet certainly not possessed of tough, unfeeling organs of sensation for either compassion or his own <note>The Academy edition reads "eigener" (his own).</note> distress—at a moment when he wishes he had never lived to see the day that exposed him to such unspeakable pain, nevertheless remains true to his resolve of honesty, without wavering or even doubting. My <margin>20</margin> youthful listener will be raised step by step from mere approval to admiration, from there to amazement, and finally to the greatest veneration and a lively wish that he himself could be such a man (though certainly not in such circumstances). And yet, virtue is worth so much here only because it costs so much, not because it brings in any profit. The entire admiration, and even the striving for likeness with this character, rests here entirely on the purity of the moral principle, <margin>30</margin> which can only be represented in a truly striking way by removing everything that men might count toward <term>happiness</term> <note>original: "Glückseligkeit" — in Kantian ethics, this refers to the sum of all satisfaction of desires and inclinations, which he distinguishes from moral duty.</note> from the <term>incentives</term> <note>original: "Triebfedern" — literally the "mainsprings" or drivers of an action.</note> of the action. Thus, <term>morality</term> <note>original: "Sittlichkeit".</note> must have all the more power over the human heart the more purely it is presented. From this it follows that, if the moral law and the image of holiness and virtue are to exert any influence on our soul at all, they <note>Or perhaps "it," referring specifically to the moral law.</note> can do so only insofar as...

<summary>Kant concludes his thought experiment by arguing that the purity of a moral act is most visible when it is performed at great personal cost, proving that moral law inspires more respect when separated from self-interest.</summary>

## Second Part.

<margin>10, [157], 20</margin>
...can exert [influence], only when it is laid upon the heart in its pure form, unmixed with intentions for one's own well-being as an <term>incentive<note>original: "Triebfeder" — literally the "driving spring" or motive that pushes a person to act.</note></term>. This is because virtue shows itself most gloriously in the midst of suffering. However, whatever thing's removal strengthens the effect of a moving force must have been an obstacle. Consequently, any mixture of incentives taken from one's own <term>happiness<note>original: "Glückseligkeit" — the state of well-being or contentment that individuals naturally seek for themselves.</note></term> is a hindrance to providing the <term>moral law<note>The objective rule of conduct that applies to all rational beings regardless of their desires.</note></term> with influence over the human heart. — I further maintain that even in that admired action, if the motive from which it occurred was high regard for one's <term>duty<note>original: "Pflicht" — the necessity of an action done out of respect for the law.</note></term>, then it is precisely this <term>respect<note>original: "Achtung" — the specific feeling of reverence one has for the moral law.</note></term> for the law—not some claim to an inner reputation for magnanimity or a noble, meritorious way of thinking—that has the greatest power over the <term>mind<note>original: "Gemüt" — refers to the human spirit, disposition, or the seat of feeling and thought.</note></term> of the observer. Therefore, duty, not merit, must have not only the most definite influence, but also, when presented in the proper light of its inviolability, the most penetrating influence on the spirit.

<margin>30, 40</margin>
In our times, where people hope to achieve more through melting, soft-hearted feelings or high-flying, puffed-up pretensions—which make the heart wither rather than strengthen it—than through the dry and earnest presentation of duty, which is more appropriate to human imperfection and progress in the good, the pointing out of this method is more necessary than ever. To present actions to children as "noble," "magnanimous," or "meritorious" as a model, with the intention of winning them over by instilling an enthusiasm for such things, is entirely counterproductive. For since they are still so far behind in the observation of the most common duty—and even in the correct judgment of it—this is equivalent to making them into <term>fantasts<note>original: "Phantasten" — individuals who lose themselves in unrealistic, emotional moral ideals instead of practical, grounded duty.</note></term> prematurely. But even for the more instructed and experienced part of humanity, this supposed incentive has—if not a detrimental effect—at least no genuine moral effect on the heart, which is what one had hoped to achieve through it.

### Doctrine of Method of Pure Practical Reason.

...glances <note>This continues from the previous page, likely referring to "glances" or "impressions" of noble deeds.</note>, as they are in their intensity and, before they fade away, perform their effect; otherwise, they do nothing. For the heart naturally returns to its natural, moderate movement of life and thus falls back into the <term>languor<note>original: "Mattigkeit" — a state of sluggishness or lack of energy.</note></term> that was previously its own; this happens because while something was brought to it that stimulated it, nothing was brought that strengthened it. **Principles** must be built upon **concepts**; on any other foundation, only fits of feeling <margin>10</margin> can arise, which can provide the person with no moral value—not even a confidence in oneself, without which the consciousness of one’s moral disposition and such a character (the highest good in man) cannot exist at all. Now, if these concepts are to become subjectively practical, they must not stop at the objective laws of morality simply to admire them and prize them highly in relation to humanity; instead, one must consider the representation of these laws in relation to the human being and to his own individuality. For then, that <margin>20 [158]</margin> law appears in a form that is indeed highly worthy of respect, but not as pleasant as if it belonged to the elements to which he is naturally accustomed. Instead, it appears as something that often forces him to leave those accustomed ways—not without <term>self-denial<note>original: "Selbstverleugnung" — the act of setting aside one's own desires or interests for the sake of a higher principle.</note></term>—and to enter into a higher state, in which he can only maintain himself with effort and with a constant fear of backsliding. In a word, the **moral law** demands compliance from **duty**, not from preference, which one cannot and should not even presuppose. <margin>30</margin>

Let us now see by way of example whether there lies more subjective moving force in the representation of an action as a "noble" and "magnanimous" deed than if this action is represented merely as a **duty** in relation to the earnest moral law. The action of someone seeking to save people from a shipwreck at the greatest risk to his own life—even if he eventually loses his life in the attempt—is indeed reckoned on the one hand as a duty, but on the other hand (and for the most part) as a meritorious action; <margin>40</margin> but our high esteem for it is greatly affected by the concept of duty toward oneself,

<summary>Kant argues that moral character must be built on rational concepts of duty rather than fleeting emotions or "noble" impulses. He introduces an example of a life-saving act during a shipwreck to distinguish between acting from duty and acting from a sense of merit.</summary>

More decisive is the magnanimous sacrifice of one’s life for the preservation of the fatherland; and yet, whether it be so completely a duty to devote oneself to this purpose on one’s own and unbidden, about this some <term>scruple<note>original: "Skrupel" — a feeling of doubt or hesitation regarding the morality of an action.</note></term> remains, and the action does not contain within itself the full force of a model and an incentive for imitation. <margin>10</margin> law in itself and without regard to human welfare, thereby treading its holiness underfoot (the kind of duties one is accustomed to call duties toward God, because we think of the ideal of holiness in substance within Him), then we devote the most perfect respect to its observance, even with the sacrifice of everything that might ever have value for the most inward of all our inclinations. We find our soul strengthened and elevated by such an example, when we 
<margin>20</margin> can convince ourselves through it that human nature is capable of such a great elevation above everything that nature may ever bring to bear as an incentive to the contrary. # Doctrine of Method of Pure Practical Reason.

If we can bring anything flattering regarding the meritorious into our actions, then the <term>incentive<note>original: "Triebfeder" — the subjective ground of desire that moves a person to act.</note></term> is already somewhat mixed with self-love, and thus receives some assistance from the side of <term>sensibility<note>original: "Sinnlichkeit" — the capacity of the mind to be affected by sensory objects.</note></term>. But to subordinate everything else to the holiness of duty alone, and to become aware that one *can* do so because our own reason recognizes this as its command and says that one *ought* to do it—this is, as it were, to raise oneself entirely above the sensory world itself. In the same consciousness of the lawᵃ<margin>10</margin> this is also inseparable as an incentive of a faculty that rules over sensibility. Although this is not always connected with an immediate effect, through frequent engagement with this lawᵇ and the initially smaller attempts at its use, it gives hope for its realization, so as to gradually produce in us the greatest, yet pureᶜ, moral interest in it.

The method, therefore, takes the following course. First, it is simply a matter of making the judgment according to <margin>20</margin> moral laws a natural occupation that accompanies all our own free actions as well as the observation of those of others, and making it, as it were, a habit; one must sharpen this judgment by first asking: whether the action is objectively in accordance with the moral law, and with which one. In doing so, one distinguishes the attention toward that law which merely provides a ground for obligation from that which is in fact obligating (<term>laws providing grounds for obligation<note>Latin: "leges obligandi"</note></term> from <term>obligating laws<note>Latin: "legibus obligantibus"</note></term>). For example, one might distinguish between the law of what the *needs* of men demand of me, in contrast to what the *rights* of men demand of me; the latter involves essential duties, while the former involves only non-essential duties—<margin>30</margin>

---

<note>Phalaris was a tyrant of Akragas, Sicily; the artist Perillus was forced to create a brazen bull for him in which criminals were killed by a fire placed underneath. [Author's Note]</note>
<note>a) Likely better: "and from the same consciousness, that of the law."</note>
<note>b) This likely refers to the "incentive."</note>
<note>c) "Pure" [per Natorp]? See, however, page 193, line 7.</note>

<summary>Kant explains that the awareness of duty allows a person to rise above sensory inclinations, and he outlines a method for moral education that begins by training the mind to habitually judge actions against the moral law.</summary>
<keywords>incentive, duty, sensibility, moral law, habit, obligation, rights versus needs, Phalaris, Perillus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Doctrine of Method," which describes how to make the moral law a powerful influence on the human heart. It completes the discussion on the two points of moral appraisal: first, whether an action is objectively right, and second (on this page), whether it was done with a pure moral intention.</meta>

...prescribes duties) and thus teaches how to distinguish various duties that converge in a single action. The other point to which attention must be directed is the question: whether the action also (subjectively) occurred for the sake of the **moral law**, and thus whether it possesses not only moral correctness as a deed, but also moral worth as a disposition according to its maxim?

<margin>10</margin>Now, there is no doubt that this exercise, and the consciousness of a resulting cultivation of our <term>reason<note>original: "Vernunft" — here referring to our ability to judge what we ought to do.</note></term> in its practical application, must gradually produce a certain interest, even in the law of reason itself, and consequently in morally good actions. For we eventually come to love that which allows us to feel the expanded use of our cognitive powers, especially that which promotes the moral correctness we encounter; this is because reason can only truly feel at home in an order of things where it can determine *a priori* <margin>[160]</margin> <note>meaning: prior to experience, based on pure principles of thought</note> <margin>20</margin>according to principles what ought to happen. Indeed, a natural observer eventually grows fond of objects that were initially offensive to his senses when he discovers the great purposiveness of their organization, and thus feeds his reason upon their contemplation. For example, Leibniz carefully returned an insect, which he had observed closely through a microscope, back to its leaf, because he had found himself instructed by the sight of it and had, as it were, received a benefit from it.

<margin>30</margin>But this occupation of <term>judgment<note>original: "Urteilskraft" — the talent of applying general rules to specific instances.</note></term>, which lets us feel our own cognitive powers, is not yet the interest in the actions and their <term>morality</term> itself. It merely makes one enjoy engaging in such appraisal and gives <term>virtue<note>original: "Tugend" — the moral strength of a person's will in fulfilling their duty.</note></term>, or the way of thinking according to moral laws, a form of beauty that is admired but not yet sought after ("it is praised and freezes")<sup>a)</sup> <note>original: *laudatur et alget* — a quote from the Roman satirist Juvenal, meaning something is admired in theory but left neglected in practice.</note>; just as everything whose contemplation subjectively produces a consciousness...

---

a) = "It is praised and dies of cold" (Juvenal 1, 74).
->[Editor's Note]<-

<summary>Kant explains that while intellectual appraisal of moral actions is a pleasant exercise of reason, it is not yet true morality. He uses the example of Leibniz and an insect to show how intellectual discovery creates affection, but warns that virtue must be more than something merely admired from a distance.</summary>

<keywords>moral law, disposition, cognitive powers, Leibniz, Juvenal, judgment, virtue, moral worth</keywords>

--- Page 257 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Doctrine of Method," which explores how to make the moral law a powerful influence on the human heart. It bridges the gap between aesthetic appreciation—where we enjoy the harmony of our own mental faculties—and the "second exercise" of moral education, which focuses on recognizing the purity of the human will.</meta>

### Doctrine of Method of Pure Practical Reason.

...brings about the harmony of our powers of representation, and whereby we feel our entire cognitive faculty (understanding and imagination) strengthened, producing a satisfaction that can also be communicated to others. In this state, the existence of the object remains a matter of indifference to us, as it is viewed only as the occasion to become aware of the predisposition of talents within us that are elevated above <term>animality<note>original: "Tierheit" — referring to the biological and instinctive drives humans share with animals.</note></term>. 

Now, however, the second exercise begins its task: namely, to make the **purity of the will** noticeable through the 
<margin>10</margin> vivid presentation of moral disposition in examples. At first, this is presented only as a "negative perfection" of the will—insofar as, in an action performed as a duty, no <term>incentives<note>original: "Triebfedern" — the psychological "springs" or motives that push us to act, such as hunger, fear, or vanity.</note></term> of our <term>inclinations<note>original: "Neigungen" — our natural desires and emotional pulls.</note></term> flow into it as determining grounds. By this means, the student’s attention is kept focused on the consciousness of their **freedom**. Although this self-denial causes an initial feeling of pain, it nevertheless—by removing the student from the compulsion of even 
<margin>20</margin> true needs—simultaneously announces a liberation from the manifold discontent in which all these needs entangle them. This makes the mind receptive to the feeling of satisfaction from other sources. [161] 

The heart is indeed freed and relieved of a burden that always secretly presses upon it when, through examples of pure moral resolutions, an inner power is revealed to the human being—one that is otherwise not even properly known to them. This is 
<margin>30</margin> **inner freedom**: the capacity to detach oneself from the unruly pushiness of one’s inclinations so thoroughly that not even the most beloved desire has any influence on a resolution for which we must now use our **reason**. 

In a case where I alone know that the wrong is on my side, and although the free confession of it and the offer of satisfaction finds such great opposition from my vanity, my self-interest, and even from my otherwise not-unlawful resentment against the one whose rights 
<margin>40</margin> I have diminished—if I can nevertheless set myself above all these hesitations, there is a consciousness of an <unclear>independ-</unclear> <note>The word "Unabhängigkeit" (independence) is cut off at the page break.</note>

<summary>Kant explains that moral education must transition from mere intellectual appreciation to a "second exercise" where the student recognizes their "inner freedom"—the power to act on moral principles regardless of personal desires or needs.</summary>
<keywords>Doctrine of Method, inner freedom, purity of will, inclinations, incentives of action, moral education, reason, independence</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page concludes the "Doctrine of Method," continuing the discussion on how the consciousness of moral freedom serves as a psychological foundation for character. It transitions from the "negative perfection" of resisting desires to the "positive value" of self-respect.</meta>

## Second Part.

...independence from <term>inclinations<note>original: "Neigungen" — our natural desires and emotional pulls.</note></term> and from circumstances of luck, and the possibility of being self-sufficient, which is beneficial to me everywhere and in other respects as well. And now the law of duty finds easier entry through the positive value that its observance lets us feel, by means of respect for ourselves in the consciousness of our **freedom**. Upon this freedom—when it is well-founded, when a person fears nothing more than to find themselves 
<margin>10</margin> contemptible and reprehensible in their own eyes during internal self-examination—every good <term>moral disposition<note>original: "sittliche Gesinnung" — the underlying character or internal attitude behind an action.</note></term> can now be grafted; because this is the best, indeed the only, guardian to prevent the entry of ignoble and corrupting impulses from the mind.

I have intended here only to point out the most general <term>maxims<note>original: "Maximen" — the personal rules or principles we set for ourselves when acting.</note></term> of the <term>doctrine of method<note>original: "Methodenlehre" — the study of how to communicate and instill philosophical principles in the human mind.</note></term> for moral education and practice. Since the variety of duties for each type would still require special 
<margin>20</margin> determinations and would thus constitute an extensive business, I trust I will be excused if, in a writing such as this—which is only a preliminary exercise—I let the matter rest with these basic outlines.

<summary>Kant concludes his "Doctrine of Method" by arguing that self-respect, rooted in the awareness of one's own freedom, is the only effective guardian of a moral character. He clarifies that this section serves as a general outline for moral education rather than an exhaustive list of every specific duty.</summary>
<keywords>freedom, self-respect, moral education, maxims, doctrine of method, duty, self-examination, inclinations</keywords>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>This page begins the famous "Conclusion" of the "Critique of Practical Reason," moving from the technical "Methodology" of moral education to a lyrical and profound reflection on the human condition, situated between the vastness of the cosmos and the internal moral law.</meta>

# Conclusion.

Two things fill the mind with ever new and increasing admiration and awe, the more often and steadily reflection dwells upon them: **the starry heavens above me and the moral law within me.** Both of these I need not seek as if veiled in darkness or in the <term>transcendent<note>original: "Überschwenglichen" — referring to things that lie beyond the limits of human experience or the physical world.</note></term> beyond my horizon, nor merely suspect them; I see them before me and connect them 
<margin>10</margin>immediately with the consciousness of my existence. The first begins from the place I occupy in the external <term>world of the senses<note>original: "Sinnenwelt" — the physical, material world we perceive through our eyes, ears, and touch.</note></term>, and expands the connection in which I stand into the immeasurably vast—with worlds upon worlds and systems of systems—and moreover into the boundless times of their periodic motion, their beginning and duration. The second begins from my invisible self, my **personality**, and represents me in a world that has true infinity, but is perceptible only to the understanding; and with this world (thereby also simultaneously with all those visible worlds) I 
<margin>20</margin>recognize myself not as being in a merely accidental connection, but in a universal and necessary one. The former view of a countless multitude of worlds annihilates, as it were, my importance as an *animal creature*, which must return the matter from which it was made to the planet (a mere speck in the universe) after being provided with vital force for a short time (no one knows how). The second, by contrast, infinitely elevates my value as an 
<margin>30</margin><term>intelligence<note>original: "Intelligenz" — in this context, a being capable of rational thought and self-determination, distinct from a mere biological organism.</note></term> through my personality, in which the moral law reveals to me a life independent of animality and even of the entire world of the senses—at least as far as can be inferred from the purp- <note>The text breaks off here at "zweck-," likely continuing to "zweckmäßigen Bestimmung" (purposive determination) on the next page.</note>

<summary>In this iconic passage, Kant contrasts the insignificance of human physical existence in a vast universe with the infinite dignity granted to humans through the internal moral law. This marks the transition from his technical philosophical system to its profound existential implications.</summary>
<keywords>starry heavens, moral law, personality, intelligence, world of the senses, infinity, human dignity</keywords>

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Conclusion" of the Critique of Practical Reason. The text flows from the previous page's discussion on how the moral law elevates human value beyond mere animal existence, connecting the individual to an infinite, rational order.</meta>

...purposive determination of my existence through this law, which is not restricted to the conditions and limits of this life, but extends into the infinite.

Yet, while admiration and respect can indeed stimulate inquiry, they cannot compensate for the lack of it. What must now be done to conduct this inquiry in a useful way, appropriate to the sublimity of the subject? Examples may serve here
<margin>10</margin> as a warning, but also for imitation. The contemplation of the world began with the most magnificent sight that human senses can ever present and which our understanding, in its vast scope, can ever bear to pursue, and it ended—with <term>astrology<note>original: "Sterndeutung" — the interpretation of stars; Kant is contrasting the pseudo-science of astrology with the later success of astronomy.</note></term>. Morality began with the noblest quality in moral nature, the development and cultivation of which aims at infinite benefit, and it ended—with <term>fanaticism<note>original: "Schwärmerei" — a term Kant uses to describe a state of deluded, pseudo-religious exaltation or "visionary" thinking that lacks a basis in reason.</note></term> or superstition. This is the fate of all crude attempts in
<margin>20</margin> which the principal part of the task depends on the use of <term>reason<note>original: "Vernunft" — the highest human faculty, which seeks universal principles.</note></term>—which, unlike the use of our feet, does not come about by itself through frequent practice, especially when it concerns qualities
<margin>[163]</margin> that cannot be so directly presented in common experience. But after the <term>maxim<note>original: "Maxime" — a subjective rule or principle that a person follows.</note></term> had come into vogue (albeit late) of carefully considering in advance all the steps that reason intends to take, and of letting it proceed only along the tracks of a well-thought-out method, the judgment of the <term>cosmic structure<note>original: "Weltgebäudes" — literally the "world-building," referring to the physical universe as a structured system.</note></term> 
<margin>30</margin> took on a completely different direction and, with it, an incomparably more successful outcome. The fall of a stone, the motion of a sling—resolved into their elements and the forces manifesting within them, and then treated mathematically—finally produced that clear and, for all future time, unchangeable insight into the structure of the world which, with continued observation, can hope to only ever expand, and never need fear having to retreat.

<margin>40</margin> To now take this same path in the treatment of the <term>moral predispositions<note>original: "moralischen Anlagen" — the inherent capacities within human nature for moral development.</note></term> of our nature: that example can advise us and give us hope for a similar [success]...

<summary>Kant argues that while awe of the cosmos and the moral law is a starting point, true understanding requires a rigorous, scientific method. He compares the shift from astrology to astronomy with the need to move from moral superstition to a systematic study of human reason.</summary>
<keywords>methodology, reason, astrology, fanaticism, moral predispositions, scientific method, cosmic structure</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>This page concludes the "Conclusion" of the Critique of Practical Reason. The text follows the previous page's comparison between the development of astronomy (from astrology) and the potential development of a scientific approach to morality (from fanaticism), emphasizing the need for a methodical, "chemical" separation of rational and empirical elements.</meta>

# Conclusion.

...a successful result. After all, we have at hand examples of <term>reason<note>original: "Vernunft" — in Kant’s philosophy, the highest faculty of the human mind, which seeks universal principles and laws.</note></term> as it makes moral judgments. To break these down into their **elementary concepts**—and, in the absence of mathematics, to adopt a procedure similar to that of **chemistry** (the separation of the <term>empirical<note>original: "Empirischen" — knowledge derived from sensory experience or observation.</note></term> from the <term>rational<note>original: "Rationalen" — knowledge derived from pure logic and reason, independent of experience.</note></term> that might be found in them) through repeated experiments on common human understanding—can make both of these clear to us in their purity. It allows us to know with certainty what each can achieve on its own. This method can prevent, on the one hand, the errors of a still 
<margin>10</margin>crude, unpracticed judgment, and on the other hand (which is far more necessary), it can prevent those <term>flights of genius<note>original: "Genieschwüngen" — refers to the tendency of some thinkers to leap to profound conclusions through "intuition" or "inspiration" rather than rigorous logic.</note></term>. By these leaps, as is common among <term>adepts of the philosopher’s stone<note>A reference to alchemists who claimed they could turn lead into gold; Kant uses them as a metaphor for people who promise "get-rich-quick" spiritual or intellectual results without doing the hard work of science.</note></term>, dreamed-of treasures are promised and true ones are squandered, all without any methodical investigation or knowledge of nature. In a word: **Science** (sought through critique and introduced methodically) is the narrow gate that leads to the **doctrine of wisdom**, if by this we understand not merely what one should *do*, but what should serve as a 
<margin>20</margin>**guideline** for teachers. This guide allows them to well and clearly pave the way to wisdom, which everyone should travel, and to secure others against taking the wrong paths. **Philosophy** must always remain the guardian of this science. While the general public has no part in philosophy's subtle investigations, they certainly have an interest in the teachings which, only after such rigorous processing, can finally shine with true clarity.

<summary>Kant concludes by arguing that morality must be studied like a science, using a "chemical" analysis to separate pure reason from mere experience. He characterizes philosophy as the "narrow gate" and guardian of wisdom, protecting people from both crude judgment and the delusions of "visionary" geniuses.</summary>
<keywords>moral reason, elementary concepts, chemistry metaphor, empirical vs rational, flights of genius, philosopher's stone, science, doctrine of wisdom, philosophy as guardian</keywords>

## G.

**Feeling**, always sensory 98 150 or <term>pathological<note>In a philosophical context, "pathological" does not mean a medical illness; it refers to being "affected" by sensory desires, emotions, or physical needs rather than by pure reason.</note></term> (see that term) 97,

# THE WORKS OF ARISTOTLE
->In three half-parchment volumes<-

**Metaphysics.** Translated, explained, and provided with a biography by Dr. E. Rolfes. 2nd, improved edition. Part 1 (Books I—VII). 1921. XXIV, 209 pages.
— Part 2. (Books VIII—XIV). 1921. 226 pages.
> The translation, truly no easy task, has been excellently successful; it bears witness everywhere to a profound understanding. This is particularly evident in the third part of the work: in the notes to the individual books. Everywhere, one sees a thorough knowledge of Platonic and Aristotelian philosophy and complete familiarity with the relevant literature.
->*Prof. A. Stölzle in the Theological Review.*<-

**Politics.** Newly translated by Doctor of Theology Eugen Rolfes. 3rd, revised edition. 1922. XXXI, 341 pages.
> An extremely precise rendering of the text with conscientious utilization of previous works! The author understands excellently how to reproduce the sober clarity of the language of the great Greek in German <note>rendered here as English</note>. Through his translation, one gains an immediate view of the weighing caution and restraint that is particularly characteristic of this specific Aristotelian work.
->*Theological Literature Gazette.*<-

**Three Books on the Soul.** <note>original: "Drei Bücher über die Seele," known by the Latin title "De Anima."</note> Newly translated by Gymnasium Director Dr. A. Busse. 2nd edition. 1922. XX, 94 and 27 pages.
> This achievement far surpasses those previously available, to the extent that one should now only rely on this translation if one is ignorant of Greek. But it will also provide a welcome aid for the reader of the difficult original.
->*Literary Advisor of the Dürerbund.*<-

**Nicomachean Ethics.** Newly translated and explained by Doctor of Theology Eugen Rolfes. 2nd, improved edition, expanded with an index. 1921. XXIV, 268 pages.

**Poetics.** Newly translated by Prof. Dr. A. Gudeman. 1921. XXIV, 91 pages.

**Organon.** <note>The "Organon" is the traditional name for Aristotle's collected works on logic.</note> Translated and provided with notes by Dr. E. Rolfes.
— **Categories** (along with the introduction by Porphyry <note>original: "Isagoge"</note>). 1920. VIII, 86 pages.
— **On Interpretation** <note>original: "Peri hermenias"</note> or: Doctrine of the Sentence. 1920. VII, 42 pages.
— **Theory of the Syllogism**, or: Prior Analytics. 1922. X, 209 pages.
— **Theory of Proof**, or: Posterior Analytics. 1922. XVIII, 164 pages.
— **Topics.** 1919. XVII, 227 pages.
— **Sophistical Refutations.** 1918. IX and 80 pages.

---

## History of Aristotelian Philosophy in Protestant Germany
->1921 By Peter Petersen XII, 534 pages.<-

> An exhaustive presentation of the influence of Aristotle on the thought-world of Protestantism was previously missing. One was almost inclined to assume that the effects of Aristotle were determined by denomination. Now this book shows that the Reformers Luther and Melanchthon themselves started from Aristotle. It follows the effects of Aristotle further through Nicolaus Taurellus to Leibniz, via Pufendorf and Christian Wolff to Gottsched, Lessing, Goethe, and the other poetic heroes.

---

->**PUBLISHED BY FELIX MEINER IN LEIPZIG**<-

# THE WORKS OF RENÉ DESCARTES

->Bound in two gift volumes<-

This most comprehensive German edition of Descartes acquires special value through an extensive general index, which represents a long-missing and valuable tool for the study of the philosopher's writings.

**Discourse on the Method.** <note>original: "Abhandlung über die Methode"</note> 3rd edition. 1919. 82 pages.
**Rules for the Direction of the Mind. The Search for Truth** <note>The "natural light" mentioned here refers to human reason as an innate capacity to discover truth without divine revelation.</note> through the natural light. 2nd edition. 1920. 168 pages.
The "Rules" and "The Search for Truth" appear here for the very first time in German translation. The "Rules" form the foundational methodological work of Descartes' philosophy: they contain the epistemological investigations and the studies on the fundamental problems of mathematics in a clarity that is not surpassed, indeed hardly ever reached, by his later works. "The Search," however, forms an important supplement to many questions touched upon in the "Rules."
Latin Edition. **Rules for the Direction of the Mind.** <note>original: "Regulae ad directionem ingenii"</note> Edited from the original 1701 edition by A. Buchenau. 1907. IV, 66 pages. (Outside of the collected works edition)

**Meditations on the Foundations of Philosophy** <note>original: "Meditationes de prima philosophia"</note> with all objections and replies. In the sixth edition, translated in full for the first time. 1915. XIV, 493 pages.
A long-held wish of those organizing philosophical seminar exercises has found fulfillment here. The Objections and Replies <note>original: "Objectiones und Responsiones"</note> belong organically together with Descartes' Meditations. They represent a natural commentary that cannot be replaced by anything else. Admittedly, a high degree of publishing idealism was required to venture the complete translation of these pieces, which take up more than five times as much space as the Meditations themselves. Everything has been done to create an accurate and readable text.
— *Theological Literature Report.*

---
— (Text of the Meditations only.) 78 pages.
Latin Edition. **Meditations on First Philosophy.** <note>original: "Meditationes de prima philosophia"</note> Supervised by A. Buchenau. 1913. (Outside of the collected works edition)

**The Principles of Philosophy.** With an appendix containing remarks on a certain program. 4th edition. 1922. 48, 310 pages.
The "Principles" constitute Descartes' attempt to apply his fundamental methodological views to the entire field of metaphysics and natural science. Here we learn to appreciate this spirit—as comprehensive as it is sharp—in all its greatness; a spirit that created a complete world-view with such sparse means, the effect of which will endure forever. — *Erich Becher in the "Literary Central Journal."*

**On the Passions of the Soul.** 3rd edition. 1911. XXXII, 120 and 30 pages. With the general index.

**Jungmann, K. René Descartes.** An introduction to his works. 1908. VIII, 234 pages.

**Schneider, H. Gassendi’s Position toward Descartes.** <note>Pierre Gassendi (1592–1655) was a French philosopher and scientist who famously challenged Descartes' dualism.</note> 1904. 68 pages.

# SPINOZA, COLLECTED WORKS
### Translated by O. Baensch, A. Buchenau, and C. Gebhardt
->In 3 half-parchment volumes<-
<note>Half-parchment refers to a style of bookbinding where the spine is covered in parchment and the rest of the cover in paper or cloth.</note>

This is the only German edition of Spinoza’s works based on the revolutionary results of modern textual criticism. <note>Textual criticism is the scholarly process of comparing different manuscript versions to reconstruct the author's original intended words.</note> Thus, its textual arrangement offers the most reliable foundation for research; the introductions strive to ensure a comprehensive understanding of Spinoza’s writings from all perspectives.

->Available individually:<-

**Treatise on God, Man, and His Well-Being.** <note>original: "Abhandlung von Gott, dem Menschen und dessen Glück," often referred to as the Short Treatise.</note> 4th edition. Newly translated by C. Gebhardt. 1922. XXVIII, 156 pages.
**Ethics.** Translated and provided with an introduction and index by Otto Baensch. 10th edition. 1922. 29, 276, and 39 pages. In half-parchment.
> Recent research on the Spinoza text is treated with great precision. The introduction is among the best that can be given as an entry point into Spinoza's way of thinking. The significance of this translation may be seen in the way it clarifies the shifts in thought within Spinoza’s work, which have often become difficult for us to follow.
->Journal for German Instruction.<- <note>original: "Zeitschrift für den deutschen Unterricht."</note>

**Theologico-Political Treatise.** 4th edition. Translated and introduced by C. Gebhardt. 1921. 34, 362, and 61 pages.
**Descartes' Principles of Philosophy, Proven in a Geometric Manner. — Appendix, containing Metaphysical Thoughts.** 3rd edition. Newly translated by A. Buchenau. 1907. 8, 190 pages. <note>Spinoza initially wrote these to explain Descartes' system before publishing his own original philosophy.</note>
**Treatise on the Emendation of the Intellect. — Political Treatise.** 4th edition. Translated and introduced by Carl Gebhardt, 1907. 32, 181, and 33 pages.
**Correspondence.** Translated and provided with an introduction, notes, and index by Carl Gebhardt. 1914. 38, 438 pages.
> Goethe called Spinoza’s correspondence the most interesting book one could read in the world in terms of sincerity and love for humanity. For us, it represents the necessary supplement to Spinoza's Ethics, as it reveals the deep and pure humanity that stands behind the mathematically rigid propositions of that book.
->Journal for German Instruction.<-

**Biographies and Conversations.** Edited by C. Gebhardt. 1914. XI, 147 pages. With illustration.
> A completely new phenomenon in German literature is Gebhardt’s translation of the old biographies of Spinoza, to which are added the traditional recorded statements or conversations of Spinoza, as well as all sources relating to his life. It is a most rewarding book that deserves full recognition. Spinoza belongs to those philosophers whose doctrine requires the supplement of the man’s personal image. Therefore, the biographies of Spinoza deserve strong interest as a reflection of a great human being.
->Journal for German Instruction.<-

**Spinoza Breviary.** <note>A "breviary" is a concise collection or handbook of key texts.</note> Compiled and provided with an introduction by A. Liebert. 2nd edition. 1918. XXXIV, 169 pages.
> It should be described as a fortunate idea of Liebert’s that in his Breviary, the most significant passages of the "Ethics" have been freed from the narrow shackles of the geometric method. <note>Spinoza wrote the Ethics using the "geometric method," meaning he structured his arguments like mathematical proofs with definitions, axioms, and propositions.</note> He himself provides information in a substantial preface about the principles that guided him... To all those who cannot find the necessary leisure and patience to reach for the original works of the philosopher, but who may find interest in that "great and free view over the sensory and moral world" that seemed to open up to Goethe from Spinoza's writings, Liebert's Breviary is highly recommended.
->Vienna Foreign Journal.<- <note>original: "Wiener Fremdenblatt."</note>

<meta>This page continues the publisher’s catalog for the Felix Meiner publishing house in Leipzig. While the previous page focused on specific editions of Spinoza’s collected works, this page introduces a thematic series titled "Knowledge and Research," which offers accessible introductions to the major figures and branches of philosophy.</meta>

# Knowledge and Research
## Writings for Introduction to Philosophy

This collection is intended to serve the need for explanations of specific philosophical classics and for introductions to the fundamental problems of philosophy. Free from any one-sidedness and acknowledging the diversity of contemporary philosophical schools, it aims to form a central point for all efforts that, from a scientific basis and in generally understandable language, intend to introduce the reader to the vast field of philosophical reading and research.

**Vol. I: Kant's Doctrine of the Categorical Imperative.** <note>The "Categorical Imperative" is the central ethical principle in Immanuel Kant's philosophy, suggesting one should act only according to rules that could become universal laws.</note> An introduction to the fundamental questions of Kantian ethics in connection with the "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals." By Dr. A. Buchenau. 1913. XII, 125 pages.

**Vol. II: Contemporary Philosophy and Christian Religion.** Presented in connection with Vaihinger, Rehmke, and Eucken by Dr. H. Hegenwald. 1913. XII, 196 pages.

**Vol. III: Fundamental Problems of the Critique of Pure Reason.** An introduction to Kantian <term>epistemology</term> <note>Epistemology is the branch of philosophy concerned with the nature and limits of human knowledge.</note>. By City School Councilor Dr. Artur Buchenau. 1914. VI, 194 pages.

**Vol. IV: How is Critical Philosophy Possible at All?** A contribution to the systematic <term>phenomenology</term> <note>Phenomenology is the philosophical study of structures of consciousness and the way things appear to us.</note> of philosophy. By Prof. Dr. Arthur Liebert. 1919. XVII, 228 pages.

**Vol. V: Outline of Aesthetics.** By Benedetto Croce. German translation by Dr. Th. Poppe. 1913. IV, 85 pages.

**Vol. VI: The Soul.** Its relationship to consciousness and the body. By Jos. Geyser. 1914. VI, 117 pages.

**Vol. VII: The Founders of Modern Psychology.** Lotze, Fechner, Helmholtz, Wundt. By Stanley Hall, President of Clark University. Translated and provided with notes by Raym. Schmidt. With a foreword by Max Brahn. 1914. 28, 392 pages.

**Vol. VIII: Introduction to Philosophy.** From the standpoint of <term>criticism</term> <note>In this context, "Criticism" refers to the philosophical method of Immanuel Kant, which examines the foundations and limits of human reason.</note>. By Dr. Kurt Sternberg. 1919. XIII, 291 pages.

**Vol. IX: Pestalozzi's Social Philosophy.** A presentation based on the "Inquiries into the Course of Nature in the Development of the Human Race." By Dr. Art. Buchenau. 1919. VIII, 183 pages.

**Vol. X: Moral Demands and the Question of Their Validity.** By Gust. Störring. 1920. VIII, 136 pages.

**Vol. XI: Introduction to Epistemology.** By Aug. Messer. 2nd, revised edition. 1921. IV, 221 pages.

**Vol. XII: Philosophy of History.** An introduction. By Prof. Dr. Otto Braun. 1921. VIII, 120 pages.

**Vol. XIII: Commentary on Zarathustra.** <note>This refers to a commentary on Friedrich Nietzsche's famous work, "Thus Spoke Zarathustra."</note> By Hans Weichelt. 2nd, newly revised edition. 1922. VI, 366 pages.
